================================================================================
  STAGE 11: UNDERGRADUATE FINAL YEAR / HONOURS — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
  Advanced electives
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 18 topics (11.1 to 11.18, the same as in topic.txt), in five
  strands: Analysis (11.1-11.3), Number theory and arithmetic geometry
  (11.4, 11.5, 11.10), Geometry, algebra and topology (11.6-11.9, 11.11,
  11.17), Applied and discrete mathematics (11.12-11.16) and the Research
  project (11.18).
  Each topic has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of definitions, theorems,
                                  standard examples, counterexamples and
                                  methods in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): proofs,
                                  explanations, derivations and worked
                                  numerical problems, each with the answer
                                  and "Teacher explains" (the method step
                                  by step as a teacher shows it on the
                                  board, common mistakes, and a "Now you
                                  try" question)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure drills with
                                  answers. Most have many parts (a, b,
                                  c ...) so that EVERY case of the topic is
                                  practised again and again, from the
                                  easiest to the hardest — many measures
                                  and integrals, many norms and operators,
                                  many number fields and curves, many
                                  groups, rings and characters, many
                                  chains, maps and graphs, many codes and
                                  data sets — not just one example of each
                                  idea.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the student struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 216 descriptive + 216 mathematical questions, then a mixed
  revision test (equal descriptive and mathematical parts, two questions
  from every topic in each part) and a readiness checklist for Stage 12.
- Level: final-year honours mathematics: Measure and Integration, Lp
  spaces, Functional Analysis, Algebraic and Analytic Number Theory,
  Manifolds, Lie Groups and Lie Algebras, Commutative Algebra,
  Representation Theory, Elliptic Curves, Riemann Surfaces, Stochastic
  Processes, Dynamical Systems and Chaos, Graph Theory and Algorithms,
  Information Theory, Multivariate Statistics and Statistical Learning,
  Differential Topology, and the Research Project.
- What is new in Stage 11: DEPTH AND CHOICE. The student meets the
  modern forms of the subjects of Stage 10 — the Lebesgue integral that
  makes limits and integrals commute, spaces of functions treated as
  geometric spaces, primes studied through rings and through complex
  analysis, curved spaces that are locally Euclidean, groups studied
  through matrices, randomness and chaos as mathematical objects — and
  begins to read and write mathematics like a researcher.
- Conventions: N = {1, 2, 3, ...} unless 0 is stated; log means ln unless
  a base is written (log₂ in information theory); m or λ is Lebesgue
  measure; a.e. = almost everywhere; ‖·‖ is a norm; ⟨·,·⟩ an inner
  product (linear in the first slot); Zₙ = Z/nZ; Fₚ is the field with p
  elements; ≅ means isomorphic; □ ends a proof; exact answers first,
  decimals with ≈.
- Method: for every theorem know its hypotheses and a counterexample when
  one is dropped; for every computation know which theorem justifies it.
  Use Python/SageMath/R to CHECK calculations and to explore, and write
  every solution in LaTeX.
- Stage 11 is a menu of electives: a typical honours student takes 6-10
  of them in depth and meets the others at the level of the checklist and
  Part B. Do 2-3 hours a day: 2 descriptive questions (write the full
  proof or solution) and 2 drills per session, plus weekly mixed revision.
  Topic 11.18 (the project) runs alongside everything else all year.
- Revise Stage 10 (A10.txt) whenever the student finds something
  difficult; especially 10.1-10.5 (analysis, metric spaces) before
  11.1-11.3, 10.15-10.21 (groups, rings, fields, Galois) before 11.4,
  11.8, 11.9, 11.10, 10.9-10.11 (complex analysis) before 11.5 and 11.11,
  10.7-10.8 and 10.25-10.28 (inverse function theorem, forms, topology,
  surfaces, π₁) before 11.6, 11.7 and 11.17, 10.33 (phase plane) before
  11.13, 10.35-10.37 (probability, statistics) before 11.12 and 11.16, and
  10.43-10.46 (combinatorics, computation, coding) before 11.14 and 11.15.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.1 — MEASURE THEORY AND LEBESGUE INTEGRATION: SIGMA-ALGEBRAS,
LEBESGUE MEASURE, MEASURABLE FUNCTIONS, MONOTONE AND DOMINATED CONVERGENCE,
FUBINI
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - σ-ALGEBRA on X: a family 𝒜 of subsets with X ∈ 𝒜, closed under
    complements and COUNTABLE unions (hence countable intersections,
    differences, lim sup and lim inf of sets). (X, 𝒜) is a MEASURABLE
    SPACE. The σ-algebra GENERATED by a family 𝒞, σ(𝒞), is the
    intersection of all σ-algebras containing 𝒞. BOREL sets 𝔅(R) =
    σ(open sets) = σ(intervals (a, b]) = σ(rays (−∞, a]).
  - MEASURE: μ : 𝒜 → [0, ∞] with μ(∅) = 0 and COUNTABLE ADDITIVITY
    μ(⋃Aₙ) = ∑μ(Aₙ) for disjoint Aₙ. Consequences: monotone, countably
    SUBadditive, CONTINUITY FROM BELOW (Aₙ ↑ A ⇒ μ(Aₙ) ↑ μ(A)) and FROM
    ABOVE (Aₙ ↓ A and μ(A₁) < ∞ ⇒ μ(Aₙ) ↓ μ(A); fails without μ(A₁) < ∞:
    Aₙ = [n, ∞)). Examples: counting measure, Dirac measure δₓ, Lebesgue
    measure, probability measures.
  - LEBESGUE OUTER MEASURE m*(E) = inf{∑ ℓ(Iₖ) : E ⊆ ⋃Iₖ, Iₖ open
    intervals}; m*(interval) = length; m* is translation invariant and
    countably subadditive; countable sets have measure 0. CARATHÉODORY:
    E is measurable iff m*(A) = m*(A ∩ E) + m*(A ∖ E) for every A; the
    measurable sets form a σ-algebra ℒ ⊇ 𝔅 on which m is a complete
    measure. REGULARITY: m(E) = inf{m(U) : U ⊇ E open} = sup{m(K) : K ⊆ E
    compact}. The VITALI set shows not every subset of R is measurable.
  - NULL SETS AND THE CANTOR SET: Q, any countable set, and the Cantor set C
    (uncountable!) have measure 0; fat Cantor sets are nowhere dense with
    positive measure. "Almost everywhere" (a.e.) = outside a null set.
  - MEASURABLE FUNCTIONS: f : X → [−∞, ∞] with {f > a} ∈ 𝒜 for every a
    (equivalently f⁻¹(B) ∈ 𝒜 for Borel B). Continuous and monotone
    functions are Borel measurable; sums, products, max, min, sup, inf,
    lim sup, lim inf and pointwise limits of measurable functions are
    measurable. SIMPLE functions ∑ aₖ𝟙_{Aₖ}; every f ≥ 0 measurable is the
    increasing limit of simple functions φₙ = min(n, 2⁻ⁿ⌊2ⁿf⌋).
  - LEBESGUE INTEGRAL: ∫∑aₖ𝟙_{Aₖ} = ∑aₖμ(Aₖ) for simple φ ≥ 0; ∫f =
    sup{∫φ : 0 ≤ φ ≤ f simple} for f ≥ 0; f is INTEGRABLE (f ∈ L¹) if
    ∫|f| < ∞, and then ∫f = ∫f⁺ − ∫f⁻. Linear, monotone, |∫f| ≤ ∫|f|,
    ∫|f| = 0 ⇔ f = 0 a.e. MARKOV/CHEBYSHEV: μ{|f| ≥ t} ≤ (1/t)∫|f|.
  - RIEMANN VS LEBESGUE: a bounded f on [a, b] is Riemann integrable iff it
    is continuous a.e. (Lebesgue's criterion), and then the two integrals
    agree. The Dirichlet function 𝟙_Q is not Riemann integrable but ∫𝟙_Q
    dm = 0. Absolutely convergent improper Riemann integrals are Lebesgue
    integrals; ∫₀^∞ (sin x)/x dx converges only improperly (not in L¹).
  - MONOTONE CONVERGENCE THEOREM (MCT): 0 ≤ f₁ ≤ f₂ ≤ ... measurable, fₙ →
    f ⇒ ∫fₙ → ∫f. Corollary: ∫∑gₙ = ∑∫gₙ for gₙ ≥ 0. FATOU'S LEMMA: ∫lim
    inf fₙ ≤ lim inf ∫fₙ for fₙ ≥ 0 (strict for moving bumps).
  - DOMINATED CONVERGENCE THEOREM (DCT): fₙ → f a.e. and |fₙ| ≤ g with g ∈
    L¹ ⇒ ∫|fₙ − f| → 0 and ∫fₙ → ∫f. Standard failures without a
    dominating g: n𝟙_(0,1/n), 𝟙_[n,n+1], (1/n)𝟙_[0,n]. Uses: limits under
    the integral, continuity and DIFFERENTIATION UNDER THE INTEGRAL SIGN
    (|∂f/∂t| ≤ g ∈ L¹).
  - MODES OF CONVERGENCE: a.e., in measure, in L¹. L¹ ⇒ in measure ⇒ a
    subsequence a.e.; on a finite measure space a.e. ⇒ in measure
    (EGOROFF: a.e. ⇒ uniform off a set of small measure). The "typewriter"
    sequence converges in L¹ but nowhere pointwise.
  - PRODUCT MEASURE AND FUBINI-TONELLI: on σ-finite spaces there is a unique
    μ × ν with (μ × ν)(A × B) = μ(A)ν(B). TONELLI: for f ≥ 0 measurable the
    iterated integrals (in either order) equal ∬ f d(μ × ν). FUBINI: the
    same for f ∈ L¹(μ × ν). Check ∬|f| < ∞ first (by Tonelli), or the
    orders may disagree: f = (x² − y²)/(x² + y²)² on (0, 1)² gives ±π/4.
  - LEBESGUE-STIELTJES MEASURES AND DENSITIES: an increasing right-
    continuous F gives μ_F(a, b] = F(b) − F(a). If g ≥ 0 then ν(A) = ∫_A g
    dμ is a measure with ∫f dν = ∫fg dμ (Radon-Nikodym in reverse); change
    of variables ∫_{T(A)} f = ∫_A (f ∘ T)|det DT|.
  - CONTINUITY OF THE INTEGRAL: if f ∈ L¹ then for every ε > 0 there is δ >
    0 with μ(A) < δ ⇒ ∫_A |f| < ε; F(x) = ∫₀ˣ f is (absolutely) continuous,
    and F′ = f a.e. (Lebesgue differentiation theorem).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Let X = {1, 2, 3, 4}. (a) Find σ(𝒞) for 𝒞 = {{1}, {2}} and list its
    elements. (b) Is the family {∅, X, {1}, {2, 3, 4}, {1, 2}, {3, 4}} a
    σ-algebra? (c) Prove that an intersection of σ-algebras is a
    σ-algebra but a union need not be.
    Answer: (a) 8 sets: generated by the partition {1}, {2}, {3, 4}.
    (b) No. (c) Proof below; union counterexample on X = {1, 2, 3}.
    Teacher explains: A σ-algebra on a FINITE set is always generated by a
    partition into "atoms": every set in it is a union of atoms, so it has
    2^(number of atoms) elements. Here the atoms are the smallest non-empty
    sets we can make from {1}, {2} and complements: {1}, {2}, and X ∖ {1, 2}
    = {3, 4}. So σ(𝒞) consists of all unions of these three atoms:
       ∅, {1}, {2}, {3,4}, {1,2}, {1,3,4}, {2,3,4}, X     (2³ = 8 sets).
    (b) It contains {1} and {1, 2} but not {1}ᶜ ∩ {1, 2} = {2}; indeed
    {2} = {1, 2} ∩ {2, 3, 4} must lie in any σ-algebra containing both, so
    the family is not closed under intersections. (c) If each 𝒜ᵢ contains
    X and is closed under complements and countable unions, so is
    ⋂𝒜ᵢ: e.g. if Aₙ ∈ ⋂𝒜ᵢ then Aₙ ∈ 𝒜ᵢ for every i, so ⋃Aₙ ∈ 𝒜ᵢ for
    every i. Union: on {1, 2, 3}, 𝒜₁ = {∅, X, {1}, {2, 3}} and
    𝒜₂ = {∅, X, {2}, {1, 3}}; 𝒜₁ ∪ 𝒜₂ contains {1} and {2} but not
    {1, 2}. Common mistakes: forgetting ∅ and X; listing only the given
    sets and their complements (you must also take unions). Now you try:
    how many sets are in σ({1, 2}, {2, 3}) on {1, 2, 3, 4}? (atoms {1}, {2},
    {3}, {4}: 16)

D2. Prove, from countable additivity, (a) monotonicity, (b) countable
    subadditivity, (c) continuity from below and (d) continuity from above
    when μ(A₁) < ∞. Show (d) fails for Aₙ = [n, ∞) in R.
    Answer: proofs by "disjointification"; m([n, ∞)) = ∞ for all n but
    ⋂[n, ∞) = ∅.
    Teacher explains: (a) If A ⊆ B, B = A ∪ (B ∖ A) disjointly, so μ(B) =
    μ(A) + μ(B ∖ A) ≥ μ(A). (b) Given A₁, A₂, ..., put B₁ = A₁ and Bₙ = Aₙ
    ∖ (A₁ ∪ ... ∪ Aₙ₋₁). The Bₙ are disjoint, Bₙ ⊆ Aₙ and ⋃Bₙ = ⋃Aₙ, so
    μ(⋃Aₙ) = ∑μ(Bₙ) ≤ ∑μ(Aₙ). (c) If A₁ ⊆ A₂ ⊆ ..., the rings Bₙ =
    Aₙ ∖ Aₙ₋₁ (A₀ = ∅) are disjoint with A_N = B₁ ∪ ... ∪ B_N, so
       μ(⋃Aₙ) = ∑_{n=1}^{∞} μ(Bₙ) = lim_N ∑_{n≤N} μ(Bₙ) = lim_N μ(A_N).
    (d) If A₁ ⊇ A₂ ⊇ ... and μ(A₁) < ∞, apply (c) to A₁ ∖ Aₙ ↑ A₁ ∖ A:
    μ(A₁) − μ(Aₙ) → μ(A₁) − μ(A); subtraction is legal because everything is
    finite. Counterexample: m([n, ∞)) = ∞ for all n, but the intersection
    is ∅ of measure 0. Common mistakes: writing μ(B ∖ A) = μ(B) − μ(A)
    when μ(A) = ∞ (∞ − ∞ is meaningless). Now you try: in counting measure
    on N, find decreasing sets with infinite measure and empty
    intersection. ({n, n + 1, ...})

D3. (a) Show that every countable set E ⊆ R has Lebesgue measure 0.
    (b) Construct the Cantor set C, show m(C) = 0 and that C is
    uncountable. (c) Build a "fat" Cantor set of measure 1/2.
    Answer: (a) cover the k-th point by an interval of length ε/2ᵏ.
    (b) removed length 1, C ↔ ternary expansions with digits 0, 2.
    (c) at step n remove 2ⁿ⁻¹ middle intervals of length 1/4ⁿ.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let E = {x₁, x₂, ...} and ε > 0. Iₖ = (xₖ −
    ε/2ᵏ⁺¹, xₖ + ε/2ᵏ⁺¹) has length ε/2ᵏ, and the Iₖ cover E, so m*(E) ≤
    ∑ε/2ᵏ = ε. As ε is arbitrary, m(E) = 0. In particular m(Q) = 0 and
    m([0, 1] ∖ Q) = 1. (b) C₀ = [0, 1]; Cₙ₊₁ removes the open middle third
    of each of the 2ⁿ intervals of Cₙ (each of length 3⁻ⁿ); C = ⋂Cₙ.
    m(Cₙ) = (2/3)ⁿ → 0, so m(C) = 0 by continuity from above. Equivalently
    the removed length is 1/3 + 2/9 + 4/27 + ... = (1/3)/(1 − 2/3) = 1.
    C is exactly the set of x with a ternary expansion using only digits 0
    and 2; the map 0.d₁d₂d₃...(base 3) ↦ 0.(d₁/2)(d₂/2)...(base 2) sends C
    onto [0, 1], so C is uncountable. (c) At step n remove from the middle
    of each of the 2ⁿ⁻¹ remaining intervals an open interval of length
    4⁻ⁿ. The total removed is ∑_{n≥1} 2ⁿ⁻¹4⁻ⁿ = (1/4)∑(1/2)ⁿ⁻¹ = 1/2, so the
    set left is closed, nowhere dense and has measure 1/2. Common mistakes:
    "measure zero means countable" (C is a counterexample); "nowhere dense
    means measure zero" (the fat Cantor set). Now you try: remove middle
    fifths instead of thirds: the measure of what remains? (0: (4/5)ⁿ → 0)

D4. Explain the construction of a non-measurable (Vitali) set and why no
    translation-invariant, countably additive measure can measure every
    subset of R with m([0, 1]) = 1.
    Answer: choose one point from each coset of Q in [0, 1]; its rational
    translates would give 1 ≤ ∑m(V) ≤ 3, impossible.
    Teacher explains: Declare x ~ y if x − y ∈ Q. Each class meets [0, 1];
    by the axiom of choice pick one point from each class, forming V ⊆
    [0, 1]. Enumerate the rationals q₁, q₂, ... of [−1, 1] and put Vₖ =
    V + qₖ. (i) The Vₖ are DISJOINT: if v + qₖ = w + qⱼ then v − w ∈ Q, so v
    = w (one point per class) and qₖ = qⱼ. (ii) [0, 1] ⊆ ⋃Vₖ ⊆ [−1, 2]:
    every x ∈ [0, 1] is equivalent to some v ∈ V with x − v ∈ [−1, 1] ∩ Q.
    If V were measurable, translation invariance gives m(Vₖ) = m(V), and
    countable additivity gives
       1 ≤ ∑_{k=1}^{∞} m(V) ≤ 3.
    If m(V) = 0 the sum is 0 < 1; if m(V) > 0 the sum is ∞ > 3. Both are
    contradictions, so V is not measurable. This is why measure theory
    works with a σ-algebra rather than all subsets. Common mistakes:
    thinking V is "small" or "large" — it has outer measure > 0 but no
    measure; forgetting that the proof needs choice (Solovay: without it,
    all sets can be measurable). Now you try: why can we not use all reals
    q (not just rationals) in the argument? (then the union is uncountable
    and additivity says nothing)

D5. Prove that the sum and the product of two measurable functions f, g :
    X → R are measurable, and that sup fₙ and lim sup fₙ are measurable.
    Answer: {f + g > a} = ⋃_{r∈Q} ({f > r} ∩ {g > a − r}); fg = ((f + g)² −
    (f − g)²)/4; {sup fₙ > a} = ⋃{fₙ > a}.
    Teacher explains: f + g > a ⇔ f > a − g ⇔ there is a rational r with f
    > r > a − g (density of Q). So
       {f + g > a} = ⋃_{r ∈ Q} ({f > r} ∩ {g > a − r}),
    a COUNTABLE union of measurable sets. For squares: {f² > a} = X if a <
    0 and {f > √a} ∪ {f < −√a} if a ≥ 0; −f and cf are clearly
    measurable, so fg = ((f + g)² − (f − g)²)/4 is measurable. For
    suprema: sup fₙ(x) > a ⇔ some fₙ(x) > a, so {sup fₙ > a} = ⋃ₙ{fₙ >
    a}. Similarly inf fₙ = −sup(−fₙ), and lim sup fₙ = inf_k sup_{n≥k} fₙ,
    so it is measurable; lim fₙ, where it exists, equals lim sup fₙ. The
    key point: everything uses only COUNTABLE operations. Common mistakes:
    writing {f + g > a} = {f > a/2} ∪ {g > a/2} (false: f = 3, g = −1,
    a = 1); taking a supremum over uncountably many functions (not
    measurable in general). Now you try: show max(f, g) is measurable.
    ({max > a} = {f > a} ∪ {g > a})

D6. State the monotone convergence theorem. Use it to (a) show that
    ∫₀¹ (−ln x)/(1 − x) dx = ∑_{n≥1} ∫₀¹ xⁿ⁻¹(−ln x) dx and evaluate it;
    (b) justify ∫₀^∞ x/(eˣ − 1) dx = π²/6.
    Answer: both equal ∑1/n² = π²/6 ≈ 1.64493.
    Teacher explains: MCT: if 0 ≤ f₁ ≤ f₂ ≤ ... and fₙ → f pointwise, then
    ∫fₙ → ∫f (the value ∞ is allowed). Its corollary for series with
    NON-NEGATIVE terms: ∫∑gₙ = ∑∫gₙ (apply MCT to the partial sums).
    (a) On (0, 1), −ln x ≥ 0 and 1/(1 − x) = ∑_{n≥1} xⁿ⁻¹, so the integrand
    is ∑ gₙ with gₙ = xⁿ⁻¹(−ln x) ≥ 0. By parts (or the substitution
    x = e⁻ᵗ),
       ∫₀¹ xⁿ⁻¹(−ln x) dx = ∫₀^∞ t e^(−nt) dt = 1/n².
    Therefore ∫₀¹ (−ln x)/(1 − x) dx = ∑1/n² = π²/6. (b) For x > 0,
    x/(eˣ − 1) = x e⁻ˣ/(1 − e⁻ˣ) = ∑_{n≥1} x e^(−nx), terms ≥ 0, and ∫₀^∞
    x e^(−nx) dx = 1/n². So the integral is ∑1/n² = π²/6. Without MCT we
    would need uniform convergence, which FAILS near x = 0 in (a). Common
    mistakes: exchanging sum and integral for series with terms of both
    signs without checking ∑∫|gₙ| < ∞. Now you try: ∫₀^∞ x²/(eˣ − 1) dx.
    (2ζ(3) ≈ 2.40411)

D7. State Fatou's lemma and prove it from the MCT. Show that the
    inequality can be strict and that the hypothesis fₙ ≥ 0 cannot be
    dropped.
    Answer: ∫lim inf fₙ ≤ lim inf ∫fₙ; strict for fₙ = n𝟙_(0,1/n); fails for
    fₙ = −n𝟙_(0,1/n).
    Teacher explains: Put gₖ = inf_{n≥k} fₙ. Then 0 ≤ g₁ ≤ g₂ ≤ ... and gₖ ↑
    lim inf fₙ, so by MCT ∫gₖ → ∫lim inf fₙ. But gₖ ≤ fₙ for every n ≥ k,
    so ∫gₖ ≤ inf_{n≥k} ∫fₙ. Letting k → ∞,
       ∫ lim inf fₙ = lim ∫gₖ ≤ lim inf ∫fₙ.  □
    Strict: fₙ = n𝟙_(0,1/n) on [0, 1] has fₙ(x) → 0 for every x, so ∫lim
    inf fₙ = 0, while ∫fₙ = 1 for all n. The mass "escapes" into a
    spike. Negative functions: fₙ = −n𝟙_(0,1/n) has lim inf fₙ = 0 but
    ∫fₙ = −1, and 0 ≤ −1 is false. Picture Fatou as "mass can only be lost
    in the limit, never created". Common mistakes: using lim instead of
    lim inf when the limit does not exist; forgetting that ∫gₖ ≤ ∫fₙ needs
    n ≥ k. Now you try: fₙ = 𝟙_[n,n+1] on R. Compute both sides. (0 < 1)

D8. State the dominated convergence theorem. Evaluate, with full
    justification,
    (a) lim_{n→∞} ∫₀^∞ (1 + x/n)⁻ⁿ sin(x/n) dx,
    (b) lim_{n→∞} ∫₀^n (1 − x/n)ⁿ dx,
    (c) lim_{n→∞} ∫₀¹ n x/(1 + n²x²) dx.
    Answer: (a) 0; (b) 1; (c) 0 (but the integrals behave like
    (ln(1 + n²))/(2n)).
    Teacher explains: DCT: fₙ → f a.e., |fₙ| ≤ g with ∫g < ∞ ⇒ ∫fₙ → ∫f.
    The whole art is finding g. (a) For n ≥ 2, (1 + x/n)ⁿ ≥ 1 + x +
    (n(n − 1)/2)(x/n)² ≥ 1 + x²/4 (binomial theorem, (n − 1)/n ≥ 1/2), so
    |fₙ| ≤ 1/(1 + x²/4) = g, with ∫₀^∞ g = π. Pointwise fₙ(x) → e⁻ˣ · 0 = 0.
    So the limit is 0. (b) Write fₙ = (1 − x/n)ⁿ𝟙_[0,n](x). Since
    1 − t ≤ e⁻ᵗ, 0 ≤ fₙ ≤ e⁻ˣ = g ∈ L¹, and fₙ(x) → e⁻ˣ. Limit ∫₀^∞ e⁻ˣ dx
    = 1. (Check: ∫₀ⁿ(1 − x/n)ⁿ dx = n/(n + 1) → 1 ✓.) (c) fₙ(x) → 0 for
    every x (at x = 0, fₙ = 0). Is there a dominating g? sup_n fₙ(x) = 1/2
    (attained at n = 1/x), so g = 1/2 works on [0, 1]: limit 0. Directly:
    ∫₀¹ nx/(1 + n²x²) dx = ln(1 + n²)/(2n) → 0 ✓. Common mistakes: using the
    pointwise limit without a dominating function; choosing a g that
    depends on n. Now you try: lim ∫₀^∞ n sin(x/n)/(x(1 + x²)) dx. (π/2:
    |fₙ| ≤ 1/(1 + x²), fₙ → 1/(1 + x²))

D9. Using DCT, justify differentiation under the integral sign and compute
    F(t) = ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) cos(tx) dx by solving an ODE for F.
    Answer: F′(t) = −(t/2)F(t), F(0) = √π/2, so F(t) = (√π/2)e^(−t²/4).
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: if f(·, t) ∈ L¹ for each t, ∂f/∂t exists
    and |∂f/∂t(x, t)| ≤ g(x) ∈ L¹ for all t, then F′(t) = ∫∂f/∂t dx. Proof:
    the difference quotient (f(x, t + h) − f(x, t))/h equals ∂f/∂t(x, ξ)
    by the MVT, so it is bounded by g; let h → 0 along any sequence and
    use DCT. Here ∂f/∂t = −x e^(−x²) sin(tx), bounded by x e^(−x²) ∈ L¹.
    So
       F′(t) = −∫₀^∞ x e^(−x²) sin(tx) dx.
    Integrate by parts with u = sin(tx), dv = −x e^(−x²) dx, v = e^(−x²)/2:
       F′(t) = [e^(−x²) sin(tx)/2]₀^∞ − (t/2)∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) cos(tx) dx
             = −(t/2)F(t).
    The ODE F′ = −(t/2)F gives F(t) = F(0)e^(−t²/4) with F(0) = ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²)
    dx = √π/2. Check at t = 2: F(2) = (√π/2)e⁻¹ ≈ 0.32603. Common
    mistakes: a bound g that depends on t; forgetting to check that the
    boundary term vanishes. Now you try: G(t) = ∫₀^∞ e^(−tx) dx = 1/t;
    differentiate twice to get ∫₀^∞ x² e^(−tx) dx. (2/t³)

D10. State Tonelli's and Fubini's theorems. (a) Compute ∫₀^∞ ∫₀^∞
     e^(−(x²+y²)) dx dy in polar coordinates and deduce ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) dx.
     (b) Show that for f(x, y) = (x² − y²)/(x² + y²)² on (0, 1)² the two
     iterated integrals are π/4 and −π/4, and explain which hypothesis
     fails.
     Answer: (a) π/4, so ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) dx = √π/2. (b) ∬|f| = ∞.
     Teacher explains: TONELLI (f ≥ 0 measurable, σ-finite spaces): both
     iterated integrals equal the double integral, finite or not. FUBINI:
     the same if ∬|f| < ∞. (a) The integrand is positive, so Tonelli lets
     us compute in any order or coordinates:
        ∬_{first quadrant} e^(−r²) r dr dθ = (π/2)·(1/2) = π/4.
     The left side is (∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) dx)², so the integral is √π/2.
     (b) ∂/∂y (y/(x² + y²)) = (x² − y²)/(x² + y²)². Hence
        ∫₀¹ f(x, y) dy = [y/(x² + y²)]₀¹ = 1/(1 + x²),
     and ∫₀¹ dx/(1 + x²) = π/4. By the antisymmetry f(y, x) = −f(x, y), the
     other order gives −π/4. Fubini does not apply: in polar coordinates
     |f| = |cos 2θ|/r², and ∬|f| r dr dθ contains ∫₀ dr/r = ∞ near the
     origin. Common mistakes: swapping the order without checking
     integrability; thinking Fubini fails for positive functions (Tonelli
     always works for f ≥ 0). Now you try: compute ∫₀¹∫₀¹ xy dx dy both
     ways. (1/4)

D11. Explain the four modes of convergence (uniform, a.e., in measure, in
     L¹) with the "typewriter" sequence, the escaping bump 𝟙_[n,n+1] and the
     spike n𝟙_(0,1/n). State Egoroff's theorem and apply it to xⁿ on [0, 1].
     Answer: typewriter: L¹ and in measure, nowhere a.e.; escaping bump:
     pointwise to 0, not in measure (infinite space) nor L¹; spike: a.e. and
     in measure, not L¹.
     Teacher explains: TYPEWRITER: list the intervals [0, 1], [0, ½], [½, 1],
     [0, ⅓], [⅓, ⅔], [⅔, 1], ... and let fₙ be the indicator of the n-th.
     ∫fₙ = length → 0, so fₙ → 0 in L¹ and in measure. But every x lies in
     infinitely many and misses infinitely many intervals, so fₙ(x)
     oscillates between 0 and 1 for every x: no pointwise limit. (A
     subsequence — the intervals [0, 1/k] — does converge a.e., as the
     theory predicts.) ESCAPING BUMP on R: fₙ(x) → 0 for every x, but
     m{fₙ = 1} = 1 for all n: not in measure, ∫fₙ = 1 ↛ 0. Egoroff needs
     finite measure. SPIKE n𝟙_(0,1/n) on [0, 1]: → 0 everywhere, m{fₙ > ε}
     = 1/n → 0 (in measure), but ∫fₙ = 1: not L¹. EGOROFF: on a finite
     measure space, fₙ → f a.e. ⇒ for every δ > 0 there is E with m(E) < δ
     and fₙ → f uniformly off E. For xⁿ on [0, 1]: take E = (1 − δ, 1];
     on [0, 1 − δ], sup xⁿ = (1 − δ)ⁿ → 0. Common mistakes: believing a.e.
     convergence implies L¹ convergence. Now you try: does fₙ = 𝟙_[0,1/n]
     converge a.e.? in L¹? (yes to 0 except at x = 0; yes)

D12. Show that if f ∈ L¹(R) then (a) for every ε > 0 there is δ > 0 such
     that m(A) < δ ⇒ ∫_A|f| < ε; (b) F(x) = ∫_{−∞}^x f is uniformly
     continuous; (c) lim_{h→0} ∫|f(x + h) − f(x)| dx = 0 (continuity of
     translation), first for f = 𝟙_[a,b], then in general.
     Answer: (a) truncate f at level N; (b) |F(x) − F(y)| ≤ ∫_[x,y]|f|;
     (c) ∫|𝟙_[a,b](x + h) − 𝟙_[a,b](x)| dx = 2|h| for |h| ≤ b − a, then
     approximate.
     Teacher explains: (a) Let fₙ = min(|f|, N). By MCT, ∫fₙ ↑ ∫|f|, so
     choose N with ∫(|f| − min(|f|, N)) < ε/2. Then for m(A) < δ = ε/(2N),
        ∫_A|f| ≤ ∫(|f| − min(|f|, N)) + ∫_A N < ε/2 + Nδ = ε.
     (b) For x < y, |F(y) − F(x)| ≤ ∫_{[x,y]}|f| < ε whenever y − x < δ,
     by (a). (c) For f = 𝟙_[a,b] and 0 < h ≤ b − a, f(x + h) − f(x) is ±1
     exactly on [a − h, a) and [b − h, b), so the integral is 2h → 0. By
     linearity the same holds for step functions. Step functions are
     dense in L¹: given ε, pick a step s with ∫|f − s| < ε; then
        ∫|f(x + h) − f(x)| ≤ ∫|f(x + h) − s(x + h)| + ∫|s(x + h) − s(x)|
                             + ∫|s − f| < 3ε for small h.
     This "ε/3 argument" is the model for many proofs in 11.2 and 11.3.
     Common mistakes: using uniform continuity of f (f need not be
     continuous). Now you try: compute ∫|𝟙_[0,1](x + h) − 𝟙_[0,1](x)| dx for
     h = 3. (2: the intervals no longer overlap)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. σ-algebras on finite sets: give the number of sets in σ(𝒞) on X.
    a) X = {1, 2, 3}, 𝒞 = {{1}}          b) X = {1, 2, 3}, 𝒞 = {{1}, {2}}
    c) X = {1, ..., 4}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2}}
    d) X = {1, ..., 4}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}}
    e) X = {1, ..., 5}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2}, {3}}
    f) X = {1, ..., 6}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2, 3}, {3, 4}}
    g) X = {1, ..., 6}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2}, {3, 4}, {5, 6}}
    h) X = {1, ..., 8}, 𝒞 = {{1, ..., 4}, {1, 2, 5, 6}, {1, 3, 5, 7}}
    i) X = {1, ..., 10}, 𝒞 = {{1, 2}, {2, 3}, {3, 4}}
    j) X = N, 𝒞 = {{n} : n ∈ N}: which sets?
    k) X = R, 𝒞 = {{x} : x ∈ R}: which sets?
    l) X = R, 𝒞 = {[0, 1]}: list the sets
    m) number of σ-algebras on a 3-element set
    n) number of σ-algebras on a 4-element set
    Answers:
      a) 4         b) 8          c) 4           d) 16 (atoms {1}, {2}, {3}, {4})
      e) 8         f) 16 (atoms {1,2}, {3}, {4}, {5,6})    g) 8
      h) 256 (all singletons are atoms)      i) 32 (atoms {1}, {2}, {3}, {4},
         {5, ..., 10})
      j) all subsets of N (every set is a countable union of singletons)
      k) the countable and co-countable subsets of R
      l) ∅, R, [0, 1], R ∖ [0, 1]
      m) 5 (Bell number B₃)                   n) 15 (Bell number B₄)

M2. Find the Lebesgue measure.
    a) [2, 7)                          b) {3}
    c) Q ∩ [0, 5]                      d) [0, 5] ∖ Q
    e) ⋃_{n≥1} (n, n + 2⁻ⁿ)            f) ⋃_{n≥1} [0, 1 − 1/n]
    g) ⋂_{n≥1} (−1/n, 1 + 1/n)         h) ⋂_{n≥1} [n, ∞)
    i) the Cantor set                  j) [0, 1] minus the Cantor set
    k) {x ∈ [0, 1] : the decimal expansion of x has no digit 7}
    l) {x ∈ [0, 1] : the ternary expansion of x has no digit 1 in the
       first 4 places}
    m) {(x, y) ∈ R² : x² + y² < 4}      n) {(x, y) : 0 ≤ y ≤ x², 0 ≤ x ≤ 3}
    o) the graph {(x, sin x) : x ∈ R} in R²
    p) {x ∈ R : sin x > 0} ∩ [0, 10π]
    q) {x ∈ [0, 1] : x = p/q with q ≤ 100}   r) {x ∈ R : x² < 7}
    s) ⋃_{k≥1} (qₖ − 2⁻ᵏ⁻², qₖ + 2⁻ᵏ⁻²), q₁, q₂, ... all rationals:
       an upper bound for its measure (it is open and dense!)
    t) {x ∈ [0, 1]: the binary digit in place 3 is 1}
    Answers:
      a) 5        b) 0        c) 0        d) 5        e) 1
      f) 1 (the union is [0, 1))           g) 1         h) 0
      i) 0        j) 1        k) 0 (measure (9/10)ⁿ → 0)
      l) (2/3)⁴ = 16/81       m) 4π       n) 9
      o) 0        p) 5π       q) 0        r) 2√7
      s) 1/2 (sum of lengths ∑2⁻ᵏ⁻¹): an open dense set of small measure
      t) 1/2

M3. Let μ be a measure. Compute or say "cannot be determined" (c.b.d.).
    a) μ(A) = 3, μ(B) = 4, A ⊆ B: μ(B ∖ A)
    b) μ(A) = 3, μ(B) = 4, μ(A ∩ B) = 1: μ(A ∪ B)
    c) μ(A) = 3, μ(B) = 4: μ(A ∪ B)
    d) μ(A) = ∞, A ⊆ B: μ(B ∖ A)
    e) Aₙ ↑ A, μ(Aₙ) = 5 − 1/n: μ(A)
    f) Aₙ ↓ A, μ(Aₙ) = 1 + 1/n: μ(A)
    g) Aₙ ↓ A, μ(Aₙ) = ∞ for all n: μ(A)
    h) disjoint Aₙ with μ(Aₙ) = 2⁻ⁿ: μ(⋃Aₙ)
    i) μ(Aₙ) = 3⁻ⁿ (not disjoint): an upper bound for μ(⋃Aₙ)
    j) Borel-Cantelli: ∑μ(Aₙ) < ∞ ⇒ μ(lim sup Aₙ) = ?
    k) Dirac δ₂ on R: δ₂([0, 3]), δ₂((2, 5)), δ₂(Q)
    l) counting measure on N: μ({even numbers}), μ({1, 5, 9})
    m) μ = δ₀ + 2δ₁ + 3δ₂: μ([0, 1]), μ((0, 2]), μ(R)
    n) μ(A) = ∫_A e⁻ˣ dx on [0, ∞): μ([0, 1]), μ([1, ∞)), μ([0, ∞))
    o) Lebesgue-Stieltjes with F(x) = x³: μ((1, 2])
    p) F(x) = ⌊x⌋: μ((0, 3]), μ({2}), μ((0.5, 0.9))
    q) F = 0 for x < 0, x for 0 ≤ x < 1, 2 for x ≥ 1: μ({1}), μ([0, 1]),
       μ(R)
    Answers:
      a) 1       b) 6       c) c.b.d. (between 4 and 7)       d) c.b.d.
      e) 5       f) 1       g) c.b.d. (e.g. [n, ∞) gives 0)   h) 1
      i) 1/2     j) 0       k) 1, 0, 0         l) ∞, 3
      m) 3, 5, 6            n) 1 − e⁻¹ ≈ 0.63212, e⁻¹ ≈ 0.36788, 1
      o) 7       p) 3, 1, 0              q) 1, 2, 2

M4. Measurable or not? (L = Lebesgue measurable, B = Borel, N = need not
    be measurable). Assume f, g, fₙ are Lebesgue measurable R → R.
    a) f + g                          b) fg
    c) |f|                            d) f²
    e) 𝟙_Q                            f) 𝟙_V (V a Vitali set)
    g) sup_n fₙ                        h) lim sup fₙ
    i) sup_{t ∈ R} f_t (uncountable family)
    j) a monotone function R → R      k) a continuous function
    l) f ∘ g (both Lebesgue measurable)
    m) f ∘ g with f continuous         n) {x : fₙ(x) converges}
    o) the Cantor function            p) 1/f (where f ≠ 0)
    q) the derivative f′ of a differentiable f
    r) max(f, 0)
    Answers:
      a) L      b) L      c) L      d) L      e) B (Q is Borel)
      f) N (not measurable)            g) L     h) L
      i) N (need not be)               j) B     k) B
      l) N (need not be; composition of Lebesgue functions can fail)
      m) L      n) L (defined by lim sup = lim inf)  o) B (continuous)
      p) L      q) B (a pointwise limit of difference quotients)  r) L

M5. Integrals of simple and non-negative functions (Lebesgue measure on
    the given interval unless stated).
    a) ∫(2𝟙_[0,3] + 5𝟙_[1,2])         b) ∫(𝟙_Q + 3𝟙_[0,1]) on [0, 2]
    c) ∫⌊x⌋ dx on [0, 4]              d) ∫⌊x²⌋ dx on [0, 2]
    e) ∫x dδ₃(x)                       f) ∫x² d(counting on {1, 2, 3})
    g) ∫f dμ, f(n) = 2⁻ⁿ, μ counting on N
    h) ∫f dμ, f(n) = 1/n, μ counting on N
    i) the Cantor function's integral ∫₀¹ c(x) dx
    j) ∫₀¹ f, f(x) = 1 on the Cantor set and x² elsewhere
    k) ∫₀¹ f, f(x) = 0 on Q, x elsewhere
    l) φ₂ = min(2, 2⁻²⌊2²f⌋) for f(x) = x on [0, 2]: ∫φ₂
    m) ∫_R e^(−|x|) dx                  n) ∫_R dx/(1 + x²)
    o) ∫₀¹ x^(−1/2) dx                  p) ∫₀¹ x⁻¹ dx
    q) ∫_{[1,∞)} x⁻ᵖ dx for p = 2, 1, 1/2
    r) ∫₀^∞ x⁻¹ sin x dx: is x⁻¹ sin x in L¹(0, ∞)?
    Answers:
      a) 11      b) 3       c) 6       d) 5 − √2 − √3 ≈ 1.85374
      e) 3       f) 14      g) 1       h) ∞
      i) 1/2 (symmetry c(1 − x) = 1 − c(x))      j) 1/3
      k) 1/2     l) 7/4 (steps of width 1/4 at heights 0, 1/4, ..., 7/4)
      m) 2       n) π       o) 2       p) ∞
      q) 1, ∞, ∞            r) no (∫|sin x|/x = ∞); the improper
         integral is π/2

M6. Monotone convergence: compute, justifying by MCT or its series form.
    a) lim ∫₀¹ x^(1/n) dx               b) lim ∫₀^n e⁻ˣ dx
    c) lim ∫₀¹ (1 − xⁿ) dx               d) lim ∫₀^∞ (1 − e^(−nx)) e⁻ˣ dx
    e) ∑_{n≥0} ∫₀¹ xⁿ(1 − x) dx          f) ∫₀¹ x⁻¹ ln(1/(1 − x)) dx
    g) ∫₀^∞ x/(eˣ − 1) dx               h) ∫₀^∞ x³/(eˣ − 1) dx
    i) ∫₀¹ (ln x)²/(1 − x) dx           j) ∫₀¹ ln(1 + x)/x dx (terms
                                           alternate: use DCT instead)
    k) lim ∫₀^n (1 + x/n)ⁿ e^(−2x) dx    l) lim ∫₀¹ n(x^(1/n) − 1) dx
    m) ∫₀^∞ ∑_{n≥1} e^(−n²x) dx          n) ∫₀¹ ∑_{n≥1} xⁿ/n dx
    Answers:
      a) 1       b) 1       c) 1       d) 1
      e) 1 (terms 1/((n + 1)(n + 2)))    f) π²/6 ≈ 1.64493
      g) π²/6    h) π⁴/15 ≈ 6.49394     i) 2ζ(3) ≈ 2.40411
      j) π²/12 ≈ 0.82247 (partial sums of the alternating series
         ∑(−1)ⁿxⁿ/(n + 1) are bounded by 1; term by term ∑(−1)ⁿ/(n + 1)²)
      k) 1 (integrand ↑ e⁻ˣ)
      l) −1 (−n(x^(1/n) − 1) ≥ 0 increases to −ln x; MCT gives −∫₀¹ ln x
         = 1; directly ∫ = −n/(n + 1) → −1)
      m) π²/6 (∫₀^∞ e^(−n²x) dx = 1/n²)
      n) 1 (∑1/(n(n + 1)))

M7. Dominated convergence (or its failure): find lim_{n→∞}. Say where no
    integrable dominating function exists.
    a) ∫₀¹ xⁿ dx                         b) ∫₀¹ n xⁿ(1 − x) dx
    c) ∫₀^∞ e⁻ˣ cos(x/n) dx               d) ∫₀¹ sin(nx)/(nx) dx
    e) ∫_R e^(−x²) cos(x/n) dx            f) ∫₀^∞ n/(1 + n²x²) dx
    g) ∫₀¹ (1 + nx²)/(1 + x²)ⁿ dx         h) ∫₀^∞ (1 + x/n)⁻ⁿ dx
    i) ∫₀¹ n²x e^(−nx) dx                 j) ∫₀¹ n xⁿ⁻¹/(1 + x) dx
    k) ∫₀^∞ dx/((1 + x²)(1 + xⁿ))         l) ∫₀¹ n^(3/2) x/(1 + n²x²) dx
    m) ∫₀^∞ n ln(1 + x/n) e⁻ˣ dx
    n) lim_{t→0⁺} ∫₀^∞ ((1 − e^(−tx))/t) e⁻ˣ dx
    Answers:
      a) 0         b) 0 (= n/((n + 1)(n + 2)))       c) 1 (= 1/(1 + 1/n²))
      d) 0 (bound 1)                     e) √π (= √π e^(−1/(4n²)))
      f) π/2 for every n, although the integrand → 0 for x > 0: no
         dominating function
      g) 0 (bound 1, since (1 + x²)ⁿ ≥ 1 + nx²)
      h) 1 (bound (1 + x/2)⁻² for n ≥ 2; exactly n/(n − 1))
      i) 1 (put t = nx: ∫₀ⁿ t e⁻ᵗ dt → 1): no dominating function
      j) 1/2 (the mass n xⁿ⁻¹ dx piles up at x = 1)
      k) π/4 (limit 1/(1 + x²) on [0, 1), 0 for x > 1)
      l) 0 (= ln(1 + n²)/(2√n))
      m) 1 (n ln(1 + x/n) ↑ x; MCT)       n) 1 ((1 − e^(−tx))/t ≤ x)

M8. Fubini and Tonelli: evaluate (check the hypothesis you use).
    a) ∫₀¹∫₀² xy² dy dx                 b) ∫₀¹∫ₓ¹ e^(y²) dy dx
    c) ∫₀¹∫_y¹ (sin x)/x dx dy          d) ∫₀^∞∫₀^∞ e^(−(x+y)) dx dy
    e) ∫₀^∞ (e⁻ˣ − e^(−3x))/x dx, via ∫₁³ e^(−xy) dy = (e⁻ˣ − e^(−3x))/x
    f) ∬_{x²+y²≤1} (x² + y²) dA         g) ∫_R e^(−x²/2) dx
    h) ∫₀¹∫₀¹ (x − y)/(x + y)³ dy dx, and the other order
    i) ∫₀¹∫₀¹ dx dy/(1 − xy)            j) ∫₀^∞∫₀^∞ e^(−x(1 + y²)) dx dy, and
                                          deduce ∫₀^∞ x^(−1/2) e⁻ˣ dx
    k) the volume of the unit ball in R³ by slicing
    l) ∫₀¹ (x − 1)/ln x dx, via ∫₀¹ xʸ dy
    m) counting measure on N × N, aₘₙ = 1 if m = n, −1 if m = n + 1, 0
       otherwise: ∑ₘ(∑ₙ aₘₙ) and ∑ₙ(∑ₘ aₘₙ)
    n) ∫₀^∞ (sin x)/x dx, via 1/x = ∫₀^∞ e^(−xy) dy (as lim_{A→∞} ∫₀^A)
    Answers:
      a) 4/3       b) (e − 1)/2 ≈ 0.85914       c) 1 − cos 1 ≈ 0.45970
      d) 1         e) ln 3 ≈ 1.09861           f) π/2
      g) √(2π) ≈ 2.50663
      h) 1/2 and −1/2 (inner integral 1/(1 + x)²; ∬|f| = ∞, Fubini fails)
      i) π²/6 (expand ∑(xy)ⁿ, Tonelli)
      j) π/2; the x-first order gives (√π/2)∫₀^∞ x^(−1/2)e⁻ˣ dx, so the
         integral is √π
      k) 4π/3 (∫₋₁¹ π(1 − z²) dz)       l) ln 2 ≈ 0.69315
      m) 1 and 0 (∑|aₘₙ| = ∞, so Fubini does not apply)
      n) π/2 (∫₀^∞ dy/(1 + y²))

M9. Differentiation under the integral sign (justify with a dominating
    function on a neighbourhood of the parameter).
    a) from ∫₀^∞ e^(−tx) dx = 1/t: ∫₀^∞ x e^(−tx) dx
    b) ∫₀^∞ x⁵ e⁻ˣ dx (differentiate ∫₀^∞ e^(−tx) dx five times, t = 1)
    c) from ∫₀¹ xᵃ dx = 1/(a + 1): ∫₀¹ x² ln x dx
    d) ∫₀¹ x²(ln x)² dx
    e) F(t) = ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) cos(tx) dx: F(1)
    f) I(t) = ∫₀¹ (xᵗ − 1)/ln x dx: I′(t) and I(3)
    g) ∫₀^∞ e⁻ˣ (sin tx)/x dx at t = 1
    h) J(a) = ∫₀^π ln(1 − 2a cos x + a²) dx: J(1/2) and J(2)
    i) from ∫₀^∞ e^(−ax²) dx = ½√(π/a): ∫₀^∞ x² e^(−x²) dx
    j) ∫₀^∞ (e^(−2x) − e^(−5x))/x dx
    k) Γ′(1) = ∫₀^∞ (ln x) e⁻ˣ dx
    l) F(t) = ∫₀^t cos(tx) dx: F′(1) by the Leibniz rule
    Answers:
      a) 1/t²      b) 120       c) −1/9      d) 2/27
      e) (√π/2)e^(−1/4) ≈ 0.69019        f) I′(t) = 1/(t + 1), I(3) = ln 4
      g) π/4 (the integral is arctan t)
      h) 0 and 2π ln 2 ≈ 4.35517         i) √π/4 ≈ 0.44311
      j) ln(5/2) ≈ 0.91629                k) −γ ≈ −0.57722
      l) 2cos 1 − sin 1 ≈ 0.23913 (F(t) = sin(t²)/t)

M10. For each sequence give (a.e.? in measure? in L¹?) convergence to 0 —
     answer Y or N three times. Lebesgue measure on the given space.
     a) 𝟙_[0,1/n] on [0, 1]             b) n𝟙_[0,1/n] on [0, 1]
     c) √n 𝟙_[0,1/n] on [0, 1]          d) 𝟙_[n,n+1] on R
     e) (1/n)𝟙_[0,n] on R               f) the typewriter sequence on [0, 1]
     g) xⁿ on [0, 1]                    h) n xⁿ on [0, 1]
     i) sin(nx) on [0, 2π]              j) the constants (−1)ⁿ/n on [0, 1]
     k) n²x e^(−nx) on [0, 1]           l) e^(−nx²) on R
     Answers:
       a) Y Y Y     b) Y Y N     c) Y Y Y     d) Y N N     e) Y Y N
       f) N Y Y     g) Y Y Y     h) Y Y N     i) N N N (∫|sin nx| = 4)
       j) Y Y Y     k) Y Y N (∫ → 1)           l) Y Y Y (∫ = √(π/n))

M11. Is f Riemann integrable (R) or not (N) on [0, 1] (proper Riemann
     integral, bounded f only)? Give its Lebesgue integral (∞ if f ≥ 0 is
     not integrable, "not L¹" otherwise).
     a) 𝟙_Q                             b) 𝟙_C (C the Cantor set)
     c) Thomae's function (1/q at x = p/q in lowest terms, 0 at irrationals)
     d) 𝟙_F, F a fat Cantor set of measure 1/2
     e) sin(1/x), f(0) = 0              f) x^(−1/2), f(0) = 0
     g) 1/x, f(0) = 0                   h) 1 at the points 1/n, 0 elsewhere
     i) ⌊1/x⌋, f(0) = 0                 j) the fractional part {1/x}, f(0) = 0
     k) x² on the irrationals, 0 on Q
     l) f = (−1)ⁿ n on (1/(n + 1), 1/n]
     Answers:
       a) N, 0      b) R, 0      c) R, 0
       d) N (discontinuous on F, of positive measure), 1/2
       e) R (one discontinuity), sin 1 − Ci(1) ≈ 0.50407
       f) N (unbounded), 2        g) N, ∞     h) R, 0
       i) N (unbounded), ∞ (∑n(1/n − 1/(n + 1)) = ∑1/(n + 1))
       j) R (countably many discontinuities), 1 − γ ≈ 0.42278
       k) N, 1/3
       l) N; not L¹ (∫|f| = ∑1/(n + 1) = ∞), although the improper limit
          of ∫_ε¹ f exists and equals ln 2 − 1 ≈ −0.30685

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every subset of a Lebesgue null set is Lebesgue measurable.
     b) Every Lebesgue measurable set is a Borel set.
     c) A set of Lebesgue measure 0 is countable.
     d) An open dense subset of [0, 1] has measure 1.
     e) If ∫|f| = 0 then f = 0 everywhere.
     f) If fₙ → f pointwise on [0, 1] then ∫fₙ → ∫f.
     g) If fₙ ↓ f ≥ 0 pointwise then ∫fₙ → ∫f.
     h) Convergence in L¹ implies convergence a.e.
     i) On [0, 1], a.e. convergence implies convergence in measure.
     j) The two iterated integrals agree for every measurable f on [0, 1]².
     k) Tonelli's theorem holds for every measurable f ≥ 0 on a product of
        σ-finite spaces.
     l) A bounded f on [a, b] is Riemann integrable iff it is continuous
        a.e.
     m) If f ∈ L¹(R) then f(x) → 0 as x → ∞.
     n) If f ∈ L¹(R) is uniformly continuous then f(x) → 0 as x → ±∞.
     o) A continuous function maps null sets to null sets.
     p) A Lipschitz function R → R maps null sets to null sets.
     q) If m(E) > 0 then E − E = {x − y : x, y ∈ E} contains an interval
        around 0.
     r) Every Lebesgue measurable function is a.e. equal to a Borel function.
     Answers:
       a) T (completeness)
       b) F: a non-Borel subset of the Cantor set (there are 2^𝔠 subsets of
          C, all null, but only 𝔠 Borel sets)
       c) F: the Cantor set          d) F: M2 s) (measure ≤ 1/2)
       e) F: 𝟙_Q                     f) F: n𝟙_(0,1/n)
       g) F: 𝟙_[n,∞) (true if ∫f₁ < ∞)
       h) F: typewriter (a subsequence does converge a.e.)
       i) T                          j) F: M8 h)
       k) T                          l) T (Lebesgue's criterion)
       m) F: a spike of height 1 and width 2⁻ⁿ at each integer n
       n) T                          o) F: the Cantor function maps C onto
                                        [0, 1]
       p) T                          q) T (Steinhaus theorem)
       r) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: start every convergence question by DRAWING fₙ for n = 1,
  2, 5, 20 and asking "where does the area go?" — a spike (n𝟙_(0,1/n)),
  a wanderer (𝟙_[n,n+1]) or a flattening (1/n)𝟙_[0,n]); these three
  pictures explain every failure of "lim ∫ = ∫ lim". Insist on a written
  line "0 ≤ fₙ ↑ f, so MCT" or "|fₙ| ≤ g with ∫g = ..., so DCT" before any
  exchange of limit and integral, and "∬|f| < ∞ by Tonelli, so Fubini"
  before any change of order. For σ-algebras on finite sets, always find
  the atoms first. Harder: prove Carathéodory's theorem that the
  measurable sets form a σ-algebra and m is countably additive on it;
  prove that Lebesgue measure is the only translation-invariant Borel
  measure with m[0, 1] = 1; construct the Cantor function and show it is
  continuous, increasing, with derivative 0 a.e. yet c(0) = 0, c(1) = 1
  (so the fundamental theorem of calculus fails without absolute
  continuity); prove Egoroff and Lusin's theorems; prove the Lebesgue
  differentiation theorem via the Vitali covering lemma; and show that
  convergence in measure is metrisable but a.e. convergence is not.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.2 — Lp SPACES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - DEFINITION: for 1 ≤ p < ∞, Lᵖ(X, μ) is the set of measurable f with
    ‖f‖ₚ = (∫|f|ᵖ dμ)^(1/p) < ∞, where functions EQUAL A.E. ARE IDENTIFIED
    (so ‖f‖ₚ = 0 ⇔ f = 0 a.e. makes ‖·‖ₚ a norm, not just a seminorm). For
    p = ∞: ‖f‖_∞ = ESS SUP |f| = inf{M : |f| ≤ M a.e.}; it ignores null
    sets (‖x + 𝟙_Q‖_∞ = 1 on [0, 1] although the sup is 2). ℓᵖ = Lᵖ(N,
    counting measure): ‖x‖ₚ = (∑|xₙ|ᵖ)^(1/p), ‖x‖_∞ = sup|xₙ|.
  - CONJUGATE EXPONENTS: 1/p + 1/q = 1, q = p/(p − 1); pairs (1, ∞),
    (2, 2), (3, 3/2), (4, 4/3). YOUNG'S INEQUALITY: ab ≤ aᵖ/p + b^q/q for
    a, b ≥ 0, with equality iff aᵖ = b^q (proof: concavity of log).
  - HÖLDER: ∫|fg| ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q (1 ≤ p ≤ ∞); equality (1 < p < ∞) iff |f|ᵖ
    and |g|^q are proportional a.e. p = 2 is CAUCHY-SCHWARZ. Generalised:
    ‖fg‖ᵣ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q when 1/r = 1/p + 1/q. MINKOWSKI: ‖f + g‖ₚ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ +
    ‖g‖ₚ (1 ≤ p ≤ ∞), proved from Hölder; it FAILS for 0 < p < 1.
  - RIESZ-FISCHER: Lᵖ is COMPLETE (a Banach space) for 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞; every
    Cauchy (or convergent) sequence in Lᵖ has a subsequence converging
    a.e. — but the whole sequence need not converge a.e. (typewriter).
  - POWERS: on (0, 1), xᵃ ∈ Lᵖ ⇔ ap > −1; on (1, ∞), xᵃ ∈ Lᵖ ⇔ ap < −1;
    on (0, ∞) no power xᵃ (a ≠ 0) is in any Lᵖ. Local singularities are
    tamed by SMALL p, slow decay at infinity by LARGE p.
  - INCLUSIONS: if μ(X) < ∞ and p < q then L^q ⊆ Lᵖ with ‖f‖ₚ ≤
    μ(X)^(1/p − 1/q)‖f‖_q (on a probability space ‖f‖ₚ increases with p).
    For sequences the inclusion REVERSES: ℓᵖ ⊆ ℓ^q, ‖x‖_q ≤ ‖x‖ₚ. On R
    there is no inclusion either way (x^(−1/2)𝟙_(0,1) ∈ L¹ ∖ L²,
    (1 + |x|)⁻¹ ∈ L² ∖ L¹), but Lᵖ ∩ L^q ⊆ Lʳ for p < r < q.
  - INTERPOLATION (LYAPUNOV): if 1/r = θ/p + (1 − θ)/q then ‖f‖ᵣ ≤
    ‖f‖ₚ^θ‖f‖_q^(1−θ) (log-convexity of p ↦ ‖f‖ₚ in 1/p). On a finite
    measure space ‖f‖ₚ → ‖f‖_∞ as p → ∞.
  - DENSITY AND SEPARABILITY: for 1 ≤ p < ∞ the simple functions, the step
    functions and C_c(R) are dense in Lᵖ(R), and Lᵖ is SEPARABLE (rational
    step functions). For p = ∞ all three fail: C[0, 1] is not dense in
    L^∞[0, 1] and L^∞ is not separable ({𝟙_[0,t]} are 1 apart).
  - DUALITY: for 1 ≤ p < ∞ (σ-finite if p = 1), (Lᵖ)* ≅ L^q via
    φ_g(f) = ∫fg, with ‖φ_g‖ = ‖g‖_q (extremal f = |g|^(q−1) sgn g).
    (ℓ¹)* = ℓ^∞, c₀* = ℓ¹, but (L^∞)* and (ℓ^∞)* are STRICTLY bigger than
    L¹ and ℓ¹. Lᵖ is REFLEXIVE exactly for 1 < p < ∞; L² is a HILBERT
    space with ⟨f, g⟩ = ∫f ḡ (the only Lᵖ whose norm satisfies the
    parallelogram law).
  - CONVOLUTION: (f ∗ g)(x) = ∫f(x − y)g(y) dy. ‖f ∗ g‖₁ ≤ ‖f‖₁‖g‖₁,
    ‖f ∗ g‖ₚ ≤ ‖f‖₁‖g‖ₚ, ‖f ∗ g‖_∞ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q; YOUNG: ‖f ∗ g‖ᵣ ≤
    ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q when 1 + 1/r = 1/p + 1/q. APPROXIMATE IDENTITIES φ_ε(x) =
    ε⁻¹φ(x/ε) (φ ≥ 0, ∫φ = 1): f ∗ φ_ε → f in Lᵖ for p < ∞, and smooth φ
    (MOLLIFIERS) give C^∞ approximants.
  - CONVERGENCE: CHEBYSHEV μ{|f| ≥ t} ≤ ‖f‖ₚᵖ/tᵖ, so Lᵖ convergence ⇒
    convergence in measure. DCT in Lᵖ: fₙ → f a.e., |fₙ| ≤ g ∈ Lᵖ ⇒ fₙ → f
    in Lᵖ. RIESZ (BREZIS-LIEB): fₙ → f a.e. and ‖fₙ‖ₚ → ‖f‖ₚ (p < ∞) ⇒
    ‖fₙ − f‖ₚ → 0. Standard examples: spikes n^a𝟙_(0,1/n) (‖·‖ₚ =
    n^(a − 1/p)), escaping bumps 𝟙_[n,n+1], flattening n^(−1/2)𝟙_[0,n].

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Define Lᵖ[0, 1] for 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and explain why functions equal a.e.
    must be identified. For f(x) = x compute ‖f‖ₚ for p = 1, 2, 3, 4 and
    ‖f‖_∞, and find lim_{p→∞} ‖f‖ₚ. Find ‖g‖_∞ for g = x + 𝟙_Q.
    Answer: ‖x‖ₚ = (p + 1)^(−1/p): 1/2, 1/√3 ≈ 0.57735, 4^(−1/3) ≈ 0.62996,
    5^(−1/4) ≈ 0.66874; ‖x‖_∞ = 1 = lim ‖x‖ₚ; ‖g‖_∞ = 1.
    Teacher explains: ‖f‖ₚ = (∫|f|ᵖ)^(1/p) is positive-homogeneous and (by
    Minkowski, D4) satisfies the triangle inequality, but ‖f‖ₚ = 0 only
    gives ∫|f|ᵖ = 0, i.e. f = 0 a.e. (11.1). The function 𝟙_Q is not the
    zero function yet has norm 0. So an element of Lᵖ is an EQUIVALENCE
    CLASS [f] = {g : g = f a.e.}; on classes ‖·‖ₚ is a genuine norm. For
    p = ∞, ‖f‖_∞ = inf{M : |f| ≤ M a.e.}, the smallest bound after
    throwing away a null set. For f(x) = x:
       ∫₀¹ xᵖ dx = 1/(p + 1),   so  ‖x‖ₚ = (p + 1)^(−1/p)
       p = 1: 1/2   p = 2: 1/√3 ≈ 0.57735   p = 3: 4^(−1/3) ≈ 0.62996
       p = 4: 5^(−1/4) ≈ 0.66874           p = 10: 11^(−1/10) ≈ 0.78679
    and (p + 1)^(−1/p) = exp(−ln(p + 1)/p) → e⁰ = 1 = ‖x‖_∞. The norms
    INCREASE with p (true on any probability space, D6). For g = x + 𝟙_Q:
    g = x a.e., so ‖g‖_∞ = ‖x‖_∞ = 1, although sup g = g(1) = 2. Common
    mistakes: writing ‖f‖_∞ = sup|f| without the "ess"; forgetting the
    p-th root at the end. Now you try: f = 𝟙_[0,1] + 2𝟙_(1,3] on [0, 3];
    find ‖f‖₁, ‖f‖₂, ‖f‖_∞. (5; √(1 + 8) = 3; 2)

D2. State and prove Young's inequality ab ≤ aᵖ/p + b^q/q (a, b ≥ 0,
    1 < p < ∞, 1/p + 1/q = 1) with its equality case. Check it for a = 2,
    b = 3, p = 3 and for a = 2, b = 4, p = 3, and derive the "ε-version"
    ab ≤ εa²/2 + b²/(2ε).
    Answer: 6 ≤ 8/3 + 2√3 ≈ 6.13077; 8 = 8/3 + 16/3 (equality, since
    aᵖ = 8 = b^q); ε-version from p = q = 2 applied to (√ε a)(b/√ε).
    Teacher explains: If a = 0 or b = 0 there is nothing to prove. Else
    log is CONCAVE, so for weights 1/p + 1/q = 1,
       log(aᵖ/p + b^q/q) ≥ (1/p) log aᵖ + (1/q) log b^q = log a + log b
    and exponentiating gives ab ≤ aᵖ/p + b^q/q. Strict concavity gives
    equality iff aᵖ = b^q. (Picture: the area under y = x^(p−1) from 0 to
    a plus the area to the left of it from 0 to b covers the rectangle
    ab.) Here q = p/(p − 1) = 3/2.
       a = 2, b = 3:  aᵖ/p = 8/3,  b^q/q = 3^(3/2)/(3/2) = 2√3 ≈ 3.46410,
                      sum ≈ 6.13077 ≥ 6 ✓ (strict: 8 ≠ 3√3)
       a = 2, b = 4:  aᵖ = 8, b^q = 4^(3/2) = 8: equality,
                      8/3 + 8/(3/2) = 8/3 + 16/3 = 8 = ab ✓
    ε-version: with p = q = 2, ab = (√ε a)(b/√ε) ≤ εa²/2 + b²/(2ε). This
    trades a small constant on one factor for a large one on the other,
    a standard trick in PDE estimates. Common mistakes: using q = p − 1
    instead of p/(p − 1); forgetting the 1/p and 1/q weights. Now you try:
    with a = 2 and p = 4, which b gives equality? Check it. (b^(4/3) = 16,
    b = 8; ab = 16 = 16/4 + 16/(4/3) = 4 + 12)

D3. Prove Hölder's inequality ∫|fg| ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q and state when equality
    holds. For x = (1, 2, 3), y = (4, 5, 6) compare |∑xₖyₖ| with the
    Hölder bounds for p = 1, 2, 3, ∞.
    Answer: ∑xₖyₖ = 32; bounds 36 (p = 1), √1078 ≈ 32.83291 (p = 2),
    ≈ 34.57057 (p = 3), 45 (p = ∞).
    Teacher explains: The cases p = 1 or ∞ are immediate: |fg| ≤
    |f|·‖g‖_∞ a.e. For 1 < p < ∞: if ‖f‖ₚ = 0 or ‖g‖_q = 0 then fg = 0 a.e.
    Otherwise NORMALISE: F = |f|/‖f‖ₚ, G = |g|/‖g‖_q, so ‖F‖ₚ = ‖G‖_q = 1.
    Young pointwise and integrate:
       ∫FG ≤ ∫Fᵖ/p + ∫G^q/q = 1/p + 1/q = 1,
    i.e. ∫|fg| ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q. Equality forces equality in Young a.e.:
    Fᵖ = G^q a.e., i.e. |f|ᵖ and |g|^q proportional. Numbers:
       p = 1:   ‖x‖₁‖y‖_∞ = 6·6 = 36
       p = 2:   ‖x‖₂‖y‖₂ = √14·√77 = √1078 ≈ 32.83291
       p = 3:   ‖x‖₃ = 36^(1/3) ≈ 3.30193,
                ‖y‖_{3/2} = (8 + 5√5 + 6√6)^(2/3) ≈ 10.46982,
                product ≈ 34.57057
       p = ∞:   ‖x‖_∞‖y‖₁ = 3·15 = 45
    All are ≥ 32 ✓. p = 2 is closest because x and y point in similar
    directions, and Cauchy-Schwarz is sharp for parallel vectors. Common
    mistakes: pairing ‖x‖₃ with ‖y‖₃ (the exponents must be conjugate);
    forgetting the absolute values. Now you try: for x = (1, 2, 3) and
    p = 3, which y gives equality? (y proportional to |x|^(p−1) = (1, 4, 9))

D4. Prove Minkowski's inequality ‖f + g‖ₚ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ + ‖g‖ₚ for 1 ≤ p < ∞.
    Check it in ℓ³ for x = (1, 2, 3), y = (4, 5, 6). Show that for
    p = 1/2 the "norm" (∫|f|^(1/2))² violates the triangle inequality.
    Answer: ‖x + y‖₃ ≈ 10.61772 ≤ 3.30193 + 7.39864 = 10.70057; for
    p = 1/2 with f = 𝟙_[0,1/2], g = 𝟙_(1/2,1]: 1 > 1/4 + 1/4.
    Teacher explains: p = 1 is the pointwise triangle inequality. For
    p > 1: first f + g ∈ Lᵖ, because |f + g|ᵖ ≤ (2 max(|f|, |g|))ᵖ ≤
    2ᵖ(|f|ᵖ + |g|ᵖ). Then write
       |f + g|ᵖ ≤ |f|·|f + g|^(p−1) + |g|·|f + g|^(p−1)
    and apply Hölder with q = p/(p − 1), noting (p − 1)q = p:
       ∫|f + g|ᵖ ≤ (‖f‖ₚ + ‖g‖ₚ)(∫|f + g|ᵖ)^(1/q).
    If ∫|f + g|ᵖ > 0 divide by (∫|f + g|ᵖ)^(1/q); since 1 − 1/q = 1/p this
    is ‖f + g‖ₚ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ + ‖g‖ₚ. Numbers: x + y = (5, 7, 9),
       ‖x + y‖₃ = (125 + 343 + 729)^(1/3) = 1197^(1/3) ≈ 10.61772
       ‖x‖₃ + ‖y‖₃ = 36^(1/3) + 405^(1/3) ≈ 3.30193 + 7.39864 = 10.70057 ✓
    For p = 1/2 on [0, 1]: N(f) = (∫|f|^(1/2))² gives N(f) = (1/2)² = 1/4,
    N(g) = 1/4, N(f + g) = N(1) = 1 > 1/2. The unit ball of such an Lᵖ is
    not convex (only N(f + g) ≤ 2^(1/p − 1)(N(f) + N(g)) holds). Common
    mistakes: dividing by ‖f + g‖ₚ before knowing it is finite; applying
    Minkowski for p < 1. Now you try: show that for p = 1/2 in R² the
    vectors (1, 0), (0, 1) break the triangle inequality. ((1 + 1)² = 4 >
    1 + 1 = 2)

D5. Determine exactly for which p ∈ [1, ∞] the function x^(−a) (a > 0)
    lies in Lᵖ(0, 1), in Lᵖ(1, ∞) and in Lᵖ(0, ∞). Deduce that neither of
    L¹(0, ∞), L²(0, ∞) contains the other. Compute ‖x^(−1/3)‖₂ on (0, 1).
    Answer: Lᵖ(0, 1) ⇔ ap < 1 (and L^∞ never); Lᵖ(1, ∞) ⇔ ap > 1 (and
    L^∞ always); Lᵖ(0, ∞) never. ‖x^(−1/3)‖_{L²(0,1)} = √3.
    Teacher explains: |x^(−a)|ᵖ = x^(−ap), and the model integrals are
       ∫₀¹ x^(−s) dx = 1/(1 − s) < ∞  ⇔  s < 1
       ∫₁^∞ x^(−s) dx = 1/(s − 1) < ∞  ⇔  s > 1
    with s = ap. At 0 the singularity is harmless only if ap < 1, i.e.
    p < 1/a: SMALL p. At ∞ the decay is fast enough only if p > 1/a:
    LARGE p. On (0, ∞) we need both, impossible (at s = 1 both diverge).
    For p = ∞: x^(−a) is unbounded near 0 (not in L^∞(0, 1)) and ≤ 1 on
    (1, ∞). Non-inclusion on (0, ∞):
       f = x^(−1/2)𝟙_(0,1):  ∫f = 2,  ∫f² = ∫₀¹ dx/x = ∞:  L¹ ∖ L²
       g = x⁻¹𝟙_(1,∞):       ∫g = ∞,  ∫g² = 1:            L² ∖ L¹
    Finally ∫₀¹ x^(−2/3) dx = 1/(1/3) = 3, so ‖x^(−1/3)‖₂ = √3. Common
    mistakes: getting the direction of the inequality wrong at ∞ versus
    at 0; forgetting that the borderline case s = 1 (1/x) diverges at
    BOTH ends. Now you try: for which p is x^(−2/3) in Lᵖ(0, 1)? in
    Lᵖ(1, ∞)? (p < 3/2; p > 3/2, including p = ∞)

D6. Prove that on a finite measure space ‖f‖ₚ ≤ μ(X)^(1/p − 1/q)‖f‖_q for
    1 ≤ p < q ≤ ∞, and that for sequences ‖x‖_q ≤ ‖x‖ₚ. Check the first
    for f(x) = x on [0, 2] with p = 1, q = 2, and give sequences in ℓ²∖ℓ¹
    and ℓ³∖ℓ².
    Answer: 2 ≤ √2·√(8/3) = 4/√3 ≈ 2.30940 ✓; (1/n) ∈ ℓ² ∖ ℓ¹;
    (n^(−1/2)) ∈ ℓ³ ∖ ℓ².
    Teacher explains: Apply Hölder to |f|ᵖ·1 with exponents r = q/p > 1
    and r′ = q/(q − p):
       ∫|f|ᵖ ≤ (∫|f|^q)^(p/q) (∫1)^(1 − p/q) = ‖f‖_q^p μ(X)^(1 − p/q),
    and take p-th roots: ‖f‖ₚ ≤ μ(X)^(1/p − 1/q)‖f‖_q. (For q = ∞ simply
    ∫|f|ᵖ ≤ ‖f‖_∞ᵖ μ(X).) So L^q ⊆ Lᵖ: on a finite space, BIGGER exponent
    means SMALLER space. For sequences, scale so that ‖x‖ₚ = 1; then every
    |xₙ| ≤ 1, hence |xₙ|^q ≤ |xₙ|ᵖ, ∑|xₙ|^q ≤ 1 and ‖x‖_q ≤ 1: now ℓᵖ ⊆ ℓ^q,
    the OPPOSITE direction (the danger for sequences is at infinity, not
    at a point). Check: ‖x‖₁ on [0, 2] = 2, ‖x‖₂ = (∫₀² x²)^(1/2) =
    √(8/3) ≈ 1.63299, and μ(X)^(1 − 1/2) = √2:
       2 ≤ √2·√(8/3) = √(16/3) = 4/√3 ≈ 2.30940 ✓
    Sequences: ∑1/n = ∞ but ∑1/n² = π²/6, so (1/n) ∈ ℓ² ∖ ℓ¹; ∑1/n = ∞
    but ∑n^(−3/2) < ∞, so (n^(−1/2)) ∈ ℓ³ ∖ ℓ². Common mistakes: stating
    L^q ⊆ Lᵖ on R (false: the constant 1 is in L^∞ only); reversing the
    inclusion for ℓᵖ. Now you try: the best C with ‖f‖₂ ≤ C‖f‖₄ on
    [0, 16]. (16^(1/2 − 1/4) = 2, equality for constants)

D7. Prove the Riesz-Fischer theorem: Lᵖ (1 ≤ p < ∞) is complete, and
    every Cauchy sequence has an a.e. convergent subsequence. Illustrate
    with the truncations x⁽ⁿ⁾ = (1, 1/2, ..., 1/n, 0, 0, ...) in ℓ².
    Answer: fast Cauchy subsequence + Minkowski + MCT + Fatou;
    ‖x⁽ᵐ⁾ − x⁽ⁿ⁾‖₂² = ∑_{n<k≤m} 1/k² < 1/n, and x⁽ⁿ⁾ → (1/k) in ℓ².
    Teacher explains: Let (fₙ) be Cauchy. STEP 1: choose n₁ < n₂ < ...
    with ‖f_{n_{k+1}} − f_{n_k}‖ₚ < 2⁻ᵏ. STEP 2: put
       g = |f_{n₁}| + ∑_{k≥1} |f_{n_{k+1}} − f_{n_k}|.
    By Minkowski each partial sum has norm ≤ ‖f_{n₁}‖ₚ + 1, and by MCT
    ‖g‖ₚ ≤ ‖f_{n₁}‖ₚ + 1 < ∞, so g < ∞ a.e. STEP 3: where g < ∞ the
    telescoping series f_{n₁} + ∑(f_{n_{k+1}} − f_{n_k}) converges
    absolutely; call the limit f (put f = 0 elsewhere). So f_{n_k} → f
    a.e., and |f| ≤ g gives f ∈ Lᵖ. STEP 4: by Fatou,
       ‖f − f_{n_k}‖ₚᵖ ≤ lim inf_j ∫|f_{n_j} − f_{n_k}|ᵖ ≤ (2^(1−k))ᵖ → 0.
    STEP 5: a Cauchy sequence with a convergent subsequence converges.
    (For p = ∞: off a null set the fₙ are uniformly Cauchy.) Example: for
    m > n, ‖x⁽ᵐ⁾ − x⁽ⁿ⁾‖₂² = ∑_{k=n+1}^{m} 1/k² < ∑_{k>n} 1/(k(k − 1)) =
    1/n, so the truncations are Cauchy and converge to (1/k) ∈ ℓ². The
    SAME truncations are Cauchy in ℓ¹ only if ∑1/k < ∞, which fails: they
    do not converge in ℓ¹. Common mistakes: forgetting to prove the limit
    lies in Lᵖ; believing the whole sequence converges a.e. (the
    typewriter of 11.1 D11 converges to 0 in every Lᵖ, p < ∞, but at no
    point). Now you try: in L¹[0, 1] is fₙ = 𝟙_[0,1/n] Cauchy, and what is
    its limit? (‖fₙ − fₘ‖₁ = |1/n − 1/m|: Cauchy, limit 0)

D8. (a) Prove that on a finite measure space ‖f‖ₚ → ‖f‖_∞ as p → ∞.
    (b) Prove the interpolation inequality ‖f‖ᵣ ≤ ‖f‖ₚ^θ‖f‖_q^(1−θ) for
    1/r = θ/p + (1 − θ)/q, 0 < θ < 1. (c) Find θ for p = 1, q = 3, r = 2
    and check it for f(x) = x on [0, 1].
    Answer: (c) θ = 1/4; 1/√3 ≈ 0.57735 ≤ (1/2)^(1/4)(1/4)^(1/4) =
    8^(−1/4) ≈ 0.59460.
    Teacher explains: (a) Upper bound: |f| ≤ ‖f‖_∞ a.e., so ‖f‖ₚ ≤
    μ(X)^(1/p)‖f‖_∞ → ‖f‖_∞. Lower bound: for M < ‖f‖_∞ the set
    E = {|f| > M} has μ(E) > 0, so ‖f‖ₚ ≥ (Mᵖμ(E))^(1/p) = Mμ(E)^(1/p) → M.
    Hence lim inf ‖f‖ₚ ≥ M for every M < ‖f‖_∞, and the limit is ‖f‖_∞
    (also when ‖f‖_∞ = ∞). (b) Split |f|ʳ = |f|^(θr)·|f|^((1−θ)r) and apply
    Hölder with exponents p/(θr) and q/((1 − θ)r); these are conjugate
    because θr/p + (1 − θ)r/q = r(1/r) = 1. So
       ∫|f|ʳ ≤ (∫|f|ᵖ)^(θr/p)(∫|f|^q)^((1−θ)r/q) = ‖f‖ₚ^(θr)‖f‖_q^((1−θ)r).
    Take r-th roots. (c) 1/2 = θ + (1 − θ)/3 ⇒ 3/2 = 3θ + 1 − θ ⇒ θ = 1/4.
    With ‖x‖₁ = 1/2, ‖x‖₃ = (1/4)^(1/3):
       RHS = (1/2)^(1/4)·((1/4)^(1/3))^(3/4) = (1/2)^(1/4)(1/4)^(1/4)
           = 8^(−1/4) ≈ 0.59460 ≥ ‖x‖₂ = 0.57735 ✓
    Common mistakes: in (a) forgetting the factor μ(X)^(1/p) (on R the
    constant 1 has ‖1‖ₚ = ∞ for all p < ∞); solving for θ with p and q
    instead of 1/p and 1/q. Now you try: lim_{p→∞} ‖(1, 2, 3)‖ₚ and
    ‖(1, 2, 3)‖₁₀. (3; 60074^(1/10) ≈ 3.00517)

D9. Show that for 1 ≤ p < ∞ the continuous functions are dense in
    Lᵖ[0, 1] and Lᵖ[0, 1] is separable, while in L^∞[0, 1] the function
    𝟙_[0,1/2] has distance at least 1/2 from C[0, 1], and L^∞[0, 1] is not
    separable. For the continuous ramp gₙ equal to 1 on [0, 1/2 − 1/n],
    0 on [1/2, 1] and linear between, compute ‖𝟙_[0,1/2] − gₙ‖ₚ.
    Answer: ‖𝟙_[0,1/2] − gₙ‖ₚ = (1/(n(p + 1)))^(1/p) → 0 for p < ∞, but
    ‖𝟙_[0,1/2] − gₙ‖_∞ = 1 for every n.
    Teacher explains: DENSITY (p < ∞): (i) simple functions are dense:
    for f ≥ 0 take the standard simple φₖ ↑ f; |f − φₖ|ᵖ ≤ |f|ᵖ ∈ L¹, so by
    DCT ‖f − φₖ‖ₚ → 0. (ii) Each 𝟙_A (A measurable) is approximated by 𝟙_U
    (U open ⊇ A, m(U ∖ A) small; 11.1 regularity), then by finite unions
    of intervals, i.e. step functions. (iii) Each 𝟙_[a,b] is approximated
    by a continuous trapezoid, exactly as in the ramp. Ramp: 𝟙_[0,1/2] −
    gₙ vanishes except on [1/2 − 1/n, 1/2], where it rises linearly from 0
    to 1; with t the rescaled variable,
       ∫|𝟙 − gₙ|ᵖ = (1/n)∫₀¹ tᵖ dt = 1/(n(p + 1)),
    so the distance is (n(p + 1))^(−1/p) → 0. SEPARABILITY: step functions
    with rational heights and rational endpoints form a countable dense
    set. p = ∞: if g is continuous and ‖𝟙_[0,1/2] − g‖_∞ = δ < 1/2, then
    g ≥ 1 − δ a.e. on [0, 1/2] and g ≤ δ a.e. on (1/2, 1], so by
    continuity g(1/2) ≥ 1 − δ > δ ≥ g(1/2): impossible. Non-separable:
    ‖𝟙_[0,s] − 𝟙_[0,t]‖_∞ = 1 for s ≠ t, so the uncountably many balls
    B(𝟙_[0,t], 1/2) are disjoint and a countable set cannot meet them all.
    Common mistakes: claiming continuous functions are dense in L^∞
    "because they are dense in every Lᵖ"; confusing sup with ess sup in the
    ramp (the difference approaches 1 on sets of positive measure). Now
    you try: ‖𝟙_[0,1/2] − gₙ‖₁ for n = 10. (1/20)

D10. State the Riesz representation theorem for (Lᵖ)*, 1 ≤ p < ∞, and
     prove that φ_g(f) = ∫fg has norm ‖g‖_q. For g(x) = x on [0, 1] find
     ‖φ_g‖ on L³, L¹ and L^∞ and an extremal f where one exists. Explain
     why (L^∞)* is bigger than L¹.
     Answer: on L³: ‖x‖_{3/2} = (2/5)^(2/3) ≈ 0.54288, f = x^(1/2); on L¹:
     ‖x‖_∞ = 1, not attained; on L^∞: ‖x‖₁ = 1/2, f = 1.
     Teacher explains: THEOREM: for 1 ≤ p < ∞ (μ σ-finite when p = 1)
     every bounded linear functional on Lᵖ is φ_g for a unique g ∈ L^q, and
     ‖φ_g‖ = ‖g‖_q; so (Lᵖ)* ≅ L^q isometrically (proof via Radon-Nikodym:
     ν(A) = φ(𝟙_A) is a measure ≪ μ, g = dν/dμ). NORM: Hölder gives
     |φ_g(f)| ≤ ‖g‖_q‖f‖ₚ. For 1 < p < ∞ test f = |g|^(q−1) sgn g:
        φ_g(f) = ∫|g|^q,   ‖f‖ₚ = (∫|g|^((q−1)p))^(1/p) = ‖g‖_q^(q/p)
     since (q − 1)p = q; the ratio is ‖g‖_q^(q − q/p) = ‖g‖_q. For g = x:
        L³ (q = 3/2): ‖x‖_{3/2} = (∫x^(3/2))^(2/3) = (2/5)^(2/3) ≈ 0.54288,
                      attained at f = x^(1/2)
        L¹ (q = ∞):   ‖x‖_∞ = 1; with f = n𝟙_[1−1/n,1] (‖f‖₁ = 1),
                      φ(f) = 1 − 1/(2n) → 1, but no f attains it
        L^∞ (q = 1):  ‖x‖₁ = 1/2, attained at f = 1
     (L^∞)*: on the subspace C[−1, 1] ⊆ L^∞[−1, 1] the functional
     Λ(f) = f(0) has norm 1; extend it by Hahn-Banach (11.3). If Λ = φ_g
     with g ∈ L¹, take continuous fₙ with 0 ≤ fₙ ≤ 1, fₙ(0) = 1, support in
     [−1/n, 1/n]: then 1 = Λ(fₙ) = ∫fₙg → 0 by DCT, a contradiction. So
     not every functional on L^∞ comes from L¹. Common mistakes: assuming
     the sup is always attained (it fails for p = 1); writing (L^∞)* = L¹.
     Now you try: the norm of f ↦ ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx on L²[0, 1] and the
     extremal f of norm 1. (1/√3; f = √3 x)

D11. Define convolution and prove ‖f ∗ g‖₁ ≤ ‖f‖₁‖g‖₁ and ‖f ∗ g‖_∞ ≤
     ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q. Compute h = 𝟙_[0,1] ∗ 𝟙_[0,1] and its L¹, L², L^∞ norms.
     State Young's convolution inequality and check one case. For the box
     kernel φ_ε = (2ε)⁻¹𝟙_[−ε,ε] (0 < ε < 1/2) compute ‖f − f ∗ φ_ε‖₁ for
     f = 𝟙_[0,1].
     Answer: h(x) = x on [0, 1], 2 − x on [1, 2], 0 elsewhere (a tent);
     ‖h‖₁ = 1, ‖h‖₂ = √(2/3) ≈ 0.81650, ‖h‖_∞ = 1; ‖f − f ∗ φ_ε‖₁ = ε.
     Teacher explains: (f ∗ g)(x) = ∫f(x − y)g(y) dy. By Tonelli and
     translation invariance,
        ∫|f ∗ g| ≤ ∫∫|f(x − y)||g(y)| dx dy = ∫|g(y)|(∫|f|) dy = ‖f‖₁‖g‖₁,
     so f ∗ g is defined a.e. and in L¹. By Hölder in y for fixed x,
     |f ∗ g(x)| ≤ ‖f(x − ·)‖ₚ‖g‖_q = ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q. YOUNG: ‖f ∗ g‖ᵣ ≤
     ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q when 1 + 1/r = 1/p + 1/q (r = p when q = 1). The tent:
     h(x) = m([0, 1] ∩ [x − 1, x]) = length of overlap, which grows as x
     on [0, 1] and falls as 2 − x on [1, 2]. So ‖h‖_∞ = 1, ‖h‖₁ = 1 = ‖f‖₁²
     (equality for f, g ≥ 0), ‖h‖₂² = 2∫₀¹x² = 2/3. Young with p = 1, q = 2,
     r = 2: √(2/3) ≤ 1·1 ✓; with p = q = 2, r = ∞: 1 ≤ 1·1 (equality). BOX
     SMOOTHING: f ∗ φ_ε(x) is the average of f over [x − ε, x + ε]: 0 for
     x ≤ −ε, rising linearly to 1 at x = ε, 1 up to 1 − ε, falling
     linearly to 0 at 1 + ε. On [−ε, ε] |f − f ∗ φ_ε| forms two triangles
     of area ε/4 each; the same at 1: total ε → 0, as the approximate
     identity theorem predicts. Common mistakes: forgetting the reflection
     x − y; computing ‖h‖₂ as √(∫h) instead of √(∫h²). Now you try:
     𝟙_[0,1] ∗ 𝟙_[0,2] and its L¹, L², L^∞ norms. (x on [0, 1], 1 on [1, 2],
     3 − x on [2, 3]; 2, √(5/3) ≈ 1.29099, 1)

D12. Prove Chebyshev's inequality m{|f| ≥ t} ≤ ‖f‖ₚᵖ/tᵖ and use it on
     f(x) = x, [0, 1], t = 0.9 with p = 1, 2, 4, 10. For the spikes
     fₙ = n^(1/2)𝟙_(0,1/n) decide convergence to 0 in L¹, L², L³. Prove:
     if fₙ → f a.e. and ‖fₙ‖ₚ → ‖f‖ₚ (1 ≤ p < ∞) then fₙ → f in Lᵖ.
     Answer: exact 0.1; bounds 0.55556, 0.41152, 0.30483, 0.26072; spikes:
     ‖fₙ‖ₚ = n^(1/2 − 1/p): → 0 in L¹, = 1 in L², → ∞ in L³.
     Teacher explains: CHEBYSHEV: tᵖ𝟙_{|f|≥t} ≤ |f|ᵖ; integrate. Hence Lᵖ
     convergence implies convergence in measure. For f = x the bound is
     (1/(p + 1))/0.9ᵖ:
        p = 1: 0.5/0.9 ≈ 0.55556      p = 2: (1/3)/0.81 ≈ 0.41152
        p = 4: 0.2/0.6561 ≈ 0.30483   p = 10: (1/11)/0.9¹⁰ ≈ 0.26072
     against the true value m{x ≥ 0.9} = 0.1: higher moments give better
     tail bounds. Spikes: ‖fₙ‖ₚᵖ = n^(p/2)·(1/n), so ‖fₙ‖ₚ = n^(1/2 − 1/p):
     n^(−1/2) → 0 (p = 1), 1 (p = 2), n^(1/6) → ∞ (p = 3). fₙ → 0 a.e. in
     all cases: a.e. convergence alone gives nothing. THEOREM (Riesz):
     by convexity |a − b|ᵖ ≤ 2^(p−1)(|a|ᵖ + |b|ᵖ), so
        hₙ = 2^(p−1)(|fₙ|ᵖ + |f|ᵖ) − |fₙ − f|ᵖ ≥ 0,   hₙ → 2ᵖ|f|ᵖ a.e.
     Fatou: 2ᵖ∫|f|ᵖ ≤ lim inf ∫hₙ = 2ᵖ∫|f|ᵖ − lim sup ∫|fₙ − f|ᵖ (here we
     used ∫|fₙ|ᵖ → ∫|f|ᵖ). Hence lim sup ‖fₙ − f‖ₚ = 0. The spikes in L²
     show the hypothesis ‖fₙ‖ₚ → ‖f‖ₚ cannot be dropped (1 ↛ 0). Common
     mistakes: thinking Chebyshev gives the exact measure; forgetting the
     norm condition. Now you try: ‖f‖₂ = 3 on some space; bound
     μ{|f| ≥ 6}. (9/36 = 1/4)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. p-norms of vectors: give ‖x‖₁, ‖x‖₂, ‖x‖₄, ‖x‖_∞.
    a) (3, 4)                          b) (1, 1)
    c) (1, −2, 2)                      d) (1, 1, 1, 1)

    m) (1, 1, ..., 1) (nine ones)      n) (3, −4, 12)
    o) (1 + i, 1 − i) in C²            p) (1, 1, ..., 1) (n ones)

      e) 5, 3, 33^(1/4) ≈ 2.39678, 2
      f) 17, 13, 21361^(1/4) ≈ 12.08942, 12
      g) 6, √14 ≈ 3.74166, 98^(1/4) ≈ 3.14635, 3

M2. Lᵖ norms of functions (Lebesgue measure on the stated set).
    a) x on [0, 1]: p = 1, 2, ∞        b) x² on [0, 1]: p = 2
    c) 1 on [0, 3]: p = 1, 2, ∞        d) sin x on [0, π]: p = 1, 2, ∞
    e) eˣ on [0, 1]: p = 2             f) e⁻ˣ on (0, ∞): p = 1, 2, 3
    g) 1/(1 + x²) on R: p = 1, 2       h) x^(−1/4) on (0, 1): p = 2, 4
    i) ln x on (0, 1): p = 1, 2, ∞     j) 2𝟙_[0,1] − 3𝟙_(1,2]: p = 1, 2, ∞
    k) e^(−|x|) on R: p = 1, 2         l) x(1 − x) on [0, 1]: p = 1, 2, ∞
    m) cos x on [0, 2π]: p = 2, 4      n) e^(−x²) on R: p = 1, 2
    o) xⁿ on [0, 1]: general p         p) x + 5𝟙_C (C Cantor) on [0, 1]:
                                          p = 1, ∞
    q) 𝟙_Q on R: every p               r) x^(−1/2) on (0, 4): p = 1, 2
    Answers:
      a) 1/2, 1/√3 ≈ 0.57735, 1        b) 1/√5 ≈ 0.44721
      c) 3, √3 ≈ 1.73205, 1            d) 2, √(π/2) ≈ 1.25331, 1
      e) √((e² − 1)/2) ≈ 1.78732
      f) 1, 1/√2 ≈ 0.70711, 3^(−1/3) ≈ 0.69336   (‖·‖ₚ = p^(−1/p))
      g) π, √(π/2) ≈ 1.25331           h) √2 ≈ 1.41421, ∞
      i) 1, √2, ∞                      j) 5, √13 ≈ 3.60555, 3
      k) 2, 1                          l) 1/6, 1/√30 ≈ 0.18257, 1/4
      m) √π ≈ 1.77245, (3π/4)^(1/4) ≈ 1.23895
      n) √π ≈ 1.77245, (π/2)^(1/4) ≈ 1.11952
      o) (np + 1)^(−1/p)               p) 1/2, 1 (the Cantor set is null)
      q) 0 (𝟙_Q = 0 a.e.)              r) 4, ∞

M3. For which p ∈ [1, ∞] does f lie in Lᵖ of the given set?
    a) x^(−1/2) on (0, 1)              b) x^(−1/2) on (1, ∞)
    c) x^(−1/2) on (0, ∞)              d) x⁻² on (1, ∞)
    e) x⁻² on (0, 1)                   f) x^(−1/3) on (0, 1)
    g) x^(1/2) on (0, 1)               h) x^(1/2) on (1, ∞)
    i) x⁻¹ on (1, ∞)                   j) ln x on (0, 1)
    k) 1/(1 + x) on (0, ∞)             l) 1/√(x(1 + x)) on (0, ∞)
    m) x^(−1/2)/(1 + x) on (0, ∞)      n) (sin x)/x on (0, ∞)
    o) eˣ on (0, ∞)                    p) 1/(x ln² x) on (0, 1/2)
    q) |x|^(−1/2) e^(−|x|) on R
    r) f = n on (n, n + n⁻³) for each n ∈ N, 0 elsewhere, on (0, ∞)
    s) xᵃ on (0, 1) (a ∈ R)            t) xᵃ e⁻ˣ on (0, ∞) (a ∈ R)
    Answers:
      a) 1 ≤ p < 2       b) 2 < p ≤ ∞       c) none
      d) all p ∈ [1, ∞]  e) none            f) 1 ≤ p < 3
      g) all p ∈ [1, ∞]  h) none            i) 1 < p ≤ ∞
      j) 1 ≤ p < ∞ (not L^∞)                k) 1 < p ≤ ∞
      l) 1 < p < 2 (x^(−1/2) at 0, x⁻¹ at ∞)
      m) 1 ≤ p < 2 (x^(−3/2) at ∞ is always fine)
      n) 1 < p ≤ ∞ (|sin x|ᵖ/xᵖ; for p = 1 it diverges like ∑1/k)
      o) none            p) p = 1 only (∫ du/u² = 1/ln 2 with u = ln x)
      q) 1 ≤ p < 2       r) 1 ≤ p < 2 (∫|f|ᵖ = ∑ n^(p−3))
      s) ap > −1 (all p ∈ [1, ∞] if a ≥ 0; p = ∞ iff a ≥ 0)
      t) ap > −1, as in s) (the factor e⁻ˣ kills infinity)

M4. Conjugate exponents, Young's inequality and exponent bookkeeping.
    Conjugate q of p:
    a) p = 2                           b) p = 3
    c) p = 4                           d) p = 3/2
    e) p = 1                           f) p = 5/4
    g) p = 1.1                         h) p = 10
    i) p = 6                           j) p such that q = 2p
    k) p such that q = p
    l) r with 1/r = 1/2 + 1/3 (generalised Hölder ‖fg‖ᵣ ≤ ‖f‖₂‖g‖₃)
    m) s such that f ∈ L², g ∈ L⁴, h ∈ Lˢ guarantees fgh ∈ L¹
    n) Young convolution: r for p = q = 4/3
    Young's inequality ab ≤ aᵖ/p + b^q/q:
    o) a = 2, b = 3, p = 2: both sides
    p) a = 1, b = 8, p = 4: both sides
    q) the b giving equality when a = 3, p = 2
    r) the b giving equality when a = 2, p = 4
    s) a = 4, p = 3/2: the b giving equality, and check
    t) ab ≤ εa²/2 + b²/(2ε) with a = 1, b = 4: the best ε and the bound
    Answers:
      a) 2      b) 3/2    c) 4/3    d) 3      e) ∞      f) 5
      g) 11     h) 10/9   i) 6/5    j) 3/2 (q = 3)      k) 2
      l) 6/5    m) 4      n) 2 (1 + 1/r = 3/4 + 3/4)
      o) 6 ≤ 2 + 9/2 = 13/2            p) 8 ≤ 1/4 + 12 = 49/4
      q) 3 (b² = a²)                   r) 8 (b^(4/3) = 16)
      s) q = 3, b³ = 4^(3/2) = 8, b = 2: ab = 8 = 16/3 + 8/3
      t) ε = b/a = 4: bound 2 + 2 = 4 = ab (equality)

M5. Hölder, Cauchy-Schwarz and Minkowski in numbers. x = (1, 2, 3),
    y = (4, 5, 6).
    a) ∑xₖyₖ and ‖x‖₂‖y‖₂             b) ‖x‖₃‖y‖_{3/2}
    c) ‖x‖₁‖y‖_∞                       d) ‖x‖_∞‖y‖₁
    e) ‖x + y‖₂ and ‖x‖₂ + ‖y‖₂       f) ‖x + y‖₃ and ‖x‖₃ + ‖y‖₃
    g) ∫₀¹ x·x² dx and ‖x‖₂‖x²‖₂ on [0, 1]
    h) ∫₀¹ x dx and the Hölder bound ‖x‖₂‖1‖₂
    i) max ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx over ‖f‖₂ = 1, and the maximiser
    j) max ∫₀¹ x f(x) dx over ‖f‖_∞ ≤ 1
    k) max ∫₀¹ f over ‖f‖₃ ≤ 1
    l) max ∑ xₖ/k over ‖x‖_{ℓ²} ≤ 1
    m) max ∑ xₖ2⁻ᵏ over ‖x‖₁ ≤ 1, and over ‖x‖_∞ ≤ 1
    n) ∫₀¹ eˣ dx · ∫₀¹ e⁻ˣ dx (Cauchy-Schwarz says ≥ 1)
    o) (∫₀¹ x dx)² and ∫₀¹ x² dx
    p) f = g = x^(−1/4) on (0, 1): ∫fg and ‖f‖₂‖g‖₂
    q) f = x, g = 1 − x on [0, 1]: ‖f + g‖₁ and ‖f‖₁ + ‖g‖₁
    r) p = 1/2, f = 𝟙_[0,1/2], g = 𝟙_(1/2,1]: (∫|f + g|^(1/2))² versus
       (∫|f|^(1/2))² + (∫|g|^(1/2))²
    Answers:
      a) 32, √1078 ≈ 32.83291          b) ≈ 3.30193 × 10.46982 ≈ 34.57057
      c) 36                            d) 45
      e) √155 ≈ 12.44990 ≤ √14 + √77 ≈ 12.51662
      f) 1197^(1/3) ≈ 10.61772 ≤ 36^(1/3) + 405^(1/3) ≈ 10.70057
      g) 1/4 ≤ 1/√15 ≈ 0.25820         h) 1/2 ≤ 1/√3 ≈ 0.57735
      i) 1/√3, f = √3 x                j) 1/2 (f = 1)
      k) 1 (f = 1)                     l) π/√6 ≈ 1.28255
      m) 1/2 (x = e₁); 1 (x = (1, 1, 1, ...))
      n) (e − 1)²/e ≈ 1.08616          o) 1/4 ≤ 1/3
      p) 2 = √2·√2 (equality: f = g)   q) 1 = 1/2 + 1/2 (equality)
      r) 1 > 1/4 + 1/4 = 1/2: the triangle inequality fails for p < 1

M6. Inclusions: best constants and membership.
    a) best C with ‖f‖₁ ≤ C‖f‖₂ on [0, 1]
    b) the same on [0, 4]
    c) best C with ‖f‖₂ ≤ C‖f‖₄ on [0, 8]
    d) best C with ‖f‖ₚ ≤ C‖f‖_∞ on [0, 2]

    g) best C with ‖x‖₁ ≤ C‖x‖₂ on Rⁿ
    For which p ∈ [1, ∞] is the sequence in ℓᵖ?
    h) (1/n)                           i) (1/√n)

    p) an f ∈ L¹(R) ∖ L²(R) and a g ∈ L²(R) ∖ L¹(R)

      r) 2 ≤ √2·√(8/3) = 4/√3 ≈ 2.30940

M7. ℓᵖ norms of sequences (n ≥ 1 unless stated).
    a) (2⁻ⁿ): ‖·‖₁, ‖·‖₂, ‖·‖_∞       b) (1/n): ‖·‖₂

      k) π/(2√2) ≈ 1.11072 (∑ 1/(2n − 1)² = π²/8)
      l) 1 (∑(1/n − 1/(n + 1)))        m) 2
      n) π²/6 ≈ 1.64493                o) 1
      p) (1 − |r|ᵖ)^(−1/p)

         ball of ℓᵖ is not compact)

M8. Essential suprema, limits of norms and interpolation.
    a) ‖𝟙_Q‖_∞ on R
    b) ‖f‖_∞, f = x on [0, 1] except f(1/2) = 100
    c) ‖e⁻ˣ‖_∞ on (0, ∞)               d) ‖sin x‖_∞ on [0, 2π]
    e) ‖f‖_∞, f = n on (n, n + 2⁻ⁿ), 0 elsewhere
    f) ‖f‖_∞, f = x on the irrationals, 5 on the rationals of [0, 1]
    g) ‖x e⁻ˣ‖_∞ on (0, ∞)             h) ‖1/x‖_∞ on (0, 1)
    i) lim_{p→∞} ‖x‖_{Lᵖ(0,1)}         j) lim_{p→∞} ‖x‖_{Lᵖ(0,2)}
    k) lim_{p→∞} ‖(1, 2, 3)‖ₚ          l) ‖(1, 2, 3)‖₁₀
    m) lim_{p→∞} ‖1‖_{Lᵖ(0,3)}         n) lim_{p→0⁺} ‖x‖_{Lᵖ(0,1)}
    o) θ in ‖f‖₂ ≤ ‖f‖₁^θ‖f‖₄^(1−θ)    p) θ in ‖f‖₃ ≤ ‖f‖₂^θ‖f‖_∞^(1−θ)
    q) θ in ‖f‖₄ ≤ ‖f‖₂^θ‖f‖₈^(1−θ)
    r) check o) for f = x on [0, 1]
    Answers:
      a) 0       b) 1       c) 1       d) 1       e) ∞       f) 1
      g) 1/e ≈ 0.36788      h) ∞       i) 1       j) 2       k) 3
      l) 60074^(1/10) ≈ 3.00517        m) 1 (3^(1/p) → 1)

         exp(∫ln x))
