       p) −3, −4, 125, −16807, 256, 144

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep ONE example field open all the time — Z[i] for
  splitting, Z[√−5] for non-unique factorisation, Q(∛2) for a cubic — and
  compute there before every general statement. Drill the three quadratic
  facts (O_K, d_K, splitting by Legendre symbols) until automatic; always
  compute the norm first, since "no element of norm p" is the usual proof
  of non-principality or irreducibility. Draw the lattice of Z[√−5] in the
  plane to see ideals as sublattices. Harder: prove that O_K is a free
  Z-module of rank n (via the trace form); prove Minkowski's convex body
  theorem and derive M_K; prove Dirichlet's unit theorem for real
  quadratic fields using Dirichlet's approximation theorem; compute the
  class groups of Q(√−23) (Z/3) and Q(√−14) (Z/4); show that
  x³ − x² − 2x − 8 (Dedekind's example) generates a field with no power
  basis; prove Kummer's theorem that FLT holds for regular primes; and use
  SageMath/PARI to explore class numbers of real quadratic fields.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.5 — ANALYTIC NUMBER THEORY (INTRODUCTION): DIRICHLET SERIES, PRIME
NUMBER THEOREM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - ARITHMETIC FUNCTIONS f : N → C. MULTIPLICATIVE: f(mn) = f(m)f(n) when
    gcd(m, n) = 1 (so f is fixed by its values on prime powers); COMPLETELY
    multiplicative: for all m, n. Standard ones: τ(n) = number of divisors
    = ∏(a + 1), σ(n) = sum of divisors = ∏(p^(a+1) − 1)/(p − 1), Euler φ(n) =
    n∏(1 − 1/p), MÖBIUS μ(n) = (−1)^k if n is a product of k distinct primes
    and 0 if a square > 1 divides n, LIOUVILLE λ(n) = (−1)^Ω(n), ω(n) and
    Ω(n) (number of prime factors without / with multiplicity), and VON
    MANGOLDT Λ(n) = log p if n = pᵏ (k ≥ 1), 0 otherwise (NOT multiplicative).
  - DIRICHLET CONVOLUTION (f * g)(n) = ∑_{d|n} f(d)g(n/d): commutative,
    associative, identity ε (ε(1) = 1, else 0), preserves multiplicativity;
    f has an inverse iff f(1) ≠ 0. Key identities: 1 * μ = ε (∑_{d|n} μ(d)
    = [n = 1]), 1 * 1 = τ, 1 * id = σ, φ * 1 = id, φ = μ * id, Λ * 1 = log,
    Λ = μ * log. MÖBIUS INVERSION: g = f * 1 ⇔ f = g * μ.
  - DIRICHLET SERIES F(s) = ∑ f(n)n⁻ˢ, s = σ + it. There is an ABSCISSA OF
    ABSOLUTE CONVERGENCE σ_a (absolute convergence for σ > σ_a); if
    f(n) = O(n^c) then σ_a ≤ c + 1. PRODUCT RULE: F(s)G(s) = ∑(f * g)(n)n⁻ˢ;
    UNIQUENESS: equal series (for σ large) have equal coefficients.
    Examples: ζ(s)² = ∑τ(n)n⁻ˢ, ζ(s)ζ(s − 1) = ∑σ(n)n⁻ˢ, 1/ζ(s) = ∑μ(n)n⁻ˢ,
    ζ(s − 1)/ζ(s) = ∑φ(n)n⁻ˢ, −ζ′(s)/ζ(s) = ∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ, ζ(s)/ζ(2s) = ∑|μ(n)|n⁻ˢ,
    ζ(2s)/ζ(s) = ∑λ(n)n⁻ˢ, −ζ′(s) = ∑(log n)n⁻ˢ.
  - EULER PRODUCTS: for multiplicative f with ∑|f(n)|n^(−σ) < ∞,
    ∑f(n)n⁻ˢ = ∏_p (1 + f(p)p⁻ˢ + f(p²)p⁻²ˢ + ...); ζ(s) = ∏(1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for
    σ > 1 (analytic form of unique factorisation), so ζ(s) ≠ 0 there.
    Consequences: infinitely many primes, and ∑1/p DIVERGES (Euler).
  - THE RIEMANN ZETA FUNCTION: ζ(s) = s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x^(−s−1) dx
    continues ζ to σ > 0 with a single simple POLE at s = 1, residue 1,
    ζ(s) = 1/(s − 1) + γ + O(s − 1). It continues to all of C; FUNCTIONAL
    EQUATION: ξ(s) = π^(−s/2)Γ(s/2)ζ(s) = ξ(1 − s). TRIVIAL ZEROS at −2, −4,
    ...; all other zeros lie in the CRITICAL STRIP 0 < σ < 1, symmetric about
    σ = 1/2 (first: 1/2 + 14.1347i). RIEMANN HYPOTHESIS: they all have
    σ = 1/2. Values: ζ(2k) = (−1)^(k+1)B₂ₖ(2π)^(2k)/(2(2k)!), ζ(2) = π²/6,
    ζ(4) = π⁴/90; ζ(0) = −1/2, ζ(−n) = (−1)ⁿB_{n+1}/(n + 1): ζ(−1) = −1/12.
  - CHEBYSHEV FUNCTIONS: θ(x) = ∑_{p≤x} log p, ψ(x) = ∑_{n≤x} Λ(n) =
    log lcm(1, ..., ⌊x⌋); ψ(x) − θ(x) = O(√x log x). CHEBYSHEV'S BOUNDS (from
    the central binomial coefficient C(2n, n)): θ(x) < x log 4 and
    π(x) ≍ x/log x; BERTRAND: there is a prime in (n, 2n].
  - PARTIAL (ABEL) SUMMATION: ∑_{n≤x} aₙf(n) = A(x)f(x) − ∫₁ˣ A(t)f′(t) dt,
    A(x) = ∑_{n≤x} aₙ. It converts between π(x) and θ(x):
    π(x) = θ(x)/log x + ∫₂ˣ θ(t)/(t log²t) dt.
  - PRIME NUMBER THEOREM (Hadamard, de la Vallée Poussin 1896):
    π(x) ~ x/log x. Equivalent forms: θ(x) ~ x, ψ(x) ~ x, π(x) ~ Li(x) =
    ∫₂ˣ dt/log t, pₙ ~ n log n, M(x) = ∑_{n≤x} μ(n) = o(x). Li(x) =
    x/log x + x/log²x + 2x/log³x + ... is much more accurate than x/log x.
  - PROOF STRATEGY: (i) continue ζ to σ ≥ 1, s ≠ 1; (ii) ζ(1 + it) ≠ 0 for
    t ≠ 0, from 3 + 4cos φ + cos 2φ = 2(1 + cos φ)² ≥ 0, which gives
    |ζ(σ)³ζ(σ + it)⁴ζ(σ + 2it)| ≥ 1; (iii) a TAUBERIAN theorem (Newman's
    contour argument, or Wiener-Ikehara) turns the behaviour of −ζ′/ζ on
    σ = 1 into ψ(x) ~ x. ERROR TERMS: π(x) = Li(x) + O(x e^(−c√(log x)));
    RH ⇔ π(x) = Li(x) + O(√x log x).
  - MERTENS' THEOREMS: ∑_{p≤x} (log p)/p = log x + O(1); ∑_{p≤x} 1/p =
    log log x + M + O(1/log x), M ≈ 0.26150; ∏_{p≤x}(1 − 1/p) ~ e^(−γ)/log x,
    γ ≈ 0.57722 (Euler's constant: Hₙ = log n + γ + O(1/n)).
  - AVERAGE ORDERS: ∑_{n≤x} τ(n) = x log x + (2γ − 1)x + O(√x) (Dirichlet
    HYPERBOLA method); ∑_{n≤x} φ(n) = 3x²/π² + O(x log x); ∑σ(n) ~ π²x²/12;
    the density of squarefree numbers, and the probability that two random
    integers are coprime, are both 6/π² = 1/ζ(2).
  - DIRICHLET CHARACTERS AND L-FUNCTIONS: χ mod q is a homomorphism
    (Z/q)^× → C^× extended by 0; there are φ(q) of them; orthogonality
    ∑_χ χ(a)χ̄(b) = φ(q)[a ≡ b]. L(s, χ) = ∑χ(n)n⁻ˢ = ∏(1 − χ(p)p⁻ˢ)⁻¹.
    DIRICHLET'S THEOREM: L(1, χ) ≠ 0 for χ ≠ χ₀, hence every progression
    a mod q with gcd(a, q) = 1 contains infinitely many primes; indeed
    π(x; q, a) ~ π(x)/φ(q). Example: L(1, χ₋₄) = 1 − 1/3 + 1/5 − ... = π/4.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Prove that if f is multiplicative then so is F(n) = ∑_{d|n} f(d).
    Deduce formulas for τ(n) and σ(n), and compute τ, σ and φ of 360.
    Answer: τ(n) = ∏(aᵢ + 1), σ(n) = ∏(pᵢ^(aᵢ+1) − 1)/(pᵢ − 1); for 360 =
    2³·3²·5: τ = 24, σ = 1170, φ = 96.
    Teacher explains: Let gcd(m, n) = 1. Every divisor d of mn factors
    UNIQUELY as d = d₁d₂ with d₁ | m, d₂ | n (take d₁ = gcd(d, m)), and
    gcd(d₁, d₂) = 1. So
       F(mn) = ∑_{d₁|m} ∑_{d₂|n} f(d₁d₂) = ∑_{d₁|m} f(d₁) · ∑_{d₂|n} f(d₂)
             = F(m)F(n).
    Apply it with f = 1 (F = τ) and f = id (F = σ). On a prime power pᵃ the
    divisors are 1, p, ..., pᵃ, so τ(pᵃ) = a + 1 and σ(pᵃ) = 1 + p + ... + pᵃ
    = (p^(a+1) − 1)/(p − 1). Multiplying over the prime powers:
       τ(360) = (3 + 1)(2 + 1)(1 + 1) = 24
       σ(360) = (2⁴ − 1)/1 · (3³ − 1)/2 · (5² − 1)/4 = 15·13·6 = 1170
       φ(360) = 360·(1/2)(2/3)(4/5) = 96
    (φ is multiplicative by the Chinese remainder theorem, with φ(pᵃ) =
    pᵃ − pᵃ⁻¹.) Check with a perfect number: σ(28) = 7·8 = 56 = 2·28.
    Common mistakes: multiplying τ over non-coprime factors (τ(4) ≠ τ(2)²);
    forgetting that Λ is NOT multiplicative. Now you try: τ, σ, φ of 720.
    (30, 2418, 192)

D2. Prove ∑_{d|n} μ(d) = 1 if n = 1 and 0 otherwise, and the Möbius
    inversion formula. Deduce φ = μ * id from ∑_{d|n} φ(d) = n, and check
    φ(12) = ∑_{d|12} μ(d)·12/d.
    Answer: φ(12) = 12 − 6 − 4 + 2 = 4.
    Teacher explains: 1 * μ is multiplicative, so check prime powers:
    ∑_{d|pᵃ} μ(d) = μ(1) + μ(p) = 1 − 1 = 0 for a ≥ 1 (higher powers have
    μ = 0). So 1 * μ = ε. (Alternatively, if n has k ≥ 1 distinct primes,
    the squarefree divisors give ∑_j C(k, j)(−1)ʲ = (1 − 1)ᵏ = 0.) INVERSION:
    Dirichlet convolution is associative, so if g = f * 1 then
       g * μ = f * (1 * μ) = f * ε = f,
    and conversely. For φ: the n fractions 1/n, 2/n, ..., n/n in lowest
    terms have denominators d | n, and exactly φ(d) of them have denominator
    d; so ∑_{d|n} φ(d) = n, i.e. φ * 1 = id, and inversion gives φ = μ * id:
       φ(n) = ∑_{d|n} μ(d) n/d = n∏_{p|n}(1 − 1/p).
    For n = 12 the divisors with μ ≠ 0 are 1, 2, 3, 6 (μ = 1, −1, −1, 1):
    φ(12) = 12 − 12/2 − 12/3 + 12/6 = 12 − 6 − 4 + 2 = 4 ✓ ({1, 5, 7, 11}).
    Common mistakes: applying inversion to a pointwise product instead of a
    convolution; forgetting μ(1) = 1. Now you try: show ∑_{d|n} μ(d)/d =
    φ(n)/n and check n = 30. (both 4/15)

D3. Show that if F(s) = ∑f(n)n⁻ˢ and G(s) = ∑g(n)n⁻ˢ converge absolutely
    then F(s)G(s) = ∑(f * g)(n)n⁻ˢ. Deduce the Dirichlet series of τ, μ and
    φ, find their abscissae of absolute convergence, and evaluate
    ∑τ(n)/n² and ∑φ(n)/n³.
    Answer: ζ(s)², 1/ζ(s) (σ_a = 1) and ζ(s − 1)/ζ(s) (σ_a = 2);
    ∑τ(n)/n² = π⁴/36 ≈ 2.70581, ∑φ(n)/n³ = ζ(2)/ζ(3) ≈ 1.36843.
    Teacher explains: For absolutely convergent series we may multiply out
    and regroup:
       F(s)G(s) = ∑_{m,k} f(m)g(k)(mk)⁻ˢ = ∑_n (∑_{mk=n} f(m)g(k)) n⁻ˢ.
    The inner sum is (f * g)(n). With f = g = 1: ζ(s)² = ∑τ(n)n⁻ˢ. Since
    1 * μ = ε: ζ(s)·∑μ(n)n⁻ˢ = 1, so ∑μ(n)n⁻ˢ = 1/ζ(s). Since φ = id * μ and
    ∑n·n⁻ˢ = ζ(s − 1): ∑φ(n)n⁻ˢ = ζ(s − 1)/ζ(s). ABSCISSAE: |μ| ≤ 1 and
    τ(n) = O(n^ε) give σ_a ≤ 1, and at s = 1 the series ∑|μ(n)|/n and
    ∑τ(n)/n diverge (they dominate part of ∑1/n), so σ_a = 1. For φ:
    φ(n) ≤ n gives σ_a ≤ 2, and φ(p)/p² = (p − 1)/p² makes ∑φ(n)/n²
    diverge (like ∑1/p): σ_a = 2. Values:
       ∑τ(n)/n² = ζ(2)² = π⁴/36 ≈ 2.70581
       ∑φ(n)/n³ = ζ(2)/ζ(3) = 1.64493/1.20206 ≈ 1.36843
    Common mistakes: multiplying conditionally convergent series and
    regrouping; confusing the shift ζ(s − 1) (coefficients n) with ζ(2s)
    (coefficients supported on squares). Now you try: find ∑σ(n)n⁻ˢ and
    evaluate ∑σ(n)/n³. (ζ(s)ζ(s − 1); ζ(3)ζ(2) ≈ 1.97730)

D4. Prove the Euler product ζ(s) = ∏_p (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for σ > 1. Deduce that
    ζ(s) ≠ 0 for σ > 1 and that ∑_p 1/p diverges. Check the inequality
    ∏_{p≤x}(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ ≥ ∑_{n≤x} 1/n for x = 10.
    Answer: ∏_{p≤10}(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ = 35/8 = 4.375 ≥ H₁₀ ≈ 2.92897.
    Teacher explains: For σ > 1 each factor is a geometric series,
    (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ = ∑_k p^(−ks). Multiplying the factors for p ≤ P and using
    UNIQUE FACTORISATION,
       ∏_{p≤P} (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ = ∑_{n : all prime factors ≤ P} n⁻ˢ,
    and the difference from ζ(s) is at most ∑_{n>P} n^(−σ) → 0. So the
    product converges to ζ(s). An absolutely convergent product of non-zero
    factors is non-zero (∑|p⁻ˢ| < ∞), so ζ(s) ≠ 0 for σ > 1. DIVERGENCE:
    for real σ > 1 take logs:
       log ζ(σ) = ∑_p ∑_{k≥1} p^(−kσ)/k = ∑_p p^(−σ) + R(σ),
    with 0 ≤ R(σ) ≤ ∑_p ∑_{k≥2} p⁻ᵏ = ∑_p 1/(p(p − 1)) < 1. As σ → 1⁺,
    ζ(σ) ≥ ∑_{n≤N} n^(−σ) → H_N for every N, so ζ(σ) → ∞; hence
    ∑_p p^(−σ) → ∞, and ∑1/p (which exceeds every ∑p^(−σ)) diverges. The
    finite version: ∏_{p≤x}(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ ≥ ∑ of 1/n over n whose primes are all
    ≤ x, which includes every n ≤ x. For x = 10:
       2 · 3/2 · 5/4 · 7/6 = 35/8 = 4.375 ≥ 1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/10 ≈ 2.92897.
    Since H_x > log x, this gives ∑_{p≤x} 1/p ≥ log log x − 1 (using
    −log(1 − u) ≤ u + u² for u ≤ 1/2). Common mistakes: writing
    ζ(1) = ∏(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ as a finite number; forgetting that the Euler product
    says nothing directly about σ ≤ 1. Now you try: explain why the Euler
    product shows there are infinitely many primes. (with finitely many,
    ∏(1 − 1/p)⁻¹ would be finite, but it is ≥ H_x → ∞)

D5. Prove Λ * 1 = log and −ζ′(s)/ζ(s) = ∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ. Show ψ(x) = log lcm(1, 2,
    ..., ⌊x⌋) and compute ψ(20), θ(20) and ψ(20) − θ(20).
    Answer: ψ(20) = log 232792560 ≈ 19.2657; θ(20) = log 9699690 ≈ 16.0876;
    difference 3 log 2 + log 3 ≈ 3.1781.
    Teacher explains: If n = ∏pᵃ then log n = ∑ a log p. The divisors d | n
    with Λ(d) ≠ 0 are p, p², ..., pᵃ for each p | n, each contributing
    log p, so ∑_{d|n} Λ(d) = ∑ a log p = log n. In Dirichlet series:
       ζ(s)·∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ = ∑(log n)n⁻ˢ = −ζ′(s)
    (differentiate ∑n⁻ˢ term by term), giving −ζ′/ζ = ∑Λ(n)n⁻ˢ. The same
    identity is the LOGARITHMIC DERIVATIVE of the Euler product: log ζ(s) =
    ∑_p ∑_k p^(−ks)/k, and differentiating, −ζ′/ζ = ∑_p ∑_k (log p)p^(−ks).
    LCM: the exponent of p in lcm(1, ..., N) is the
    largest k with pᵏ ≤ N, which is the number of prime powers pᵏ ≤ N; so
    log lcm = ∑_{pᵏ≤N} log p = ψ(N). For N = 20:
       lcm(1..20) = 2⁴·3²·5·7·11·13·17·19 = 232792560, ψ(20) ≈ 19.2657
       2·3·5·7·11·13·17·19 = 9699690,                θ(20) ≈ 16.0876
    The difference comes from the prime powers 4, 8, 16 (3 log 2) and 9
    (log 3): 3·0.69315 + 1.09861 = 3.17805 ✓. In general ψ(x) = θ(x) +
    θ(x^(1/2)) + θ(x^(1/3)) + ..., so ψ(x) − θ(x) = O(√x log x). Common
    mistakes: including 1 (Λ(1) = 0); counting p² as contributing log p²
    (it contributes log p, once). Now you try: ψ(10) and θ(10). (log 2520
    ≈ 7.8320; log 210 ≈ 5.3471)

D6. Using partial summation, show that ζ(s) = s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x^(−s−1) dx
    for σ > 1, and deduce that ζ extends to σ > 0 with a simple pole at
    s = 1 of residue 1, and that ζ(σ) < 0 for 0 < σ < 1.
    Answer: the integral converges for σ > 0; near 1, ζ(s) = 1/(s − 1) +
    γ + O(s − 1); ζ(1/2) ≈ −1.46035.
    Teacher explains: With A(x) = ⌊x⌋ = x − {x} and f(x) = x⁻ˢ, partial
    summation over 1 ≤ n ≤ N gives
       ∑_{n≤N} n⁻ˢ = N·N⁻ˢ + s∫₁ᴺ ⌊x⌋x^(−s−1) dx.
    Let N → ∞ (σ > 1, N^(1−s) → 0) and split ⌊x⌋ = x − {x}:
       ζ(s) = s∫₁^∞ x⁻ˢ dx − s∫₁^∞ {x}x^(−s−1) dx
            = s/(s − 1) − s∫₁^∞ {x}x^(−s−1) dx.
    Since 0 ≤ {x} < 1, the last integral converges absolutely for σ > 0
    and defines an analytic function there. So the right side continues ζ
    to σ > 0, and s/(s − 1) = 1 + 1/(s − 1) shows a simple pole at s = 1
    with residue 1. (The constant term works out to γ.) For real 0 < σ < 1:
    σ/(σ − 1) < 0 and σ∫{x}x^(−σ−1) dx > 0, so ζ(σ) < 0: in particular ζ
    has no zeros on (0, 1). Numerically ζ(1/2) ≈ −1.46035. (This is NOT
    ∑n^(−1/2), which diverges: the continuation gives new values.) Common
    mistakes: thinking ζ(1/2) = ∑1/√n = +∞; dropping the factor s in front
    of the integral. Now you try: show (1 − 2^(1−s))ζ(s) = 1 − 2⁻ˢ + 3⁻ˢ −
    ... for σ > 1 and use it at s → 1 to recover the residue. (the left
    side is ~ (s − 1) log 2 · ζ(s) and the right tends to log 2)

D7. State the functional equation of ζ in the form ζ(s) = 2ˢπ^(s−1)
    sin(πs/2)Γ(1 − s)ζ(1 − s). Use it to find ζ(−1), ζ(−2) and ζ(−3), and
    explain the trivial zeros, the critical strip and the Riemann
    hypothesis.
    Answer: ζ(−1) = −1/12, ζ(−2) = 0, ζ(−3) = 1/120.
    Teacher explains: Plug in s = −1: 2⁻¹π⁻² sin(−π/2)Γ(2)ζ(2) =
    (1/2)(1/π²)(−1)(1)(π²/6) = −1/12. s = −2: sin(−π) = 0 while Γ(3)ζ(3) is
    finite, so ζ(−2) = 0; in the same way sin(−kπ) = 0 gives the TRIVIAL
    ZEROS s = −2, −4, −6, .... s = −3: 2⁻³π⁻⁴ sin(−3π/2)Γ(4)ζ(4) =
    (1/8)(1/π⁴)(1)(6)(π⁴/90) = 6/720 = 1/120. General rule (Bernoulli
    numbers, B₂ = 1/6, B₄ = −1/30, B₆ = 1/42): ζ(1 − 2k) = −B₂ₖ/(2k), and
    ζ(2k) = (−1)^(k+1)B₂ₖ(2π)^(2k)/(2(2k)!). STRUCTURE: for σ > 1 the Euler
    product shows ζ ≠ 0; by the functional equation the only zeros with
    σ < 0 are the trivial ones. All others (NON-TRIVIAL ZEROS) lie in the
    critical strip 0 ≤ σ ≤ 1 — in fact 0 < σ < 1, since ζ(1 + it) ≠ 0 (the
    heart of the PNT) — and are symmetric under s ↦ 1 − s and s ↦ s̄. The
    first is 1/2 + 14.1347i. RIEMANN HYPOTHESIS: all non-trivial zeros
    have real part 1/2 (verified for the first 10¹³ zeros; unproved).
    Common mistakes: "1 + 2 + 3 + ... = −1/12" as an equation between
    numbers (the series diverges; −1/12 is the value of the continuation);
    thinking the trivial zeros are in the critical strip. Now you try:
    ζ(−5) and ζ(6). (−1/252; π⁶/945)

D8. Prove Chebyshev's bounds: (a) ∏_{n<p≤2n} p divides C(2n, n), hence
    θ(x) < x log 4; (b) C(2n, n) ≤ (2n)^π(2n), hence π(2n) ≥ log C(2n, n)/
    log(2n). Check (a) and (b) for n = 10.
    Answer: C(20, 10) = 184756 = 4·(11·13·17·19); (b) gives π(20) ≥ 4.05,
    so π(20) ≥ 5 (true value 8).
    Teacher explains: (a) C(2n, n) = (2n)!/(n!)² is an integer; each prime
    p ∈ (n, 2n] divides the numerator but not n!, so it divides C(2n, n).
    Since C(2n, n) ≤ (1 + 1)^(2n) = 4ⁿ,
       θ(2n) − θ(n) ≤ log C(2n, n) ≤ n log 4.
    By induction on n (for odd n = 2m + 1 use ∏_{m+1<p≤2m+1} p ≤
    C(2m + 1, m) ≤ 4ᵐ) one gets ∏_{p≤n} p < 4ⁿ, i.e. θ(x) < x log 4 ≈
    1.386x. (b) By Legendre, the exponent of p in C(2n, n)
    is ∑_k (⌊2n/pᵏ⌋ − 2⌊n/pᵏ⌋), each term 0 or 1, and zero once pᵏ > 2n; so
    p^(v_p) ≤ 2n, and only primes ≤ 2n occur. Hence C(2n, n) ≤ (2n)^π(2n).
    Also C(2n, n) ≥ 4ⁿ/(2n + 1) (the largest of 2n + 1 terms summing to
    4ⁿ), so π(2n) ≥ (n log 4 − log(2n + 1))/log(2n): of order x/log x.
    n = 10: C(20, 10) = 184756, 11·13·17·19 = 46189, and 184756 = 4·46189
    ✓. Bounds: 4¹⁰/21 ≈ 49932 ≤ 184756 ≤ 4¹⁰ = 1048576 ✓. (b): log 184756/
    log 20 = 12.127/2.996 ≈ 4.05, so π(20) ≥ 5 ✓. Common mistakes: claiming
    primes ≤ n do not divide C(2n, n) (they may: 2² ‖ C(20, 10)); using
    x/log x ~ π(x) (the PNT) inside a proof of Chebyshev's weaker bounds.
    Now you try: check (a) for n = 5. (C(10, 5) = 252 = 36·7, and 7 is the
    only prime in (5, 10])

D9. Use partial summation to prove π(x) = θ(x)/log x + ∫₂ˣ θ(t)/(t log²t)
    dt, and deduce that θ(x) ~ x implies π(x) ~ x/log x (and conversely).
    Compare π(x), x/log x and Li(x) at x = 10⁶, and show pₙ ~ n log n.
    Answer: π(10⁶) = 78498, x/log x ≈ 72382, Li(10⁶) ≈ 78627.
    Teacher explains: Put aₙ = log p if n = p is prime, else 0, so A(x) =
    θ(x), and f(t) = 1/log t. Partial summation (from 2):
       π(x) = ∑_{p≤x} log p · (1/log p) = θ(x)/log x + ∫₂ˣ θ(t)/(t log²t) dt.
    If θ(t) ~ t then θ(t) ≤ 2t for large t, and the integral is
    O(∫₂ˣ dt/log²t) = O(x/log²x) (split at √x), so π(x) = (x/log x)(1 + o(1)).
    Conversely θ(x) = π(x)log x − ∫₂ˣ π(t)/t dt, and π(t) = O(t/log t)
    makes the integral O(x/log x) = o(x). Since ψ − θ = O(√x log x), all
    three statements π ~ x/log x, θ ~ x, ψ ~ x are equivalent. Li(x) =
    ∫₂ˣ dt/log t: integrating by parts, Li(x) = x/log x + ∫ dt/log²t + ... =
    x/log x + x/log²x + O(x/log³x), so Li(x) ~ x/log x too, but the second
    term makes it far more accurate:
       π(10⁶) = 78498,  x/log x ≈ 72382 (−7.8%),  Li(10⁶) ≈ 78627 (+0.16%).
    nTH PRIME: π(pₙ) = n, so n ~ pₙ/log pₙ; taking logs, log n ~ log pₙ,
    hence pₙ ~ n log pₙ ~ n log n. (p₁₀₀₀ = 7919, 1000 log 1000 ≈ 6908; the
    refinement n(log n + log log n − 1) ≈ 7840 is closer.) Common mistakes:
    thinking π(x) ~ x/log x means π(x) − x/log x → 0 (the difference tends
    to ∞; only the RATIO tends to 1). Now you try: π(10⁴), x/log x and
    Li(x) at x = 10⁴. (1229, ≈ 1086, ≈ 1245)

D10. Prove Mertens' first theorem ∑_{p≤x} (log p)/p = log x + O(1) from
     Legendre's formula log n! = ∑_{d≤n} Λ(d)⌊n/d⌋, and deduce ∑_{p≤x} 1/p =
     log log x + O(1). Compare with the numbers at x = 100 and x = 10⁶.
     Answer: ∑_{p≤100} 1/p ≈ 1.8028 vs log log 100 + M ≈ 1.7887; at 10⁶:
     2.8873 vs 2.8873.
     Teacher explains: LEGENDRE: log n! = ∑_{m≤n} log m = ∑_{m≤n} ∑_{d|m}
     Λ(d) = ∑_{d≤n} Λ(d)⌊n/d⌋ (d divides ⌊n/d⌋ of the numbers ≤ n). Write
     ⌊n/d⌋ = n/d + O(1):
        log n! = n∑_{d≤n} Λ(d)/d + O(ψ(n)) = n∑_{d≤n} Λ(d)/d + O(n)
     by Chebyshev (ψ(n) = O(n)). STIRLING: log n! = n log n − n + O(log n).
     Divide by n: ∑_{d≤n} Λ(d)/d = log n + O(1). Prime powers pᵏ with k ≥ 2
     contribute ∑_p (log p)/(p(p − 1)) < ∞, so ∑_{p≤x} (log p)/p = log x +
     O(1). SECOND: partial summation with A(t) = ∑_{p≤t}(log p)/p = log t +
     R(t), R bounded, and f(t) = 1/log t:
        ∑_{p≤x} 1/p = A(x)/log x + ∫₂ˣ A(t)/(t log²t) dt
                    = 1 + O(1/log x) + ∫₂ˣ dt/(t log t) + ∫₂ˣ R(t)/(t log²t) dt
                    = log log x + M + O(1/log x),
     since ∫ dt/(t log t) = log log t and the R-integral converges.
     M ≈ 0.26150. Numbers: ∑_{p≤100} 1/p ≈ 1.8028, log log 100 + M ≈
     1.5272 + 0.2615 = 1.7887; at 10⁶ both are ≈ 2.8873. The growth is
     incredibly slow: ∑1/p passes 3 near x ≈ 5.2·10⁶ and will pass 4 only
     near x ≈ 2·10¹⁸. Common mistakes:
     forgetting the O(1) in ⌊n/d⌋ = n/d + O(1) summed over d ≤ n (it costs
     O(ψ(n)), not O(n log n), because Λ(d) = 0 for most d). Now you try: the
     same comparison at x = 1000. (2.1981 vs 2.1941)

D11. Define the non-trivial character χ₋₄ mod 4 and L(s, χ₋₄). Prove the
     Euler product, show L(1, χ₋₄) = π/4, and deduce that there are
     infinitely many primes p ≡ 1 (mod 4) and p ≡ 3 (mod 4), with
     ∑_{p≡1(4)} 1/p = ∞. Compare π(1000; 4, 1) and π(1000; 4, 3).
     Answer: L(1, χ₋₄) = 1 − 1/3 + 1/5 − 1/7 + ... = π/4 ≈ 0.78540;
     π(1000; 4, 1) = 80, π(1000; 4, 3) = 87.
     Teacher explains: χ₋₄(n) = 0, 1, 0, −1 for n ≡ 0, 1, 2, 3 (mod 4): it
     is completely multiplicative, so as for ζ,
        L(s, χ₋₄) = ∑χ₋₄(n)n⁻ˢ = ∏_p (1 − χ₋₄(p)p⁻ˢ)⁻¹       (σ > 1).
     The series converges (conditionally) for σ > 0 by the alternating
     series test, and at s = 1 it is Leibniz's series ∫₀¹ dx/(1 + x²) =
     arctan 1 = π/4 ≠ 0. Take logs for real σ > 1:
        log ζ(σ)       = ∑_p p^(−σ) + O(1)        (sum over all p)
        log L(σ, χ₋₄) = ∑_p χ₋₄(p)p^(−σ) + O(1)
     Adding, the odd primes ≡ 3 cancel: 2∑_{p≡1(4)} p^(−σ) = log ζ(σ) +
     log L(σ, χ₋₄) + O(1). As σ → 1⁺, log ζ(σ) → ∞ while log L(σ, χ₋₄) →
     log(π/4) stays FINITE (this is where L(1, χ) ≠ 0 is used). So
     ∑_{p≡1(4)} 1/p = ∞; subtracting instead gives ∑_{p≡3(4)} 1/p = ∞. In
     general one uses all φ(q) characters mod q and orthogonality. Counts to
     1000: 80 primes ≡ 1 and 87 ≡ 3 (mod 4): both ≈ 168/2 as π(x; q, a) ~
     π(x)/φ(q) predicts, with a small persistent excess of the class 3
     (CHEBYSHEV'S BIAS: non-squares mod q tend to lead). Common mistakes:
     forgetting that the whole proof hinges on L(1, χ) ≠ 0 (hard for real
     characters in general: it is related to the class number formula);
     thinking the bias contradicts equidistribution. Now you try: the
     characters mod 3, L(1, χ₋₃), and π(1000; 3, 1), π(1000; 3, 2).
     (χ₋₃ = 0, 1, −1 on 0, 1, 2; π/(3√3) ≈ 0.60460; 80, 87)

D12. Outline the proof of the prime number theorem. In particular prove
     that ζ(1 + it) ≠ 0 for real t ≠ 0 using 3 + 4cos φ + cos 2φ ≥ 0, and
     compare π(x) with x/log x and Li(x) for x = 10⁹.
     Answer: ζ(σ)³|ζ(σ + it)|⁴|ζ(σ + 2it)| ≥ 1 for σ > 1; π(10⁹) =
     50847534, x/log x ≈ 48254942, Li(10⁹) ≈ 50849234.
     Teacher explains: STEP 1 (the inequality): 3 + 4cos φ + cos 2φ =
     3 + 4cos φ + 2cos²φ − 1 = 2(1 + cos φ)² ≥ 0. From the Euler product,
     log|ζ(σ + it)| = Re ∑_p ∑_k p^(−kσ)e^(−ikt log p)/k
                    = ∑ cₙn^(−σ)cos(t log n)   with cₙ ≥ 0. Hence
        log|ζ(σ)³ζ(σ + it)⁴ζ(σ + 2it)| = ∑cₙn^(−σ)(3 + 4cos φₙ + cos 2φₙ) ≥ 0,
     φₙ = t log n. STEP 2: suppose ζ(1 + it) = 0 (t ≠ 0). As σ → 1⁺:
     ζ(σ)³ ~ (σ − 1)⁻³, |ζ(σ + it)|⁴ = O((σ − 1)⁴) (a zero), and ζ(σ + 2it)
     stays bounded (no pole at 1 + 2it). The product is O(σ − 1) → 0,
     contradicting ≥ 1. So ζ ≠ 0 on σ = 1. STEP 3: then −ζ′/ζ − 1/(s − 1)
     is analytic on σ ≥ 1; equivalently Φ(s) = ∑_p (log p)p⁻ˢ − 1/(s − 1)
     extends analytically to σ ≥ 1. STEP 4 (Newman's analytic theorem): if
     g is bounded and ∫₀^∞ g(t)e^(−zt) dt extends analytically to Re z ≥ 0,
     then ∫₀^∞ g(t) dt converges. Applied to g(t) = θ(eᵗ)e⁻ᵗ − 1 (bounded by
     Chebyshev) it gives ∫₁^∞ (θ(x) − x)/x² dx convergent. STEP 5: if θ(x) ≥
     λx (λ > 1) for arbitrarily large x, then since θ increases, the integral
     over [x, λx] is ≥ ∫₁^λ (λ − t)/t² dt > 0 each time, contradicting
     convergence; similarly for λ < 1. So θ(x) ~ x ⇔ PNT (D9). NUMBERS at
     10⁹: π = 50847534, x/log x ≈ 48254942 (ratio 1.0537), Li ≈ 50849234
     (error ≈ 1700, far below √x log x ≈ 6.6·10⁵, as RH predicts). Common
     mistakes: believing PNT needs RH (it only needs no zeros ON σ = 1);
     thinking Li(x) > π(x) always (Littlewood: the sign changes infinitely
     often). Now you try: evaluate 3 + 4cos φ + cos 2φ at φ = π, and explain
     why the exponents 3, 4, 1 are chosen. (0: the minimum; they are the
     coefficients of 2(1 + cos φ)² written in cos kφ)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

    (1 = constant 1, id(n) = n, ε = identity, 1_□ = indicator of squares.)

      t) 18 (∑_{d|n} τ(d) = ∏(a + 1)(a + 2)/2)

M12. Dirichlet characters, L-functions and primes in progressions.
     a) the characters mod 3           b) the characters mod 4
     c) the characters mod 5 (values at 1, 2, 3, 4)

     Answers:

       b) χ₀: 1, 1; χ₋₄: 1, −1 (at n ≡ 1, 3)
       c) 1, 1, 1, 1;  1, −1, −1, 1;  1, i, −i, −1;  1, −i, i, −1
       d) χ₀: 1, 1, 1, 1;  χ₋₄: 1, −1, 1, −1;  χ₈: 1, −1, −1, 1;
          χ₋₈: 1, 1, −1, −1
       e) 6, 4
       f) π/4 ≈ 0.78540                g) π/(3√3) ≈ 0.60460
       h) (2/√5)log((1 + √5)/2) ≈ 0.43041
       i) log(1 + √2)/√2 ≈ 0.62323     j) π/(2√2) ≈ 1.11072
       k) 11, 13                       l) 80, 87
       m) 609, 619                     n) 40, 47, 42, 38
       o) 37, 44, 43, 43               p) 84, 42, 42

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: tabulate τ, σ, φ, μ, Λ for n ≤ 30 by hand before any theory,
  and check every convolution identity numerically on n = 12 and n = 30.
  Treat a Dirichlet series as a "generating function with multiplication
  = convolution" and keep a dictionary (ζ², 1/ζ, −ζ′/ζ, ...) on one card.
  For the PNT, plot π(x), x/log x and Li(x) on the same axes in Python and
  look at the ratios; insist on the difference between "~" and "≈".
  Harder: prove ζ(s) − 1/(s − 1) extends to an entire function via the
  Euler-Maclaurin formula; prove the functional equation with the theta
  function θ(t) = ∑e^(−πn²t) and Poisson summation; work through Newman's
  short proof of the PNT in full; prove Bertrand's postulate; prove
  Dirichlet's theorem for q = 5 using all four characters; derive the
  explicit formula ψ(x) = x − ∑_ρ x^ρ/ρ − log 2π − ½log(1 − x⁻²) and plot
  its truncations; and prove that the PNT is equivalent to M(x) = o(x).


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.6 — SMOOTH MANIFOLDS (INTRODUCTION): CHARTS, TANGENT SPACES, VECTOR
FIELDS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - TOPOLOGICAL MANIFOLD of dimension n: a HAUSDORFF, SECOND COUNTABLE space
    in which every point has an open neighbourhood homeomorphic to an open
    subset of Rⁿ. The dimension is well defined (invariance of domain).
    Non-examples: the line with two origins (not Hausdorff), the long line
    (not second countable), the cross {xy = 0} and the double cone
    {x² + y² = z²} (not locally Euclidean at the singular point), the
    closed disc (a manifold WITH BOUNDARY).
  - CHARTS AND ATLASES: a chart (U, φ) is a homeomorphism φ : U → φ(U) ⊆
    Rⁿ; two charts are SMOOTHLY COMPATIBLE if the TRANSITION MAP
    ψ ∘ φ⁻¹ : φ(U ∩ V) → ψ(U ∩ V) is C^∞ with C^∞ inverse. A SMOOTH
    STRUCTURE is a maximal smooth atlas (any smooth atlas determines one).
    Compact manifolds need at least two charts.
  - STANDARD EXAMPLES: Rⁿ and open subsets (GL(n, R) ⊆ R^(n²)); graphs of
    smooth functions; Sⁿ with the two STEREOGRAPHIC charts
    σ_N(x) = (x₁, ..., xₙ)/(1 − xₙ₊₁), transition u ↦ u/|u|²; RPⁿ with n + 1
    affine charts (transition (u, v) ↦ (1/u, v/u) in RP²); CPⁿ (real
    dimension 2n); products (Tⁿ = S¹ × ... × S¹); Grassmannians Gr(k, n)
    of dimension k(n − k). Dimension n ≤ 3: one smooth structure; exotic
    R⁴ and Milnor's 28 smooth structures on S⁷ exist.
  - SMOOTH MAPS F : M → N: ψ ∘ F ∘ φ⁻¹ is C^∞ for all charts. DIFFEOMORPHISM:
    smooth bijection with smooth inverse (t ↦ t³ is a smooth homeomorphism
    of R that is NOT a diffeomorphism). BUMP FUNCTIONS and PARTITIONS OF
    UNITY exist (this is where Hausdorff and second countable are used).
  - TANGENT SPACE T_pM: the DERIVATIONS at p (linear v : C^∞(M) → R with
    v(fg) = f(p)v(g) + g(p)v(f)); equivalently velocities γ′(0) of curves
    through p. In a chart the ∂/∂xⁱ|_p form a basis, so dim T_pM = n. The
    TANGENT BUNDLE TM is a 2n-manifold; the COTANGENT space T*_pM has dual
    basis dxⁱ|_p, and df_p = ∑ (∂f/∂xⁱ) dxⁱ.
  - CHANGE OF COORDINATES: ∂/∂x̃ʲ = ∑ᵢ (∂xⁱ/∂x̃ʲ) ∂/∂xⁱ; vector components
    transform by the Jacobian, ṽʲ = ∑ᵢ (∂x̃ʲ/∂xⁱ) vⁱ, and covector components by
    its transpose. Polar: ∂/∂r = cos θ ∂x + sin θ ∂y, ∂/∂θ = −y ∂x + x ∂y,
    ∂x = cos θ ∂r − (sin θ/r) ∂θ.
  - DIFFERENTIAL (pushforward) dF_p : T_pM → T_{F(p)}N, dF_p(v)(g) = v(g ∘ F);
    in coordinates it is the JACOBIAN matrix; CHAIN RULE d(G ∘ F)_p =
    dG_{F(p)} ∘ dF_p. F is an IMMERSION (dF_p injective), SUBMERSION
    (surjective), LOCAL DIFFEOMORPHISM (bijective, inverse function theorem),
    EMBEDDING (injective immersion that is a homeomorphism onto its image;
    the figure-eight is an injective immersion that is not an embedding).
  - REGULAR VALUE THEOREM: c is a REGULAR VALUE of F : M → N if dF_p is onto
    for every p ∈ F⁻¹(c); then F⁻¹(c) is an embedded submanifold of dimension
    m − n with T_pF⁻¹(c) = ker dF_p. Examples: Sⁿ = f⁻¹(1), f = |x|²;
    SL(n, R) = det⁻¹(1) (dimension n² − 1, since d det_A(H) = det A ·
    tr(A⁻¹H)); O(n) (dimension n(n − 1)/2); hyperboloids. The converse fails
    ({z² = 0} is a plane although 0 is a critical value).
  - VECTOR FIELDS: smooth sections X of TM, X = ∑ Xⁱ ∂/∂xⁱ; they act as
    derivations f ↦ Xf of C^∞(M). Pushforward by a diffeomorphism:
    (F_*X)_{F(p)} = dF_p(X_p).
  - INTEGRAL CURVES AND FLOWS: γ′(t) = X(γ(t)) has a unique maximal solution
    (Picard); the FLOW θ_t satisfies θ₀ = id, θ_s ∘ θ_t = θ_{s+t}. On a
    compact manifold (or for compactly supported X) every field is
    COMPLETE; x²∂x on R is not (x₀/(1 − x₀t) blows up at t = 1/x₀). Linear
    fields x ↦ Ax have flow e^(tA)x.
  - LIE BRACKET: [X, Y]f = X(Yf) − Y(Xf) is again a vector field, with
    [X, Y]ʲ = ∑ᵢ (Xⁱ ∂ᵢYʲ − Yⁱ ∂ᵢXʲ); bilinear, antisymmetric, JACOBI identity;
    [∂ᵢ, ∂ⱼ] = 0; [fX, gY] = fg[X, Y] + f(Xg)Y − g(Yf)X; natural:
    F_*[X, Y] = [F_*X, F_*Y].
  - GEOMETRY OF THE BRACKET: [X, Y] = L_X Y = d/dt|₀ (θ_{−t})_* Y; going round
    the "square" (X for t, Y for t, back along X, back along Y) misses by
    t²[X, Y] + O(t³); flows commute ⇔ [X, Y] = 0; n commuting independent
    fields are coordinate fields. FROBENIUS: a distribution has integral
    submanifolds ⇔ it is closed under brackets (span{∂x, ∂y + x∂z} is not).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Show that the two stereographic projections σ_N, σ_S : S² ∖ {pole} → R²
    form a smooth atlas on S²: find the inverse of σ_N and the transition
    map. Compute σ_N and σ_S at p = (0.6, 0, 0.8), and explain why S² cannot
    be covered by a single chart.
    Answer: σ_N⁻¹(u) = (2u, |u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1); σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹(u) = u/|u|² on
    R² ∖ {0}; σ_N(p) = (3, 0), σ_S(p) = (1/3, 0).
    Teacher explains: The north pole N = (0, 0, 1). The line from N through
    x = (x₁, x₂, x₃) ∈ S² meets the plane x₃ = 0 at
       σ_N(x) = (x₁, x₂)/(1 − x₃),     σ_S(x) = (x₁, x₂)/(1 + x₃)
    (σ_S projects from S = (0, 0, −1)). Inverse: put u = σ_N(x). Using
    x₁² + x₂² = 1 − x₃², |u|² = (1 − x₃²)/(1 − x₃)² = (1 + x₃)/(1 − x₃), so
    x₃ = (|u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1), 1 − x₃ = 2/(|u|² + 1) and
       σ_N⁻¹(u) = (2u₁, 2u₂, |u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1),
    visibly C^∞ on all of R². Both charts are homeomorphisms onto R² and
    their domains cover S². Transition on σ_N(S² ∖ {N, S}) = R² ∖ {0}:
       σ_S(σ_N⁻¹(u)) = (2u/(|u|² + 1))/(1 + (|u|² − 1)/(|u|² + 1)) = u/|u|²,
    the inversion in the unit circle: smooth on R² ∖ {0} and its own
    inverse, so the charts are smoothly compatible. At p: σ_N(p) =
    (0.6, 0)/0.2 = (3, 0), σ_S(p) = (0.6, 0)/1.8 = (1/3, 0), and indeed
    (3, 0)/9 = (1/3, 0) ✓. One chart is impossible: its image φ(S²) would
    be a compact (continuous image of compact) and open (a chart image)
    non-empty subset of R², but R² is connected and not compact.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the domain of the transition map (it is
    φ(U ∩ V), here R² ∖ {0}, not R²); checking only that ψ ∘ φ⁻¹ is smooth
    and not its inverse (here the same map). Now you try: stereographic
    projection of S¹ from (0, 1): find σ_N(3/5, 4/5) and the transition.
    (σ_N(x, y) = x/(1 − y) = 3; transition u ↦ 1/u on R ∖ {0})

D2. State the definition of a topological manifold and explain why each
    axiom is needed. Show that (a) the line with two origins is locally
    Euclidean but not Hausdorff; (b) the cross X = {(x, y) : xy = 0} is not a
    manifold of any dimension; (c) the half-cone {z = √(x² + y²)} is a
    topological 2-manifold but not a smooth submanifold of R³.
    Answer: (a) the two origins have no disjoint neighbourhoods; (b) a
    punctured neighbourhood of 0 in X has 4 components; (c) it is a graph of
    a continuous function, but has no tangent plane at 0.
    Teacher explains: M is a TOPOLOGICAL n-MANIFOLD if (i) it is Hausdorff,
    (ii) it has a countable basis, (iii) every point has a neighbourhood
    homeomorphic to an open set of Rⁿ. (i) makes limits unique; (ii) gives
    partitions of unity, metrisability and embeddings in R^N; (iii) is the
    local picture. (a) Take two copies of R and glue them along x ≠ 0: the
    points 0 and 0′ are different, but every neighbourhood of 0 contains
    (−ε, 0) ∪ (0, ε), and so does every neighbourhood of 0′: they always
    meet. Each point still has a neighbourhood ≅ R, so (iii) holds and (ii)
    holds; only (i) fails. (b) Suppose the origin had a neighbourhood U ⊆ X
    homeomorphic to an open set of Rⁿ; shrink U to a small cross (four arms)
    homeomorphic to an open ball B. Removing the origin leaves 4 connected
    components (the arms). For n = 1, B minus a point has 2 components; for
    n ≥ 2 it is connected; n = 0 would make the origin isolated. So no n
    works. (c) C is the graph of the continuous map (x, y) ↦ √(x² + y²), so
    projection (x, y, z) ↦ (x, y) is a homeomorphism C → R²: a topological
    manifold (even a smooth one, with this global chart). But C is not an
    EMBEDDED smooth submanifold: near 0 it would be the graph of a smooth
    function over some coordinate plane; over the xy-plane that function
    would be √(x² + y²), not differentiable at 0, and C is not a graph over
    the xz- or yz-plane near 0 (the lines z = ±x on C sit over the same
    points). Common mistakes: "every subset of Rⁿ that looks like a curve is
    a 1-manifold" (the cross, the figure-eight); confusing "is a topological
    manifold" with "is a smooth submanifold". Now you try: is {(x, y) : y² =
    x²(x + 1)} a 1-manifold? (No: the node at 0 again has 4 arms)

D3. Define real projective space RPⁿ = (Rⁿ⁺¹ ∖ {0})/(x ~ λx). Write down the
    standard charts of RP², compute the transition map from U₀ to U₁ and
    find the coordinates of the point [1 : 2 : 4] in each chart. Show that
    RP¹ ≅ S¹.
    Answer: φᵢ[x₀ : x₁ : x₂] = the two ratios xⱼ/xᵢ (j ≠ i); φ₁ ∘ φ₀⁻¹(u, v) =
    (1/u, v/u) on u ≠ 0; [1 : 2 : 4] ↦ (2, 4), (1/2, 2), (1/4, 1/2).
    Teacher explains: Uᵢ = {[x] : xᵢ ≠ 0} is well defined (scaling does not
    change whether xᵢ = 0) and open; the Uᵢ cover RP² because x ≠ 0.
       φ₀[x₀ : x₁ : x₂] = (x₁/x₀, x₂/x₀),   φ₀⁻¹(u, v) = [1 : u : v]
       φ₁[x₀ : x₁ : x₂] = (x₀/x₁, x₂/x₁)
       φ₂[x₀ : x₁ : x₂] = (x₀/x₂, x₁/x₂)
    The ratios do not depend on the representative, and φ₀ is a bijection
    U₀ → R². Transition: φ₀(U₀ ∩ U₁) = {(u, v) : u ≠ 0} and
       φ₁(φ₀⁻¹(u, v)) = φ₁[1 : u : v] = (1/u, v/u),
    smooth on u ≠ 0, with inverse of the same form. Point: [1 : 2 : 4] =
    [2 : 4 : 8], giving φ₀ = (2, 4), φ₁ = (1/2, 4/2) = (1/2, 2), φ₂ = (1/4,
    2/4) = (1/4, 1/2); check φ₁ ∘ φ₀⁻¹(2, 4) = (1/2, 4/2) ✓. RPⁿ is compact
    and Hausdorff because it is the quotient of Sⁿ by the antipodal map
    x ↦ −x, so it is a compact n-manifold (n + 1 charts). RP¹: [x₀ : x₁] ↦
    the angle 2α where (cos α, sin α) is the unit representative (α is
    defined mod π, so 2α is defined mod 2π): a homeomorphism onto S¹. In
    charts it is u ↦ ((1 − u²), 2u)/(1 + u²), a stereographic inverse.
    Common mistakes: using the ratio xⱼ/xᵢ with the wrong denominator in the
    transition; forgetting that [x] and [λx] are the same point. Now you
    try: coordinates of [3 : 0 : −6] in U₀ and U₂. ((0, −2); (−1/2, 0))

D4. On R consider the atlas 𝒜 = {id} and the atlas ℬ = {φ}, φ(x) = x³.
    (a) Show that 𝒜 and ℬ are not smoothly compatible. (b) Show that
    F(x) = x³ is a diffeomorphism (R, ℬ) → (R, 𝒜). (c) Which of the
    functions f(x) = x and g(x) = x³ are smooth on (R, ℬ)?
    Answer: (a) id ∘ φ⁻¹(u) = u^(1/3) is not C^∞ at 0. (b) in charts F is the
    identity u ↦ u. (c) g is smooth, f is not.
    Teacher explains: A smooth structure is not just a topology: it is the
    choice of which functions count as smooth. (a) Transition id ∘ φ⁻¹(u) =
    u^(1/3), whose derivative (1/3)u^(−2/3) blows up at 0; so {id, φ} is not
    a smooth atlas and the two maximal atlases are DIFFERENT. (b) Read F in
    the charts φ (source) and id (target):
       id ∘ F ∘ φ⁻¹(u) = F(u^(1/3)) = (u^(1/3))³ = u,
    and the inverse F⁻¹(y) = y^(1/3) reads φ ∘ F⁻¹ ∘ id⁻¹(y) = (y^(1/3))³ =
    y. Both are C^∞, so F is a diffeomorphism: the two structures are
    different but DIFFEOMORPHIC. (c) f is smooth on (R, ℬ) iff f ∘ φ⁻¹ is
    C^∞: x ∘ φ⁻¹(u) = u^(1/3) — not smooth; x³ ∘ φ⁻¹(u) = u — smooth. The
    same trick shows every smooth structure on R is diffeomorphic to the
    standard one. The deep facts: in dimensions ≤ 3 every topological
    manifold has a unique smooth structure up to diffeomorphism, but R⁴ has
    uncountably many (exotic R⁴'s) and S⁷ has 28 (Milnor). Common mistakes:
    thinking "t ↦ t³ is not a diffeomorphism" is a statement about the
    function alone (it depends on the structures used on each side);
    confusing "different atlases" with "non-diffeomorphic manifolds". Now
    you try: is F(x) = x a diffeomorphism (R, 𝒜) → (R, ℬ)? (No: φ ∘ F ∘
    id⁻¹(x) = x³ is smooth but its inverse u^(1/3) is not)

D5. State and sketch the proof of the regular value theorem. Apply it to
    F(x, y, z) = x² + y² − z²: find all regular values, describe F⁻¹(1),
    F⁻¹(−1) and F⁻¹(0), and find the tangent plane of F⁻¹(1) at (1, 1, 1).
    Answer: every c ≠ 0 is regular; F⁻¹(1) is a hyperboloid of one sheet,
    F⁻¹(−1) of two sheets (both 2-manifolds), F⁻¹(0) is a double cone (not a
    manifold); T_(1,1,1) = {(a, b, c) : a + b − c = 0}.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: if F : M^m → N^n is smooth and dF_p is onto
    for every p ∈ F⁻¹(c), then F⁻¹(c) is an embedded submanifold of dimension
    m − n and T_pF⁻¹(c) = ker dF_p. Sketch: in charts, F : R^m → R^n with
    Jacobian of rank n at p; renumber so the last n columns form an
    invertible n × n block. By the IMPLICIT FUNCTION THEOREM, near p the set
    F = c is the graph x″ = g(x′) of a smooth g of the first m − n
    coordinates, and x′ is a chart for F⁻¹(c). If γ is a curve in F⁻¹(c)
    then F(γ(t)) = c, so dF_p(γ′(0)) = 0: T_p ⊆ ker dF_p, and both have
    dimension m − n. Here
       dF = (2x, 2y, −2z),
    which vanishes only at the origin, where F = 0. So every c ≠ 0 is a
    regular value and F⁻¹(c) is a smooth surface. c = 1: x² + y² = 1 + z², a
    circle of radius √(1 + z²) at each height: one sheet (≅ S¹ × R). c = −1:
    z² = 1 + x² + y² ≥ 1, two sheets z ≥ 1 and z ≤ −1 (each ≅ R²). c = 0 is
    a CRITICAL value, and F⁻¹(0) = {z = ±√(x² + y²)} is the double cone:
    removing the vertex from a small neighbourhood leaves two pieces, so it
    is not a manifold. Tangent plane at (1, 1, 1) (F = 1 + 1 − 1 = 1 ✓):
    ker dF = ker (2, 2, −2), i.e. a + b − c = 0, spanned by (1, 0, 1) and
    (0, 1, 1). Common mistakes: "c is a critical value, so F⁻¹(c) is not a
    manifold" (the theorem is only sufficient: G = z² has G⁻¹(0) a plane);
    computing dF only at one point of F⁻¹(c) (it must be onto at EVERY
    point). Now you try: for f(x, y) = x³ − 3x + y², find the critical values.
    (critical points (±1, 0); critical values −2 and 2)

D6. Show that SL(n, R) = {A : det A = 1} is a smooth submanifold of
    M(n, R) ≅ R^(n²) of dimension n² − 1 and that T_I SL(n, R) is the set of
    trace-zero matrices. For A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]] find the equation of
    T_A SL(2, R) and check that H = [[1, 1], [0, 0]] lies in it.
    Answer: d det_A(H) = det A · tr(A⁻¹H) ≠ 0 at H = A; T_A SL(2) = {H :
    h₁₁ − h₁₂ − h₂₁ + 2h₂₂ = 0}; H satisfies it (in fact det(A + tH) = 1).
    Teacher explains: First differentiate det. For invertible A,
       det(A + tH) = det A · det(I + tA⁻¹H) = det A (1 + t tr(A⁻¹H) + O(t²)),
    because det(I + tK) = ∏(1 + tλᵢ) = 1 + t ∑λᵢ + O(t²) = 1 + t tr K + O(t²).
    So d det_A(H) = det A · tr(A⁻¹H), and at A = I simply tr H. For A ∈ SL(n)
    take H = A: d det_A(A) = 1 · tr(I) = n ≠ 0, so the linear map
    d det_A : M(n) → R is onto and 1 is a regular value. By the regular
    value theorem SL(n, R) is a submanifold of dimension n² − 1 and
       T_A SL(n) = ker d det_A = {H : tr(A⁻¹H) = 0},   T_I SL(n) = {tr H = 0}.
    For A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]] (det 2 − 1 = 1 ✓), A⁻¹ = [[1, −1], [−1, 2]], and
       tr(A⁻¹H) = (h₁₁ − h₂₁) + (−h₁₂ + 2h₂₂).
    For H = [[1, 1], [0, 0]]: 1 − 0 − 1 + 0 = 0 ✓. Indeed det(A + tH) =
    det [[2 + t, 1 + t], [1, 1]] = 2 + t − 1 − t = 1: the whole line stays in
    SL(2). Note 0 is a CRITICAL value of det (d det₀ = 0 for n ≥ 2), and
    det⁻¹(0) in M(2) is the cone ad = bc, singular at the zero matrix.
    Common mistakes: writing T_A SL(n) = {tr H = 0} at every A (that is only
    at the identity; in general T_A = A·sl(n) = {AX : tr X = 0}); forgetting
    that d det_A(H) is linear in H. Now you try: find d det_A(I) for
    A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. (det(A + tI) = t² + 5t − 2, derivative 5 = tr adj A)

D7. Define a tangent vector at p ∈ Rⁿ as a derivation of C^∞(Rⁿ) at p.
    Prove that every derivation is v = ∑ vⁱ ∂/∂xⁱ|_p with vⁱ = v(xⁱ), so that
    dim T_pRⁿ = n. Compute v(f) for v = 2∂x − ∂y at p = (1, 2) and
    f = x²y + e^(y − 2x), and check it as the derivative along a curve.
    Answer: Hadamard's lemma gives v(f) = ∑ v(xⁱ) ∂ᵢf(p); here v(f) = 2.
    Teacher explains: A DERIVATION at p is a linear map v : C^∞ → R with
    v(fg) = f(p)v(g) + g(p)v(f). Step 1: constants are killed: v(1) =
    v(1·1) = 2v(1), so v(1) = 0 and v(c) = 0. Step 2 (HADAMARD): for
    f ∈ C^∞(Rⁿ),
       f(x) = f(p) + ∑ᵢ (xⁱ − pⁱ) gᵢ(x),   gᵢ(x) = ∫₀¹ ∂ᵢf(p + s(x − p)) ds,
    with gᵢ(p) = ∂ᵢf(p) (fundamental theorem of calculus along the segment).
    Step 3: apply v and the Leibniz rule:
       v(f) = 0 + ∑ᵢ [ (pⁱ − pⁱ) v(gᵢ) + gᵢ(p) v(xⁱ) ] = ∑ᵢ v(xⁱ) ∂ᵢf(p).
    So v = ∑ vⁱ ∂ᵢ|_p with vⁱ = v(xⁱ); the ∂ᵢ|_p are independent because
    ∂ᵢ|_p(xʲ) = δᵢⱼ. Hence dim T_pRⁿ = n; on a manifold the same holds in
    a chart (derivations are LOCAL: if f = g near p then v(f) = v(g), by a
    bump function). Example: f_x = 2xy − 2e^(y − 2x) = 4 − 2 = 2 and
    f_y = x² + e^(y − 2x) = 1 + 1 = 2 at (1, 2), so
       v(f) = 2·2 − 1·2 = 2.
    Along γ(t) = (1 + 2t, 2 − t), with γ(0) = p, γ′(0) = (2, −1):
    d/dt f(γ(t)) at 0 = ∇f · γ′(0) = 2 ✓ (curves and derivations give the
    same tangent space). Common mistakes: thinking f ↦ f″(p) or f ↦ f(p) are
    derivations (they fail Leibniz); forgetting that v(xⁱ) are exactly the
    components. Now you try: v = ∂x + 3∂y at (0, 0), f = sin(x + y²) + xy.
    (f_x = 1, f_y = 0 at 0: v(f) = 1)

D8. On R² ∖ {0} use polar coordinates x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ. Express
    ∂/∂r and ∂/∂θ in terms of ∂x, ∂y, and ∂x, ∂y in terms of ∂r, ∂θ. Find
    the polar components of v = ∂x at p = (1, 1) in two ways.
    Answer: ∂r = cos θ ∂x + sin θ ∂y, ∂θ = −r sin θ ∂x + r cos θ ∂y;
    ∂x = cos θ ∂r − (sin θ/r) ∂θ, ∂y = sin θ ∂r + (cos θ/r) ∂θ;
    at (1, 1): ∂x = (1/√2) ∂r − (1/2) ∂θ.
    Teacher explains: The RULE (chain rule): if x̃ are new coordinates,
       ∂/∂x̃ʲ = ∑ᵢ (∂xⁱ/∂x̃ʲ) ∂/∂xⁱ.
    With x̃ = (r, θ): ∂x/∂r = cos θ, ∂y/∂r = sin θ, ∂x/∂θ = −r sin θ,
    ∂y/∂θ = r cos θ, giving the first pair; in Cartesian form
       ∂r = (x ∂x + y ∂y)/r,    ∂θ = −y ∂x + x ∂y
    (∂θ is the rotation field). Inverting the 2 × 2 matrix [[cos θ, sin θ],
    [−r sin θ, r cos θ]] (determinant r) gives ∂x and ∂y. Components: a
    vector v = vⁱ ∂ᵢ has new components ṽʲ = v(x̃ʲ) = ∑ᵢ (∂x̃ʲ/∂xⁱ) vⁱ. For
    v = ∂x at (1, 1), where r = √2 and θ = π/4:
       way 1: ṽ^r = ∂r/∂x = x/r = 1/√2,  ṽ^θ = ∂θ/∂x = −y/r² = −1/2;
       way 2: cos θ = 1/√2, sin θ/r = (1/√2)/√2 = 1/2 in the formula.
    So ∂x = (1/√2) ∂r − (1/2) ∂θ at (1, 1). Check on f = r: ∂x(r) = x/r =
    1/√2 ✓. Vector components transform with the Jacobian ∂x̃/∂x
    ("contravariantly"), the BASIS vectors with its inverse. Common
    mistakes: thinking ∂/∂r is unchanged if we only change the angle
    coordinate (∂/∂x̃ʲ depends on ALL the new coordinates); forgetting the
    factor r in ∂θ. Now you try: write x ∂x + y ∂y and x ∂y − y ∂x in polar
    form. (r ∂r and ∂θ)

D9. Let F : R² → R², F(x, y) = (x² − y², 2xy) (that is, z ↦ z²). Find the
    Jacobian, compute dF_p(v) for p = (1, 2), v = (3, −1), and check it with
    the chain rule along a curve. Where is F an immersion, a submersion, a
    local diffeomorphism? Is it a diffeomorphism of R² ∖ {0}?
    Answer: dF = [[2x, −2y], [2y, 2x]], det = 4(x² + y²); dF_p(v) = (10, 10);
    F is a local diffeomorphism exactly on R² ∖ {0}, but it is 2-to-1, so
    not a diffeomorphism.
    Teacher explains: In coordinates the differential IS the Jacobian
    matrix. At p = (1, 2): [[2, −4], [4, 2]], so
       dF_p(3, −1) = (2·3 + (−4)(−1), 4·3 + 2·(−1)) = (10, 10).
    Chain rule check: γ(t) = (1 + 3t, 2 − t) has γ(0) = p, γ′(0) = v, and
       F(γ(t)) = ((1 + 3t)² − (2 − t)², 2(1 + 3t)(2 − t)),
    whose derivative at 0 is (6 + 4, 2(6 − 1)) = (10, 10) ✓. In complex
    notation dF_z(w) = 2zw: 2(1 + 2i)(3 − i) = 2(5 + 5i) = 10 + 10i ✓.
    det dF = 4x² + 4y² vanishes only at 0 (where dF = 0, rank 0). So for
    p ≠ 0, dF_p is invertible: F is simultaneously an immersion and a
    submersion there, i.e. a LOCAL diffeomorphism (inverse function
    theorem). At 0 it is neither. Globally F(z) = F(−z), so F is 2-to-1 on
    R² ∖ {0}: a local diffeomorphism need not be injective. Definitions to
    keep apart: IMMERSION = dF_p injective everywhere; SUBMERSION = onto
    everywhere; EMBEDDING = immersion + homeomorphism onto the image. The
    figure-eight t ↦ (sin 2t, sin t), t ∈ (−π, π), is an injective immersion
    whose image is not homeomorphic to (−π, π): not an embedding. Common
    mistakes: "invertible Jacobian everywhere ⇒ global diffeomorphism";
    evaluating the Jacobian at the image point instead of at p. Now you try:
    G(x, y) = (eˣ cos y, eˣ sin y): det dG and is G injective? (det = e^(2x) >
    0, local diffeomorphism; not injective: period 2π in y)

D10. Define the integral curves and the flow of a vector field. Find the
     flows of (a) X = x ∂x and (b) X = x² ∂x on R and of (c) X = −y ∂x + x ∂y
     on R². Check θ_s ∘ θ_t = θ_{s+t}. Which are complete? For (b) starting
     at x₀ = 2 find the position at t = 1/4 and the escape time.
     Answer: (a) θ_t(x) = xeᵗ; (b) θ_t(x) = x/(1 − tx); (c) rotation by angle
     t. (a), (c) complete, (b) not; x(1/4) = 4, escape at t = 1/2.
     Teacher explains: An INTEGRAL CURVE is γ with γ′(t) = X(γ(t)); by the
     existence and uniqueness theorem for ODEs there is a unique maximal
     one through each point, depending smoothly on the start. The FLOW is
     θ_t(p) = γ_p(t); uniqueness gives θ₀ = id and θ_s ∘ θ_t = θ_{s+t}
     (both sides solve the same ODE with the same value at s = 0). (a)
     x′ = x: x(t) = x₀eᵗ, defined for all t; θ_s(θ_t(x)) = xeᵗeˢ ✓.
     (b) x′ = x²: separating, −1/x = t − 1/x₀, so x(t) = x₀/(1 − x₀t) (and
     x ≡ 0 if x₀ = 0). Group law:
        θ_s(θ_t(x)) = (x/(1 − tx))/(1 − sx/(1 − tx)) = x/(1 − (s + t)x) ✓.
     From x₀ = 2: x(1/4) = 2/(1 − 1/2) = 4, and x → ∞ as t → 1/2: the curve
     leaves every compact set in finite time, so X is NOT complete.
     (c) x′ = −y, y′ = x: (x, y) rotates, θ_t = [[cos t, −sin t], [sin t,
     cos t]] = e^(tJ), J = [[0, −1], [1, 0]]; complete, period 2π. THEOREM:
     a compactly supported field (so every field on a compact manifold) is
     complete: on its support the curve cannot escape. In general a linear
     field x ↦ Ax has flow e^(tA)x. Common mistakes: forgetting the
     solution x ≡ 0 in (b); thinking a smooth field on R must be complete.
     Now you try: flow of X = y ∂x on R², and of (1 + x²)∂x on R: complete?
     ((x + ty, y), complete; tan(arctan x + t), not complete)

D11. Show that for vector fields X, Y on M the composite XY is not a
     vector field but [X, Y] = XY − YX is, and derive the coordinate formula
     [X, Y]ʲ = ∑ᵢ (Xⁱ ∂ᵢYʲ − Yⁱ ∂ᵢXʲ). Compute [∂x, x ∂y], [x ∂y, y ∂x] and
     [x ∂x + y ∂y, −y ∂x + x ∂y]. State the algebraic properties.
     Answer: [∂x, x ∂y] = ∂y; [x ∂y, y ∂x] = x ∂x − y ∂y; the last is 0.
     Teacher explains: A vector field must be a DERIVATION: D(fg) =
     f D(g) + g D(f). Compute
        XY(fg) = X(f Yg + g Yf) = (Xf)(Yg) + f XYg + (Xg)(Yf) + g XYf.
     The terms (Xf)(Yg) + (Xg)(Yf) spoil Leibniz for XY (in coordinates XY
     contains second derivatives: ∂x∂x is not a vector field). They are
     SYMMETRIC in X, Y, so they cancel in XY − YX:
        [X, Y](fg) = f [X, Y]g + g [X, Y]f.
     Coordinates: XYf = Xⁱ ∂ᵢ(Yʲ ∂ⱼf) = Xⁱ(∂ᵢYʲ) ∂ⱼf + XⁱYʲ ∂ᵢ∂ⱼf; the second
     term is symmetric and cancels, leaving [X, Y]ʲ = Xⁱ ∂ᵢYʲ − Yⁱ ∂ᵢXʲ.
     (i) X = (1, 0), Y = (0, x): only ∂ₓYʸ = 1 contributes: [X, Y] = ∂y. Test
     on f = y: ∂x(x·1) − x ∂y(∂x y) = 1 − 0 = 1 = ∂y(y) ✓.
     (ii) X = (0, x), Y = (y, 0): the x-component is X·∇(y) − Y·∇(0) = x, the
     y-component is X·∇(0) − Y·∇(x) = −y: [X, Y] = x ∂x − y ∂y.
     (iii) dilation and rotation: x-component X·∇(−y) − Y·∇(x) = −y + y = 0,
     y-component X·∇(x) − Y·∇(y) = x − x = 0: they COMMUTE (rotating then
     scaling = scaling then rotating). Properties: bilinear over R (not over
     C^∞: [fX, gY] = fg[X, Y] + f(Xg)Y − g(Yf)X), antisymmetric, the JACOBI
     identity [X, [Y, Z]] + [Y, [Z, X]] + [Z, [X, Y]] = 0, and [∂ᵢ, ∂ⱼ] = 0
     (equality of mixed partials). So vector fields form a Lie algebra
     (see 11.7). Common mistakes: forgetting the second term Yⁱ ∂ᵢXʲ;
     sign errors (compute [X, Y], not [Y, X]). Now you try: [∂x, eˣ ∂y] and
     [x ∂x, y ∂x]. (eˣ ∂y; −y ∂x)

D12. Let X = ∂x and Y = x ∂y on R². Starting at (0, 0), flow along X for
     time t, then Y for t, then X for −t, then Y for −t. Find the end point
     and compare with t²[X, Y]. Explain why flows commute iff the bracket
     vanishes, and use the bracket to show that the planes spanned by ∂x
     and ∂y + x ∂z in R³ are not tangent to any family of surfaces.
     Answer: the end point is (0, t²) = t²[X, Y](0, 0); flows commute ⇔
     [X, Y] = 0; [∂x, ∂y + x ∂z] = ∂z is not in the span, so the plane field
     is not integrable (Frobenius).
     Teacher explains: The flows: φ_t(x, y) = (x + t, y) for X, and
     ψ_t(x, y) = (x, y + tx) for Y (x is constant along Y). Go round:
        (0, 0) → X, t → (t, 0) → Y, t → (t, t²)
               → X, −t → (0, t²) → Y, −t → (0, t² − t·0) = (0, t²).
     The loop does not close: the gap is (0, t²) = t² ∂y = t²[X, Y] (exactly
     here; in general t²[X, Y] + O(t³)). This is the GEOMETRIC meaning of the
     bracket: the infinitesimal failure of the flows to commute. Also
     [X, Y] = L_X Y = d/dt|₀ (θ_{−t})_* Y_{θ_t(p)}, the LIE DERIVATIVE.
     THEOREM: φ_s ∘ ψ_t = ψ_t ∘ φ_s for all s, t ⇔ [X, Y] = 0. (⇒ by the square
     computation; ⇐: [X, Y] = 0 means (φ_s)_*Y = Y for all s, and a
     diffeomorphism that preserves Y maps its integral curves to integral
     curves.) Consequence: n commuting independent fields are the ∂ᵢ of a
     chart. FROBENIUS: a plane field D is tangent to a family of surfaces ⇔
     [X, Y] ∈ D whenever X, Y ∈ D. Here D = span{∂x, ∂y + x ∂z}: with
     X = (1, 0, 0), Y = (0, 1, x), [X, Y] = X·∇Y − Y·∇X = (0, 0, 1) = ∂z. At
     the origin D = span{∂x, ∂y} does not contain ∂z, so no surfaces are
     tangent to D (this is the CONTACT structure dz − x dy = 0). Common
     mistakes: composing the flows in the wrong order; expecting the gap to
     be of order t (it is t²). Now you try: repeat the loop with X = ∂x and
     Y = ∂y. (it closes: [∂x, ∂y] = 0)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Is the subset/space a manifold? Answer S = smooth manifold (give the
    dimension), T = topological manifold but not a smooth submanifold of the
    ambient Rⁿ, N = not a manifold (say why).
    a) the open unit disc in R²        b) the closed unit disc
    c) S¹ ⊆ R²                         d) {(x, y) : xy = 0}
    e) {(x, y) : y = |x|}              f) {(x, y) : y² = x³}
    g) {x² + y² = z²} ⊆ R³             h) {z = x² + y²} ⊆ R³
    i) the figure-eight image of t ↦ (sin 2t, sin t)
    j) GL(n, R) ⊆ R^(n²)                k) the line with two origins
    l) S¹ × S¹                         m) RP³
    n) {x³ + y³ + z³ = 1} ⊆ R³          o) {x² + y² = 0} ⊆ R²
    p) Q ⊆ R                           q) Z ⊆ R
    r) the graph of a continuous nowhere differentiable f : R → R
    s) Sⁿ × R                          t) the open Möbius band
    Answers:
      a) S, 2          b) N (manifold with boundary)     c) S, 1
      d) N (4 arms at 0)                 e) T, 1 (corner)
      f) T, 1 (cusp)   g) N (vertex)     h) S, 2 (graph)
      i) N (4 arms at the crossing)      j) S, n² (open subset)
      k) N (not Hausdorff)               l) S, 2          m) S, 3
      n) S, 2 (∇ = 3(x², y², z²) ≠ 0 on the set)
      o) S, 0 (a point)                  p) N (not locally Euclidean)
      q) S, 0 (countable discrete)       r) T, 1
      s) S, n + 1      t) S, 2 (non-orientable)

M2. Charts and transition maps. σ_N, σ_S are the stereographic charts of
    Sⁿ, φᵢ the affine charts of RPⁿ (divide by xᵢ).
    a) σ_N(0, 0, −1)                   b) σ_N(0.6, 0, 0.8)
    c) σ_S(0.6, 0, 0.8)                d) σ_N(2/3, 2/3, 1/3)
    e) σ_S(2/3, 2/3, 1/3)              f) σ_N⁻¹(2, 0)
    g) σ_N⁻¹(1, 2)                     h) σ_N⁻¹(0, 0)
    i) σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹(3, 4)
    j) det of the Jacobian of σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹ at u, and at u = (1, 1)
    k) on S¹, σ_N(x, y) = x/(1 − y): σ_N(3/5, 4/5) and σ_N(−4/5, −3/5)
    l) on S¹: the transition σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹ and its value at 3
    m) [1 : 2 : 4] ∈ RP² in φ₀, φ₁, φ₂
    n) the transition φ₂ ∘ φ₀⁻¹ on RP² and its Jacobian determinant
    o) [1 : 0 : 2 : −2] ∈ RP³ in φ₃
    p) CP¹: the transition z ↦ 1/z in real coordinates, and its Jacobian
       determinant
    q) S¹ with angle charts θ ∈ (0, 2π) and θ̃ ∈ (−π, π): the transition on
       each component of the overlap
    r) (0.6, 0, 0.8) in the graph chart (x, y) of the upper hemisphere and
       in spherical coordinates (polar angle θ, azimuth φ)
    Answers:
      a) (0, 0)                     b) (3, 0)
      c) (1/3, 0)                   d) (1, 1)
      e) (1/2, 1/2)                 f) (4/5, 0, 3/5)
      g) (1/3, 2/3, 2/3)            h) (0, 0, −1)
      i) (3/25, 4/25)               j) −1/|u|⁴ (orientation-reversing); −1/4
      k) 3; −1/2                    l) u ↦ 1/u on R ∖ {0}; 1/3
      m) (2, 4), (1/2, 2), (1/4, 1/2)
      n) (u, v) ↦ (1/v, u/v) on v ≠ 0; determinant 1/v³
      o) (−1/2, 0, −1)
      p) (x, y) ↦ (x, −y)/(x² + y²); determinant +1/(x² + y²)²
      q) θ̃ = θ on (0, π); θ̃ = θ − 2π on (π, 2π)
      r) (0.6, 0); θ = arccos 0.8 ≈ 0.64350, φ = 0

M3. Regular values. Is c a regular value of F? If so give dim F⁻¹(c).
    a) f = x² + y² on R², c = 1         b) f = x² + y², c = 0
    c) f = x² + y² − z², c = 1 and c = −1
    d) f = x² + y² − z², c = 0          e) f = x² − y³, c = 0
    f) f = xy on R², c = 0 and c = 5
    g) f = x³ − 3x + y²: all critical values
    h) F = (x² + y² + z², x + y + z) : R³ → R², at (1, 0) and at (3, 3)
    i) det : M(2, R) → R, c = 1 and c = 0
    j) F(A) = AᵀA : M(3, R) → Sym(3), c = I
    k) f = z² on R³, c = 0 (and is f⁻¹(0) a manifold?)
    l) f = x₁² + ... + xₙ², c = 1
    m) f = (√(x² + y²) − 2)² + z² on {x² + y² > 0}: critical values; c = 1
    n) f = y² − x³ + x: critical values; c = 0
    o) F = (x² + y², z² + w²) : R⁴ → R²: critical values; (1, 1)
    p) f = xyz on R³, c = 1 and c = 0
    q) f = (x − z)² + y², c = 1         r) f = x⁴ + y⁴ on R², c = 16
    Answers:
      a) yes, 1 (S¹)                  b) no (0 = f(0), df₀ = 0)
      c) yes, 2 (one sheet); yes, 2 (two sheets)       d) no (cone)
      e) no (critical point 0)        f) no; yes, 1 (hyperbola)
      g) −2 and 2 (critical points (±1, 0))
      h) (1, 0) regular, dim 1 (a circle of radius 1); (3, 3) critical
         (gradients parallel at x = y = z = 1: the plane touches the sphere)
      i) yes, 3 (SL(2, R)); no (the zero matrix is a critical point)
      j) yes, 9 − 6 = 3 (O(3))        k) no; yes, it is the plane z = 0
      l) yes, n − 1                   m) only 0 (the circle ρ = 2, z = 0);
         c = 1 regular, dim 2 (a torus)
      n) ±2√3/9 ≈ ±0.38490 (at x = ∓1/√3, y = 0); 0 is regular, dim 1
      o) {(a, b) : a = 0 or b = 0}; (1, 1) regular, dim 2 (a torus)
      p) yes, 2; no (critical where two coordinates vanish)
      q) yes, 2 (a cylinder)          r) yes, 1

M4. Differentials. Compute dF_p(v) (for matrix maps, dF_A(H)).
    a) f = x²y, p = (1, 2), v = (1, 1)
    b) f = e^(xy), p = (0, 3), v = (2, 5)
    c) F = (x² − y², 2xy), p = (1, 2), v = (3, −1)
    d) F(t) = (cos t, sin t, t), p = π/2, v = 1
    e) F = (x + y, xy, x − y), p = (1, 1), v = (1, 0)

    g) σ_N⁻¹ : R² → S², p = (0, 0), v = (1, 0)
    h) f = z on S², p = (1, 0, 0), v = (0, 0, 1)
    i) det, A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]], H = I
    j) det, A = I, H = [[2, 1], [5, −7]]
    k) F(A) = A², A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]], H = [[0, 0], [1, 0]]
    l) F(A) = AᵀA, A = I, H = [[0, 1], [0, 0]]
    m) F(A) = A⁻¹, A = diag(2, 1), H = [[1, 1], [1, 1]]
    n) (f ∘ γ)′(π/4) for f = x² + y², γ(t) = (cos t, 2 sin t)
    o) F = (xy, yz), p = (1, 2, 3), v = (1, 1, 1)
    p) det of the Jacobian of spherical coordinates (r, θ, φ) ↦ x at r = 2,
       θ = π/6
    q) F(z) = z³ on C = R², p = 1 + i, v = (1, 0)
    r) f = x on S¹ in the angle chart: df(∂θ) at θ = π/3
    Answers:
