      e) (1, 1, 1)    f) (−2, 1)      g) (2, 0, 0)      h) 1
      i) 5            j) −5           k) [[1, 0], [2, 1]]
      l) [[0, 1], [1, 0]]             m) −[[1/4, 1/2], [1/2, 1]]
      n) 3 (f ∘ γ = 1 + 3 sin² t)     o) (3, 5)
      p) r² sin θ = 2                 q) (0, 6) (3z² = 6i)
      r) −√3/2

M5. Classify F: LD = local diffeomorphism, I = immersion (not LD),
    S = submersion (not LD), N = none of these. Add E if F is an embedding.
    a) t ↦ (t, t²)                     b) t ↦ (t², t³)
    c) t ↦ (cos t, sin t), R → R²      d) t ↦ (t³ − t, t²)
    e) t ↦ (sin 2t, sin t), (−π, π) → R²
    f) (x, y) ↦ x                      g) (x, y) ↦ x² + y² on R² ∖ {0}
    h) (x, y) ↦ x² + y² on R²          i) z ↦ z² on C ∖ {0}
    j) (x, y) ↦ (eˣ cos y, eˣ sin y)   k) (x, y) ↦ (x, y, x² + y²)
    l) the height z : S² → R           m) the Hopf map S³ → S²
    n) Sⁿ → RPⁿ, x ↦ [x]               o) det : GL(n, R) → R
    p) A ↦ AᵀA : GL(n, R) → Sym(n)      q) (x, y) ↦ (x, xy) on R²
    r) t ↦ e^(it), R → S¹              s) x ↦ x³, R → R
    t) S¹ → R², the inclusion
    Answers:
      a) I, E         b) N (γ′(0) = 0; injective, cusp)
      c) I (not injective)             d) I (t = ±1 both give (0, 1))
      e) I (injective, not E)          f) S
      g) S            h) N (df₀ = 0)   i) LD (2-to-1)
      j) LD (onto R² ∖ {0}, not injective)               k) I, E
      l) N (critical at the poles)     m) S
      n) LD (2-to-1 covering)          o) S
      p) S (H = (1/2)A⁻ᵀS solves dF_A(H) = S)
      q) N (det = x vanishes on x = 0; LD on x ≠ 0)
      r) LD            s) N (F′(0) = 0: a homeomorphism, not a
                          diffeomorphism)                t) I, E

M6. Tangent spaces. Give T_pM as an equation or a spanning set.
    a) S² at (0, 0, 1)                 b) S² at (1, 1, 1)/√3
    c) x² + y² − z² = 1 at (1, 1, 1)    d) z = x² + y² at (1, 2, 5)
    e) torus (√(x² + y²) − 2)² + z² = 1 at (3, 0, 0)
    f) SL(2, R) at I                   g) SL(2, R) at diag(2, 1/2)
    h) O(2) at I                       i) O(n) at a general A
    j) S³ ⊆ R⁴ at (1, 0, 0, 0)
    k) S² ∩ {x + y + z = 0} at (1, −1, 0)/√2
    l) y² = x³ − x at (1, 0)           m) S¹ ⊆ C at i
    n) the graph z = sin x cos y at (0, 0, 0)
    o) the helix (cos t, sin t, t) at t = 0
    p) dimensions: T_p of S⁵, of GL(3, R), of O(4), of T³, of RP⁴
    Answers:
      a) c = 0: span{e₁, e₂}           b) a + b + c = 0
      c) a + b − c = 0                  d) 2a + 4b − c = 0
      e) a = 0: span{e₂, e₃}           f) {H : tr H = 0}
      g) h₁₁/2 + 2h₂₂ = 0, i.e. h₁₁ + 4h₂₂ = 0
      h) skew matrices: span{[[0, −1], [1, 0]]}
      i) {H : AᵀH is skew} = A·so(n)   j) the first coordinate is 0
      k) span{(1, 1, −2)}              l) span{(0, 1)} (vertical)
      m) the real direction: span{1}   n) span{(1, 0, 1), (0, 1, 0)}
      o) span{(0, 1, 1)}               p) 5, 9, 6, 3, 4

M7. Vector fields acting on functions; polar forms.
    a) ∂x(x²y) at (1, 2)              b) (x ∂y)(x²y) at (1, 2)
    c) (y ∂x − x ∂y)(x² + y²)          d) (x ∂x + y ∂y)(x³ + xy²) at (1, 1)
    e) (−y ∂x + x ∂y)(xy)              f) ∂r in Cartesian form
    g) ∂θ in Cartesian form            h) ∂x in polar form
    i) ∂y in polar form                j) x ∂x + y ∂y in polar form
    k) x ∂y − y ∂x in polar form       l) (−y ∂x + x ∂y)(z) on S²
    m) (z ∂x − x ∂z)(x² + 2z²) at (1, 0, 1)
    n) 3∂x − ∂y at (1, 1) in polar components
    o) Which are derivations at 0 on C^∞(R)?  f ↦ f′(0); f ↦ f″(0);
       f ↦ f(0); f ↦ 2f′(0); f ↦ f′(0) + f(0); f ↦ f′(0)f(0)
    p) (∂x + ∂y)(eˣ sin y) at (0, π/2)
    Answers:
      a) 4            b) 1            c) 0            d) 6 (Euler: 3f)
      e) x² − y²      f) (x ∂x + y ∂y)/r               g) −y ∂x + x ∂y
      h) cos θ ∂r − (sin θ/r) ∂θ      i) sin θ ∂r + (cos θ/r) ∂θ
      j) r ∂r         k) ∂θ           l) 0            m) −2
      n) √2 ∂r − 2 ∂θ
      o) only f ↦ f′(0) and f ↦ 2f′(0)
      p) 1

M8. Lie brackets [X, Y] (coordinates x, y, z; ∂r, ∂θ polar).
    a) [∂x, ∂y]                        b) [∂x, x ∂y]
    c) [x ∂x, ∂x]                      d) [x ∂x, x² ∂x]
    e) [∂x, eˣ ∂x]                     f) [x ∂y, y ∂x]
    g) [x ∂x + y ∂y, −y ∂x + x ∂y]     h) [∂x, y ∂x + x ∂y]
    i) [−y ∂x + x ∂y, −z ∂y + y ∂z]    j) [x² ∂x, y² ∂y]
    k) [x ∂x, y ∂x]                    l) [∂x + ∂y, xy ∂x]
    m) [sin x ∂y, cos x ∂y]            n) [∂r, ∂θ]
    o) [x ∂x + 2y ∂y, x² ∂y]           p) [eˣ ∂y, eʸ ∂x]
    q) [−z ∂y + y ∂z, z ∂x − x ∂z]     r) [x ∂y, x ∂x]
    s) [∂x + y ∂z, ∂y − x ∂z]          t) [xy ∂x, xy ∂y]
    Answers:
      a) 0                  b) ∂y                 c) −∂x
      d) x² ∂x              e) eˣ ∂x              f) x ∂x − y ∂y
      g) 0                  h) ∂y                 i) −z ∂x + x ∂z
      j) 0                  k) −y ∂x              l) (x + y) ∂x
      m) 0                  n) 0                  o) 0
      p) e^(x+y)(∂x − ∂y)   q) y ∂x − x ∂y        r) −x ∂y
      s) −2 ∂z              t) −x²y ∂x + xy² ∂y

M9. Flows. Give θ_t(p) and say complete (C) or not (NC).
    a) ∂x on R²                        b) x ∂x on R
    c) x² ∂x on R                      d) −y ∂x + x ∂y
    e) y ∂x + x ∂y                     f) ∂x + x ∂y
    g) (1 + x²) ∂x on R                h) x ∂x − y ∂y
    i) y ∂x                            j) (x + y) ∂x + y ∂y
    k) −x³ ∂x on R                     l) eˣ ∂x on R
    m) (1 − x²) ∂x on (−1, 1)          n) ∂x on (0, 1)
    o) for c): the position at t = 1/2 from x = 1, and the maximal interval
    p) for k): the position at t = 4 from x = 1
    q) a ∂θ₁ + b ∂θ₂ on the torus T² (angle coordinates)
    Answers:
      a) (x + t, y), C
      b) xeᵗ, C
      c) x/(1 − tx), NC
      d) (x cos t − y sin t, x sin t + y cos t), C
      e) (x cosh t + y sinh t, x sinh t + y cosh t), C
      f) (x + t, y + xt + t²/2), C
      g) tan(arctan x + t), NC
      h) (xeᵗ, ye⁻ᵗ), C
      i) (x + ty, y), C
      j) (eᵗ(x + ty), eᵗy), C (flow e^(tA), A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]])
      k) x/√(1 + 2x²t), NC (complete forwards, not backwards)
      l) −ln(e⁻ˣ − t) for t < e⁻ˣ, NC
      m) tanh(artanh x + t), C
      n) x + t, NC
      o) 2; (−∞, 1)
      p) 1/3
      q) (θ₁ + at, θ₂ + bt) mod 2π, C (orbits dense iff a/b is irrational)

M10. Pushforwards F_*X of vector fields by diffeomorphisms (target
     coordinates u, v).
     a) F(x) = 2x, X = ∂x              b) F(x) = 2x, X = x ∂x
     c) F(x) = eˣ : R → (0, ∞), X = ∂x
     d) F(x, y) = (y, x), X = x ∂y
     e) F(x, y) = (x, y + x²), X = ∂x  f) same F, X = ∂y
     g) F(x, y) = (2x, 3y), X = x ∂x + y ∂y
     h) F(x, y) = (−y, x), X = ∂x      i) F(x, y) = (eˣ, y), X = x ∂y
     j) F(x) = Ax with A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]], X = x ∂x − y ∂y
     k) the polar map (r, θ) ↦ (x, y): X = ∂θ and X = r ∂r
     l) F as in e): F_*[∂x, x ∂y] and [F_*∂x, F_*(x ∂y)]
     m) F(x) = tan x : (−π/2, π/2) → R, X = ∂x
     n) F(x) = tanh x : R → (−1, 1), X = ∂x
     o) F(x) = x³ + x (a diffeomorphism of R), X = ∂x, value of F_*X at
        u = 2
     Answers:
       a) 2 ∂u                        b) u ∂u
       c) u ∂u                        d) v ∂u
       e) ∂u + 2u ∂v                  f) ∂v
       g) u ∂u + v ∂v                 h) ∂v
       i) (ln u) ∂v                   j) (u − 2v) ∂u − v ∂v (ABA⁻¹)
       k) −y ∂x + x ∂y; x ∂x + y ∂y
       l) both ∂v (naturality of the bracket)
       m) (1 + u²) ∂u                 n) (1 − u²) ∂u
       o) 4 ∂u (x = 1, F′(1) = 4)

M11. Covectors and differentials of functions.
     a) d(x²y), and its value at (1, 2)
     b) d(x² + y²)                     c) dr in Cartesian form
     d) dθ in Cartesian form           e) dx in polar form
     f) dy in polar form               g) x dy − y dx in polar form
     h) df(X), f = xy, X = x ∂x − y ∂y
     i) df(X), f = x² + y², X = −y ∂x + x ∂y
     j) df(X), f = eˣ sin y, X = ∂x + ∂y, at (0, π/2)
     k) the dual basis of v₁ = ∂x + ∂y, v₂ = ∂x − ∂y
     l) y dx at (1, 1) in polar components
     m) dz on S² in the chart σ_N⁻¹, and at u = (1, 0)
     n) dim T*_pS³ and dim T*S³
     o) df_p for f = x² + y² at p = 0, and what this says about p
     Answers:
       a) 2xy dx + x² dy; 4 dx + dy   b) 2x dx + 2y dy
       c) (x dx + y dy)/r             d) (−y dx + x dy)/(x² + y²)
       e) cos θ dr − r sin θ dθ       f) sin θ dr + r cos θ dθ
       g) r² dθ                       h) 0
       i) 0                           j) 1
       k) (dx + dy)/2, (dx − dy)/2
       l) (1/√2) dr − dθ
       m) 4(u₁ du₁ + u₂ du₂)/(|u|² + 1)²; du₁
       n) 3; 6                        o) 0: p is a critical point

M12. Dimensions and facts.
     a) S⁵                             b) TS²
     c) RP⁴                            d) CP³ (real dimension)
     e) GL(3, R)                       f) SL(3, R)
     g) O(4)                           h) Gr(2, 4)
     i) Gr(3, 7)                       j) orthonormal 2-frames in R³
     k) T⁵ × S²                        l) rank-1 real 2 × 2 matrices
     m) symmetric 3 × 3 real matrices  n) unit tangent vectors of S²
     o) the space of (affine) lines in R²
     p) ordered triples of distinct points of R³
     q) the least number of charts in an atlas of S², of S¹, of R²
     r) True/false: every manifold is orientable
     s) True/false: every vector field on a compact manifold is complete
     t) True/false: a bijective immersion is a diffeomorphism
     Answers:
       a) 5     b) 4     c) 4     d) 6     e) 9     f) 8     g) 6
       h) 4     i) 12    j) 3     k) 7     l) 3     m) 6     n) 3
       o) 2 (an open Möbius band)          p) 9
       q) 2, 2, 1                          r) F (RP², Möbius band)
       s) T              t) F in general (the injective figure-eight
          immersion is not a homeomorphism onto its image); T between
          manifolds of the same dimension (inverse function theorem)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw the charts: two stereographic pictures of S² with the
  inversion u ↦ u/|u|² between them, and the three affine pictures of RP².
  Insist on writing the DOMAIN of every transition map. For tangent
  vectors, keep three pictures side by side (arrow, derivation, velocity
  of a curve) and check every computation on a test function. For
  brackets, use the table [X, Y]ʲ = X·∇Yʲ − Y·∇Xʲ one component at a time,
  and check the answer on f = x and f = y. Harder: prove the existence of
  partitions of unity and deduce that every compact manifold embeds in
  some R^N; show that TS² is not diffeomorphic to S² × R² (use the hairy
  ball theorem of 11.17); prove that RP³ ≅ SO(3) and that TS³ is trivial;
  prove that commuting flows give coordinate charts and prove the
  Frobenius theorem; find the maximal flow domain of x²∂x as an open
  subset of R × R and check the group law on it.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.7 — INTRODUCTION TO LIE GROUPS AND LIE ALGEBRAS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - LIE GROUP: a group G that is a smooth manifold with smooth
    multiplication and inversion. Examples: (Rⁿ, +), S¹ = U(1), tori Tⁿ,
    GL(n, R) (open in R^(n²), two components), GL(n, C), and the closed
    subgroups below. CARTAN'S CLOSED SUBGROUP THEOREM: every closed subgroup
    of a Lie group is an embedded Lie subgroup (so every closed subgroup of
    GL(n) is a MATRIX LIE GROUP).
  - CLASSICAL GROUPS AND DIMENSIONS: SL(n, R): n² − 1; O(n), SO(n):
    n(n − 1)/2; U(n): n²; SU(n): n² − 1; Sp(2n, R) = {AᵀJA = J}: n(2n + 1);
    SL(n, C): 2n² − 2 (real); Heisenberg group: 3; affine group of R: 2;
    Euclidean group E(3): 6; Lorentz group O(1, 3): 6; Poincaré group: 10.
    Compact: O(n), SO(n), U(n), SU(n), Sp(n). SU(2) ≅ S³.
  - MATRIX EXPONENTIAL e^A = ∑ Aᵏ/k! (always converges); d/dt e^(tA) = Ae^(tA);
    e^(A+B) = e^A e^B IF AB = BA (false in general); det e^A = e^(tr A);
    ge^A g⁻¹ = e^(gAg⁻¹). Methods: diagonalise; A = λI + N with N nilpotent;
    for trace-zero 2 × 2, A² = −(det A)I gives cos/cosh formulas; Rodrigues
    e^(θK) = I + sin θ K + (1 − cos θ)K² for a unit axis in so(3).
  - ONE-PARAMETER SUBGROUPS t ↦ e^(tX) are exactly the smooth homomorphisms
    R → G. The LIE ALGEBRA of a matrix group is g = {X : e^(tX) ∈ G ∀t} =
    T_I G: gl(n) = all, sl(n) = {tr X = 0}, o(n) = so(n) = {Xᵀ = −X},
    u(n) = {X* = −X}, su(n) = u(n) ∩ {tr = 0}, sp(2n) = {XᵀJ + JX = 0}.
  - BRACKET: [X, Y] = XY − YX = d/dt|₀ Ad_{e^(tX)} Y, where Ad_g Y = gYg⁻¹
    (ADJOINT representation) and ad X = [X, ·] = d(Ad)(X); e^(t ad X) =
    Ad_{e^(tX)}. Abstractly g ≅ LEFT-INVARIANT vector fields, with the
    vector-field bracket of 11.6.
  - ABSTRACT LIE ALGEBRA: a vector space with a bilinear, antisymmetric
    bracket satisfying JACOBI; STRUCTURE CONSTANTS [eᵢ, eⱼ] = ∑ cᵢⱼᵏ eₖ.
    Key examples: so(3) ≅ su(2) ≅ (R³, ×); sl(2): [h, e] = 2e, [h, f] = −2f,
    [e, f] = h; Heisenberg [x, y] = z; the non-abelian 2-dimensional
    algebra [x, y] = y; e(2): [J, P₁] = P₂, [J, P₂] = −P₁.
  - STRUCTURE: ideals, centre, homomorphisms; DERIVED series g ⊇ [g, g] ⊇ ...
    (SOLVABLE if it reaches 0: upper triangular b(n)); LOWER CENTRAL series
    g ⊇ [g, g] ⊇ [g, [g, g]] ⊇ ... (NILPOTENT: strictly upper triangular
    n(n), Heisenberg). SIMPLE (non-abelian, no proper ideals: sl(n), so(3),
    su(n)); SEMISIMPLE (no solvable ideals; direct sum of simples);
    REDUCTIVE (gl(n) = sl(n) ⊕ R). Theorems of LIE, ENGEL, LEVI.
  - KILLING FORM B(X, Y) = tr(ad X ad Y): invariant (B([X, Y], Z) =
    B(X, [Y, Z])). CARTAN'S CRITERIA: g semisimple ⇔ B non-degenerate; g
    solvable ⇔ B(g, [g, g]) = 0. Formulas: sl(n): 2n tr(XY); gl(n):
    2n tr(XY) − 2 tr X tr Y; so(n): (n − 2) tr(XY); nilpotent: B = 0; compact
    semisimple: B negative definite.
  - LIE CORRESPONDENCE: a homomorphism φ : G → H has a differential
    dφ : g → h, a Lie algebra homomorphism with φ(e^X) = e^(dφ X); a connected
    G is generated by exp of any neighbourhood of 0; connected subgroups ↔
    subalgebras; if G is SIMPLY CONNECTED every Lie algebra homomorphism
    lifts. Ad : SU(2) → SO(3) is a 2-to-1 covering, so SO(3) ≅ SU(2)/{±I} ≅
    RP³ and π₁(SO(3)) = Z₂.
  - EXPONENTIAL MAP: a local diffeomorphism near 0; surjective for compact
    connected groups (SO(n), U(n)) and for GL(n, C), NOT for SL(2, R)
    ([[−1, 1], [0, −1]] is not an exponential). BAKER-CAMPBELL-HAUSDORFF:
    log(e^X e^Y) = X + Y + ½[X, Y] + (1/12)([X, [X, Y]] + [Y, [Y, X]]) + ...;
    e^(tX)e^(tY)e^(−tX)e^(−tY) = I + t²[X, Y] + O(t³).
  - REPRESENTATIONS OF sl(2): for each n ≥ 0 a unique irreducible Vₙ of
    dimension n + 1 (homogeneous polynomials of degree n in x, y, with
    e = x∂y, f = y∂x, h = x∂x − y∂y), weights n, n − 2, ..., −n; CASIMIR
    h²/2 + ef + fe acts as n(n + 2)/2; CLEBSCH-GORDAN Vₘ ⊗ Vₙ = V_{m+n} ⊕
    V_{m+n−2} ⊕ ... ⊕ V_{|m−n|}. Classification of simple complex Lie
    algebras: Aₙ, Bₙ, Cₙ, Dₙ, G₂, F₄, E₆, E₇, E₈.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Show that GL(n, R) is a Lie group of dimension n² with two connected
    components. Then show that the Heisenberg group H of matrices
    [[1, a, c], [0, 1, b], [0, 0, 1]] is a 3-dimensional Lie group: write
    the product and inverse in the coordinates (a, b, c), compute
    (1, 2, 3)·(4, 5, 6) and (4, 5, 6)·(1, 2, 3), and find the centre.
    Answer: (a, b, c)(a′, b′, c′) = (a + a′, b + b′, c + c′ + ab′);
    (a, b, c)⁻¹ = (−a, −b, ab − c); the products are (5, 7, 14) and
    (5, 7, 17); the centre is {(0, 0, c)} ≅ R.
    Teacher explains: GL(n, R) = det⁻¹(R ∖ {0}) is OPEN in M(n, R) ≅ R^(n²)
    because det is continuous, so it is an n²-manifold with one global
    chart. Multiplication (A, B) ↦ AB has polynomial entries and inversion
    A ↦ adj(A)/det A has rational entries with non-zero denominator: both
    smooth. Components: det is continuous and never 0, so {det > 0} and
    {det < 0} are separated; each is path connected (Gram-Schmidt deforms A
    to an orthogonal matrix, and SO(n) is connected), so there are exactly
    two. H: multiply the matrices:
       [[1, a, c], [0, 1, b], [0, 0, 1]]·[[1, a′, c′], [0, 1, b′], [0, 0, 1]]
          = [[1, a + a′, c′ + ab′ + c], [0, 1, b + b′], [0, 0, 1]].
    So H ≅ R³ as a manifold with the polynomial product (a + a′, b + b′,
    c + c′ + ab′), a Lie group. Inverse: solve a′ = −a, b′ = −b and
    c + c′ + a(−b) = 0, so c′ = ab − c. Numbers:
       (1, 2, 3)(4, 5, 6) = (5, 7, 3 + 6 + 1·5) = (5, 7, 14)
       (4, 5, 6)(1, 2, 3) = (5, 7, 6 + 3 + 4·2) = (5, 7, 17)
    so H is not abelian. (a, b, c) commutes with everything iff ab′ = a′b
    for all a′, b′, i.e. a = b = 0: the centre is {(0, 0, c)}. Its Lie
    algebra (strictly upper triangular 3 × 3 matrices) has [x, y] = z.
    Common mistakes: thinking a group of matrices is a Lie group only if it
    is compact; forgetting to check smoothness of the inverse. Now you try:
    compute the commutator ghg⁻¹h⁻¹ for g = (1, 0, 0), h = (0, 1, 0).
    ((0, 0, 1): commutators lie in the centre)

D2. Prove that O(n) = {A : AᵀA = I} is a compact Lie group of dimension
    n(n − 1)/2 whose tangent space at I is the space of skew-symmetric
    matrices. How many components does O(n) have? Give the dimensions of
    O(3), SO(4) and SO(10).
    Answer: I is a regular value of F(A) = AᵀA : M(n) → Sym(n); dim =
    n² − n(n + 1)/2 = n(n − 1)/2; two components (det = ±1); 3, 6, 45.
    Teacher explains: F takes values in the symmetric matrices Sym(n), a
    space of dimension n(n + 1)/2. Differentiate:
       F(A + tH) = AᵀA + t(AᵀH + HᵀA) + t²HᵀH,  so  dF_A(H) = AᵀH + HᵀA.
    Surjective at A ∈ O(n): given S ∈ Sym(n) put H = AS/2; then AᵀH =
    AᵀAS/2 = S/2 and HᵀA = SᵀAᵀA/2 = S/2, so dF_A(H) = S. By the regular
    value theorem (11.6) O(n) = F⁻¹(I) is a submanifold of dimension
       n² − n(n + 1)/2 = n(n − 1)/2,
    and T_I O(n) = ker dF_I = {H : H + Hᵀ = 0}, the skew matrices so(n)
    (n(n − 1)/2 free entries above the diagonal ✓). O(n) is a subgroup, and
    multiplication and inversion (A⁻¹ = Aᵀ) are restrictions of smooth
    maps, so it is a Lie group. Compact: closed (preimage of a point) and
    bounded (every column is a unit vector, so ‖A‖² = ∑aᵢⱼ² = n). det A = ±1
    since (det A)² = det(AᵀA) = 1; the continuous det separates SO(n) =
    {det = 1} from its coset {det = −1}, and SO(n) is connected, so O(n)
    has 2 components. Dimensions: O(3): 3, SO(4): 6, SO(10): 45. Common
    mistakes: counting n² − n² constraints (AᵀA = I is only n(n + 1)/2
    equations because AᵀA is automatically symmetric); thinking O(n) is
    connected. Now you try: dimension and tangent space at I of U(n) =
    {A*A = I}. (n²; skew-Hermitian matrices X* = −X)

D3. Define the matrix exponential and prove it converges. Prove that
    e^(A+B) = e^A e^B when AB = BA, and show by A = [[0, 1], [0, 0]],
    B = [[0, 0], [1, 0]] that it can fail otherwise. Prove det e^A = e^(tr A)
    and compute e^(tJ) for J = [[0, −1], [1, 0]].
    Answer: ∑‖A‖ᵏ/k! = e^‖A‖ < ∞; e^A e^B = [[2, 1], [1, 1]] but e^(A+B) =
    [[cosh 1, sinh 1], [sinh 1, cosh 1]]; e^(tJ) = rotation by t.
    Teacher explains: With a submultiplicative norm, ‖Aᵏ‖ ≤ ‖A‖ᵏ, so the
    series e^A = ∑ Aᵏ/k! converges absolutely (compare with e^‖A‖), and
    uniformly on bounded sets; differentiating term by term, d/dt e^(tA) =
    Ae^(tA). If AB = BA the binomial theorem holds for (A + B)ᵐ, and the
    Cauchy product gives
       e^A e^B = ∑_{j,k} AʲBᵏ/(j!k!) = ∑ₘ (A + B)ᵐ/m! = e^(A+B).
    In particular e^(sA)e^(tA) = e^((s+t)A) and (e^A)⁻¹ = e^(−A): e^A is always
    invertible. Counterexample: A² = B² = 0, so e^A = I + A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
    and e^B = [[1, 0], [1, 1]], with product [[2, 1], [1, 1]]; but
    (A + B)² = I, so e^(A+B) = cosh 1 I + sinh 1 (A + B) = [[cosh 1, sinh 1],
    [sinh 1, cosh 1]] ≈ [[1.543, 1.175], [1.175, 1.543]] ≠ [[2, 1], [1, 1]].
    det: over C write A = PTP⁻¹ with T upper triangular (diagonal λᵢ); then
    e^A = Pe^TP⁻¹ and e^T is upper triangular with diagonal e^(λᵢ), so
       det e^A = ∏ e^(λᵢ) = e^(∑λᵢ) = e^(tr A).
    Rotation: J² = −I, so the even terms give cos t I and the odd terms
    sin t J:
       e^(tJ) = [[cos t, −sin t], [sin t, cos t]].
    Common mistakes: using e^(A+B) = e^A e^B for non-commuting matrices;
    exponentiating entry by entry (e^A is NOT [[e^(aᵢⱼ)]]). Now you try: e^N
    for N = [[0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 1], [0, 0, 0]], and det e^A for A with
    trace 3. ([[1, 1, 1/2], [0, 1, 1], [0, 0, 1]]; e³)

D4. Compute (a) e^A for A = [[1, 2], [2, 1]] by diagonalising; (b) e^A for
    A = [[3, −1], [1, 1]] using A = λI + N; (c) a formula for e^X for every
    trace-zero 2 × 2 real matrix X, and apply it to [[0, 1], [−4, 0]] and
    [[1, 1], [−1, −1]].
    Answer: (a) ½[[e³ + e⁻¹, e³ − e⁻¹], [e³ − e⁻¹, e³ + e⁻¹]] ≈ [[10.2267,
    9.8588], [9.8588, 10.2267]]; (b) e²[[2, −1], [1, 0]]; (c) with δ = det X:
    cos√δ I + (sin√δ/√δ)X (δ > 0), I + X (δ = 0), cosh√(−δ) I +
    (sinh√(−δ)/√(−δ))X (δ < 0); [[cos 2, (sin 2)/2], [−2 sin 2, cos 2]] and
    [[2, 1], [−1, 0]].
    Teacher explains: (a) A has eigenvalues 3 (vector (1, 1)) and −1
    (vector (1, −1)). With P = [[1, 1], [1, −1]], A = P diag(3, −1)P⁻¹ and
       e^A = P diag(e³, e⁻¹)P⁻¹ = ½[[e³ + e⁻¹, e³ − e⁻¹], [e³ − e⁻¹, e³ + e⁻¹]].
    Check: det e^A = ¼((e³ + e⁻¹)² − (e³ − e⁻¹)²) = e³e⁻¹ = e² = e^(tr A) ✓.
    (b) tr A = 4, det A = 4, so λ = 2 is a double eigenvalue and
    N = A − 2I = [[1, −1], [1, −1]] has N² = 0. Since 2I and N commute,
       e^A = e²(I + N) = e²[[2, −1], [1, 0]].
    (c) Cayley-Hamilton: X² − (tr X)X + (det X)I = 0, so X² = −δI. Hence
    X^(2k) = (−δ)ᵏI and X^(2k+1) = (−δ)ᵏX. For δ > 0 put ω = √δ: the even terms
    sum to cos ω I and the odd ones to (sin ω/ω)X. For δ < 0 put ω = √(−δ):
    cosh and sinh. For δ = 0, X² = 0 and e^X = I + X. Apply: [[0, 1],
    [−4, 0]] has δ = 4, ω = 2:
       e^X = cos 2 I + (sin 2/2)X = [[cos 2, (sin 2)/2], [−2 sin 2, cos 2]];
    [[1, 1], [−1, −1]] has δ = −1 + 1 = 0: e^X = I + X = [[2, 1], [−1, 0]].
    Check with det = e^(tr) = 1: cos²2 + sin²2 = 1 ✓ and 0 + 1 = 1 ✓.
    Common mistakes: forgetting to divide by ω in the odd part; assuming
    A = λI + N works when the eigenvalues are distinct. Now you try: e^X for
    X = [[0, 1], [4, 0]]. ([[cosh 2, (sinh 2)/2], [2 sinh 2, cosh 2]])

D5. Show that exp : sl(2, R) → SL(2, R) is not surjective: prove that
    tr e^X ≥ −2 for every X ∈ sl(2, R), with equality only if e^X = −I, and
    deduce that A = [[−1, 1], [0, −1]] is not an exponential. Write A as a
    product of two exponentials. Contrast with SO(3) and U(n).
    Answer: tr e^X = 2cos√δ, 2 or 2cosh√(−δ), and = −2 forces sin√δ = 0,
    i.e. e^X = −I; A = e^([[0, π], [−π, 0]]) e^([[0, −1], [0, 0]]).
    Teacher explains: By D4(c), e^X = C I + S X with C = cos ω, cosh ω or 1.
    Since tr X = 0, tr e^X = 2C. If δ = det X ≤ 0 then C = cosh ω ≥ 1 or
    C = 1, so tr e^X ≥ 2. If δ > 0 then tr e^X = 2cos ω ≥ −2, with equality
    iff ω = kπ, k odd; then S = sin ω/ω = 0 and e^X = cos ω I = −I. So every
    exponential has trace > −2 or equals −I. But A has trace −2 and A ≠ −I:
    A is not e^X for any X ∈ sl(2, R). (A is also not e^X for X ∈ gl(2, R):
    det A = 1 = e^(tr X) forces tr X = 0.) Still A is in the identity
    component (SL(2, R) is connected), so it is a PRODUCT of exponentials:
       e^([[0, π], [−π, 0]]) = −I,   e^([[0, −1], [0, 0]]) = [[1, −1], [0, 1]],
    and (−I)[[1, −1], [0, 1]] = [[−1, 1], [0, −1]] = A ✓. By contrast, for a
    COMPACT connected group exp is onto: every rotation in SO(3) is e^(θK)
    about its axis; every unitary U = P diag(e^(iθⱼ))P* = e^X with X =
    P diag(iθⱼ)P*. Common mistakes: "SL(2, R) is connected so exp is onto"
    (connectedness only gives products of exponentials); forgetting the
    case δ ≤ 0. Now you try: is diag(−2, −1/2) in the image of exp on
    sl(2, R)? (No: trace −2.5 < −2)

D6. Using g = {X : e^(tX) ∈ G for all t}, find the Lie algebras of U(n),
    SU(n) and Sp(2n, R) = {A : AᵀJA = J}, J = [[0, I], [−I, 0]], with their
    dimensions. Give a basis of su(2) with its brackets.
    Answer: u(n) = {X* = −X}, dim n²; su(n) = {X* = −X, tr X = 0}, dim
    n² − 1; sp(2n, R) = {[[A, B], [C, −Aᵀ]] : B, C symmetric}, dim n(2n + 1);
    su(2) has basis tₖ = −iσₖ/2 with [t₁, t₂] = t₃, [t₂, t₃] = t₁,
    [t₃, t₁] = t₂.
    Teacher explains: U(n): (e^(tX))* e^(tX) = e^(tX*)e^(tX) = I for all t.
    Differentiating at t = 0 gives X* + X = 0; conversely if X* = −X then
    X* and X commute and e^(tX*)e^(tX) = e^(t(X*+X)) = I. A skew-Hermitian
    matrix has n imaginary diagonal entries and n(n − 1)/2 free complex
    entries above: n + n(n − 1) = n² real parameters. SU(n): also
    det e^(tX) = e^(t tr X) = 1 for all t, so tr X = 0 (tr X is imaginary for
    skew-Hermitian X, removing ONE real parameter): n² − 1. Sp(2n, R):
    differentiate e^(tXᵀ)Je^(tX) = J: XᵀJ + JX = 0. Write X = [[A, B], [C, D]]
    in n × n blocks:
       XᵀJ + JX = [[C − Cᵀ, D + Aᵀ], [−A − Dᵀ, Bᵀ − B]] = 0
    gives D = −Aᵀ, B = Bᵀ, C = Cᵀ: n² + 2·n(n + 1)/2 = 2n² + n = n(2n + 1).
    For n = 1: sp(2) = sl(2) (dim 3). su(2): with the Pauli matrices
    σ₁ = [[0, 1], [1, 0]], σ₂ = [[0, −i], [i, 0]], σ₃ = [[1, 0], [0, −1]],
    σ₁σ₂ = iσ₃ gives [σ₁, σ₂] = 2iσ₃, so tₖ = −iσₖ/2 satisfy
       [t₁, t₂] = (−i/2)²·2iσ₃ = −iσ₃/2 = t₃
    and cyclically: the same structure constants as (R³, ×) and so(3).
    Common mistakes: forgetting the trace condition for SU(n); counting
    complex parameters as real ones. Now you try: dim of U(3), SU(3),
    Sp(4, R) and Sp(6, R). (9, 8, 10, 21)

D7. For a matrix group define Ad_g Y = gYg⁻¹ and show that d/dt|₀ Ad_{e^(tX)} Y
    = XY − YX. Compute Ad_{e^(te)} f in sl(2, R) (e = [[0, 1], [0, 0]],
    f = [[0, 0], [1, 0]], h = [[1, 0], [0, −1]]), compare with e^(t ad e) f, and
    explain why g is closed under the bracket.
    Answer: Ad_{e^(te)} f = f + th − t²e = e^(t ad e) f; the derivative at 0 is
    h = [e, f].
    Teacher explains: For g ∈ G the map c_g(x) = gxg⁻¹ fixes I, so its
    differential Ad_g maps g to g: Ad_g Y = d/ds g e^(sY) g⁻¹ = gYg⁻¹ ∈ g.
    Now differentiate in g along e^(tX):
       d/dt|₀ e^(tX) Y e^(−tX) = XY − YX = [X, Y],
    and since Ad_{e^(tX)} Y ∈ g for all t and g is a (closed) vector space,
    the derivative [X, Y] lies in g. So g is a Lie algebra. Example:
    e^(te) = [[1, t], [0, 1]], and
       [[1, t], [0, 1]]·[[0, 0], [1, 0]]·[[1, −t], [0, 1]] = [[t, −t²], [1, −t]]
                                                     = f + th − t²e.
    The derivative at t = 0 is h = [e, f] ✓. The series side: [e, f] = h,
    [e, h] = −2e, [e, e] = 0, so
       e^(t ad e) f = f + t h + (t²/2)(−2e) + 0 = f + th − t²e,
    the same: Ad_{e^(tX)} = e^(t ad X) (both solve Φ′ = ad X ∘ Φ). Abstractly
    the LEFT-INVARIANT vector field X̃(g) = gX has flow g ↦ ge^(tX), and
    [X̃, Ỹ] = [X, Y]~ for the vector-field bracket of 11.6, which is how a
    general Lie group gets its Lie algebra. Common mistakes: writing
    Ad_g Y = g⁻¹Yg (a different convention that flips signs); forgetting that
    Ad_g is linear in Y. Now you try: Ad_{diag(a, 1/a)} e and
    Ad_{e^(te)} h. (a²e; h − 2te)

D8. Identify so(3) with (R³, ×) via L_ω v = ω × v. Show [L_a, L_b] = L_{a×b},
    derive Rodrigues' formula e^(θK) = I + sin θ K + (1 − cos θ)K² for
    K = L_n, |n| = 1, and use it to compute the rotation by 2π/3 about
    n = (1, 1, 1)/√3. Check the trace.
    Answer: the rotation is the cyclic permutation matrix [[0, 0, 1],
    [1, 0, 0], [0, 1, 0]] (e₁ → e₂ → e₃ → e₁); trace 0 = 1 + 2cos(2π/3).
    Teacher explains: L_ω = [[0, −ω₃, ω₂], [ω₃, 0, −ω₁], [−ω₂, ω₁, 0]] is
    skew, and ω ↦ L_ω is a linear isomorphism R³ → so(3). The Jacobi identity
    for × gives
       [L_a, L_b]v = a × (b × v) − b × (a × v) = (a × b) × v = L_{a×b} v,
    so so(3) ≅ (R³, ×): [L₁, L₂] = L₃ etc. For a unit vector n, K = L_n
    satisfies K² = nnᵀ − I (from n × (n × v) = (n·v)n − v) and hence
    K³ = −K. Split the series into odd and even powers:
       e^(θK) = I + (θ − θ³/3! + ...)K + (θ²/2! − θ⁴/4! + ...)K²
             = I + sin θ K + (1 − cos θ)K².
    It fixes n (Kn = 0) and rotates n^⊥ by θ (there K² = −I and K acts as
    a quarter turn). Example: sin(2π/3) = √3/2, 1 − cos(2π/3) = 3/2,
    K = (1/√3)[[0, −1, 1], [1, 0, −1], [−1, 1, 0]], K² = (1/3)𝟙 − I (𝟙 the
    all-ones matrix). So
       R = I + ½[[0, −1, 1], [1, 0, −1], [−1, 1, 0]] + (3/2)((1/3)𝟙 − I)
         = −½I + ½[[0, −1, 1], [1, 0, −1], [−1, 1, 0]] + ½𝟙
         = [[0, 0, 1], [1, 0, 0], [0, 1, 0]].
    Rotating by a third of a turn about the diagonal permutes the axes
    cyclically, as symmetry suggests. tr R = 0 = 1 + 2cos θ ✓, and in
    general the angle of a rotation is found from tr R = 1 + 2cos θ. Common
    mistakes: wrong sign pattern in L_ω; forgetting that the formula needs
    |n| = 1. Now you try: e^(θL₃). ([[cos θ, −sin θ, 0], [sin θ, cos θ, 0],
    [0, 0, 1]])

D9. In sl(2, R) with basis h, e, f compute the matrices of ad h, ad e, ad f,
    the Killing form B, and deduce that sl(2, R) is semisimple. Show that it
    is simple, and find the signature of B.
    Answer: in the basis (h, e, f): ad h = diag(0, 2, −2), ad e = [[0, 0, 1],
    [−2, 0, 0], [0, 0, 0]], ad f = [[0, −1, 0], [0, 0, 0], [2, 0, 0]];
    B = [[8, 0, 0], [0, 0, 4], [0, 4, 0]], det B = −128 ≠ 0; B(X, Y) =
    4 tr(XY); signature (2, 1).
    Teacher explains: The brackets are [h, e] = 2e, [h, f] = −2f, [e, f] = h
    (check: he − eh = [[0, 1], [0, 0]] − [[0, −1], [0, 0]] = 2e). The j-th
    column of ad X is [X, j-th basis vector]:
       ad h: h ↦ 0, e ↦ 2e, f ↦ −2f
       ad e: h ↦ −2e, e ↦ 0, f ↦ h
       ad f: h ↦ 2f, e ↦ −h, f ↦ 0
    giving the matrices above. Now B(X, Y) = tr(ad X ad Y):
       B(h, h) = tr diag(0, 4, 4) = 8,  B(e, f) = tr(ad e ad f) = 4,
       B(h, e) = B(h, f) = B(e, e) = B(f, f) = 0.
    det B = 8(0·0 − 4·4) = −128 ≠ 0, so by CARTAN'S CRITERION sl(2, R) is
    semisimple. Compare 4 tr(XY): 4 tr(h²) = 8, 4 tr(ef) = 4 ✓. Simple: let
    I ≠ 0 be an ideal and 0 ≠ X = αh + βe + γf ∈ I. If β ≠ 0 then
    [f, [f, X]] = −2βf ∈ I; if γ ≠ 0 then [e, [e, X]] = −2γe ∈ I; if X = αh
    then [e, X] = −2αe ∈ I. So I contains e or f; then it contains
    h = [e, f], and then e = [h, e]/2 and f = −[h, f]/2: I = g. Signature:
    in the basis h, e + f, e − f the form is diag(8, 8, −8): two positive,
    one negative. So sl(2, R) is not the Lie algebra of a compact group
    (compact semisimple ⇒ B negative definite, like so(3) where B = −2I).
    Common mistakes: writing rows instead of columns for ad X; forgetting
    B(e, f) (the form is not diagonal in h, e, f). Now you try: the Killing
    form of so(3) in the basis L₁, L₂, L₃. (−2I: ad L₁ = L₁ as a 3 × 3
    matrix, tr L₁² = −2)

D10. Define the derived series and the lower central series. Compute them
     for (a) the 2 × 2 upper triangular matrices b(2); (b) the Heisenberg
     algebra; (c) the 3 × 3 upper triangular matrices b(3); (d) gl(2).
     Classify each as nilpotent, solvable, semisimple or none, and state the
     theorems of Engel, Lie and Levi.
     Answer: (a) dims 3 → 1 → 0 (solvable), lower central 3 → 1 → 1 (stationary)
     (not nilpotent); (b) 3 → 1 → 0 both (nilpotent); (c) derived 6 → 3 → 1
     → 0 (solvable, not nilpotent); (d) [gl(2), gl(2)] = sl(2), which is
     perfect: neither solvable nor semisimple (reductive).
     Teacher explains: DERIVED: g⁽⁰⁾ = g, g⁽ᵏ⁺¹⁾ = [g⁽ᵏ⁾, g⁽ᵏ⁾]; SOLVABLE if
     some g⁽ᵏ⁾ = 0. LOWER CENTRAL: g₁ = g, gₖ₊₁ = [g, gₖ]; NILPOTENT if some
     gₖ = 0. Nilpotent ⇒ solvable (g⁽ᵏ⁾ ⊆ gₖ₊₁). (a) b(2) = span{E₁₁, E₂₂, E₁₂}:
     [E₁₁, E₁₂] = E₁₂, [E₂₂, E₁₂] = −E₁₂, [E₁₁, E₂₂] = 0, so [b, b] = RE₁₂
     (dim 1) and [RE₁₂, RE₁₂] = 0: solvable. But [b, RE₁₂] = RE₁₂ again, so
     the lower central series sticks at dimension 1: NOT nilpotent (this is
     the 2-dimensional algebra [x, y] = y inside). (b) [x, y] = z, z
     central: [h, h] = Rz, [h, Rz] = 0: nilpotent (so also solvable).
     (c) [b(3), b(3)] = n(3), the strictly upper triangular matrices (dim 3:
     the diagonal parts commute and [diagonal, E_ij] = (dᵢ − dⱼ)E_ij);
     [n(3), n(3)] = RE₁₃ (from [E₁₂, E₂₃] = E₁₃); then 0. Dimensions 6, 3,
     1, 0. Not nilpotent for the same reason as (a). (d) [gl(2), gl(2)] ⊆
     sl(2) (commutators have trace 0) and contains [e, f] = h, [h, e] = 2e,
     [h, f] = −2f, so it equals sl(2), and [sl(2), sl(2)] = sl(2): the
     derived series never reaches 0. gl(2) = sl(2) ⊕ R·I with the centre
     R·I a solvable ideal, so it is not semisimple. THEOREMS (over C,
     finite dimensions): ENGEL: if every ad X is nilpotent then g is
     nilpotent. LIE: a solvable g ⊆ gl(V) has a common eigenvector, so it can
     be put in upper triangular form. LEVI: g = r ⋉ s with r the largest
     solvable ideal (the RADICAL) and s semisimple. Common mistakes:
     confusing the two series; "solvable ⇒ nilpotent" (b(2) is the
     counterexample). Now you try: is e(2) ([J, P₁] = P₂, [J, P₂] = −P₁,
     [P₁, P₂] = 0) solvable? nilpotent? (solvable: [e(2), e(2)] = span{P₁,
     P₂}, abelian; not nilpotent: [J, ·] maps that span onto itself)

D11. Show that SU(2) ≅ S³, and that Ad : SU(2) → GL(su(2)) is a 2-to-1
     homomorphism onto SO(3). Verify that Ad of e^(θt₃) rotates t₁, t₂ by the
     angle θ. What does this say about π₁(SO(3)) and about the Lie algebras?
     Answer: SU(2) = {[[a, −b̄], [b, ā]] : |a|² + |b|² = 1}; Ad_{e^(θt₃)} t₁ =
     cos θ t₁ + sin θ t₂; kernel {±I}; SO(3) ≅ SU(2)/{±I} ≅ RP³, π₁ = Z₂;
     su(2) ≅ so(3).
     Teacher explains: A matrix in SU(2) has orthonormal columns and det 1;
     if the first column is (a, b) the second must be (−b̄, ā). So SU(2) =
     {(a, b) ∈ C² : |a|² + |b|² = 1} = S³ ⊆ R⁴: simply connected. Put the
     inner product ⟨X, Y⟩ = −2 tr(XY) on su(2), making t₁, t₂, t₃ orthonormal.
     Ad_U X = UXU⁻¹ preserves the trace, so Ad_U ∈ O(3); SU(2) is connected
     and Ad_I = I, so Ad lands in SO(3). Kernel: Ad_U = I means U commutes
     with all of su(2), hence with all 2 × 2 matrices, so U = ±I. The
     differential ad : su(2) → so(3) is injective, so an isomorphism
     (dimensions 3 = 3); the image of Ad is therefore open, and closed
     (compact), so Ad is ONTO SO(3). Explicit: t₃ = −iσ₃/2 = diag(−i/2,
     i/2), so U = e^(θt₃) = diag(e^(−iθ/2), e^(iθ/2)). Conjugation multiplies
     the (1, 2) entry by e^(−iθ) and the (2, 1) entry by e^(iθ):
        t₁ = [[0, −i/2], [−i/2, 0]] ↦ [[0, −ie^(−iθ)/2], [−ie^(iθ)/2, 0]]
                                    = cos θ t₁ + sin θ t₂,
     using t₂ = [[0, −1/2], [1/2, 0]]. So Ad_U is rotation by θ about the
     t₃-axis — while U itself only rotated its entries by θ/2: at θ = 2π,
     U = −I but Ad_U = I. Hence SO(3) ≅ SU(2)/{±I} ≅ S³/antipodal = RP³, with
     π₁(SO(3)) = Z₂ (the "belt trick"), and su(2) ≅ so(3) although the groups
     differ. Common mistakes: expecting the rotation angle θ/2; thinking
     isomorphic Lie algebras force isomorphic groups. Now you try: which
     rotation is Ad of e^(πt₁)? (rotation by π about the t₁-axis; e^(πt₁) =
     −iσ₁ and its negative give the same rotation)

D12. State the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula to second order. Verify it
     exactly in the Heisenberg group with X = E₁₂, Y = E₂₃ (3 × 3), and show
     that the group commutator e^X e^Y e^(−X) e^(−Y) equals e^[X, Y]. Explain
     the general statement e^(tX)e^(tY)e^(−tX)e^(−tY) = I + t²[X, Y] + O(t³)
     and state the Lie correspondence.
     Answer: e^X e^Y = [[1, 1, 1], [0, 1, 1], [0, 0, 1]] = e^(X+Y+½[X,Y]) ≠
     e^(X+Y) = [[1, 1, ½], [0, 1, 1], [0, 0, 1]]; commutator I + E₁₃ = e^(E₁₃).
     Teacher explains: BCH: for small X, Y, e^X e^Y = e^Z with
        Z = X + Y + ½[X, Y] + (1/12)([X, [X, Y]] + [Y, [Y, X]]) + ...,
     every term after X + Y being an iterated BRACKET: the group law near I
     is determined by the Lie algebra. Heisenberg: [X, Y] = E₁₂E₂₃ − E₂₃E₁₂ =
     E₁₃, which commutes with X and Y, so all higher terms vanish. Now
        e^X = I + E₁₂,  e^Y = I + E₂₃,  e^X e^Y = I + E₁₂ + E₂₃ + E₁₃,
     while (X + Y)² = E₁₂E₂₃ = E₁₃ and (X + Y)³ = 0, so
        e^(X+Y) = I + E₁₂ + E₂₃ + ½E₁₃,
        e^(X+Y+½E₁₃) = I + E₁₂ + E₂₃ + ½E₁₃ + ½E₁₃ = I + E₁₂ + E₂₃ + E₁₃ ✓.
     Commutator: e^(−X) = I − E₁₂, e^(−Y) = I − E₂₃;
        (I + E₁₂ + E₂₃ + E₁₃)(I − E₁₂)(I − E₂₃) = I + E₁₃ = e^(E₁₃) ✓.
     In general expand each factor to second order: the terms of order t
     cancel, and the t² terms combine to XY − YX: e^(tX)e^(tY)e^(−tX)e^(−tY) =
     I + t²[X, Y] + O(t³) — the group version of 11.6 D12. LIE
     CORRESPONDENCE: a Lie group homomorphism φ has a differential dφ with
     φ(e^X) = e^(dφ(X)) and dφ[X, Y] = [dφX, dφY]; connected subgroups ↔ Lie
     subalgebras; if G is connected and simply connected, every Lie
     algebra homomorphism g → h is dφ for a unique φ : G → H (e.g. the
     isomorphism su(2) → so(3) comes from Ad, but so(3) → su(2) does not
     come from any SO(3) → SU(2)). Common mistakes: forgetting that BCH
     converges only near 0 in general; using e^(X+Y) for the product. Now
     you try: X = sE₁₂, Y = tE₂₃: find the (1, 3) entry of e^X e^Y. (st)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Give the (real) dimension of the Lie group.
    a) GL(3, R)                        b) SL(3, R)
    c) GL(2, C)                        d) SL(2, C)
    e) O(3)                            f) SO(5)
    g) U(2)                            h) SU(2)
    i) SU(3)                           j) Sp(4, R)
    k) Sp(2, R)                        l) the Heisenberg group
    m) the affine group {x ↦ ax + b, a > 0}
    n) SE(3) (rigid motions of R³)     o) the Lorentz group O(1, 3)
    p) the Poincaré group              q) invertible upper triangular 3 × 3
    r) unipotent upper triangular 4 × 4
    s) the torus T⁴                    t) SO(10)
    u) U(5)                            v) Sp(6, R)
    w) the compact group of type G₂    x) the compact group of type E₈
    Answers:
      a) 9      b) 8      c) 8      d) 6      e) 3      f) 10
      g) 4      h) 3      i) 8      j) 10     k) 3      l) 3
      m) 2      n) 6      o) 6      p) 10     q) 6      r) 6
      s) 4      t) 45     u) 25     v) 21     w) 14     x) 248

M2. List all the groups among O(n), SO(n), SL(n, R), U(n), SU(n) (right
    size, real or complex as appropriate) containing the matrix; for i)–l)
    answer the question.
    a) [[0, −1], [1, 0]]               b) [[1, 0], [0, −1]]
    c) [[2, 0], [0, 1/2]]              d) [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
    e) (1/√2)[[1, i], [i, 1]]          f) diag(i, i)
    g) diag(e^(iα), e^(−iα))           h) −I₃
    i) is [[1, 2], [0, 1]] in Sp(2, R)?
    j) is [[cosh 1, sinh 1], [sinh 1, cosh 1]] in SO(1, 1) (preserving
       x² − y²)?
    k) is [[1, a, c], [0, 1, b], [0, 0, 1]] in the Heisenberg group?
    l) number of components of O(3), GL(2, R), GL(2, C), U(2), O(1, 3)
    m) [[0, 0, 1], [1, 0, 0], [0, 1, 0]]
    n) diag(1, 1, −1)                  o) −I₂
    Answers:
      a) O(2), SO(2), SL(2, R), U(2), SU(2)
      b) O(2), U(2)                   c) SL(2, R)
      d) SL(2, R)                     e) U(2), SU(2) (det = 1)
      f) U(2) (det = −1)              g) U(2), SU(2)
      h) O(3), U(3) (det = −1)        i) yes (Sp(2, R) = SL(2, R))
      j) yes                          k) yes
      l) 2, 2, 1, 1, 4                m) O(3), SO(3), SL(3, R), U(3), SU(3)
      n) O(3), U(3)                   o) O(2), SO(2), SL(2, R), U(2), SU(2)

M3. Matrix exponentials. Compute e^A.
    a) the zero matrix                 b) diag(1, 2)
    c) [[0, 1], [0, 0]]                d) [[0, −θ], [θ, 0]]
    e) [[0, 1], [1, 0]]                f) [[1, 1], [0, 1]]

M4. Properties of exp; logarithms.
    a) det e^A for tr A = 5            b) det e^A for A ∈ sl(n)

    d) is e^(A+B) = e^A e^B for A = diag(1, 2), B = diag(3, 4)?
    e) the same for A = [[0, 1], [0, 0]], B = [[0, 0], [1, 0]]
    f) (e^A)⁻¹                         g) e^(PAP⁻¹) in terms of e^A

M5. Is X in the Lie algebra? Answer with all of sl, so, su, u (or sp,
    heis where asked) that contain X.
    a) [[1, 2], [3, −1]]               b) [[0, 1], [−1, 0]]
    c) [[i, 0], [0, −i]]               d) [[i, 0], [0, i]]

    l) is [[0, a, b], [0, 0, c], [0, 0, 0]] in the Heisenberg algebra?
    m) [[0, 1], [1, 0]] in so(1, 1)?
    n) [[1, 2, 3, 4], [0, −1, 4, 5], [6, 7, −1, 0], [7, 8, −2, 1]] in sp(4, R)?
    o) the same with the entry 4 in place (2, 3) replaced by 5: in sp(4, R)?

M7. Abstract Lie algebras and structure constants. Unlisted brackets of
    basis vectors are 0.
    a) Is [x, y] = z, [y, z] = x, [z, x] = y a Lie algebra? Which one?
    b) Is [x, y] = x, [x, z] = y, [y, z] = z a Lie algebra? Identify it.
    c) Is [x, y] = y, [x, z] = y, [y, z] = x a Lie algebra?
    d) Is [x, y] = y, [x, z] = z a Lie algebra? Is it nilpotent?
    e) In sl(2) with H = h, X = e + f, Y = e − f: [H, X], [H, Y], [X, Y]
    f) In e(2) with J = rotation generator, P₁, P₂ translations: [J, P₁],
       [J, P₂], [P₁, P₂]
    g) In aff(1) with X = [[1, 0], [0, 0]], Y = [[0, 1], [0, 0]]: [X, Y]
    h) Heisenberg: [x, y] = z. [x, [x, y]] and [y, z]
    i) (R³, ×): e₁ × e₂, e₂ × e₃, (e₁ × e₂) × e₂
    j) su(2) basis tₖ = −iσₖ/2: the structure constants
    k) number of 2-dimensional real Lie algebras up to isomorphism
    l) the centre of gl(2), of sl(2), of the Heisenberg algebra
    m) dim [g, g] for g = gl(3), sl(3), heis, aff(1), e(2)
    n) the Jacobi sum [x, [y, z]] + [y, [z, x]] + [z, [x, y]] in c)
    Answers:
      a) yes: so(3) ≅ su(2) ≅ (R³, ×)
      b) yes: ≅ sl(2, R) (its Killing form is non-degenerate, signature
         (2, 1))
      c) no (Jacobi fails)
      d) yes (a solvable algebra); not nilpotent
      e) 2Y, 2X, −2H
      f) P₂, −P₁, 0                    g) Y
      h) 0; 0                          i) e₃, e₁, −e₁
      j) [t₁, t₂] = t₃, [t₂, t₃] = t₁, [t₃, t₁] = t₂ (cᵢⱼᵏ = εᵢⱼₖ)
      k) 2 (abelian, and [x, y] = y)
      l) R·I; 0; R·z
      m) 8, 8, 1, 1, 2
      n) −x ≠ 0

M10. Adjoint action Ad_g X = gXg⁻¹, ad and one-parameter subgroups.
     a) Ad_{diag(a, 1/a)} e             b) Ad_{diag(a, 1/a)} f
     c) Ad_{diag(a, 1/a)} h             d) Ad_{e^(te)} h
     e) Ad_{e^(te)} f                   f) Ad_g X for g ∈ SO(2), X ∈ so(2)
     g) Ad_R L_v for R = rotation by π/2 about e₃ and v = e₁
     h) e^(t ad h) e                    i) (ad e)³ on sl(2)
     j) Ad_g h for g = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
     k) the one-parameter subgroup e^(tX) for X = [[0, 1], [0, 0]]
     l) the one-parameter subgroups of T² = S¹ × S¹; which are closed?
     m) Heisenberg, g = (a, b, c): Ad_g x and Ad_g y (x = E₁₂, y = E₂₃)
     n) the centres of SU(2), U(n), SL(2, R), SO(3), SO(4)
     o) the kernel of Ad : G → GL(g) for connected G
     Answers:
       a) a²e          b) a⁻²f          c) h           d) h − 2te
       e) f + th − t²e                 f) X (so(2) is abelian)
       g) L_{e₂} (Ad_R L_v = L_{Rv})    h) e^(2t)e
       i) 0            j) h − 2e = [[1, −2], [0, −1]]
       k) [[1, t], [0, 1]]
       l) t ↦ (e^(iat), e^(ibt)); closed iff a/b is rational (or ab = 0)
       m) x − bz; y + az (z = E₁₃)
       n) {±I}; the scalars e^(iθ)I ≅ U(1); {±I}; {I}; {±I}
       o) the centre Z(G)

M12. Representations of sl(2) and the classification. Vₙ is the
     irreducible representation of dimension n + 1 (polynomials of degree
     n in x, y; e = x∂y, f = y∂x, h = x∂x − y∂y); C = h²/2 + ef + fe.
     a) the weights of V₃               b) dim V₆
     c) C on V₁, V₂, V₃                 d) h(x²y), e(y²), f(x²), e(xy)
     e) V₁ ⊗ V₁                         f) V₂ ⊗ V₁
     g) V₂ ⊗ V₂                         h) V₃ ⊗ V₂
     i) the representation with weights 2, 1, 1, 0, −1, −1, −2
     j) the representation with weights 4, 2, 2, 0, 0, −2, −2, −4
     k) the adjoint representation of sl(2)
     l) the dimension of the spin-j representation (n = 2j)
     m) dim of Aₙ, Bₙ, Cₙ, Dₙ (formulas)
     n) dim of A₂, B₂, C₃, D₄
     o) dim of G₂, F₄, E₆, E₇, E₈
     p) which classical algebra is so(5), so(6), sp(2)?
     Answers:
       a) 3, 1, −1, −3                 b) 7
       c) 3/2, 4, 15/2 (n(n + 2)/2)
       d) x²y (weight 1); 2xy; 2xy; x²
       e) V₂ ⊕ V₀                      f) V₃ ⊕ V₁
       g) V₄ ⊕ V₂ ⊕ V₀                 h) V₅ ⊕ V₃ ⊕ V₁
       i) V₂ ⊕ 2V₁                     j) V₄ ⊕ V₂
       k) V₂ (weights 2, 0, −2 on e, h, f)
       l) 2j + 1
       m) n(n + 2), n(2n + 1), n(2n + 1), n(2n − 1)
       n) 8, 10, 21, 28
       o) 14, 52, 78, 133, 248
       p) B₂ = C₂ (so(5) ≅ sp(4)); D₃ = A₃ (so(6) ≅ sl(4)); A₁ (sp(2) =
          sl(2))

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work only with 2 × 2 and 3 × 3 matrices at first. For every
  group write the defining equation, differentiate it along e^(tX) to get
  the Lie algebra, and count free parameters to get the dimension; check
  the count against n² − (number of independent equations). Compute
  exponentials by the three methods (diagonalise, λI + N, Cayley-Hamilton)
  and always check det e^A = e^(tr A). Keep a card with the sl(2) and so(3)
  brackets, and compute ad matrices column by column. Harder: prove
  Cartan's closed subgroup theorem for matrix groups; show that
  SO(4) ≅ (SU(2) × SU(2))/{±(I, I)} using unit quaternions acting by
  q ↦ aqb̄; prove that exp : u(n) → U(n) is onto but exp : sl(2, C) →
  SL(2, C) is not (try −[[1, 1], [0, 1]]); prove complete reducibility
  for sl(2) and classify its irreducible representations from the
  commutation relations alone; compute the root system of sl(3) and draw
  it; prove that a connected abelian Lie group is Rᵏ × Tˡ.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.8 — COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRA (INTRODUCTION): NOETHERIAN RINGS,
LOCALISATION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - RINGS are commutative with 1. An ideal P ≠ R is PRIME if ab ∈ P ⇒ a ∈ P
    or b ∈ P (⇔ R/P is an integral domain); M is MAXIMAL if no ideal lies
    strictly between M and R (⇔ R/M is a field). Maximal ⇒ prime, not
    conversely ((x) in Z[x]). By Zorn's lemma every proper ideal lies in a
    maximal ideal. Correspondence: ideals of R/I ↔ ideals of R containing I.
  - SPEC: Spec R = set of prime ideals with the ZARISKI topology, closed
    sets V(I) = {P : P ⊇ I}, basic open sets D(f) = {P : f ∉ P}.
    V(I) ∪ V(J) = V(IJ), ⋂V(Iₐ) = V(∑Iₐ). Spec Z = {(0)} ∪ {(p)}; (0) is a
    GENERIC point (its closure is everything), maximal ideals are the
    CLOSED points. Spec(A × B) = Spec A ⊔ Spec B; Spec Zₙ ↔ primes p | n.
  - RADICALS: the NILRADICAL N(R) = {nilpotents} = ⋂ (all primes); the
    JACOBSON RADICAL J(R) = ⋂ (all maximal ideals), and x ∈ J ⇔ 1 − xy is
    a unit for every y. N ⊆ J (equal in Zₙ, k[x]/(f), R[x]; not in
    k[[x]], Z₍ₚ₎). √I = {x : xⁿ ∈ I for some n} = ⋂ (primes ⊇ I);
    in Z, √(n) = (product of the distinct primes dividing n).
  - NOETHERIAN RINGS: TFAE (i) ACC: every chain I₁ ⊆ I₂ ⊆ ... stabilises;
    (ii) every ideal is finitely generated; (iii) every non-empty set of
    ideals has a maximal element. Examples: fields, PIDs (Z, k[x], Z[i]),
    finite rings, k[[x]], Z₍ₚ₎, quotients and localisations of Noetherian
    rings. Non-examples: k[x₁, x₂, ...], C[0, 1], Z + xQ[x], the ring of
    all algebraic integers, ∏ F₂, entire functions.
  - HILBERT BASIS THEOREM: R Noetherian ⇒ R[x] Noetherian (proof by ideals of
    leading coefficients); hence k[x₁, ..., xₙ]/I, Z[x₁, ..., xₙ]/I and all
    finitely generated algebras over them are Noetherian; every system of
    polynomial equations is equivalent to a finite one. A SUBRING of a
    Noetherian ring need not be Noetherian (k[x, xy, xy², ...] ⊂ k[x, y]).
  - NOETHERIAN MODULES: M is Noetherian iff every submodule is finitely
    generated; submodules, quotients and extensions of Noetherian modules
    are Noetherian; a finitely generated module over a Noetherian ring is
    Noetherian. ARTINIAN = descending chain condition; Z is Noetherian but
    not Artinian ((2) ⊃ (4) ⊃ (8) ⊃ ...).
  - LOCALISATION: S ⊆ R MULTIPLICATIVE (1 ∈ S, closed under products).
    S⁻¹R = {r/s} with r/s = r′/s′ ⇔ t(rs′ − r′s) = 0 for some t ∈ S (the t
    is needed when R has zero divisors). R → S⁻¹R, r ↦ r/1, has kernel
    {r : sr = 0 for some s ∈ S}; S⁻¹R = 0 ⇔ 0 ∈ S. UNIVERSAL PROPERTY: any
    ring map sending S to units factors uniquely through S⁻¹R.
  - STANDARD LOCALISATIONS: R_f = R[1/f] (S = {1, f, f², ...}; R_f = 0 iff
    f is nilpotent); R_P = (R ∖ P)⁻¹R at a prime P; Frac R for a domain
    (S = R ∖ {0}). Z₍ₚ₎ = {a/b : p ∤ b}, Z[1/n], Q = Frac Z,
    k(x) = Frac k[x]. Localisation commutes with quotients:
    S⁻¹R/S⁻¹I ≅ S̄⁻¹(R/I), e.g. (Z/12)₍₂₎ ≅ Z/4.
  - PRIMES OF S⁻¹R ↔ primes P of R with P ∩ S = ∅ (P ↦ S⁻¹P,
    Q ↦ Q ∩ R). So Spec R_P = primes contained in P, Spec R_f = D(f).
    R_P is LOCAL with maximal ideal PR_P and residue field Frac(R/P).
  - LOCAL RINGS: exactly one maximal ideal m ⇔ the non-units form an ideal
    ⇔ 1 + m consists of units. Examples: fields, Z₍ₚ₎, Z/pⁿ, k[[x]] (units
    = series with non-zero constant term), k[x]/(xⁿ), R_P. Zₙ is local iff
    n is a prime power. Residue field k = R/m.
  - NAKAYAMA'S LEMMA: M finitely generated, I ⊆ J(R), IM = M ⇒ M = 0.
    Over a local ring (R, m, k): m₁, ..., mₙ generate M ⇔ their images span
    M/mM; minimal number of generators μ(M) = dim_k M/mM. Fails for
    non-f.g. modules (pQ = Q over Z₍ₚ₎) and if I ⊄ J (2·Z/3 = Z/3 over Z).
  - KRULL DIMENSION: dim R = sup of lengths n of chains P₀ ⊊ P₁ ⊊ ... ⊊ Pₙ
    of primes. dim (field) = 0, dim (PID, not a field) = 1,
    dim k[x₁, ..., xₙ] = n, dim R[x] = dim R + 1 for Noetherian R
    (dim Z[x] = 2), dim R/P ≤ dim R. ARTINIAN ⇔ Noetherian of dimension 0.
    A Noetherian local ring is REGULAR if μ(m) = dim m/m² = dim R.
  - LOCAL-GLOBAL PRINCIPLE: M = 0 ⇔ M_m = 0 for every maximal m;
    injectivity, surjectivity and exactness can be checked locally;
    localisation is EXACT. (Z/n)₍ₚ₎ ≅ Z/p^(vₚ(n)). "Domain" is NOT a local
    property (Z₆ localises to the fields F₂ and F₃).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. In Z[x] decide whether each ideal is prime, maximal or neither:
    (x), (2, x), (x² + 1), (3, x² + 1), (5, x² + 1), (4, x). Use this to
    exhibit a chain of three primes in Z[x].
    Answer: (x): prime, not maximal; (2, x): maximal; (x² + 1): prime, not
    maximal; (3, x² + 1): maximal; (5, x² + 1): neither; (4, x): neither.
    Chain: (0) ⊊ (x) ⊊ (2, x).
    Teacher explains: The two tests are always QUOTIENT tests:
       P prime ⇔ R/P is an integral domain,  M maximal ⇔ R/M is a field.
    So compute each quotient.
       Z[x]/(x) ≅ Z            (put x = 0)     domain, not a field
       Z[x]/(2, x) ≅ F₂                        field
       Z[x]/(x² + 1) ≅ Z[i]    (x ↦ i)         domain, not a field
       Z[x]/(3, x² + 1) ≅ F₃[x]/(x² + 1)
    In F₃ the values of x² + 1 at 0, 1, 2 are 1, 2, 2, so it has no root;
    a quadratic without roots is irreducible, and F₃[x]/(irreducible of
    degree 2) is a field with 9 elements. For (5, x² + 1): modulo 5,
    x² + 1 ≡ x² − 4 = (x − 2)(x + 2), so (x − 2)(x + 2) lies in the ideal
    while neither factor does (the quotient is F₅[x]/((x − 2)(x + 2)) ≅
    F₅ × F₅ by the Chinese remainder theorem, which has zero divisors).
    For (4, x): the quotient is Z₄ and 2·2 = 0. Finally each ideal in
    (0) ⊊ (x) ⊊ (2, x) is prime, so Z[x] has a chain of length 2 and
    dim Z[x] ≥ 2 (in fact = 2). Common mistakes: "(x) is maximal" (it is
    maximal in Q[x], not in Z[x]); "x² + 1 is irreducible, so (x² + 1) is
    maximal" (irreducible ⇒ maximal only in a PID, and Z[x] is not one).
    Now you try: is (2, x² + x + 1) maximal in Z[x]? (Yes: x² + x + 1 has
    no root in F₂, so the quotient is the field F₄.)

D2. Prove that the nilradical of R is the intersection of all prime
    ideals. Then find N(R) and J(R) for R = Z₇₂, k[[x]] and Z[x].
    Answer: Z₇₂: N = J = (6) = {0, 6, 12, ..., 66}, 12 elements;
    k[[x]]: N = 0, J = (x); Z[x]: N = J = 0.
    Teacher explains: (⊆) If fⁿ = 0 ∈ P and P is prime then f ∈ P (induction
    on n). So every nilpotent lies in every prime. (⊇) Let f be NOT
    nilpotent. Then S = {1, f, f², ...} does not contain 0, so the
    localisation R_f = S⁻¹R is not the zero ring and has a maximal ideal m
    (Zorn). Its preimage P = {r : r/1 ∈ m} is a prime of R, and f ∉ P
    because f/1 is a unit in R_f. So f misses some prime. □
    Z₇₂: 72 = 2³·3², the primes are (2) and (3), so N = (2) ∩ (3) = (6).
    Check: 6³ = 216 = 3·72 ≡ 0. The multiples of 6 below 72 number 72/6 =
    12. In a finite ring every prime is maximal (a finite domain is a
    field), so J = N = (6). k[[x]] is a domain, so N = 0; a series is a
    unit iff its constant term is non-zero, so the non-units form the
    ideal (x), the unique maximal ideal: J = (x). Z[x] is a domain, N = 0.
    If f ∈ J then 1 − xf is a unit of Z[x], i.e. ±1, so xf = 0 or 2; xf = 2
    is impossible (degrees), hence f = 0 and J = 0. Common mistakes:
    mixing up N (nilpotents, all primes) and J (all MAXIMAL ideals);
    forgetting that the proof of ⊇ needs Zorn's lemma. Now you try: N and
    J of Z₂₀₀. (200 = 2³·5²: both are (10), with 20 elements)

D3. Prove that for a ring R the following are equivalent: (i) ACC on
    ideals; (ii) every ideal is finitely generated; (iii) every non-empty
    set of ideals has a maximal element. Show that k[x₁, x₂, ...] fails
    them. In Z, find the longest strictly increasing chain of ideals that
    starts at (360).
    Answer: (i) ⇒ (iii) ⇒ (ii) ⇒ (i) below; (x₁) ⊊ (x₁, x₂) ⊊ ... never
    stops; in Z: 6 steps, e.g. (360) ⊊ (180) ⊊ (90) ⊊ (45) ⊊ (15) ⊊ (5) ⊊ Z.
    Teacher explains: (i) ⇒ (iii): if Σ ≠ ∅ had no maximal element, pick
    I₁ ∈ Σ, then I₂ ∈ Σ with I₂ ⊋ I₁ (I₁ is not maximal), and so on: a
    strictly increasing infinite chain, contradicting ACC. (iii) ⇒ (ii):
    given an ideal I, let Σ be the finitely generated ideals contained in
    I (non-empty: (0) ∈ Σ). Take a maximal J ∈ Σ. If J ≠ I pick a ∈ I ∖ J;
    then J + (a) ∈ Σ is bigger, contradiction; so I = J is finitely
    generated. (ii) ⇒ (i): the union I of a chain I₁ ⊆ I₂ ⊆ ... is an ideal
    (because it is a CHAIN), so I = (a₁, ..., aₘ); each aⱼ lies in some
    I_{nⱼ}, so all lie in I_N with N = max nⱼ; then I ⊆ I_N ⊆ Iₙ ⊆ I for
    n ≥ N: the chain stops. □ In k[x₁, x₂, ...], x_{n+1} ∉ (x₁, ..., xₙ)
    (set x₁ = ... = xₙ = 0, x_{n+1} = 1), so the chain never stops and the
    ideal (x₁, x₂, ...) is not finitely generated. In Z, (a) ⊊ (b) ⇔ b is
    a proper divisor of a, so a longest chain removes ONE prime factor at a
    time: 360 = 2³·3²·5 has 3 + 2 + 1 = 6 prime factors (with
    multiplicity), giving 6 steps. Every chain from (360) is finite (ACC)
    but descending chains need not stop: (2) ⊋ (4) ⊋ (8) ⊋ ... . Common
    mistakes: assuming the union of any family of ideals is an ideal;
    thinking Noetherian means "finitely many ideals" (Z has infinitely
    many). Now you try: the longest strictly increasing chain from (720)
    in Z. (720 = 2⁴·3²·5: 7 steps)

D4. State and prove the Hilbert basis theorem. Deduce that
    Q[x, y, z]/(x² − yz) is Noetherian. Show that (x² + y², x² − y²) =
    (x², y²) in Q[x, y] but not in Z[x, y].
    Answer: R Noetherian ⇒ R[x] Noetherian; the quotient is Noetherian as a
    quotient of Q[x, y, z]; over Z, x² ∉ (x² + y², x² − y²).
    Teacher explains: Suppose I ⊆ R[x] is NOT finitely generated. Choose
    f₁ ∈ I of least degree, and inductively f_{k+1} ∈ I ∖ (f₁, ..., f_k) of
    least degree. Let dₖ = deg fₖ (so d₁ ≤ d₂ ≤ ...) and aₖ = leading
    coefficient of fₖ. The chain (a₁) ⊆ (a₁, a₂) ⊆ ... in R stabilises, so
    a_{N+1} = r₁a₁ + ... + r_N a_N for some rᵢ ∈ R. Put
       g = f_{N+1} − ∑_{i≤N} rᵢ x^(d_{N+1} − dᵢ) fᵢ.
    The leading terms cancel, so deg g < d_{N+1}; g ∈ I; and
    g ∉ (f₁, ..., f_N) (otherwise f_{N+1} would be in it). This contradicts
    the minimal choice of f_{N+1}. □ By induction k[x₁, ..., xₙ] is
    Noetherian, and a quotient R/I is Noetherian because its ideals are
    ideals J ⊇ I of R. Over Q: x² = ((x² + y²) + (x² − y²))/2 and
    y² = ((x² + y²) − (x² − y²))/2, so the ideals are equal. Over Z: if
    x² = a(x² + y²) + b(x² − y²), compare the degree-2 parts using the
    constant terms a₀, b₀: a₀ + b₀ = 1 and a₀ − b₀ = 0, so a₀ = 1/2 ∉ Z.
    The Z-ideal only contains 2x² and 2y². Common mistakes: believing the
    proof shows SUBRINGS of R[x] are Noetherian (it uses multiplication by
    xᵏ inside the ring); forgetting the base case (a field is Noetherian).
    Now you try: is Z[x, y]/(xy − 1) Noetherian? (Yes: a quotient of
    Z[x, y], which is Noetherian by Hilbert twice.)

D5. Show that the following rings are not Noetherian by writing down a
    strictly increasing infinite chain of ideals: (a) C[0, 1] (continuous
    real functions); (b) A = Z + xQ[x] (rational polynomials with integer
    constant term); (c) B = k[x, xy, xy², xy³, ...] ⊂ k[x, y].
    Answer: (a) Iₙ = {f : f = 0 on [0, 1/n]}; (b) (x) ⊊ (x/2) ⊊ (x/4) ⊊ ...;
    (c) (x) ⊊ (x, xy) ⊊ (x, xy, xy²) ⊊ ... .
    Teacher explains: (a) Iₙ ⊆ Iₙ₊₁ because [0, 1/(n + 1)] ⊆ [0, 1/n]. It is
    strict: the continuous function f(x) = max(0, x − 1/(n + 1)) vanishes
    on [0, 1/(n + 1)] but f(1/n) > 0, so f ∈ Iₙ₊₁ ∖ Iₙ. (b) x/2ⁿ =
    2·(x/2ⁿ⁺¹), so (x/2ⁿ) ⊆ (x/2ⁿ⁺¹). If x/2ⁿ⁺¹ = f·(x/2ⁿ) with f ∈ A,
    compare the coefficients of x: f(0)/2ⁿ = 1/2ⁿ⁺¹, so f(0) = 1/2 ∉ Z.
    Strict. (c) Every monomial of B other than 1 is divisible by x. The
    ideal (x, xy, ..., xyⁿ⁻¹) of B consists of sums xyⁱ·b with b ∈ B, and
    the only monomials of x-degree exactly 1 appearing there are
    x, xy, ..., xyⁿ⁻¹ (b must contribute its constant term). So xyⁿ is not
    in it. B is a subring of the Noetherian ring k[x, y]: Noetherianity
    does NOT pass to subrings (but it does pass to quotients and
    localisations). Common mistakes: in (a), giving functions that are
    not continuous; in (b), thinking x/2 ∈ (x) because 1/2 ∈ Q (1/2 is not
    in A). Now you try: show the ring of all algebraic integers is not
    Noetherian. ((2) ⊊ (2^(1/2)) ⊊ (2^(1/4)) ⊊ ...; 2^(-1/2ⁿ⁺¹) is not an
    algebraic integer)

D6. Construct S⁻¹R: define the relation on R × S, prove it is an
    equivalence relation, and explain why the extra factor t is needed.
    Then identify S⁻¹Z₆ for S = {1, 2, 4} and for S = {1, 3}.
    Answer: (r, s) ~ (r′, s′) ⇔ t(rs′ − r′s) = 0 for some t ∈ S;
    {1, 2, 4}⁻¹Z₆ ≅ Z₃ and {1, 3}⁻¹Z₆ ≅ Z₂.
    Teacher explains: Reflexive and symmetric are clear. Transitive: if
    u(as′ − a′s) = 0 and v(a′s″ − a″s′) = 0 multiply the first by vs″ and
    the second by us and add:
       uv(as′s″ − a′ss″ + a′ss″ − a″ss′) = uvs′(as″ − a″s) = 0,
    and uvs′ ∈ S. Without t (i.e. "rs′ = r′s") transitivity FAILS when S
    contains zero divisors, which is exactly when localisation kills
    elements. Addition r/s + r′/s′ = (rs′ + r′s)/(ss′) and multiplication
    (r/s)(r′/s′) = rr′/(ss′) are well defined; r ↦ r/1 is a ring map.
    Z₆, S = {1, 2, 4}: 3/1 = 0/1 because 2·(3·1 − 0·1) = 6 = 0. So 0, 3
    become 0, and 2 becomes a unit (2·(1/2) = 1). By CRT Z₆ ≅ Z₂ × Z₃ with
    2 ↦ (0, 2); inverting an element with a 0 in the first slot kills the
    Z₂ factor, leaving Z₃. S = {1, 3}: 3 ↦ (1, 0) kills the Z₃ factor;
    indeed 3·2 = 0 so 2/1 = 0 and S⁻¹Z₆ ≅ Z₂. Common mistakes: using the
    domain rule rs′ = r′s in rings with zero divisors; forgetting that
    S⁻¹R = 0 when 0 ∈ S (e.g. inverting a nilpotent). Now you try: Z₁₂
    with S = {1, 3, 9} (powers of 3). (3 ↦ (3, 0) in Z₄ × Z₃, so S⁻¹Z₁₂ ≅
    Z₄)

D7. Prove that the primes of S⁻¹R correspond bijectively to the primes P of
    R with P ∩ S = ∅. Deduce that R_P is local with maximal ideal PR_P.
    Describe Spec Z₍ₚ₎, Spec Z[1/6] and Spec S⁻¹Z for S = Z ∖ ((2) ∪ (5)).
    Answer: Q ↦ Q ∩ R and P ↦ S⁻¹P are inverse bijections. Spec Z₍ₚ₎ =
    {0, pZ₍ₚ₎}; Spec Z[1/6] = {0} ∪ {(q) : q prime, q ≠ 2, 3};
    Spec S⁻¹Z = {0, (2), (5)} (two maximal ideals).
    Teacher explains: If Q is a prime of S⁻¹R, its preimage Q ∩ R is prime
    (preimages of primes are prime) and misses S (an element of S becomes a
    unit, which cannot lie in Q). Conversely, if P is prime and P ∩ S = ∅,
    S⁻¹P is proper (1/1 ∈ S⁻¹P would force s ∈ P) and prime: if
    (a/s)(b/t) ∈ S⁻¹P then u·ab ∈ P for some u ∈ S, so ab ∈ P, so a or b
    ∈ P. Also S⁻¹P ∩ R = P: if r/1 = p/s with p ∈ P then u(rs − p) = 0
    for some u ∈ S, so usr = up ∈ P with us ∉ P, hence r ∈ P. Similarly
    S⁻¹(Q ∩ R) = Q, and both maps preserve inclusion. For S = R ∖ P,
    the primes of R_P ↔ primes contained in P; the largest is P itself,
    so PR_P is the UNIQUE maximal ideal. Now Z:
    Z₍ₚ₎ = (Z ∖ (p))⁻¹Z: primes of Z inside (p) are (0) and (p). Z[1/6]:
    primes missing {1, 6, 36, ...} are those not containing 6: (0) and
    (q), q ≠ 2, 3. S = Z ∖ ((2) ∪ (5)): primes contained in (2) ∪ (5) are
    (0), (2), (5) (a prime (q) ⊆ (2) ∪ (5) forces q ∈ {2, 5}). So S⁻¹Z is a
    SEMI-LOCAL ring with two maximal ideals. Common mistakes: thinking the
    primes of R_P are the primes containing P (it is those CONTAINED in P);
    forgetting (0). Now you try: Spec of Z₍₃₎ and of Q (both localisations
    of Z). ({0, (3)}; {0})

D8. Prove: R is local ⇔ its non-units form an ideal. Show Zₙ is local iff
    n is a prime power, and that k[[x]] is local. In Q[[x]] find the
    inverses of 1 − x − x² (first 7 coefficients) and of 2 + 3x (first 4).
    Answer: 1/(1 − x − x²) = 1 + x + 2x² + 3x³ + 5x⁴ + 8x⁵ + 13x⁶ + ...;
    1/(2 + 3x) = 1/2 − (3/4)x + (9/8)x² − (27/16)x³ + ... .
    Teacher explains: (⇒) If m is the only maximal ideal and x is not a
    unit, then (x) ≠ R lies in some maximal ideal, i.e. in m. So the
    non-units are exactly m. (⇐) If the non-units form an ideal I, every
    proper ideal consists of non-units, so lies in I; I is then the unique
    maximal ideal. Zₙ: maximal ideals are (p) for primes p | n, so there is
    exactly one iff n = pᵃ. k[[x]]: if a₀ ≠ 0, solve (a₀ + a₁x + ...)(b₀ +
    b₁x + ...) = 1 coefficient by coefficient: b₀ = 1/a₀, then
    bₙ = −(a₁bₙ₋₁ + ... + aₙb₀)/a₀. So the non-units are the series with
    a₀ = 0, the ideal (x). For 1 − x − x²: bₙ = bₙ₋₁ + bₙ₋₂ with b₀ = b₁ = 1:
    the Fibonacci numbers 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13. For 2 + 3x: b₀ = 1/2 and
    bₙ = −(3/2)bₙ₋₁, giving (1/2)(−3/2)ⁿ. Common mistakes: "a local ring has
    only one prime" (Z₍ₚ₎ has two: 0 and (p)); thinking Z[[x]] is local
    (2 + x is a non-unit outside (x), and (2, x), (3, x) are both
    maximal). Now you try: the inverse of 1 + x in Q[x]/(x⁴), and of 7 in
    Z₂₇. (1 − x + x² − x³; 4, since 28 ≡ 1)

D9. State and prove Nakayama's lemma. Deduce that over a local ring
    (R, m, k) the minimal number of generators of a finitely generated M
    is dim_k M/mM. Compute it for m = (x, y)R in R = k[x, y]₍ₓ,ᵧ₎, and
    explain why Nakayama does not apply to M = Q over Z₍ₚ₎ or to M = Z/3
    over Z with I = (2).
    Answer: IM = M, I ⊆ J(R), M f.g. ⇒ M = 0; μ(m) = 2; Q is not finitely
    generated; (2) ⊄ J(Z) = 0.
    Teacher explains: Let M = (m₁, ..., mₙ) with n minimal and suppose
    M ≠ 0, so n ≥ 1. Since M = IM, mₙ = a₁m₁ + ... + aₙmₙ with aᵢ ∈ I, so
       (1 − aₙ)mₙ = a₁m₁ + ... + aₙ₋₁mₙ₋₁.
    As aₙ ∈ J(R), 1 − aₙ is a unit, so mₙ ∈ (m₁, ..., mₙ₋₁): fewer
    generators, contradiction. □ COROLLARY: if N ⊆ M and M = N + mM, apply
    Nakayama to M/N: m(M/N) = M/N, so M = N. Hence x₁, ..., xₙ generate M
    ⇔ their images span the k-vector space M/mM, and μ(M) = dim_k M/mM.
    For m = (x, y) in the local ring k[x, y]₍ₓ,ᵧ₎: m/m² has basis the
    images of x and y (every element of m is ax + by + (terms in m²)), so
    μ(m) = 2: m is not principal. For Q over Z₍ₚ₎: pQ = Q and Q ≠ 0, but Q
    is not finitely generated (denominators are unbounded). For Z/3 over
    Z: 2·(Z/3) = Z/3, but (2) is not in J(Z) = 0; indeed 1 + (−4) = −3
    with −4 ∈ (2) kills Z/3, as the determinant-trick form of the proof
    predicts. Common mistakes: applying Nakayama to modules that are not
    finitely generated; forgetting the hypothesis I ⊆ J(R). Now you try:
    μ of the Z₍₃₎-module Z/9 ⊕ Z/3 ⊕ Z₍₃₎. (M/3M ≅ F₃³: 3)

D10. Define Krull dimension. Prove that a PID that is not a field has
     dimension 1. Show dim k[x, y] ≥ 2, dim Z[x] ≥ 2, and find
     dim k[x, y]/(xy), dim Z₃₆ and dim k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz).
     Answer: PID: 1; k[x, y]: 2 (chain (0) ⊊ (x) ⊊ (x, y)); Z[x]: 2
     (chain (0) ⊊ (2) ⊊ (2, x)); k[x, y]/(xy): 1; Z₃₆: 0;
     k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz): 2.
     Teacher explains: dim R is the largest n with a chain P₀ ⊊ P₁ ⊊ ... ⊊ Pₙ
     of primes (count the INCLUSIONS, not the primes). PID, not a field: a
     non-zero prime (p) has p irreducible; if (p) ⊆ (q) ≠ R then q | p, so
     q is an associate of p and (q) = (p). So every non-zero prime is
     maximal; the chains are (0) ⊊ (p), of length 1. k[x, y]: (x) is prime
     (quotient k[y]) and (x, y) is maximal (quotient k), so there is a
     chain of length 2 (the theorem dim k[x₁, ..., xₙ] = n says no longer
     chain exists). Z[x]: (2) is prime since Z[x]/(2) = F₂[x] is a domain.
     k[x, y]/(xy): primes ⊇ (xy) contain x or y; the minimal ones are (x)
     and (y), with quotients k[y] and k[x], each of dimension 1; so the
     dimension is 1 (two lines crossing). Z₃₆ is finite, so every prime is
     maximal: dimension 0. k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz): x(y, z) ⊆ P means x ∈ P or
     (y, z) ⊆ P; the minimal primes are (x) (the plane x = 0, quotient
     k[y, z], dim 2) and (y, z) (the x-axis, dim 1). The dimension is the
     maximum, 2. Common mistakes: counting the primes in the chain instead
     of the steps; thinking dimension is the same everywhere (the plane
     and the line in the last example have different dimensions). Now you
     try: dim of Z₍ₚ₎[x] and of Q[x, y]/(y² − x³). (2; 1)

D11. Define the Zariski topology on Spec R and prove V(I) ∪ V(J) = V(IJ)
     and ⋂V(Iₐ) = V(∑Iₐ). Describe the points of Spec Z, Spec R[x] and
     Spec C[x, y]; find V(12) and D(6) in Spec Z, and the closure of the
     points (0) and (5).
     Answer: V(12) = {(2), (3)}; D(6) = all primes except (2), (3);
     closure of (0) = Spec Z; closure of (5) = {(5)}.
     Teacher explains: V(I) ∪ V(J) ⊆ V(IJ) since IJ ⊆ I and IJ ⊆ J. If P ⊇ IJ
     but P ⊉ I, pick a ∈ I ∖ P; for every b ∈ J, ab ∈ P, so b ∈ P: P ⊇ J.
     And P contains every Iₐ iff it contains their sum. With V(0) = Spec R
     and V(1) = ∅ these are the axioms for closed sets. The closure of a
     point P is V(P) = {Q ⊇ P}, so P is a CLOSED point ⇔ P is maximal.
     Spec Z: (0) and (p); V(12) = primes containing 12 = {(2), (3)}, and
     its complement is D(12) = D(6) = primes not containing 6, i.e. all
     except (2) and (3). The closure of (0) is V(0) = Spec Z
     (a GENERIC point); (5) is maximal, hence closed. Closed sets other
     than Spec Z are the FINITE sets of non-zero primes. Spec R[x]: (0),
     (x − a) for a ∈ R, and (x² + bx + c) with b² < 4c (these correspond to
     pairs of complex conjugate roots). Spec C[x, y]: (0); (f) for f
     irreducible (curves); and (x − a, y − b) (points of C², by the
     Nullstellensatz). Common mistakes: thinking V(I) ∪ V(J) = V(I + J) (it
     is V(I ∩ J) = V(IJ); V(I + J) is the intersection); calling (0) a
     closed point of Spec Z. Now you try: describe V(x² − x) in
     Spec Q[x]. ({(x), (x − 1)}: two closed points)

D12. Prove the local-global principle: a module M is 0 iff M_m = 0 for
     every maximal ideal m. Localise the abelian group
     G = Z ⊕ Z/12 ⊕ Z/45 at (2), (3), (5), (7), and show that "being an
     integral domain" is not a local property.
     Answer: G₍₂₎ ≅ Z₍₂₎ ⊕ Z/4, G₍₃₎ ≅ Z₍₃₎ ⊕ Z/3 ⊕ Z/9,
     G₍₅₎ ≅ Z₍₅₎ ⊕ Z/5, G₍₇₎ ≅ Z₍₇₎; Z₆ is not a domain, but its
     localisations at (2), (3) are the fields F₂, F₃.
     Teacher explains: (⇐) Let x ∈ M, x ≠ 0. Its annihilator Ann(x) = {r :
     rx = 0} is a proper ideal (1 ∉ it), so it lies in a maximal ideal m.
     If x/1 = 0 in M_m then sx = 0 for some s ∉ m, i.e. s ∈ Ann(x) ⊆ m,
     contradiction. So M_m ≠ 0. □ Localisation is EXACT and commutes with
     direct sums, and (Z/n)₍ₚ₎ ≅ Z/p^(vₚ(n)): the factors prime to p become
     0 because an element of S = Z ∖ (p) kills them and is inverted, while
     on Z/pᵃ every s prime to p already acts invertibly. Here
     12 = 2²·3 and 45 = 3²·5, so
        at (2): Z₍₂₎ ⊕ Z/4 ⊕ 0,         at (3): Z₍₃₎ ⊕ Z/3 ⊕ Z/9,
        at (5): Z₍₅₎ ⊕ 0 ⊕ Z/5,         at (7): Z₍₇₎.
     So localisation picks out the p-primary part of the torsion and keeps
     the free part. Domain: Z₆ ≅ Z₂ × Z₃ has maximal ideals (2) and (3).
     Localising at (2) inverts 3, which is (1, 0) in Z₂ × Z₃ and so kills
     the Z₃ factor: (Z₆)₍₂₎ ≅ F₂; similarly (Z₆)₍₃₎ ≅ F₃. Both are fields,
     yet Z₆ is not a domain. Common mistakes: "M_P = 0 for one P ⇒ M = 0";
     thinking all properties are local (being a domain, being principal
     are not; being reduced, being zero, injectivity are). Now you try:
     (Z/360)₍₃₎ and (Z/360)₍₇₎. (Z/9; 0)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Is the ideal maximal (M), prime but not maximal (P), or not prime (N)?
    a) (0) in Z                        b) (7) in Z
    c) (12) in Z                       d) (2) in Z₁₂
    e) (x) in Q[x]                     f) (x² + 1) in R[x]
    g) (x² + 1) in C[x]                h) (x² − 2) in Q[x]
    i) (x) in Z[x]                     j) (2, x) in Z[x]
    k) (4, x) in Z[x]                  l) (x² + 1) in Z[x]
    m) (3, x² + 1) in Z[x]             n) (5, x² + 1) in Z[x]
    o) (2, x² + 1) in Z[x]             p) (x) in k[x, y]
    q) (x, y) in k[x, y]               r) (xy) in k[x, y]
    s) (y − x²) in k[x, y]             t) (2) in Z[i]
    u) (3) in Z[i]                     v) (1 + i) in Z[i]
    w) (x, y) in k[x, y, z]            x) (x², y) in k[x, y]
    Answers:
      a) P      b) M      c) N      d) M (Z₁₂/(2) ≅ F₂)     e) M
      f) M      g) N      h) M      i) P      j) M      k) N
      l) P (quotient Z[i])          m) M (F₉)
      n) N (x² + 1 ≡ (x − 2)(x + 2) mod 5)    o) N (≡ (x + 1)² mod 2)
      p) P      q) M      r) N      s) P (quotient ≅ k[x])
      t) N (2 = −i(1 + i)²)         u) M (Z[i]/(3) ≅ F₉)
      v) M (Z[i]/(1 + i) ≅ F₂)      w) P (quotient ≅ k[z])     x) N

M2. Find the nilradical N(R) and the Jacobson radical J(R).
    a) Z                               b) Z₁₂
    c) Z₈                              d) Z₃₀
    e) Z₇₂                             f) Q[x]/(x³)
    g) Q[x]/(x² − 1)                   h) Q[x]/(x²(x − 1))
    i) k[x]                            j) k[[x]]

    m) Z[x]                            n) Z₄[x]

    q) Z/pⁿ                            r) Q × Q[[t]]
    s) number of nilpotents in Z₃₆₀    t) number of nilpotents in Z₁₀₀₀
    u) nilpotency index of 6 in Z₇₂    v) is 1 + 2x a unit in Z₄[x]?
    Answers:
      a) 0, 0          b) (6), (6)      c) (2), (2)      d) 0, 0
      e) (6), (6)      f) (x), (x)      g) 0, 0 (≅ Q × Q)
      h) (x(x − 1)), (x(x − 1))         i) 0, 0          j) 0, (x)
      k) 0, pZ₍ₚ₎       l) 0, 6S⁻¹Z     m) 0, 0
      n) 2Z₄[x], 2Z₄[x] (for R[x], J = N)                o) (2) × (3), same
      p) (x), (x)      q) (p), (p)      r) 0 × 0, 0 × (t)
      s) 12 (multiples of 30)           t) 100 (multiples of 10)
      u) 3 (6² = 36, 6³ = 216 ≡ 0)      v) yes: (1 + 2x)² = 1 + 4x + 4x² = 1

M3. Spec of small rings: give the number of prime ideals (all are maximal
    in a)-o), which are Artinian) or describe Spec.
    a) Z₁₂                             b) Z₃₀
    c) Z_{pⁿ}                          d) Z₃₆₀
    e) Q[x]/(x² − 1)                   f) R[x]/(x² + 1)

      a) 2 ((2), (3))    b) 3             c) 1             d) 3

      k) 2 ((x + 1)(x² + x + 1))          l) 3 (x(x + 1)(x² + x + 1))
      m) 2 ((x² − 2)(x² + 2))             n) 4 (roots 1, 2, 3, 4)
      o) 3 ((x − 1)(x + 1)(x² + 1))       p) 2 (0 and (p); 1 maximal)
      q) 1               r) 2 ((2, x), (3, x))

M4. Noetherian (Y) or not (N)?
    a) Q                               b) Z
    c) Z[x]                            d) Q[x₁, ..., x₁₀]
