M10. Nakayama and minimal generators. For the local ring give the
     embedding dimension μ(m) = dim m/m², the Krull dimension, and say
     whether it is regular (R) or not (NR).
     a) Z₍ₚ₎                           b) k[x, y]₍ₓ,ᵧ₎
     c) (k[x, y]/(y² − x³)) at the origin
     d) (k[x, y]/(y − x²)) at the origin
     e) (k[x, y]/(y² − x² − x³)) at the origin
     f) (k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²)) at the origin
     g) k[x]/(x³)                      h) k[x, y]/(x², xy, y²)
     i) Z/p²                           j) Q[[x]]
     k) (k[x, y]/(y² − x³ − x)) at the origin
     l) (k[x, y, z]/(z − xy)) at the origin
     Minimal number of generators of the module:
     m) Z/4 ⊕ Z/8 ⊕ Z₍₂₎ over Z₍₂₎    n) Z/9 ⊕ Z/3 over Z₍₃₎
     o) Z₍₅₎² ⊕ Z/5 over Z₍₅₎          p) (Z/2 ⊕ Z/3)₍₂₎ over Z₍₂₎
     q) Q over Z₍ₚ₎: does pQ = Q contradict Nakayama?
     r) Z/3 over Z, I = (2): does IM = M contradict Nakayama?
     Answers:
       a) 1, 1, R       b) 2, 2, R       c) 2, 1, NR (cusp)
       d) 1, 1, R       e) 2, 1, NR (node)       f) 3, 2, NR (cone)
       g) 1, 0, NR      h) 2, 0, NR      i) 1, 0, NR      j) 1, 1, R
       k) 1, 1, R (x = y² − x³ lies in m², so y generates m)
       l) 2, 2, R (≅ k[x, y]₍ₓ,ᵧ₎)
       m) 3       n) 2       o) 3
       p) 1 (Z/3 localises to 0, leaving Z/2)
       q) no: Q is not finitely generated
       r) no: (2) ⊄ J(Z) = 0

M11. Krull dimension.
     a) Q                              b) Z
     c) Z₍ₚ₎                           d) Q[x]
     e) Q[x, y]                        f) C[x₁, ..., x₅]
     g) Z[x]                           h) Z₃₆
     i) Q[x]/(x³)                      j) k[x, y]/(xy)
     k) k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz)            l) k[[x, y]]
     m) Z[i]                           n) Z[√−5]
     o) Z[x]/(x² + 1)                  p) k[x, y]/(y² − x³)
     q) k[x, y, z]/(z)                 r) Z₍ₚ₎[x]
     s) k × k[x]                       t) Q[x₁, x₂, ...]
     u) Z[1/2]                         v) height of (x, y) in k[x, y, z]
     Answers:
       a) 0      b) 1      c) 1      d) 1      e) 2      f) 5
       g) 2      h) 0      i) 0      j) 1      k) 2      l) 2
       m) 1      n) 1      o) 1 (≅ Z[i])       p) 1      q) 2
       r) 2      s) 1 (the maximum over the factors)     t) ∞
       u) 1      v) 2 ((0) ⊊ (x) ⊊ (x, y))

M12. Radicals, V(I) and components.
     a) √(12) in Z                     b) √(72) in Z
     c) √0 in Z₁₀₀                     d) √(x³(x − 1)²) in Q[x]
     e) √(x², y²) in k[x, y]           f) √(x²y) in k[x, y]
     g) √(x², xy) in k[x, y]           h) √((x² + 1)³) in R[x]
     i) V(12) in Spec Z                j) V(0) and V(1) in Spec Z
     k) closure of {(0)} in Spec Z     l) closure of {(5)} in Spec Z
     m) D(6) in Spec Z                 n) minimal primes of k[x, y]/(xy)
     o) minimal primes of k[x, y]/(x²y)
     p) minimal primes of k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz)
     q) maximal ideals of C[x, y] containing x² + y² − 1 and x − 1
     r) closure of the point (y − x²) in Spec C[x, y]
     s) number of irreducible components of Spec Z₃₀ and of Spec Z₈
     t) is Spec Z₆ connected?
     Answers:
       a) (6)     b) (6)     c) (10)    d) (x(x − 1))
       e) (x, y)  f) (xy)    g) (x)     h) (x² + 1)
       i) {(2), (3)}         j) Spec Z, ∅
       k) Spec Z (generic point)        l) {(5)} (closed point)
       m) all primes except (2) and (3)
       n) (x), (y)           o) (x), (y)            p) (x), (y, z)
       q) only (x − 1, y) (the line is tangent: the ideal (x − 1, y²) has
          radical (x − 1, y))
       r) {(y − x²)} ∪ {(x − a, y − a²) : a ∈ C}
       s) 3 and 1            t) no (Spec Z₆ = Spec F₂ ⊔ Spec F₃, two
          points, each open)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: turn every question about an ideal into a question about a
  QUOTIENT ring ("is R/P a domain? a field?") and every question about a
  localisation into "which elements become units, and which become 0?".
  Keep a small zoo on one page — Z, Zₙ, Z₍ₚ₎, Z[1/n], k[x], k[x, y],
  k[[x]], Z[x] — and for each list its primes, maximal ideals, radicals
  and dimension; draw Spec Z as a line of points with a fuzzy generic
  point, and Spec k[x, y] as the plane with its curves and points. For
  Zₙ, use the Chinese remainder theorem to split into prime-power pieces
  first. Harder: prove that a Noetherian ring has only finitely many
  minimal primes and that every ideal has a primary decomposition
  (Lasker-Noether); prove Krull's intersection theorem ⋂mⁿ = 0 in a
  Noetherian local ring; prove that Artinian ⇔ Noetherian of dimension 0;
  prove the weak Nullstellensatz (maximal ideals of C[x₁, ..., xₙ] are
  (x₁ − a₁, ..., xₙ − aₙ)); show that Z[x] has dimension exactly 2; and
  prove that a Noetherian local domain of dimension 1 with principal
  maximal ideal is a discrete valuation ring.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.9 — REPRESENTATION THEORY OF FINITE GROUPS; CHARACTERS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A REPRESENTATION of a finite group G on a finite-dimensional complex
    vector space V is a homomorphism ρ : G → GL(V); dim V is its DEGREE.
    Equivalently a CG-module. Two representations are EQUIVALENT if an
    invertible T with Tρ(g) = ρ′(g)T exists. Examples: TRIVIAL, SIGN of
    Sₙ, PERMUTATION representations C^X of a G-set X, the REGULAR
    representation C[G], the 2-dimensional rotation-reflection
    representation of Dₙ, one-dimensional characters of Cₙ: g^k ↦ ω^(jk).
  - SUBREPRESENTATIONS (G-invariant subspaces), IRREDUCIBLE representations
    (no invariant subspaces except 0 and V), direct sums. In C^X the
    constant vectors form a trivial subrepresentation.
  - MASCHKE'S THEOREM: if char k ∤ |G| every representation is a direct sum
    of irreducibles (average a projection: π₀ = (1/|G|)∑ρ(g)πρ(g)⁻¹, or
    average an inner product to make ρ unitary). Fails for Cₚ over Fₚ and
    for the infinite group Z: g ↦ [[1, 1], [0, 1]].
  - SCHUR'S LEMMA: a G-map between irreducibles is 0 or an isomorphism; over
    C every G-endomorphism of an irreducible is a SCALAR. Consequences:
    irreducible representations of ABELIAN groups are 1-dimensional; the
    centre Z(G) acts by scalars in every irreducible representation.
  - CHARACTERS χ(g) = tr ρ(g): χ(1) = dim V; χ is a CLASS FUNCTION;
    χ(g⁻¹) = conj χ(g); χ(g) is a sum of χ(1) roots of unity of order
    dividing o(g); |χ(g)| ≤ χ(1) with equality iff ρ(g) is scalar;
    ker ρ = {g : χ(g) = χ(1)}. χ_{V⊕W} = χ_V + χ_W, χ_{V⊗W} = χ_Vχ_W,
    χ_{V*} = conj χ_V; permutation character π(g) = #fixed points; regular
    character = |G| at 1 and 0 elsewhere.
  - INNER PRODUCT ⟨χ, ψ⟩ = (1/|G|)∑_g χ(g) conj ψ(g) = ∑ over classes
    (|class|/|G|) χ conj ψ. ROW ORTHOGONALITY: the irreducible characters
    are orthonormal. Hence representations are determined by characters,
    the multiplicity of χᵢ in V is ⟨χ_V, χᵢ⟩, ⟨χ, χ⟩ = ∑mᵢ², and χ is
    irreducible ⇔ ⟨χ, χ⟩ = 1. dim Hom_G(V, W) = ⟨χ_V, χ_W⟩.
  - COUNTING: the number of irreducible characters = the number of
    conjugacy classes; ∑ dᵢ² = |G| (from C[G] = ⊕ dᵢVᵢ); each dᵢ divides
    |G|; the number of LINEAR (degree-1) characters is |G : G′| and they
    are lifted from G/G′. COLUMN ORTHOGONALITY: ∑ᵢ χᵢ(g) conj χᵢ(h) =
    |C_G(g)| if g ~ h and 0 otherwise.
  - BUILDING CHARACTER TABLES: lift from quotients G/N; permutation
    character minus trivial (irreducible iff the action is 2-transitive);
    multiply by linear characters; tensor products; SYMMETRIC and
    EXTERIOR squares χ_S(g) = (χ(g)² + χ(g²))/2, χ_Λ(g) = (χ(g)² −
    χ(g²))/2; induction; then fill the rest by orthogonality.
  - STANDARD TABLES: Cₙ, C₂ × C₂, S₃ (1, 1, 2), D₄ and Q₈ (1, 1, 1, 1, 2;
    the SAME table for non-isomorphic groups), A₄ (1, 1, 1, 3, with values
    ω, ω²), S₄ (1, 1, 2, 3, 3), D₅ (1, 1, 2, 2; values 2cos(2πk/5)), A₅
    (1, 3, 3, 4, 5; values (1 ± √5)/2).
  - INDUCED CHARACTERS: for H ≤ G and a character ψ of H, Ind ψ(g) =
    (1/|H|)∑_{x∈G, x⁻¹gx∈H} ψ(x⁻¹gx), of degree |G : H|ψ(1); Ind of the
    trivial character is the permutation character on G/H. FROBENIUS
    RECIPROCITY: ⟨Ind ψ, χ⟩_G = ⟨ψ, Res χ⟩_H.
  - APPLICATIONS: normal subgroups are intersections of kernels of
    irreducible characters (G is simple iff every non-trivial irreducible
    is faithful); Z(G) = ⋂{g : |χ(g)| = χ(1)}; G′ = ⋂ kernels of linear
    characters; g ~ g⁻¹ ⇔ all χ(g) are real; FROBENIUS-SCHUR indicator
    ν(χ) = (1/|G|)∑χ(g²) ∈ {1, 0, −1} (real, complex, quaternionic);
    BURNSIDE: the number of orbits = ⟨π, 1⟩; Burnside's pᵃqᵇ theorem.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. S₃ permutes the basis e₁, e₂, e₃ of C³ (σ·eᵢ = e_σ(i)). Show that
    C³ = U ⊕ W with U = span(e₁ + e₂ + e₃) and W = {x₁ + x₂ + x₃ = 0}, both
    invariant; write the matrices of (12) and (123) on W in the basis
    e₁ − e₂, e₂ − e₃; and prove that W is irreducible.
    Answer: ρ(12) = [[−1, 1], [0, 1]], ρ(123) = [[0, −1], [1, −1]]; traces 0
    and −1; W is irreducible.
    Teacher explains: Permuting coordinates does not change x₁ + x₂ + x₃, so
    W is invariant, and e₁ + e₂ + e₃ is fixed by every σ, so U is a
    trivial subrepresentation. U ∩ W = 0 and dimensions add to 3, so
    C³ = U ⊕ W. On W, with f₁ = e₁ − e₂, f₂ = e₂ − e₃:
       (12): f₁ ↦ e₂ − e₁ = −f₁,    f₂ ↦ e₁ − e₃ = f₁ + f₂
       (123): f₁ ↦ e₂ − e₃ = f₂,    f₂ ↦ e₃ − e₁ = −f₁ − f₂
    The images are the COLUMNS: ρ(12) = [[−1, 1], [0, 1]] and ρ(123) =
    [[0, −1], [1, −1]]. Irreducible: a proper invariant subspace of W is a
    line fixed by every ρ(g). The lines fixed by (12) are its eigenlines,
    spanned by f₁ (eigenvalue −1) and by e₁ + e₂ − 2e₃ = f₁ + 2f₂
    (eigenvalue +1). But (123) sends f₁ to f₂ ∉ span(f₁), and f₁ + 2f₂ to
    f₂ − 2f₁ − 2f₂ = −2f₁ − f₂ ∉ span(f₁ + 2f₂). So no line is invariant.
    Common mistakes: writing images as ROWS (the matrix is then the
    transpose and the map is not a homomorphism); thinking a 2-dim
    representation must split because the group is small. Now you try:
    the matrix of (13) on W in the same basis. ([[0, −1], [−1, 0]]: f₁ ↦
    e₃ − e₂ = −f₂, f₂ ↦ e₂ − e₁ = −f₁)

D2. Prove Maschke's theorem over C by averaging a projection. Carry out the
    averaging for the permutation representation of S₃ on C³ starting from
    π(x) = x₁(1, 1, 1), and show that the theorem fails for C₂ acting on
    F₂² by g ↦ [[1, 1], [0, 1]].
    Answer: π₀(x) = ((x₁ + x₂ + x₃)/3)(1, 1, 1), whose kernel is W; over F₂
    the line span(e₁) is invariant with no invariant complement.
    Teacher explains: Let W ⊆ V be invariant and π any linear projection of
    V onto W (π² = π, image W). Define
       π₀ = (1/|G|) ∑_{g∈G} ρ(g) π ρ(g)⁻¹.
    (i) Image in W: π maps into W and ρ(g) preserves W. (ii) On W: for w ∈ W,
    ρ(g)⁻¹w ∈ W, so πρ(g)⁻¹w = ρ(g)⁻¹w and each term gives w: π₀w = w.
    (iii) Equivariant: ρ(h)π₀ρ(h)⁻¹ = (1/|G|)∑ρ(hg)πρ(hg)⁻¹ = π₀ (reindex).
    So π₀ is a G-map projection onto W, and ker π₀ is an invariant
    complement. Induction on dim V finishes. Division by |G| is where
    char k ∤ |G| is used. Example: the first coordinate of ρ(σ)⁻¹x is
    x_σ(1), and ρ(σ) fixes (1, 1, 1), so ρ(σ)πρ(σ)⁻¹(x) = x_σ(1)(1, 1, 1).
    As σ runs over S₃, σ(1) takes each value twice, so
       π₀(x) = ((2x₁ + 2x₂ + 2x₃)/6)(1, 1, 1) = ((x₁ + x₂ + x₃)/3)(1, 1, 1),
    with kernel W. Over F₂: [[1, 1], [0, 1]]² = [[1, 0], [0, 1]], so this is a
    representation of C₂. Its only eigenvalue is 1 and the eigenspace is
    span(e₁); any invariant line is an eigenline, so span(e₁) is the only
    invariant line and it has no invariant complement. (Averaging would
    divide by 2 = 0 in F₂.) Common mistakes: forgetting that π₀ must be a
    projection ONTO W (check π₀|_W = id); thinking Maschke holds for
    infinite groups (Z → GL₂(C), n ↦ [[1, n], [0, 1]] is not completely
    reducible). Now you try: for C₃ over F₃ give a representation that is
    not completely reducible. (g ↦ [[1, 1], [0, 1]], which has order 3)

D3. State and prove Schur's lemma. Deduce that every irreducible complex
    representation of a finite abelian group is 1-dimensional, list the
    irreducible representations of C₄ and of C₂ × C₂, and show that r²
    acts as −I in the 2-dimensional representation of D₄.
    Answer: C₄ = ⟨g⟩: χⱼ(g) = iʲ (j = 0, 1, 2, 3); C₂ × C₂ = ⟨a, b⟩: the four
    characters a ↦ ±1, b ↦ ±1; ρ(r²) = rotation by π = −I.
    Teacher explains: Let T : V → W be a G-map between irreducibles. ker T
    and im T are invariant subspaces, so ker T = 0 or V and im T = 0 or W:
    T = 0 or T is an isomorphism. Over C, a G-map T : V → V has an
    eigenvalue λ (algebraically closed); T − λI is a G-map with non-zero
    kernel, hence 0; so T = λI. If G is abelian, each ρ(h) commutes with
    all ρ(g), so ρ(h) is a G-map, hence a scalar; then EVERY subspace is
    invariant, and irreducibility forces dim V = 1. For C₄ a 1-dim
    representation is fixed by χ(g), a 4th root of unity: 1, i, −1, −i;
    four characters (as many as elements, since ∑dᵢ² = 4). For C₂ × C₂,
    χ(a), χ(b) ∈ {±1}: four characters. In D₄ (symmetries of a square), r
    is rotation by π/2 and r² is rotation by π, the matrix −I. r² is
    central, so Schur predicts a scalar, and indeed |χ(r²)| = |−2| = χ(1).
    Common mistakes: using Schur over R (rotation by π/2 on R² is an
    endomorphism of an irreducible real representation of C₄ that is not
    a scalar); forgetting that the eigenvalue argument needs C. Now you
    try: the irreducible representations of C₆. (g ↦ e^(2πik/6),
    k = 0, ..., 5)

D4. Prove that a character is a class function, that χ(1) = dim V,
    χ(g⁻¹) = conj χ(g) and |χ(g)| ≤ χ(1). Compute the permutation
    characters of S₄ acting on the 4 points and on the 6 two-element
    subsets, on the classes e, (12), (12)(34), (123), (1234) (sizes 1, 6,
    3, 8, 6).
    Answer: on points π₄ = (4, 2, 0, 1, 0); on pairs π₆ = (6, 2, 2, 0, 0).
    Teacher explains: tr(ABA⁻¹) = tr B gives χ(hgh⁻¹) = χ(g). χ(1) = tr I =
    dim V. Since gⁿ = 1 (n = o(g)), ρ(g) satisfies xⁿ − 1, which has
    distinct roots, so ρ(g) is DIAGONALISABLE with eigenvalues λᵢ that are
    n-th roots of unity, |λᵢ| = 1. Then χ(g) = ∑λᵢ, χ(g⁻¹) = ∑λᵢ⁻¹ =
    ∑conj λᵢ, and |χ(g)| ≤ ∑|λᵢ| = dim V, with equality iff all λᵢ are
    equal, i.e. ρ(g) is a scalar. A permutation matrix has a 1 on the
    diagonal exactly at fixed points, so π(g) = #fixed points. On the 4
    points: e fixes 4, (12) fixes 3 and 4, (12)(34) none, (123) fixes 4,
    (1234) none. On 2-subsets, a pair is fixed iff it is a union of cycles
    of g:
       e: all 6;   (12): {1,2}, {3,4};   (12)(34): {1,2}, {3,4};
       (123): none (cycle lengths 3, 1);   (1234): none.
    Common mistakes: "a pair is fixed only if both points are fixed" ({1, 2}
    is fixed by (12)); forgetting that characters are constant on classes
    (so only one representative per class is needed). Now you try: the
    permutation character of S₄ on the 3 ways of splitting {1, 2, 3, 4}
    into two pairs. ((3, 1, 3, 0, 1))

D5. State the orthogonality relations and deduce: (a) the multiplicity of
    an irreducible χᵢ in V is ⟨χ_V, χᵢ⟩; (b) ⟨χ, χ⟩ = ∑mᵢ², so χ is
    irreducible iff ⟨χ, χ⟩ = 1. Decompose the characters π₄ and π₆ of D4, and so
    construct a new irreducible character of S₄.
    Answer: π₄ = 1 + χ₃ with χ₃ = (3, 1, −1, 0, −1); π₆ = 1 + χ₃ + χ₂ with
    χ₂ = (2, 0, 2, −1, 0), both irreducible.
    Teacher explains: ROW ORTHOGONALITY: for irreducible χᵢ, χⱼ,
    ⟨χᵢ, χⱼ⟩ = δᵢⱼ (proof: average a linear map between the two
    representations to get a G-map and apply Schur). If V = ⊕ mᵢVᵢ then
    χ_V = ∑mᵢχᵢ, so ⟨χ_V, χⱼ⟩ = mⱼ and ⟨χ_V, χ_V⟩ = ∑mᵢ². A sum of squares
    of non-negative integers equals 1 only if one mᵢ is 1 and the rest 0.
    Now compute, weighting by the class sizes 1, 6, 3, 8, 6, over 24:
       ⟨π₄, π₄⟩ = (16 + 6·4 + 0 + 8·1 + 0)/24 = 48/24 = 2,
    so π₄ is the sum of TWO different irreducibles; one is trivial
    (⟨π₄, 1⟩ = (4 + 12 + 0 + 8 + 0)/24 = 1), so χ₃ = π₄ − 1 = (3, 1, −1, 0,
    −1) is irreducible. Next
       ⟨π₆, π₆⟩ = (36 + 6·4 + 3·4 + 0 + 0)/24 = 72/24 = 3,
       ⟨π₆, 1⟩ = (6 + 12 + 6)/24 = 1,  ⟨π₆, χ₃⟩ = (18 + 12 − 6)/24 = 1,
    so π₆ = 1 + χ₃ + χ₂ with χ₂ = π₆ − 1 − χ₃ = (2, 0, 2, −1, 0) a third
    irreducible character (check ⟨χ₂, χ₂⟩ = (4 + 12 + 8)/24 = 1). Common
    mistakes: forgetting the class sizes in the sum; forgetting the
    complex conjugate for non-real characters. Now you try: decompose the
    permutation character (3, 1, 3, 0, 1) of S₄ on pairings. (1 + χ₂)

D6. Construct the character table of S₃ from scratch, using only: the
    number of irreducibles equals the number of classes, ∑dᵢ² = |G|,
    linear characters come from G/G′, and orthogonality. Check column
    orthogonality.
    Answer:
                  e    (12)   (123)
       size       1     3      2
       1          1     1      1
       ε          1    −1      1
       ρ          2     0     −1
    Teacher explains: Classes by cycle type: {e}, the 3 transpositions, the
    2 three-cycles: 3 irreducibles with d₁² + d₂² + d₃² = 6 and d₁ = 1
    (trivial). Only 1 + 1 + 4 works: degrees 1, 1, 2. G′ = A₃ and
    G/A₃ ≅ C₂ gives the trivial and SIGN characters. The last row
    (2, a, b): orthogonality with 1 and with ε gives
       2 + 3a + 2b = 0   and   2 − 3a + 2b = 0,
    so a = 0 and b = −1. (It is D1's W: traces 2, 0, −1.) Column
    orthogonality: column of (12): 1² + (−1)² + 0² = 2 = |C(12)| = 6/3 ✓;
    column of (123): 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 = 6/2 ✓; columns e and (12):
    1 − 1 + 0 = 0 ✓. The regular character (6, 0, 0) = 1 + ε + 2ρ, each
    irreducible appearing (degree) times. Common mistakes: trying degrees
    1, 1, 1, 1, ... without counting classes; forgetting class sizes in
    the orthogonality equations. Now you try: the character table of C₃.
    (rows (1, 1, 1), (1, ω, ω²), (1, ω², ω), ω = e^(2πi/3))

D7. Find the character tables of D₄ = ⟨r, s : r⁴ = s² = 1, srs = r⁻¹⟩ and
    of Q₈ = {±1, ±i, ±j, ±k} (s a reflection in a diagonal of the
    square), and show they are identical. Show that the
    Frobenius-Schur indicator ν = (1/|G|)∑χ(g²) of the 2-dimensional
    character distinguishes them.
    Answer: both tables (classes of sizes 1, 1, 2, 2, 2):
       D₄:     1    r²   {r, r³}  {s, sr²}  {sr, sr³}
       Q₈:     1    −1   {±i}     {±j}      {±k}
       χ₁      1     1     1        1         1
       χ₂      1     1     1       −1        −1
       χ₃      1     1    −1        1        −1
       χ₄      1     1    −1       −1         1
       χ₅      2    −2     0        0         0
    ν(χ₅) = +1 for D₄ and −1 for Q₈.
    Teacher explains: Both groups have order 8, centre of order 2 ({1, r²},
    {±1}) and 5 classes, so 5 irreducibles with ∑dᵢ² = 8: degrees 1, 1, 1,
    1, 2. In both, G′ = Z(G) and G/G′ ≅ C₂ × C₂ (D₄/⟨r²⟩ is generated by the
    images of r, s; Q₈/{±1} by those of i, j), giving the four linear
    characters: ±1 on the two generators, and the product on the third
    class. The last row is (2, a, b, c, d); orthogonality of each column
    with the first column (1, 1, 1, 1, 2) gives 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 2a = 0, so
    a = −2, and 1 + 1 − 1 − 1 + 2b = 0, so b = 0; similarly c = d = 0.
    So the tables coincide: the character table does not determine the
    group. Squares: in D₄, g² = r² for g = r, r³ and g² = 1 for the other
    six elements, so ∑χ₅(g²) = 6·2 + 2·(−2) = 8, ν = 1 (realisable over R:
    the symmetries of a square). In
    Q₈, g² = −1 for the six elements ±i, ±j, ±k and 1 for ±1: ∑χ₅(g²) =
    2·2 + 6·(−2) = −8, ν = −1 (quaternionic: not realisable over R).
    Common mistakes: putting r and r³ in different classes; assuming
    isomorphic tables imply isomorphic groups. Now you try: ν for the
    character χ(g) = ω of C₃. (0: (1 + ω² + ω)/3 = 0, a non-real character)

D8. Construct the character table of A₄ (classes e, (12)(34), (123),
    (132) of sizes 1, 3, 4, 4), using the quotient A₄/V₄ ≅ C₃ and the
    permutation character on 4 points. Explain why (123) and (132) are not
    conjugate in A₄.
    Answer:
                 e   (12)(34)  (123)  (132)
       1         1      1        1      1
       λ         1      1        ω      ω²
       λ̄         1      1        ω²     ω
       ψ         3     −1        0      0
    Teacher explains: The centraliser of (123) in S₄ is ⟨(123)⟩ (order 3),
    which lies in A₄; so its A₄-class has 12/3 = 4 elements, and the eight
    3-cycles split into two classes of size 4. Four classes ⇒ four
    irreducibles. V₄ = {e, (12)(34), (13)(24), (14)(23)} is normal with
    A₄/V₄ ≅ C₃ (generated by the image of (123)); the three characters of
    C₃ lift to 1, λ, λ̄ with λ(123) = ω, λ(132) = ω² and λ = 1 on V₄. Then
    12 = 1 + 1 + 1 + d², d = 3. The permutation character on 4 points is
    π = (4, 0, 1, 1), and ⟨π, π⟩ = (16 + 0 + 4 + 4)/12 = 2 (A₄ is
    2-transitive), so ψ = π − 1 = (3, −1, 0, 0) is irreducible. Check
    ⟨ψ, λ⟩ = (3 − 3 + 0 + 0)/12 = 0 ✓. The value λ(123) = ω is not real,
    so (123) is not conjugate to its inverse (132) in A₄ (in S₄ it is).
    Common mistakes: using S₄'s classes for A₄; forgetting conjugates:
    ⟨χ, λ⟩ uses conj λ. Now you try: decompose ψ ⊗ ψ. ((9, 1, 0, 0) =
    1 + λ + λ̄ + 2ψ)

D9. Construct the character table of S₄ (classes e, (12), (12)(34),
    (123), (1234) with sizes 1, 6, 3, 8, 6) and verify both orthogonality
    relations for one row and one column.
    Answer:
                 e   (12)  (12)(34)  (123)  (1234)
       1         1     1       1        1       1
       ε         1    −1       1        1      −1
       χ₂        2     0       2       −1       0
       χ₃        3     1      −1        0      −1
       χ₃′       3    −1      −1        0       1
    Teacher explains: Five classes (cycle types), so five irreducibles.
    Linear: S₄′ = A₄, giving 1 and ε. χ₃ = π₄ − 1 is irreducible (D5), and
    so is χ₃′ = εχ₃ (multiplying by a linear character preserves ⟨χ, χ⟩);
    χ₃′ ≠ χ₃ since they differ at (12). Then 24 − 1 − 1 − 9 − 9 = 4 gives
    one more of degree 2: it is lifted from S₄/V₄ ≅ S₃ (the 2-dim ρ of S₃,
    with (12) ↦ transposition, (12)(34) ↦ e, (123) ↦ 3-cycle, (1234) ↦
    transposition), giving (2, 0, 2, −1, 0) — the χ₂ found in D5. Row
    check for χ₃′: (9 + 6·1 + 3·1 + 0 + 6·1)/24 = 24/24 = 1 ✓. Column
    check for (12)(34): 1 + 1 + 4 + 1 + 1 = 8 = |C(12)(34)| = 24/3 ✓.
    Common mistakes: listing both 3-dim characters as the same; forgetting
    that V₄ ◁ S₄ and S₄/V₄ ≅ S₃. Now you try: the kernel of χ₂. (V₄: the
    classes where χ₂ = χ₂(1) = 2)

D10. Show that if ρ(g) has eigenvalues λ₁, ..., λ_d then
     χ_{Sym²}(g) = (χ(g)² + χ(g²))/2 and χ_{Λ²}(g) = (χ(g)² − χ(g²))/2.
     Decompose χ₃ ⊗ χ₃, Sym²χ₃ and Λ²χ₃ for S₄.
     Answer: χ₃² = (9, 1, 1, 0, 1) = 1 + χ₂ + χ₃ + χ₃′; Sym²χ₃ = (6, 2, 2, 0,
     0) = 1 + χ₂ + χ₃; Λ²χ₃ = (3, −1, −1, 0, 1) = χ₃′.
     Teacher explains: With an eigenbasis vᵢ, V ⊗ V = Sym² ⊕ Λ² has bases
     vᵢvⱼ (i ≤ j) and vᵢ ∧ vⱼ (i < j), with eigenvalues λᵢλⱼ. Since
       (∑λᵢ)² = ∑λᵢ² + 2∑_{i<j}λᵢλⱼ   and   ∑λᵢ² = χ(g²),
     we get χ_Λ = ∑_{i<j}λᵢλⱼ = (χ(g)² − χ(g²))/2 and χ_S = χ_Λ + χ(g²) =
     (χ(g)² + χ(g²))/2. For S₄ the squares of e, (12), (12)(34), (123),
     (1234) lie in the classes e, e, e, (123), (12)(34), so
       χ₃(g) = (3, 1, −1, 0, −1),   χ₃(g²) = (3, 3, 3, 0, −1),
       χ₃² = (9, 1, 1, 0, 1),
       Sym² = ((9 + 3)/2, (1 + 3)/2, (1 + 3)/2, 0, (1 − 1)/2) = (6, 2, 2, 0, 0),
       Λ² = ((9 − 3)/2, (1 − 3)/2, (1 − 3)/2, 0, (1 + 1)/2) = (3, −1, −1, 0, 1).
     Λ² = χ₃′ exactly; Sym² − 1 − χ₃ = (2, 0, 2, −1, 0) = χ₂; so χ₃ ⊗ χ₃ =
     1 + χ₂ + χ₃ + χ₃′ (dimensions 1 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 9 ✓). The trivial summand
     appears once because χ₃ is real and irreducible: ⟨χ², 1⟩ = ⟨χ, χ̄⟩.
     Common mistakes: using χ(g)² for χ(g²); forgetting which class g²
     lies in. Now you try: Sym² and Λ² of χ₂. ((3, 1, 3, 0, 1) = 1 + χ₂
     and (1, −1, 1, 1, −1) = ε)

D11. Define the induced character and prove Frobenius reciprocity for
     characters. Compute Ind_{A₃}^{S₃} ψ with ψ(123) = ω, Ind_{⟨(12)⟩}^{S₃} 1,
     and Ind_{C₄}^{D₄} ψ with ψ(r) = i, and decompose them.
     Answer: Ind_{A₃}ψ = (2, 0, −1) = ρ; Ind_{⟨(12)⟩}1 = (3, 1, 0) = 1 + ρ;
     Ind_{C₄}ψ = (2, −2, 0, 0, 0) = χ₅.
     Teacher explains: Ind ψ(g) = (1/|H|)∑_{x∈G} ψ̇(x⁻¹gx), where ψ̇ = ψ on H
     and 0 off H. Reciprocity: for a class function χ of G,
        ⟨Ind ψ, χ⟩_G = (1/|G||H|) ∑_{g,x} ψ̇(x⁻¹gx) conj χ(g)
                     = (1/|G||H|) ∑_{x} ∑_{h∈H} ψ(h) conj χ(xhx⁻¹)
                     = (1/|H|) ∑_{h∈H} ψ(h) conj χ(h) = ⟨ψ, Res χ⟩_H,
     substituting h = x⁻¹gx and using that χ is a class function. For a
     NORMAL subgroup of index 2, Ind ψ(g) = ψ(g) + ψ(sgs⁻¹) for g ∈ H (s ∉ H)
     and 0 off H. A₃ in S₃: (12)(123)(12) = (132), so Ind ψ(123) = ω + ω² =
     −1, Ind ψ(e) = 2, Ind ψ(12) = 0: (2, 0, −1) = ρ. ⟨(12)⟩: Ind 1 is the
     permutation character on the 3 cosets, i.e. on {1, 2, 3}: (3, 1, 0) =
     1 + ρ. Reciprocity check: ⟨Ind 1, ρ⟩ = ⟨1, Res ρ⟩ = (2 + 0)/2 = 1 ✓.
     C₄ in D₄: srs⁻¹ = r³, so Ind ψ(r) = i + i³ = 0, Ind ψ(r²) = (−1) + (−1) =
     −2, Ind ψ(1) = 2, and 0 on reflections: χ₅, irreducible. Common
     mistakes: forgetting the factor 1/|H| (or summing over coset
     representatives AND over H); forgetting the zero values off the
     conjugates of H. Now you try: Ind_{C₄}^{D₄} of the trivial
     character. ((2, 2, 2, 0, 0) = χ₁ + χ₂)

D12. Construct the character table of A₅ (classes e, (12)(34), (123),
     (12345), (13524) of sizes 1, 15, 20, 12, 12) from the permutation
     characters on the 5 points and on the 6 Sylow 5-subgroups, the
     degree equation, and column orthogonality. Deduce that A₅ is simple.
     Answer (φ = (1 + √5)/2, so 1 − φ = (1 − √5)/2):
                 e   (12)(34)  (123)  (12345)  (13524)
       1         1      1        1       1        1
       χ₃        3     −1        0       φ       1 − φ
       χ₃′       3     −1        0      1 − φ     φ
       χ₄        4      0        1      −1       −1
       χ₅        5      1       −1       0        0
     Teacher explains: The 5-cycles split into two A₅-classes (a 5-cycle's
     S₅-centraliser ⟨c⟩ lies in A₅), giving 5 classes. Points: π₅ = (5, 1,
     2, 0, 0); ⟨π₅, π₅⟩ = (25 + 15 + 80)/60 = 2, so χ₄ = π₅ − 1 = (4, 0, 1,
     −1, −1). Sylow 5-subgroups: there are 6, A₅ acts by conjugation; an
     involution fixes 2 of them, a 3-cycle none, a 5-cycle exactly its own:
     π₆ = (6, 2, 0, 1, 1), ⟨π₆, π₆⟩ = (36 + 60 + 12 + 12)/60 = 2, so χ₅ =
     π₆ − 1 = (5, 1, −1, 0, 0). Degrees: 60 − 1 − 16 − 25 = 18 = 3² + 3².
     Let the two 3-dim characters take values a, b on a class. Column
     orthogonality with the identity column and |C(g)|:
        (12)(34): 1 + 0 + 5 + 3a + 3b = 0, 1 + 0 + 1 + a² + b² = 4 ⇒ a = b = −1
        (123):    1 + 4 − 5 + 3(a + b) = 0, 1 + 1 + 1 + a² + b² = 3 ⇒ a = b = 0
        (12345):  1 − 4 + 0 + 3(a + b) = 0, 1 + 1 + 0 + a² + b² = 5
     so a + b = 1, a² + b² = 3, ab = −1: a, b = (1 ± √5)/2 = φ, 1 − φ.
     Simple: a normal subgroup is a union of classes and an intersection of
     kernels; each non-trivial χ equals χ(1) only at e, so every kernel is
     trivial and the only normal subgroups are 1 and A₅. (Check by
     counting: a normal subgroup is a union of classes containing e, of
     order dividing 60, and no sum of 1 with some of 15, 20, 12, 12
     divides 60 except 1 and 60.) Common mistakes: treating the two
     5-cycle classes as one; mixing up φ and 1 − φ between the rows (the
     rows are swapped by the outer automorphism). Now you try: decompose
     the permutation character (10, 2, 1, 0, 0) of A₅ on the 2-subsets of
     {1, ..., 5}. (1 + χ₄ + χ₅)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Linear characters and abelian groups.
    Number of linear characters |G : G′|:
    a) C₆                              b) C₂ × C₂
    c) S₃                              d) D₄
    e) Q₈                              f) A₄
    g) S₄                              h) D₅
    i) D₆ (order 12)                   j) A₅
    k) S₅                              l) C₂ × C₄
    m) C₃ × S₃                         n) Q₁₂ (dicyclic, order 12)
    o) D₇                              p) D₈ (order 16)
    Values (ωₙ = e^(2πi/n), χⱼ(gᵏ) = ωₙ^(jk) on Cₙ = ⟨g⟩):
    q) χ₃(g⁵) on C₈                    r) χ₂(g³) on C₆
    s) χ₁(g²)χ₁(g⁴) on C₆              t) all χ with χ(g) real on C₁₀
    Answers:
      a) 6      b) 4      c) 2      d) 4      e) 4      f) 3
      g) 2      h) 2      i) 4      j) 1      k) 2      l) 8
      m) 6      n) 4 (Q₁₂/Q₁₂′ ≅ C₄)          o) 2      p) 4
      q) ω₈¹⁵ = ω₈⁷ = (1 − i)/√2      r) ω₆⁶ = 1
      s) ω₆⁶ = 1                      t) χ₀ and χ₅ (values ±1)

    a) S₃                              b) D₄

      m) 1, 1, 1, 3, 3 (5)           n) nine 1's and two 3's (11)
      o) eight 1's + two 2's, or four 1's + three 2's
      p) no (3 ∤ 10)
      q) 1, 1, 1, 3 (it is A₄)       r) 5

M11. Reading a character table: kernels, centre, normal subgroups.
     a) ker ε in S₄                    b) ker χ₂ in S₄

        g = 1
     r) |G : G′| read off the table of D₅

       a) A₄        b) V₄        c) {e}       d) 1, V₄, A₄, S₄
       e) ⟨r⟩ = {1, r, r², r³}; {1, r², s, sr²}; {1, r², sr, sr³}
       f) {1, r²}   g) {e}       h) {±1}      i) A₄
       j) {1, r²}   k) V₄        l) 1, V₄, A₄ (V₄ = ker λ)
       m) yes (every non-trivial kernel is {e})
       n) χ₃ and χ₃′
       o) 2 (1 and ψ); 5 (all); 5 (all)
       p) no (λ(123) = ω is not real); yes
       q) Z(G) = {1}                   r) 2

M12. Mixed applications (π = permutation character).
     a) ⟨π, π⟩ for S₄ on 4 points      b) ⟨π, π⟩ for S₄ on 2-subsets
     c) ⟨π, π⟩ for S₅ on 2-subsets     d) ⟨π, π⟩ for S₆ on 3-subsets
     e) dim Hom_{S₄}(C^{2-subsets}, C^{points})
     f) orbits of D₄ on 2-colourings of the vertices of a square
     g) the same with 3 colours        h) the same under C₄ with 3 colours
     i) necklaces of 6 beads, 2 colours, under C₆
     j) bracelets of 6 beads, 2 colours, under D₆
     k) 2-colourings of the faces of a cube up to rotation
     l) ∑ dᵢ for S₄ and #{g : g² = 1} in S₄
     m) ∑ νᵢdᵢ for Q₈ and #{g : g² = 1} in Q₈
     n) the multiplicity of χ₃ in C[S₄]
     o) the number of irreducible characters of S₆
     p) can a group of order 30 have an irreducible of degree 4?
     q) ∑_{g∈G} χ(g) for a non-trivial irreducible χ
     r) the number of 1-dim summands of C[G] for G = A₅
     Answers:
       a) 2         b) 3         c) 3         d) 4
       e) 2 (common constituents 1 and χ₃)
       f) 6 ((16 + 2 + 4 + 2 + 8 + 8 + 4 + 4)/8)
       g) 21 ((81 + 3 + 9 + 3 + 27 + 27 + 9 + 9)/8)
       h) 24        i) 14 ((64 + 2 + 4 + 8 + 4 + 2)/6)
       j) 13 ((84 + 3·16 + 3·8)/12)
       k) 10 ((64 + 6·8 + 3·16 + 8·4 + 6·8)/24)
       l) 10 and 10 (1 + 6 + 3; all ν = 1)
       m) 2 and 2 (±1)                 n) 3
       o) 11 (partitions of 6)         p) no (4 ∤ 30)
       q) 0 (= |G|⟨χ, 1⟩)              r) 1

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make a fixed routine for every table: (1) list classes with
  sizes and centraliser orders; (2) find the linear characters from G/G′;
  (3) write the degree equation ∑dᵢ² = |G|; (4) produce more characters
  from permutation actions (count fixed points!) and products; (5) use
  ⟨χ, χ⟩ to test and orthogonality to finish. Always weight the sums by
  class sizes and conjugate non-real values. Let the student check every
  row and column before using a table, and verify small cases with
  explicit matrices (D1) so that "character" stays concrete. Harder:
  prove the orthogonality relations from Schur's lemma; prove that dᵢ
  divides |G| using algebraic integers; prove Burnside's pᵃqᵇ theorem;
  compute the character tables of S₅, of the Frobenius group of order 20
  and of GL₂(F₃); show that the number of real irreducible characters
  equals the number of real classes; construct the irreducible
  representations of Sₙ from Young tableaux (Specht modules) and check
  the hook-length formula for n ≤ 5; and prove Mackey's formula for
  Res ∘ Ind.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.10 — ELLIPTIC CURVES (INTRODUCTION): GROUP LAW, RATIONAL POINTS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - WEIERSTRASS EQUATIONS: over a field of characteristic ≠ 2, 3 every
    elliptic curve has a SHORT form E : y² = x³ + ax + b with DISCRIMINANT
    Δ = −16(4a³ + 27b²) ≠ 0 (⇔ the cubic has distinct roots ⇔ the curve is
    NON-SINGULAR). j-INVARIANT j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ + 27b²) classifies curves
    up to isomorphism over C (j = 0 for y² = x³ + b, j = 1728 for
    y² = x³ + ax). General form y² + a₁xy + a₃y = x³ + a₂x² + a₄x + a₆ is
    reduced to short form by completing the square and the cube.
  - PROJECTIVE PICTURE: Y²Z = X³ + aXZ² + bZ³ has exactly one point at
    infinity O = [0 : 1 : 0], a flex, which is the IDENTITY. By BÉZOUT a line
    meets E in exactly 3 points counted with multiplicity (a vertical line
    passes through O).
  - SINGULAR CUBICS (Δ = 0): a NODE (double root, two tangents, e.g.
    y² = (x − 1)²(x + 2)) or a CUSP (triple root, y² = x³). The non-singular
    points still form a group: ≅ multiplicative group (node) or ≅ additive
    group (cusp, via (x, y) ↦ x/y).
  - CHORD-TANGENT GROUP LAW: P + Q + R = O when P, Q, R are collinear. So
    −(x, y) = (x, −y), and for P = (x₁, y₁), Q = (x₂, y₂):
       λ = (y₂ − y₁)/(x₂ − x₁)  (P ≠ Q),   λ = (3x₁² + a)/(2y₁)  (P = Q),
       x₃ = λ² − x₁ − x₂,   y₃ = λ(x₁ − x₃) − y₁.
    If x₁ = x₂ and y₁ = −y₂ (vertical line) then P + Q = O. Doubling:
    x(2P) = (x⁴ − 2ax² − 8bx + a²)/(4y²). E(K) is an ABELIAN group for every
    field K ⊇ Q(a, b); ASSOCIATIVITY is the deep axiom (Cayley-Bacharach or
    Riemann-Roch; or check the formulas by computer algebra).
  - TORSION: P has order n if nP = O. E[2] = {O} ∪ {(e, 0) : e a root of the
    cubic}; 3-torsion points are the FLEXES, with x a root of ψ₃ = 3x⁴ + 6ax²
    + 12bx − a². Over C, E ≅ C/Λ (a torus) and E[n] ≅ (Z/n)², n² points.
  - NAGELL-LUTZ: on y² = x³ + ax + b with a, b ∈ Z, a rational torsion point
    P ≠ O has INTEGER coordinates, and y = 0 or y² | 4a³ + 27b². Consequence:
    a rational point with a non-integral coordinate (or an nP that is
    non-integral) has INFINITE order. MAZUR: E(Q)_tors is one of Z/n
    (1 ≤ n ≤ 10, n = 12) or Z/2 × Z/2m (1 ≤ m ≤ 4): 15 groups.
  - REDUCTION MOD p: for p ∤ Δ (GOOD reduction) E mod p is an elliptic curve
    over F_p, and for odd good p the torsion of E(Q) INJECTS into E(F_p);
    so #E(Q)_tors divides gcd of #E(F_p) over good odd p.
  - COUNTING POINTS OVER F_p: #E(F_p) = p + 1 + ∑_x (x³ + ax + b | p)
    (Legendre symbols); write #E(F_p) = p + 1 − a_p. HASSE: |a_p| ≤ 2√p.
    E(F_p) ≅ Z/n₁ × Z/n₂ with n₁ | n₂ and n₁ | p − 1. SUPERSINGULAR examples:
    y² = x³ − x for p ≡ 3 (mod 4) and y² = x³ + 1 for p ≡ 2 (mod 3) have
    exactly p + 1 points.
  - MORDELL(-WEIL) THEOREM: E(Q) is finitely generated: E(Q) ≅ E(Q)_tors ×
    Z^r, r = the RANK. Proof: WEAK Mordell-Weil (E(Q)/2E(Q) is finite, by
    2-DESCENT) plus a HEIGHT argument (h(P) = log max(|m|, |e|) for x = m/e²;
    h(2P) ≈ 4h(P), so descent terminates). Examples: y² = x³ − x has
    E(Q) ≅ (Z/2)², rank 0; y² = x³ − 2 has rank 1, generator (3, 5);
    y² = x³ + 17 has rank 2.
  - INTEGRAL POINTS: finitely many (Siegel), e.g. y² = x³ − 2 only (3, ±5)
    (Fermat); y² = x³ + 17 has 16 integral points, the largest at
    x = 5234. MORDELL EQUATIONS y² = x³ + k.
  - CONGRUENT NUMBERS: n is the area of a rational right triangle ⇔
    y² = x³ − n²x has a point with y ≠ 0 ⇔ (since the torsion is only E[2])
    its rank is positive. Triangle (a, b, c) → point (c²/4, c(b² − a²)/8);
    point (x, y) → triangle (|x² − n²|/y, 2nx/y, (x² + n²)/y) (absolute
    values). 5, 6, 7 are congruent; 1, 2, 3 are not (Fermat: 1 is not,
    by descent).
  - OUTLOOK: L-functions L(E, s) = ∏(1 − a_p p⁻ˢ + p^(1−2s))⁻¹ (good p);
    BIRCH-SWINNERTON-DYER conjecture: rank = ord_{s=1} L(E, s); modularity
    (Wiles et al.) and Fermat's Last Theorem; elliptic curve CRYPTOGRAPHY
    (ECDH, ECDSA) rests on the hardness of the discrete logarithm kP ↦ k in
    E(F_p); Lenstra's factoring method uses E(Z/N).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Decide which of the cubics y² = x³ − x, y² = x³ − 3x + 2, y² = x³ and
    y² = x³ − 3x − 2 are elliptic curves. For the singular ones find the
    singular point and say whether it is a node or a cusp; for the
    non-singular one give Δ and j.
    Answer: y² = x³ − x: Δ = 64, j = 1728, non-singular. x³ − 3x + 2 =
    (x − 1)²(x + 2): node at (1, 0). y² = x³: cusp at (0, 0). x³ − 3x − 2 =
    (x + 1)²(x − 2): node at (−1, 0) (an isolated real point).
    Teacher explains: A curve F(x, y) = y² − f(x) = 0 is singular at P when
    F = F_x = F_y = 0 there: F_y = 2y = 0 gives y = 0, and then f(x) = 0,
    f′(x) = 0: x is a REPEATED root of f. So non-singular ⇔ f has distinct
    roots ⇔ its discriminant −(4a³ + 27b²) ≠ 0. Compute 4a³ + 27b²:
       x³ − x:       4(−1) + 0 = −4,       Δ = −16(−4) = 64 ≠ 0
       x³ − 3x + 2:  4(−27) + 27·4 = 0     singular
       x³:           0                     singular
       x³ − 3x − 2:  4(−27) + 27·4 = 0     singular
    j(x³ − x) = 1728·4(−1)/(−4) = 1728. Near a double root e, f(x) =
    (x − e)²g(x) with g(e) ≠ 0, so y² ≈ g(e)(x − e)²: for y² = (x − 1)²(x + 2)
    this is y² ≈ 3(x − 1)² near x = 1, so two
    real tangents y = ±√3(x − 1): a NODE (the curve crosses itself). For
    y² = (x + 1)²(x − 2) ≈ −3(x + 1)², the tangents y = ±√−3(x + 1) are
    complex: still a node, but over R the point (−1, 0) is ISOLATED (an
    acnode). y² = x³ has a triple root: a single tangent y² = 0 (doubled),
    a CUSP. Common mistakes: using Δ = 4a³ + 27b² with the wrong sign (the
    sign does not affect singularity but does affect the value of Δ);
    thinking a singular cubic has no group law (its smooth points form Gₘ
    or Gₐ). Now you try: is y² = x³ − 12x + 16 singular? (yes: 4(−1728) +
    27·256 = 0; (x − 2)²(x + 4), node at (2, 0))

D2. Derive the addition formulas: for P = (x₁, y₁), Q = (x₂, y₂) on
    y² = x³ + ax + b with x₁ ≠ x₂, show that the third intersection of the
    chord PQ with E has x₃ = λ² − x₁ − x₂, and that P + Q = (x₃, λ(x₁ − x₃)
    − y₁). Derive the tangent slope for doubling.
    Answer: λ = (y₂ − y₁)/(x₂ − x₁) or (3x₁² + a)/(2y₁); P + Q is the
    reflection of the third point.
    Teacher explains: The line through P and Q is y = λx + ν with λ = (y₂ −
    y₁)/(x₂ − x₁), ν = y₁ − λx₁. Substitute into the curve:
       (λx + ν)² = x³ + ax + b  ⇔  x³ − λ²x² + (a − 2λν)x + (b − ν²) = 0.
    This cubic has roots x₁, x₂ and a third root x₃; comparing the x²
    coefficient with −(x₁ + x₂ + x₃): x₁ + x₂ + x₃ = λ², so
       x₃ = λ² − x₁ − x₂,   and the third point is R = (x₃, λx₃ + ν).
    By the rule P + Q + R = O, P + Q = −R = (x₃, −(λx₃ + ν)) and −(λx₃ + ν) =
    −λx₃ − y₁ + λx₁ = λ(x₁ − x₃) − y₁. For P = Q the chord becomes the
    TANGENT: differentiate implicitly, 2y y′ = 3x² + a, so λ = (3x₁² +
    a)/(2y₁) (y₁ ≠ 0), and the same formulas hold because x₁ is now a
    DOUBLE root. If y₁ = 0 the tangent is vertical and 2P = O. Why the
    reflection? With O at infinity on every vertical line, the three
    collinear points R, −R, O show −R is the reflection of R; the law
    "collinear points sum to O" then forces P + Q = −R. Common mistakes:
    forgetting to reflect (giving R = −(P + Q)); using x₃ = λ² − 2x₁ with a
    chord; dividing by 2y₁ = 0. Now you try: derive x(2P) = (x⁴ − 2ax² −
    8bx + a²)/(4y²). (substitute λ = (3x² + a)/(2y), y² = x³ + ax + b into
    λ² − 2x)

D3. On E : y² = x³ − 2 let P = (3, 5). Compute 2P and show that P has
    infinite order. Explain why this gives infinitely many rational
    solutions of y² = x³ − 2 but only two integral ones.
    Answer: 2P = (129/100, −383/1000); P has infinite order by
    Nagell-Lutz; the integral points are (3, ±5) only.
    Teacher explains: Doubling with a = 0:
       λ = 3x₁²/(2y₁) = 27/10
       x₃ = λ² − 2x₁ = 729/100 − 6 = 129/100
       y₃ = λ(x₁ − x₃) − y₁ = (27/10)(171/100) − 5 = 4617/1000 − 5000/1000
          = −383/1000
    Check: (129/100)³ − 2 = 2146689/10⁶ − 2 = 146689/10⁶ = (383/1000)² ✓
    (383² = 146689). NAGELL-LUTZ: a torsion point has integer coordinates;
    2P does not, so 2P is NOT torsion, so P is not torsion (if nP = O then
    n(2P) = O). Hence P, 2P, 3P, ... are all distinct rational points
    (3P has x = 164323/29241): infinitely many rational solutions. But
    the denominators grow fast (the height roughly quadruples under
    doubling), and a theorem of Fermat/Euler says (3, ±5) are the only
    integral points: 27 − 2 = 25 = 5². Common mistakes: arithmetic slips in
    fractions (always verify the new point lies on the curve); concluding
    "infinite order" from one non-integral point on a NON-integral model.
    Now you try: on y² = x³ + 3 show that P = (1, 2) has infinite order by
    doubling. (2P = (−23/16, −11/64), not integral)

D4. On E : y² = x³ + 17 let P = (−2, 3), Q = (−1, 4). Compute P + Q, 2P and
    2Q, and show how the integral points (2, 5) and (8, 23) arise.
    Answer: P + Q = (4, −9); 2P = (8, −23); 2Q = (137/64, −2651/512).
    Teacher explains: P + Q: λ = (4 − 3)/(−1 + 2) = 1; x₃ = 1 − (−2) − (−1)
    = 4; y₃ = 1·(−2 − 4) − 3 = −9. Check: 64 + 17 = 81 = 9² ✓. So the third
    point on the chord is (4, 9) and P + Q = (4, −9). 2P: λ = 3·4/(2·3) =
    2; x₃ = 4 + 4 = 8; y₃ = 2(−2 − 8) − 3 = −23: 512 + 17 = 529 = 23² ✓.
    2Q: λ = 3/8; x₃ = 9/64 + 2 = 137/64; y₃ = (3/8)(−1 − 137/64) − 4 =
    (3/8)(−201/64) − 4 = −603/512 − 2048/512 = −2651/512. Other integral
    points come from combinations, e.g. Q + 2P = (−1, 4) + (8, −23): λ =
    −27/9 = −3, x₃ = 9 + 1 − 8 = 2, y₃ = −3(−1 − 2) − 4 = 5: Q + 2P =
    (2, 5) ✓. The Mordell-Weil group here is Z² generated by P
    and Q, and all 16 integral points ((−2, ±3), (−1, ±4), (2, ±5), (4, ±9),
    (8, ±23), (43, ±282), (52, ±375), (5234, ±378661)) are small
    combinations aP + bQ. Common mistakes: forgetting the sign flip (the
    chord gives (4, 9), the SUM is (4, −9)); slope errors with negative
    coordinates. Now you try: compute (2, 5) + (4, 9). (λ = 2, x₃ = −2,
    y₃ = 3: (−2, 3))

D5. Explain why O is the identity, why −P = (x, −y), and why the law is
    associative. Verify associativity numerically on y² = x³ + 17 with
    P = (−2, 3), Q = (−1, 4), R = (2, 5).
    Answer: (P + Q) + R = P + (Q + R) = (43, 282).
    Teacher explains: IDENTITY: the line through P and O is the vertical
    line through P; its third point is P′ = (x, −y), and reflecting gives P
    back: P + O = P. INVERSE: P, (x, −y), O are collinear, so P + (x, −y) =
    O. COMMUTATIVITY: the line PQ is the line QP. ASSOCIATIVITY: let ℓ₁ =
    PQ, ℓ₂ = (Q + R)O, ℓ₃ = R(P + Q) and m₁ = QR, m₂ = (P + Q)O, m₃ =
    P(Q + R) be lines (XY = the line through X and Y).
    The cubics ℓ₁ℓ₂ℓ₃ and m₁m₂m₃ share 8 of their 9 intersection points
    with E (O, P, Q, R, ±(P + Q), ±(Q + R)); by the CAYLEY-BACHARACH
    theorem they share the 9th, which is −((P + Q) + R) for the first and
    −(P + (Q + R)) for the second. Numbers: P + Q = (4, −9) (D4). Then
    (4, −9) + (2, 5): λ = (5 + 9)/(2 − 4) = −7, x₃ = 49 − 4 − 2 = 43,
    y₃ = −7(4 − 43) + 9 = 282. Other side: Q + R = (−1, 4) + (2, 5): λ =
    1/3, x₃ = 1/9 + 1 − 2 = −8/9, y₃ = (1/3)(−1 + 8/9) − 4 = −109/27. Then
    P + (Q + R) = (−2, 3) + (−8/9, −109/27): λ = (−109/27 − 3)/(−8/9 + 2) =
    (−190/27)/(10/9) = −19/3, x₃ = 361/9 + 2 + 8/9 = 43, y₃ = (−19/3)(−2 −
    43) − 3 = 285 − 3 = 282 ✓. Both give (43, 282): 43³ + 17 = 79524 =
    282² ✓. Common mistakes: believing associativity is "obvious" from the
    picture (it is a genuine theorem); not treating P = Q or vertical lines
    as separate cases in a proof by formulas. Now you try: on y² = x³ + 1
    check that (2, 3) + (−(2, 3)) = O and (2, 3) + O = (2, 3) using the
    projective point O = [0 : 1 : 0]. (vertical line x = 2 meets E at
    (2, ±3) and O)

D6. Find the 2-torsion and 3-torsion points of E : y² = x³ + 1 over Q, and
    show that P = (2, 3) has order 6. Deduce E(Q)_tors ≅ Z/6.
    Answer: E[2](Q) = {O, (−1, 0)}; rational 3-torsion (0, ±1); multiples
    of (2, 3): (0, 1), (−1, 0), (0, −1), (2, −3), O.
    Teacher explains: 2-TORSION: 2P = O ⇔ the tangent is vertical ⇔ y = 0:
    x³ + 1 = (x + 1)(x² − x + 1), the quadratic has no real roots, so the
    only rational point of order 2 is (−1, 0) (over C there are three).
    3-TORSION: 3P = O ⇔ 2P = −P ⇔ x(2P) = x(P) ⇔ P is a FLEX; the condition
    is ψ₃(x) = 3x⁴ + 6ax² + 12bx − a² = 3x⁴ + 12x = 3x(x³ + 4) = 0. x = 0
    gives (0, ±1); x = −∛4 is irrational. MULTIPLES of P = (2, 3):
       2P: λ = 3·4/6 = 2, x = 4 − 4 = 0, y = 2(2 − 0) − 3 = 1:  (0, 1)
       3P = P + 2P: λ = (1 − 3)/(0 − 2) = 1, x = 1 − 2 − 0 = −1,
            y = 1·(2 + 1) − 3 = 0:                             (−1, 0)
       4P = −2P = (0, −1),  5P = −P = (2, −3),  6P = O   (3P has order 2)
    So P has order 6. NAGELL-LUTZ bound: 4a³ + 27b² = 27, so torsion points
    have y = 0 or y² | 27, i.e. y ∈ {0, ±1, ±3}: y = ±1 ⇒ x = 0; y = ±3 ⇒
    x = 2; y = 0 ⇒ x = −1. That is exactly the 5 points above plus O:
    E(Q)_tors = ⟨(2, 3)⟩ ≅ Z/6. Common mistakes: taking y² | Δ with the
    wrong Δ (either 4a³ + 27b² or 16 times it works as a SEARCH bound, but
    use one consistently); forgetting to check that each candidate really
    has finite order. Now you try: find E(Q)_tors for y² = x³ + 4. (y² | 432:
    candidates give (0, ±2), order 3: Z/3)

D7. State the Nagell-Lutz theorem and use it to find E(Q)_tors for
    E : y² = x³ − 43x + 166, showing that P = (3, 8) has order 7.
    Answer: E(Q)_tors ≅ Z/7 = {O, (3, ±8), (−5, ±16), (11, ±32)}.
    Teacher explains: NAGELL-LUTZ: if a, b ∈ Z and P = (x, y) ∈ E(Q) has
    finite order, then x, y ∈ Z and either y = 0 or y² | D = 4a³ + 27b².
    Here D = 4(−79507) + 27·27556 = −318028 + 744012 = 425984 = 2¹⁵·13.
    Multiples of P = (3, 8):
       2P: λ = (27 − 43)/16 = −1, x = 1 − 6 = −5, y = −(3 + 5) − 8 = −16
       3P = P + 2P: λ = (−16 − 8)/(−5 − 3) = 3, x = 9 − 3 + 5 = 11,
            y = 3(3 − 11) − 8 = −32
       4P = 3P + P: λ = (8 + 32)/(3 − 11) = −5, x = 25 − 11 − 3 = 11,
            y = −5(11 − 11) + 32 = 32   ⇒ 4P = (11, 32) = −3P
    So 7P = 4P + 3P = O and P has order 7 (7 is prime and P ≠ O). The
    points are (3, ±8), (−5, ±16), (11, ±32): y² = 64, 256, 1024 all divide
    2¹⁵·13 ✓. The cubic x³ − 43x + 166 has no integer root (try divisors of
    166), so there is no 2-torsion, and by Mazur the group is exactly Z/7
    (or: a reduction mod 3 gives #E(F₃) = 7, bounding the torsion by 7).
    Common mistakes: stopping at 3P (you must reach O or a point already
    seen); assuming Nagell-Lutz's condition is sufficient (it is only
    necessary: (1, 3) on y² = x³ + 8 satisfies it but has infinite order).
    Now you try: show (−12, 108) on y² = x³ − 432x + 8208 has order 5.
    (2P = (24, −108), 3P = (24, 108) = −2P)

D8. Count the points of E : y² = x³ + x + 1 over F₅ using Legendre symbols,
    list them, check Hasse's bound and show E(F₅) is cyclic of order 9,
    generated by (0, 1).
    Answer: #E(F₅) = 9: O, (0, ±1), (2, ±1), (3, ±1), (4, ±2); a₅ = −3,
    |a₅| ≤ 2√5 ≈ 4.47; (0, 1) has order 9.
    Teacher explains: For each x, the number of y with y² = f(x) is 1 +
    (f(x)/p) (0 if f(x) is a non-square, 1 if f(x) = 0, 2 if a non-zero
    square). Squares mod 5: 0, 1, 4.
       x:      0   1   2   3   4
       f(x):   1   3   1   1   4       (f(2) = 11, f(3) = 31, f(4) = 69)
       (f/5): +1  −1  +1  +1  +1
    #E = 5 + 1 + (1 − 1 + 1 + 1 + 1) = 9 (including O). Points: y² = 1 ⇒
    y = ±1 (= 1, 4); y² = 4 ⇒ y = ±2. a₅ = p + 1 − #E = −3 and |−3| ≤ 2√5 ✓.
    A group of order 9 is Z/9 or Z/3 × Z/3; the latter needs 3 | p − 1 = 4,
    false, so E(F₅) is cyclic. Explicitly, with G = (0, 1) and arithmetic
    mod 5 (2⁻¹ = 3):
       2G: λ = (3·0 + 1)/(2·1) = 3, x = 9 − 0 = 4, y = 3(0 − 4) − 1 = −13 ≡ 2
       so 2G = (4, 2), and continuing: 3G = (2, 1), 4G = (3, 4), 5G = (3, 1),
       6G = (2, 4), 7G = (4, 3), 8G = (0, 4) = −G, 9G = O.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the point at infinity; counting f(x) = 0 as
    two points; dividing mod p without inverting (λ = 1/2 means 1·3 = 3
    mod 5). Now you try: count y² = x³ + 1 over F₅. (6: O, (0, ±1),
    (2, ±2), (4, 0); 5 ≡ 2 mod 3 so #E = p + 1)

D9. Show that reduction mod p is injective on torsion for good odd p and use
    it to prove that y² = x³ + 3 has trivial torsion over Q. Deduce that
    P = (1, 2) has infinite order and that the rank is at least 1.
    Answer: #E(F₅) = 6 and #E(F₇) = 13 have gcd 1, so E(Q)_tors = {O}.
    Teacher explains: Δ = −16·27·9 = −3888 = −2⁴·3⁵, so every p ≥ 5 is a
    good prime. THEOREM (proof via Nagell-Lutz type estimates or formal
    groups): for good p ≠ 2 the map E(Q)_tors → E(F_p), P ↦ P mod p, is an
    injective homomorphism. So #E(Q)_tors divides #E(F_p). Counts:
       F₅: f(x) = x³ + 3 takes values 3, 4, 1, 0, 2 at x = 0..4;
           squares 0, 1, 4: points (1, ±2), (2, ±1), (3, 0), O: #E = 6
       F₇: cubes mod 7 are 0, ±1, so f(x) ∈ {3, 4, 2}: f = 3 (x = 0) is a
           non-square, f = 4 (x = 1, 2, 4) and f = 2 (x = 3, 5, 6) are
           squares (4 = 2², 2 = 3²): 6·2 + 1 = 13
    gcd(6, 13) = 1, so E(Q)_tors is trivial. Hence P = (1, 2) (1 + 3 = 4 ✓)
    is a point of INFINITE order, and E(Q) contains a copy of Z: rank ≥ 1.
    Common mistakes: using a bad prime (p = 3
    here: the reduction is singular and injectivity fails); using p = 2
    (excluded). Now you try: bound the torsion of y² = x³ − x using
    p = 3, 5, 7. (#E = 4, 8, 8: gcd 4, and E[2] gives 4 points: Z/2 × Z/2)

D10. State Mordell's theorem and Mazur's theorem. Show that 6 and 5 are
     congruent numbers using the curves y² = x³ − n²x, and produce a second,
     different right triangle of area 6 from the point 2(−3, 9).
     Answer: (3, 4, 5) ↔ (−3, 9) on y² = x³ − 36x; (3/2, 20/3, 41/6) ↔
     (−4, 6) on y² = x³ − 25x; 2(−3, 9) = (25/4, −35/8) gives the triangle
     (7/10, 120/7, 1201/70) of area 6.
     Teacher explains: MORDELL: E(Q) ≅ E(Q)_tors × Z^r. MAZUR: E(Q)_tors is
     one of 15 groups (Z/n, n ≤ 10 or 12; Z/2 × Z/2m, m ≤ 4). CONGRUENT
     NUMBERS: if a² + b² = c² and ab/2 = n, then x = (c/2)², y = c(b² −
     a²)/8 satisfies y² = x³ − n²x; conversely from (x, y) with y ≠ 0,
     a = |x² − n²|/|y|, b = 2n|x|/|y|, c = (x² + n²)/|y|. n = 6:
        (−3, 9): (−27) + 108 = 81 ✓;  a = |9 − 36|/9 = 3, b = 36/9 = 4,
        c = 45/9 = 5.
     n = 5: (−4, 6): −64 + 100 = 36 ✓; a = 9/6 = 3/2, b = 40/6 = 20/3,
     c = 41/6, and (3/2)(20/3)/2 = 5 ✓. TORSION of y² = x³ − n²x is only
     E[2] = {O, (0, 0), (±n, 0)} (reduce mod good primes: for n = 6,
     #E(F₅) = 8, #E(F₇) = 8, #E(F₁₁) = 12, #E(F₁₃) = 20, gcd 4). So (−3, 9)
     has infinite order and there are INFINITELY many triangles of area 6.
     Doubling: λ = (27 − 36)/18 = −1/2, x = 1/4 + 6 = 25/4,
     y = −(1/2)(−3 − 25/4) − 9 = 37/8 − 9 = −35/8. Then
        a = |625/16 − 36|/(35/8) = (49/16)(8/35) = 7/10
        b = 2·6·(25/4)/(35/8) = 75·8/35 = 120/7
        c = (625/16 + 36)/(35/8) = (1201/16)(8/35) = 1201/70
     Check: (7/10)(120/7)/2 = 6 and 49/100 + 14400/49 = 1442401/4900 =
     (1201/70)² ✓. Common mistakes: using a 2-torsion point (y = 0) — it gives
     no triangle; forgetting absolute values. Now you try: the point
     (25, 120) on y² = x³ − 49x shows 7 is congruent: find the triangle.
     (24/5, 35/12, 337/60)

D11. Prove by complete 2-descent that E : y² = x³ − x has rank 0, so
     E(Q) = {O, (0, 0), (1, 0), (−1, 0)}. Deduce that x⁴ − y⁴ = z² has no
     solutions with xyz ≠ 0 (Fermat), hence Fermat's Last Theorem for
     exponent 4, and that 1 is not a congruent number.
     Answer: the descent map E(Q)/2E(Q) → (Q^×/Q^×²)² has image of size 4,
     equal to the image of the torsion, so E(Q)/2E(Q) ≅ (Z/2)² = E[2] and
     r = 0.
     Teacher explains: Write x³ − x = (x − e₁)(x − e₂)(x − e₃), e₁ = 0,
     e₂ = 1, e₃ = −1. THE MAP: for P = (x, y) not 2-torsion,
        φ(P) = (x − e₁, x − e₂) = (x, x − 1)   modulo squares;
     at 2-torsion points use the product of the other two factors:
     φ(0, 0) = (−1, −1), φ(1, 0) = (1, 2), φ(−1, 0) = (−1, −2), φ(O) = (1, 1).
     FACTS: φ is a homomorphism with kernel 2E(Q), and each coordinate is ±
     a product of primes dividing (eᵢ − eⱼ), here only 2: so bᵢ ∈ {±1, ±2}.
     SIGNS: x = b₁z₁², x − 1 = b₂z₂², x + 1 = b₁b₂z₃². If b₁ > 0 then x > 0,
     x + 1 > 0, so b₁b₂ > 0 and b₂ > 0; if b₁ < 0 then x < 0, x − 1 < 0, b₂
     < 0. So the image lies in the group of 8 pairs {(1, 1), (1, 2), (2, 1),
     (2, 2), (−1, −1), (−1, −2), (−2, −1), (−2, −2)}; the torsion gives
     the subgroup {(1, 1), (−1, −1), (1, 2), (−1, −2)} of index 2. It is
     enough to rule out ONE element of the other coset, say (2, 1). Write
     x = m/e² in lowest terms (x-coordinates of rational points have
     square denominators). Then x = 2z₁² and x + 1 = 2z₃² give m = 2r²,
     m + e² = 2t² with r, t ∈ Z; subtracting, e² = 2(t² − r²) is even, so
     e is even, but m = 2r² is even too, contradicting gcd(m, e) = 1. So
     #φ(E(Q)) = 4 = #E[2], hence E(Q)/2E(Q) ≅ E(Q)_tors/2E(Q)_tors and r = 0.
     Torsion (Nagell-Lutz, y² | 4) is E[2]. FERMAT: if a⁴ − b⁴ = c² with
     abc ≠ 0, then X = a²/b², Y = ac/b³ satisfies X(X² − 1) = (a²/b²)·
     (c²/b⁴) = Y² with Y ≠ 0: impossible. In particular x⁴ + y⁴ = z⁴ has no
     solution with xyz ≠ 0 (take a = z, b = y, c = x²). And 1 is not a
     congruent number, since y² = x³ − 1²·x is exactly E.
     Common mistakes: forgetting to rule out the whole non-trivial coset
     (one element suffices since the image is a group); mis-assigning φ at
     the 2-torsion points. Now you try: why can bᵢ only involve primes
     dividing (e₁ − e₂)(e₁ − e₃)(e₂ − e₃) = −2? (a prime p dividing the
     square-free part of x − e₁ to an odd power must also divide one of the
     other factors x − eⱼ, hence divide eⱼ − e₁)

D12. Elliptic curve Diffie-Hellman: on E : y² = x³ + 2x + 2 over F₁₇ with
     G = (5, 1), compute 2G, 3G and 7G, show #E(F₁₇) = 19, and carry out a
     key exchange with Alice's secret a = 3 and Bob's secret b = 7.
     Answer: 2G = (6, 3), 3G = (10, 6), 7G = (0, 6); #E = 19 (prime), so
     G has order 19; shared key 21G = 2G = (6, 3).
     Teacher explains: Arithmetic mod 17 (inverses: 2⁻¹ = 9, 5⁻¹ = 7,
     6⁻¹ = 3, 4⁻¹ = 13).
        2G: λ = (3·25 + 2)/(2·1) = 77·9 = 693 ≡ 13;
            x = 169 − 10 = 159 ≡ 6; y = 13(5 − 6) − 1 = −14 ≡ 3: (6, 3)
        3G = G + 2G: λ = (3 − 1)/(6 − 5) = 2; x = 4 − 11 = −7 ≡ 10;
            y = 2(5 − 10) − 1 = −11 ≡ 6: (10, 6)
     Continuing, 4G = (3, 1), 5G = (9, 16), 6G = (16, 13), 7G = (0, 6), ...,
     18G = (5, 16) = −G, 19G = O. Hasse allows #E ∈ [18 − 8.2, 18 + 8.2] =
     [10, 26]; the order of G divides #E and is 19, so #E = 19 (a direct
     count agrees): prime order, every point ≠ O generates. KEY EXCHANGE:
     public (E, G). Alice sends A = 3G = (10, 6), Bob sends B = 7G = (0, 6).
     Alice computes 3B = 21G, Bob computes 7A = 21G; 21 ≡ 2 (mod 19), so
     both get (6, 3). An eavesdropper must solve the ECDLP: find a from G
     and aG. Real systems use p ≈ 2²⁵⁶, where the best known attacks take
     about √p ≈ 2¹²⁸ steps (Pollard rho), with fast double-and-add for the
     honest parties (about 2 log₂ k group operations). Common mistakes:
     reducing λ mod p but forgetting to reduce x₃, y₃; treating k mod p
     instead of k mod the ORDER of G (19, not 17). Now you try: find k with
     kG = (16, 4). (k = 13: 6G = (16, 13), so 13G = −6G = (16, −13) =
     (16, 4))
