M9. Determine the group structure of E(F_p) (as Z/n or Z/n₁ × Z/n₂).
    a) x³ + x + 1, F₅                  b) x³ + x + 1, F₇
    c) x³ + x + 1, F₁₁                 d) x³ − x, F₃
    e) x³ − x, F₅                      f) x³ − x, F₇
    g) x³ − x, F₁₁                     h) x³ − x, F₁₇
    i) x³ + 1, F₅                      j) x³ + 1, F₇
    k) x³ + 1, F₁₁                     l) x³ + 3, F₁₃
    m) x³ + 2x + 2, F₁₇                n) x³ − 2, F₃₁
    o) x³ − 36x, F₁₃                   p) x³ + 2x + 3, F₇
    q) the order of (2, 1) on x³ + x + 1 over F₅
    r) the order of (3, 0) on x³ + 3 over F₅
    Answers:
      a) Z/9                           b) Z/5
      c) Z/14                          d) Z/2 × Z/2
      e) Z/2 × Z/4                     f) Z/2 × Z/4
      g) Z/2 × Z/6                     h) Z/4 × Z/4
      i) Z/6                           j) Z/2 × Z/6
      k) Z/12                          l) Z/3 × Z/3
      m) Z/19                          n) Z/2 × Z/14
      o) Z/2 × Z/10                    p) Z/6
      q) 3                             r) 2

M10. Bound E(Q)_tors using #E(F_p) at good odd primes; then decide the
     order of the given point.
     a) x³ + 3 (p = 5, 7); P = (1, 2)
     b) x³ − 2 (p = 5, 7); P = (3, 5)
     c) x³ + 17 (p = 5, 7); P = (−2, 3)
     d) x³ + x + 1 (p = 3, 5); P = (0, 1)
     e) x³ + 1 (p = 5, 7, 11, 13)
     f) x³ − x (p = 3, 5, 7)
     g) x³ − 43x + 166 (p = 3, 5, 7)
     h) x³ − 36x (p = 5, 7, 11, 13); P = (−3, 9)
     i) the bad primes of x³ + 3, x³ − 2, x³ + 17, x³ − 43x + 166
     j) why p = 2 is never used
     Answers:
       a) 6, 13: gcd 1, trivial; P has infinite order
       b) 6, 7: gcd 1, trivial; infinite order
       c) 6, 13: gcd 1, trivial; infinite order
       d) 4, 9: gcd 1, trivial; infinite order
       e) 6, 12, 12, 12: gcd 6 = #⟨(2, 3)⟩: Z/6
       f) 4, 8, 8: gcd 4 = #E[2]: Z/2 × Z/2
       g) 7, 7, 7: torsion divides 7, and (3, 8) has order 7: Z/7
       h) 8, 8, 12, 20: gcd 4 = #E[2]: P has infinite order
       i) {2, 3}; {2, 3}; {2, 3, 17}; {2, 13}
       j) short Weierstrass form always has 2 | Δ, and reduction mod 2 is
          not injective on torsion in general

M11. Congruent numbers and Mordell-Weil groups.
     a) the point on y² = x³ − 36x from the triangle (3, 4, 5)
     b) the triangle from (−3, 9) on y² = x³ − 36x
     c) the triangle from (12, 36) on y² = x³ − 36x
     d) the triangle from (18, 72) on y² = x³ − 36x
     e) the triangle from 2(−3, 9) = (25/4, −35/8)
     f) the triangle from (−4, 6) on y² = x³ − 25x
     g) the point from the triangle (3/2, 20/3, 41/6), n = 5
     h) the triangle from (25, 120) on y² = x³ − 49x
     i) the point from the triangle (35/12, 24/5, 337/60), n = 7 (x only)
     j) the point from the triangle (5, 12, 13), n = 30
     k) which of n = 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7 are congruent
     l) E(Q) for y² = x³ − x, x³ + 1, x³ − 2, x³ + 17, x³ − 36x
     m) the number of possible torsion groups over Q (Mazur)
     n) the largest possible order of a rational torsion point
     Answers:
       a) (25/4, 35/8)                 b) (3, 4, 5)
       c) (3, 4, 5)                    d) (4, 3, 5)
       e) (7/10, 120/7, 1201/70)       f) (3/2, 20/3, 41/6)
       g) (1681/144, 62279/1728) (= −2(−4, 6))
       h) (24/5, 35/12, 337/60)        i) x = (337/120)² = 113569/14400
       j) (169/4, 1547/8)              k) 5, 6, 7 (not 1, 2, 3)
       l) Z/2 × Z/2; Z/6; Z (gen. (3, 5)); Z² (gen. (−2, 3), (−1, 4));
          Z/2 × Z/2 × Z
       m) 15                           n) 12

M12. Elliptic curve cryptography on E : y² = x³ + 2x + 2 over F₁₇,
     G = (5, 1) of order 19 (and one question over F₅).
     a) 2G                             b) 3G
     c) 4G                             d) 5G
     e) 10G                            f) 18G
     g) 19G and 20G                    h) 100G
     i) k with kG = (0, 6)             j) k with kG = (16, 4)
     k) k with kG = (13, 10)           l) Alice a = 3, Bob b = 7: A, B and
                                          the shared key
     m) Alice a = 5, Bob b = 11: the shared key
     n) −(9, 16) and (9, 16) + (9, 1)
     o) on y² = x³ + x + 1 over F₅, G = (0, 1): 2G, 4G, and k with
        kG = (2, 4)
     p) why prime group order is preferred
     Answers:
       a) (6, 3)                       b) (10, 6)
       c) (3, 1)                       d) (9, 16)
       e) (7, 11)                      f) (5, 16) = −G
       g) O; G = (5, 1)                h) 5G = (9, 16) (100 ≡ 5 mod 19)
       i) 7                            j) 13
       k) 11                           l) A = (10, 6), B = (0, 6); 21G =
                                          2G = (6, 3)
       m) 55G = 17G = (6, 14)          n) (9, 1); O (5G + 14G = 19G)
       o) (4, 2), (3, 4); k = 6
       p) every point ≠ O generates the group and there are no small
          subgroups for Pohlig-Hellman attacks

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw y² = x³ − x and y² = x³ + 1 over R and do the chord and
  tangent constructions with a ruler before any algebra; then do the same
  computations with exact fractions and ALWAYS check the answer lies on
  the curve. Tabulate all points of small curves over F₅ and F₇ by hand
  (squares table first) and build the addition table of a group of order 5
  or 6. Keep Nagell-Lutz as a two-step recipe: list candidates, then test
  orders. Harder: prove associativity with a computer algebra system; prove
  Nagell-Lutz via the filtration by p-adic valuations; carry out a
  2-isogeny descent for y² = x³ − 2x or y² = x³ + 17; implement double-and-
  add and Schoof-free point counting in Python; prove Hasse's bound for
  y² = x³ − x via Jacobi sums; compute L(E, 1) numerically for a rank-0
  curve and compare with BSD; and read about Tunnell's theorem on
  congruent numbers and Lenstra's elliptic curve factoring method.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.11 — ALGEBRAIC CURVES AND RIEMANN SURFACES (INTRODUCTION)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PLANE CURVES: an affine curve f(x, y) = 0 over C and its PROJECTIVE
    CLOSURE F(x, y, z) = z^d f(x/z, y/z) = 0 in P² = {[x : y : z]}; the
    POINTS AT INFINITY are the solutions with z = 0 (the factors of the
    top-degree part of f). Every two distinct lines of P² meet in exactly
    one point; circles all pass through the CIRCULAR POINTS [1 : ±i : 0].
  - SINGULAR POINTS: P is singular if F = F_x = F_y = F_z = 0 at P (affine:
    f = f_x = f_y = 0). The MULTIPLICITY m_P is the lowest degree of the
    Taylor expansion at P; its lowest form is the TANGENT CONE. Types:
    NODE (two distinct tangents, y² = x² + x³), CUSP (one double tangent,
    y² = x³), TACNODE (y² = x⁴), ordinary m-fold point (m distinct tangents).
  - INTERSECTION MULTIPLICITY I_P(F, G) = dim O_P/(F, G): it is ≥ m_P(F)m_P(G)
    with equality iff no common tangents; I_P(F, G) = I_P(F, G + AF); for a
    line or parametrised branch, substitute and take the order of vanishing.
    BÉZOUT: projective curves of degrees m, n without common component meet
    in exactly mn points counted with multiplicity.
  - GENUS OF PLANE CURVES: a smooth plane curve of degree d has genus
    g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 (0, 0, 1, 3, 6, 10, ...: no smooth plane curve of
    genus 2); an irreducible curve with singular points has geometric genus
    g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 − ∑δ_P, with δ = 1 for a node or ordinary cusp,
    δ = 2 for a tacnode or y² = x⁵, δ = m(m − 1)/2 for an ordinary m-fold
    point. g = 0 ⇔ RATIONAL (parametrisable): conics, nodal and cuspidal
    cubics (lines through the singular point).
  - RIEMANN SURFACES: a connected Hausdorff space with charts to C whose
    transition maps are holomorphic. Examples: C, the Riemann sphere
    Ĉ = P¹ (charts z and 1/z), complex tori C/Λ, smooth affine and projective
    curves (implicit function theorem: x is a local coordinate where
    F_y ≠ 0), the surface of √z. Compact Riemann surfaces are orientable
    surfaces with g handles: Euler characteristic χ = V − E + F = 2 − 2g.
  - HOLOMORPHIC MAPS f : X → Y between compact Riemann surfaces: constant or
    surjective, open, with LOCAL NORMAL FORM z ↦ z^e (e = e_p the
    RAMIFICATION INDEX); the DEGREE n = ∑_{p∈f⁻¹(q)} e_p is the same for
    every q. Meromorphic functions on X = holomorphic maps X → P¹ (plus ∞);
    a compact X has no non-constant holomorphic functions (maximum
    principle), and a meromorphic function has as many zeros as poles.
  - RIEMANN-HURWITZ: 2g_X − 2 = n(2g_Y − 2) + ∑_{p∈X}(e_p − 1). For a rational
    function of degree n on P¹, ∑(e_p − 1) = 2n − 2. HYPERELLIPTIC curves
    y² = f(x), f squarefree of degree 2g + 1 or 2g + 2, have genus g (2g + 2
    branch points of x); yⁿ = f(x) and Fermat curves xⁿ + yⁿ = 1 by the same
    method. Unramified covers: g_X − 1 = n(g_Y − 1).
  - DIVISORS: D = ∑ n_P P (finite sums), deg D = ∑ n_P; div f = zeros minus
    poles (with orders), deg div f = 0; principal divisors, LINEAR
    EQUIVALENCE D ~ D′ ⇔ D − D′ = div f; effective D ≥ 0. MEROMORPHIC
    DIFFERENTIALS ω = h(z) dz; div ω is well defined; any two differ by a
    function, so all CANONICAL divisors K = div ω are linearly equivalent;
    residue theorem ∑ Res_P ω = 0. On P¹, div dz = −2∞.
  - L(D) = {f : div f + D ≥ 0} ∪ {0}, ℓ(D) = dim L(D) (finite). ℓ(0) = 1,
    ℓ(D) = 0 if deg D < 0, ℓ(D) ≤ deg D + 1. On P¹, ℓ(n∞) = n + 1 (basis
    1, z, ..., zⁿ).
  - RIEMANN-ROCH: ℓ(D) − ℓ(K − D) = deg D + 1 − g. Consequences: ℓ(K) = g
    (= dimension of the space of holomorphic differentials), deg K = 2g − 2,
    ℓ(D) = deg D + 1 − g if deg D > 2g − 2; genus 0 ⇒ X ≅ P¹; genus 1 ⇒ X is
    a cubic y² = 4x³ − g₂x − g₃ ≅ C/Λ; genus 2 ⇒ hyperelliptic.
  - ELLIPTIC CURVES AND FUNCTIONS: the Weierstrass ℘(z) = 1/z² + ∑′(1/(z − ω)²
    − 1/ω²) is even, Λ-periodic with a double pole; (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃;
    z ↦ (℘(z), ℘′(z)) identifies C/Λ with a smooth cubic. An elliptic
    function has as many zeros as poles, residues summing to 0, and at
    least 2 poles. j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ + 27b²) for y² = x³ + ax + b.
  - DIFFERENTIALS IN PRACTICE: on y² = f(x) of genus g a basis of holomorphic
    differentials is x^k dx/y (0 ≤ k ≤ g − 1); on a smooth plane curve of
    degree d a basis is P(x, y) dx/F_y with deg P ≤ d − 3; on C/Λ it is dz.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Find the projective closure and the points at infinity of (a) the
    cubic y² = x³ − x, (b) the hyperbola xy = 1, (c) the circle
    x² + y² = 1, and check that (a) is smooth at infinity. Explain why
    every circle passes through [1 : i : 0] and [1 : −i : 0].
    Answer: (a) y²z = x³ − xz², one point [0 : 1 : 0], smooth; (b) xy = z²,
    points [1 : 0 : 0] and [0 : 1 : 0]; (c) x² + y² = z², points [1 : ±i : 0].
    Teacher explains: HOMOGENISE: multiply each term by the power of z that
    brings it up to the degree d. For (a), d = 3: y²z = x³ − xz². At infinity
    put z = 0: 0 = x³, so x = 0 and the only point is [0 : 1 : 0] (y ≠ 0
    after scaling). To test smoothness go to the chart y = 1, where the
    curve is z = x³ − xz², i.e. g(x, z) = z − x³ + xz² = 0; at (0, 0),
    ∂g/∂z = 1 ≠ 0, so the point is smooth (its tangent is z = 0, the line at
    infinity, met with multiplicity 3: it is a FLEX). (b) xy = z²; at z = 0,
    xy = 0: [1 : 0 : 0] and [0 : 1 : 0], the directions of the two
    asymptotes. (c) x² + y² = z²; at z = 0, x² + y² = 0, i.e. y = ±ix: two
    complex points. A general circle x² + y² + axz + byz + cz² = 0 has the
    same top-degree part x² + y², so it passes through the same two points.
    This explains why two circles meet in at most 2 finite points although
    Bézout promises 4 (see D3). Common mistakes: homogenising by
    substituting x/z only and forgetting to clear denominators; looking for
    points at infinity with z = 0 AND x = 0 AND y = 0 (not a point of P²).
    Now you try: the points at infinity of x² − y² = 1 and of x³ + y³ = 1.
    ([1 : ±1 : 0]; [1 : −1 : 0], [1 : −ω : 0], [1 : −ω² : 0], ω = e^(2πi/3))

D2. Find all singular points (including at infinity) of the lemniscate
    (x² + y²)² = x² − y², and the type of the singularity at the origin.
    Deduce its genus from the degree formula and confirm with a rational
    parametrisation.
    Answer: singular points (0, 0), [1 : i : 0], [1 : −i : 0], all nodes;
    g = 3 − 3 = 0; x = t(1 + t²)/(1 + t⁴), y = t(1 − t²)/(1 + t⁴).
    Teacher explains: Affine part: f = (x² + y²)² − x² + y², with
       f_x = 4x(x² + y²) − 2x = 2x(2(x² + y²) − 1),
       f_y = 4y(x² + y²) + 2y = 2y(2(x² + y²) + 1).
    If x ≠ 0 and y ≠ 0 we would need x² + y² = 1/2 and = −1/2: impossible.
    If x = 0: f_y = 0 needs y = 0 or y² = −1/2, and f = y⁴ + y² = 0 then
    forces y = 0. If y = 0 similarly x = 0. So the only affine singular
    point is the origin. Its lowest-order terms are −x² + y², so the
    multiplicity is 2 and the tangent cone y² = x² splits into y = ±x: a
    NODE (the figure-eight crossing). At infinity: F = (x² + y²)² −
    z²(x² − y²), and z = 0 gives (x² + y²)² = 0: the circular points
    [1 : ±i : 0], each a DOUBLE root, which already suggests singularity.
    There every partial derivative vanishes (F_x, F_y contain the factor
    x² + y², F_z contains z), and in local coordinates the tangent cone is
    a pair of distinct lines: two more nodes. Degree 4 gives
    (4 − 1)(4 − 2)/2 = 3, minus δ = 1 for each node: g = 0. Rational
    check: x = t(1 + t²)/(1 + t⁴), y = t(1 − t²)/(1 + t⁴) give
    x² + y² = 2t²(1 + t⁴)/(1 + t⁴)² = 2t²/(1 + t⁴) and
    x² − y² = 4t⁴/(1 + t⁴)², so (x² + y²)² = x² − y² ✓.
    Common mistakes: forgetting singular points at infinity (the genus
    would come out as 2); calling the origin a cusp (a cusp has ONE
    tangent). Now you try: the singular points of y² = x²(x + 1) and their
    type. (only (0, 0), a node with tangents y = ±x; genus 0)

D3. Define intersection multiplicity through its computable properties and
    state Bézout's theorem. Find all intersections, with multiplicities, of
    (a) the circles x² + y² = 1 and x² + y² = 2x; (b) the circles
    x² + y² = 1 and x² + y² = 4; (c) the cubic y² = x³ and the line y = 0.
    Answer: (a) (1/2, ±√3/2) and [1 : ±i : 0], each of multiplicity 1;
    (b) only [1 : ±i : 0], each of multiplicity 2; (c) only [0 : 0 : 1],
    multiplicity 3. Totals 4, 4, 3 = mn.
    Teacher explains: I_P(F, G) is the unique function with: I_P = 0 if P
    is not on both; I_P = 1 if F and G are smooth at P with different
    tangents; symmetric; I_P(F, GH) = I_P(F, G) + I_P(F, H); and
    I_P(F, G) = I_P(F, G + AF). In practice: subtract multiples to simplify,
    and when one curve is a line (or parametrised by t) substitute and
    take the order of vanishing at P. BÉZOUT: ∑_P I_P(F, G) = deg F·deg G.
    (a) Homogeneous forms F = x² + y² − z², G = x² + y² − 2xz. G − F =
    z² − 2xz = z(z − 2x), so I(F, G) = I(F, z) + I(F, z − 2x). The line
    z = 2x, i.e. x = 1/2, meets the circle at (1/2, ±√3/2); the line z = 0
    meets it at [1 : ±i : 0]; all transversally: 4 points of multiplicity
    1. (b) G − F = −3z², so I_P(F, G) = 2·I_P(F, z): the circles are
    TANGENT at both circular points, and each has multiplicity 2: total 4,
    although no finite point is common. (c) Homogenise: y²z = x³, y = 0:
    x³ = 0, so only [0 : 0 : 1] with multiplicity 3 (the line is the cusp
    tangent). Common mistakes: counting only real affine points and
    "disproving" Bézout; forgetting multiplicities at tangencies. Now you
    try: y = x² and y = x² + 1. (no affine point; at [0 : 1 : 0]
    I = 2·I(yz − x², z) = 2·2 = 4)

D4. State the genus formula for plane curves. Compute the genus of the
    Fermat curves xᵈ + yᵈ = zᵈ for d = 3, 4, 5, and of a quartic with one
    node. Parametrise the nodal cubic y² = x³ + x² and the cuspidal cubic
    y² = x³ by lines through the singular point.
    Answer: g = 1, 3, 6; quartic with a node: 2; nodal cubic:
    x = t² − 1, y = t(t² − 1); cuspidal cubic: x = t², y = t³.
    Teacher explains: For a smooth plane curve of degree d,
    g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 (proof by Riemann-Hurwitz: project from a point
    not on the curve; see D8). The Fermat curve is smooth (the partials dxᵈ⁻¹,
    dyᵈ⁻¹, −dzᵈ⁻¹ vanish together only at x = y = z = 0), so g = 1, 3, 6 for
    d = 3, 4, 5. Each node or cusp lowers the genus by 1: a quartic with one
    node has g = 3 − 1 = 2. Nodal cubic: every line y = tx through the node
    meets the cubic at the node with multiplicity 2, so by Bézout in
    exactly ONE further point. Substitute: t²x² = x³ + x², so x²(x + 1 − t²)
    = 0 and x = t² − 1, y = tx = t(t² − 1). Check: y² = t²(t² − 1)² and
    x²(x + 1) = (t² − 1)²t² ✓. The parameters t = ±1 both map to the node
    (the two branches with slopes ±1). Cuspidal: y = tx gives t²x² = x³,
    x = t², y = t³. A curve with a rational parametrisation has genus 0.
    Common mistakes: applying (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 to a singular curve;
    forgetting singularities at infinity. Now you try: the genus of a
    quintic with 2 nodes and 1 cusp, and of a smooth sextic. (6 − 3 = 3;
    10)

D5. Define a Riemann surface. Give the atlas of the Riemann sphere and of
    a torus C/Λ, and show that a smooth affine curve f(x, y) = 0 is a
    Riemann surface. For the circle x² + y² = 1 over C say which
    coordinate is a local coordinate near (1, 0) and near (0, 1).
    Answer: P¹: charts z on C and w = 1/z on Ĉ ∖ {0}, transition w = 1/z on
    C ∖ {0}; C/Λ: local inverses of the projection, transitions z ↦ z + ω;
    circle: y near (1, 0), x near (0, 1).
    Teacher explains: A RIEMANN SURFACE is a connected Hausdorff (second
    countable) space X with an open cover by sets Uₐ and homeomorphisms
    φₐ : Uₐ → Vₐ ⊆ C such that every TRANSITION φ_β ∘ φₐ⁻¹ is holomorphic
    where defined. So "holomorphic" makes sense on X. The sphere Ĉ = C ∪ {∞}
    uses U₁ = C with z and U₂ = Ĉ ∖ {0} with w = 1/z (w(∞) = 0); on the
    overlap C ∖ {0} the transition z ↦ 1/z is holomorphic. The torus: the
    projection π : C → C/Λ restricted to a small disc (containing no two
    points differing by a lattice vector) is a homeomorphism onto its
    image; two such inverse charts differ by a translation z ↦ z + ω,
    holomorphic. A curve: at a point where f_y ≠ 0 the holomorphic
    implicit function theorem writes the curve as y = h(x) near the point,
    so x is a chart; where f_x ≠ 0, y is a chart; on overlaps the
    transition is x ↦ h(x) or its inverse, holomorphic. Smoothness is
    exactly what guarantees one of the two works. For the circle f_x = 2x,
    f_y = 2y: at (1, 0), f_y = 0 but f_x ≠ 0, so y is the coordinate
    (x = √(1 − y²) near there); at (0, 1), x is the coordinate. Common
    mistakes: using x as a coordinate at a branch point (x is 2-to-1 near
    (1, 0)); forgetting to check that transitions are holomorphic, not just
    continuous. Now you try: on w² = z (the surface of √z), which
    function is a coordinate near (0, 0)? (w, since z = w²; z itself is
    not)

D6. Prove the local normal form: a non-constant holomorphic map f : X → Y
    looks like z ↦ z^e near each point. Deduce that ∑_{p∈f⁻¹(q)} e_p is
    independent of q. For f(z) = z³ − 3z as a map P¹ → P¹ find the degree,
    the ramification points and their indices, and the fibre over 2.
    Answer: degree 3; ramification at z = 1 (e = 2, value −2), z = −1
    (e = 2, value 2) and ∞ (e = 3); f⁻¹(2) = {−1 (e = 2), 2 (e = 1)}.
    Teacher explains: In charts centred at p and f(p), f(z) = z^e h(z) with
    h(0) ≠ 0 (e = order of vanishing of f(z) − f(p)). Near 0, h has a
    holomorphic e-th root: h = k^e. The new coordinate ζ = z·k(z) has
    ζ′(0) ≠ 0, so it is a chart, and f = ζ^e: NORMAL FORM. Hence near p
    every nearby value q′ ≠ f(p) has exactly e preimages close to p. Let
    d(q) = ∑ e_p over f⁻¹(q); this is locally constant (each p contributes
    e_p preimages to every nearby value, and no preimages come from
    elsewhere since X is compact), hence constant on connected Y: the
    DEGREE n. For z³ − 3z: degree 3 (a polynomial of degree 3 has 3
    roots with multiplicity for each value, and ∞ ↦ ∞ with e = 3).
    Finite ramification where f′(z) = 3z² − 3 = 0: z = ±1, e = 2, with
    f(1) = −2 and f(−1) = 2. Fibre over 2: z³ − 3z − 2 = (z + 1)²(z − 2),
    so z = −1 counts twice: 2 + 1 = 3 = n ✓. Total ramification
    ∑(e − 1) = 1 + 1 + 2 = 4 = 2·3 − 2, as Riemann-Hurwitz predicts for
    P¹ → P¹. Common mistakes: forgetting ramification at ∞ (for a
    polynomial of degree n, e_∞ = n); thinking the degree changes over
    branch values. Now you try: the ramification of z + 1/z. (degree 2;
    z = ±1 with e = 2, values ±2)

D7. State and prove the Riemann-Hurwitz formula using triangulations. Use
    it to show that the hyperelliptic curve y² = f(x), f squarefree of
    degree 2g + 1 or 2g + 2, has genus g, and compute the genus of
    y² = x⁵ − x and of y² = x⁶ − 1.
    Answer: 2g_X − 2 = n(2g_Y − 2) + ∑(e_p − 1); both curves have genus 2.
    Teacher explains: Triangulate Y so that every branch value is a vertex
    (V, E, F). Lift through f: every open edge and face is covered n times
    (away from branch values f is an n-sheeted covering), so X gets nE
    edges and nF faces; above a vertex q there are n − ∑_{p↦q}(e_p − 1)
    points. So
       χ(X) = nV − ∑(e_p − 1) − nE + nF = nχ(Y) − ∑(e_p − 1),
    and with χ = 2 − 2g this is 2g_X − 2 = n(2g_Y − 2) + ∑(e_p − 1). □
    Hyperelliptic: x : X → P¹ has degree 2. Over a root aᵢ of f there is one
    point (y = 0) with e = 2; over other finite x, two points. At ∞: if
    deg f is ODD, y² ~ x^(odd) has one branch (e = 2); if EVEN, two points.
    So the branch points number 2g + 2 in both cases (2g + 1 roots + ∞, or
    2g + 2 roots), and
       2g_X − 2 = 2(−2) + (2g + 2) = 2g − 2  ⇒  g_X = g.
    y² = x⁵ − x = x(x − 1)(x + 1)(x − i)(x + i): 5 roots + ∞ = 6 branch
    points, g = 2. y² = x⁶ − 1: the 6 sixth roots of unity, ∞ unbranched,
    g = 2. Note the plane quintic y² = x⁵ − x is singular at infinity (so the
    degree formula would wrongly give 6): Riemann-Hurwitz works on the
    smooth compact model. Common mistakes: forgetting ∞ as a branch point
    when deg f is odd; using the degree formula for singular models. Now
    you try: the genus of y² = x⁷ + 1 and of y² = x⁸ − 1. (3; 3)

D8. Use Riemann-Hurwitz to find the genus of the Fermat curve
    xᵈ + yᵈ = zᵈ via the map [x : y : z] ↦ [x : z], and check that it agrees
    with the degree formula. Give the Euler characteristic and the number
    of handles for d = 4 and d = 5.
    Answer: g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2; d = 4: g = 3, χ = −4; d = 5: g = 6,
    χ = −10.
    Teacher explains: The map is defined everywhere ([0 : 1 : 0] is not on
    the curve since 1 ≠ 0). Degree: over [x₀ : z₀] the points are the
    solutions of yᵈ = z₀ᵈ − x₀ᵈ: d of them if z₀ᵈ ≠ x₀ᵈ, and a single point
    y = 0 with e = d if z₀ᵈ = x₀ᵈ, i.e. at the d values x₀/z₀ = ζ (d-th roots
    of unity). No other ramification (z = 0 gives xᵈ + yᵈ = 0: d distinct
    points). So
       2g − 2 = d(0 − 2) + d(d − 1) = d² − 3d,   g = (d² − 3d + 2)/2
             = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2,
    matching the degree formula. The same argument for any smooth plane
    curve of degree d, projected from a general point, has d(d − 1)
    simple ramification points (tangent lines through the point) and gives
    the genus formula. d = 4: g = 3, χ = 2 − 6 = −4 (a sphere with 3
    handles); d = 5: g = 6, χ = −10. Common mistakes: counting a totally
    ramified point as contributing e instead of e − 1; forgetting to check
    the points at infinity. Now you try: the genus of yᵈ = xᵈ − 1 using
    the map to x (the same curve in another chart). ((d − 1)(d − 2)/2)

D9. On P¹ find div f for f = (z − 1)²/z³ and div(dz). On the elliptic curve
    E : y² = (x − e₁)(x − e₂)(x − e₃) (distinct eᵢ) with points Pᵢ = (eᵢ, 0)
    and O at infinity, find div(x − e₁), div y, div dx and div(dx/y), and
    deduce that the genus of E is 1.
    Answer: div f = 2[1] + [∞] − 3[0]; div dz = −2[∞];
    div(x − e₁) = 2P₁ − 2O, div y = P₁ + P₂ + P₃ − 3O,
    div dx = P₁ + P₂ + P₃ − 3O, div(dx/y) = 0, so deg K = 0 = 2g − 2.
    Teacher explains: On P¹, at ∞ use w = 1/z: f = (1/w − 1)²w³ =
    w(1 − w)², a simple ZERO at ∞. So div f = 2[1] − 3[0] + [∞], degree 0.
    dz = d(1/w) = −dw/w²: a double pole at ∞ and no zeros, so
    div dz = −2[∞] and deg K = −2 = 2·0 − 2. On E: near Pᵢ the local
    coordinate is y (because f_x ≠ 0 there), and x − eᵢ = y²/((x − eⱼ)(x −
    e_k)) vanishes to order 2. Near O use t = x/y: x has a pole of order 2,
    y of order 3 (compare: y² ~ x³). So div(x − e₁) = 2P₁ − 2O and div y =
    P₁ + P₂ + P₃ − 3O (degree 0 ✓). dx: at a finite point with y ≠ 0, x is
    a coordinate, dx ≠ 0; at Pᵢ, dx = d(x − eᵢ) has order 2 − 1 = 1; at O,
    x ~ t⁻² gives dx ~ −2t⁻³ dt, order −3. Hence div dx = P₁ + P₂ + P₃ − 3O
    and div(dx/y) = 0: dx/y is a holomorphic differential WITHOUT zeros.
    Therefore K ~ 0, deg K = 0 = 2g − 2 and g = 1; the space of
    holomorphic differentials is C·dx/y (ℓ(K) = ℓ(0) = 1 = g). Common
    mistakes: using x as a local coordinate at Pᵢ (it is 2-to-1 there);
    forgetting that orders of differentials change by −1 under e = 2
    ramification. Now you try: div((x − e₁)/y) on E. (P₁ − P₂ − P₃ + O)

D10. State the Riemann-Roch theorem and derive: ℓ(K) = g, deg K = 2g − 2,
     ℓ(D) = deg D + 1 − g for deg D > 2g − 2, and "g = 0 ⇒ X ≅ P¹". For a
     curve of genus 2 tabulate ℓ(D) for deg D = −1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5.
     Answer: ℓ(D) − ℓ(K − D) = deg D + 1 − g. Genus 2: deg −1: 0; deg 0: 1
     if D ~ 0, else 0; deg 1: 1 if D is effective, else 0; deg 2: 2 if
     D ~ K, else 1; deg 3, 4, 5: 2, 3, 4.
     Teacher explains: D = 0: ℓ(0) − ℓ(K) = 1 − g; since ℓ(0) = 1
     (holomorphic functions on compact X are constant), ℓ(K) = g. D = K:
     ℓ(K) − ℓ(0) = deg K + 1 − g, so g − 1 = deg K + 1 − g, deg K = 2g − 2.
     If deg D > 2g − 2 then deg(K − D) < 0, so ℓ(K − D) = 0 and
     ℓ(D) = deg D + 1 − g. If g = 0: ℓ(P) = 1 + 1 − 0 = 2, so there is a
     non-constant f with at most a simple pole at P: a meromorphic function
     of degree 1, i.e. an isomorphism X → P¹. Genus 2 (deg K = 2):
        deg −1: ℓ = 0 (negative degree).
        deg 0:  ℓ = 1 iff D = div f for some f (D ~ 0), else 0.
        deg 1:  ℓ(D) − ℓ(K − D) = 0 with deg(K − D) = 1; e.g. ℓ(P) = 1 (only
                constants: a function with one simple pole would make X ≅
                P¹).
        deg 2:  ℓ(D) − ℓ(K − D) = 1 with deg(K − D) = 0: ℓ(D) = 2 if D ~ K,
                else 1.
        deg ≥ 3 (> 2g − 2 = 2): ℓ = deg D − 1: 2, 3, 4.
     Common mistakes: using ℓ(D) = deg D + 1 − g below the range
     deg D > 2g − 2; forgetting that ℓ(D) depends only on the linear
     equivalence class of D. Now you try: on a genus-3 curve, ℓ(D) for
     deg D = 5 and ℓ(2K). (3; 3g − 3 = 6)

D11. Let Λ be a lattice and E = C/Λ. Explain why an elliptic function has
     at least two poles, why ℓ(nO) = n for n ≥ 1, and how the functions
     1, ℘, ℘′, ℘², ℘℘′, ℘³, ℘′² in L(6O) force a cubic equation. Find
     the j-invariant of y² = x³ − x and y² = x³ + 1.
     Answer: a single simple pole contradicts ∑ Res = 0; Riemann-Roch with
     g = 1; seven functions in a 6-dimensional space give
     (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃; j = 1728 and 0.
     Teacher explains: For elliptic f, integrate f dz round a period
     parallelogram: opposite sides cancel by periodicity, so ∑ Res = 0 and
     one simple pole is impossible. Holomorphic elliptic functions are
     bounded, hence constant (Liouville). By Riemann-Roch with g = 1 and
     K = 0 (dz is holomorphic without zeros): ℓ(nO) = n for n ≥ 1. Pole
     orders at O: ℘ has 2, ℘′ has 3, so
        L(O) = ⟨1⟩, L(2O) = ⟨1, ℘⟩, L(3O) = ⟨1, ℘, ℘′⟩,
        L(4O) adds ℘², L(5O) adds ℘℘′, L(6O) adds ℘³ AND ℘′².
     Seven functions in a 6-dimensional space are dependent, and the
     relation must involve both ℘′² and ℘³ (the only terms with a 6-fold
     pole). Comparing Laurent expansions ℘ = z⁻² + (g₂/20)z² + ...,
     ℘′ = −2z⁻³ + ... gives (℘′)² = 4℘³ − g₂℘ − g₃. So z ↦ (℘, ℘′) maps E
     onto the smooth cubic y² = 4x³ − g₂x − g₃. For y² = x³ + ax + b,
     j = 1728·4a³/(4a³ + 27b²): a = −1, b = 0 gives 1728·(−4)/(−4) = 1728;
     a = 0, b = 1 gives 0. Common mistakes: forgetting that the relation
     needs ℘′² (so ℓ(6O) = 6, not 7); dividing by 4a³ + 27b² = 0 (then the
     cubic is singular). Now you try: j of y² = x³ − 3x + 3. (1728·(−108)/
     135 = −6912/5)

D12. On the hyperelliptic curve X : y² = f(x), f squarefree of degree
     2g + 1, prove that x^k dx/y (0 ≤ k ≤ g − 1) is a basis of the
     holomorphic differentials, and find their divisors for y² = x⁵ − x.
     Explain why every curve of genus 2 is hyperelliptic.
     Answer: div(dx/y) = (2g − 2)∞; x^k dx/y is holomorphic iff k ≤ g − 1;
     for y² = x⁵ − x: div(dx/y) = 2∞, div(x dx/y) = 2P₀ with P₀ = (0, 0).
     Teacher explains: Points: Pᵢ = (aᵢ, 0) for the 2g + 1 roots, and one
     point ∞ (odd degree). As in D9: at Pᵢ, y is a coordinate and dx has a
     simple zero; y has a simple zero; so dx/y has order 0. At ∞, with a
     coordinate t, x ~ t⁻², y ~ t^(−(2g+1)), dx ~ −2t⁻³ dt, so dx/y ~
     t^(2g+1−3) dt: order 2g − 2. Elsewhere dx/y is holomorphic and non-zero.
     So div(dx/y) = (2g − 2)∞ (degree 2g − 2 ✓). Multiplying by x^k adds k·
     div x, whose pole at ∞ has order 2k; hence x^k dx/y is holomorphic
     iff 2k ≤ 2g − 2, i.e. k ≤ g − 1. These g differentials are linearly
     independent (distinct orders at ∞), and ℓ(K) = g, so they form a
     basis. For y² = x⁵ − x (g = 2): div(dx/y) = 2∞; div x = 2P₀ − 2∞, so
     div(x dx/y) = 2P₀. Genus 2: ℓ(K) = 2 and deg K = 2; for any P,
     ℓ(K − P) = 1 < 2 (Riemann-Roch: ℓ(P) = 1), so |K| has no base points
     and the ratio of two basis differentials ω₂/ω₁ is a meromorphic
     function of degree 2: a double cover X → P¹. Common mistakes: taking
     dx as holomorphic (it has poles at ∞); forgetting that the basis has
     exactly g elements. Now you try: a basis of holomorphic differentials
     on y² = x⁷ − 1. (dx/y, x dx/y, x² dx/y)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Homogenise and give the points at infinity; say whether the curve is
    smooth (S) or singular (N) there.
    a) y = x²                          b) xy = 1
    c) x² + y² = 1                     d) x² − y² = 1
    e) y² = x³ − x                     f) y² = x⁴ − 1
    g) x³ + y³ = 1                     h) y = x³
    i) x²y = 1                         j) y² = x⁵ − x
    k) y = 2x + 1                      l) x⁴ + y⁴ = 1
    m) y² = x²(x + 1)                  n) (x² + y²)² = x² − y²
    o) y² = x⁶ − 1                     p) y³ = x⁴ − 1
    Answers:
      a) yz = x²; [0 : 1 : 0], S       b) xy = z²; [1 : 0 : 0], [0 : 1 : 0], S
      c) x² + y² = z²; [1 : ±i : 0], S d) x² − y² = z²; [1 : ±1 : 0], S
      e) y²z = x³ − xz²; [0 : 1 : 0], S (a flex)
      f) y²z² = x⁴ − z⁴; [0 : 1 : 0], N (tacnode)
      g) x³ + y³ = z³; [1 : −1 : 0], [1 : −ω : 0], [1 : −ω² : 0], S
      h) yz² = x³; [0 : 1 : 0], N (cusp)
      i) x²y = z³; [1 : 0 : 0] S, [0 : 1 : 0] N (cusp)
      j) y²z³ = x⁵ − xz⁴; [0 : 1 : 0], N
      k) y = 2x + z; [1 : 2 : 0], S
      l) x⁴ + y⁴ = z⁴; [1 : ζ : 0] with ζ⁴ = −1 (4 points), S
      m) y²z = x³ + x²z; [0 : 1 : 0], S
      n) (x² + y²)² = z²(x² − y²); [1 : ±i : 0], N (nodes)
      o) y²z⁴ = x⁶ − z⁶; [0 : 1 : 0], N
      p) y³z = x⁴ − z⁴; [0 : 1 : 0], S (the whole curve is smooth)

M2. Find the affine singular points and their type (node, cusp, tacnode,
    ordinary triple point, higher cusp) or write "smooth".
    a) y² = x³ + x²                    b) y² = x³
    c) y² = x³ − x                     d) y² = x²(x − 1)
    e) x³ + y³ = 3xy                   f) y² = x⁵
    g) (x² + y²)² = x² − y²            h) xy(x − y) = x⁴ + y⁴
    i) (y − 1)² = x³                   j) x² + y² = 0
    k) y² = x(x − 1)²                  l) y² = (x − 1)³(x + 2)
    m) y² = x³ + x² + x                n) y² = x³ − 3x + 2
    o) y² = x³ − 3x − 2                p) y² = x⁴ (over C)
    q) y³ = x²                         r) x⁴ + y⁴ = 1
    Answers:
      a) (0, 0), node (y = ±x)          b) (0, 0), cusp (y² = 0)
      c) smooth                         d) (0, 0), node (y = ±ix; an
                                           isolated real point)
      e) (0, 0), node (x = 0, y = 0)    f) (0, 0), higher cusp (δ = 2)
      g) (0, 0), node (y = ±x)
      h) (0, 0), ordinary triple point (x = 0, y = 0, y = x)
      i) (0, 1), cusp                   j) (0, 0), node (y = ±ix)
      k) (1, 0), node (y = ±(x − 1))    l) (1, 0), cusp
      m) smooth (x(x² + x + 1) has distinct roots)
      n) (1, 0), node (y = ±√3(x − 1))  o) (−1, 0), node (y = ±i√3(x + 1))
      p) (0, 0), tacnode (y = ±x²)      q) (0, 0), cusp (x² = 0)
      r) smooth

M3. Intersection multiplicity at the origin I₀(F, G).
    a) y, y − x²                       b) y, y − x³
    c) x, y − x²                       d) y² − x³, y
    e) y² − x³, x                      f) y² − x³, y − x
    g) y² − x² − x³, y                 h) y² − x² − x³, y − x
    i) y − x², y − x³                  j) y² − x³, y² − x⁵
    k) x² + y² − 2y, x² + y² − 4y      l) xy, x − y
    m) xy, y − x²                      n) y² − x³, y² + x³
    o) x³ − y², x² − y³                p) y² − x³, y − x²
    q) y − x², y − 2x²                 r) x² + y² − 2y, y
    Answers:
      a) 2      b) 3      c) 1      d) 3      e) 2      f) 2
      g) 2      h) 3 (y = x is a tangent at the node)   i) 2
      j) 6 (param (t², t³): t⁶ − t¹⁰)  k) 2 (tangent circles)
      l) 2      m) 3      n) 6 (t⁶ + t⁶)
      o) 4 (= 2·2, no common tangent)  p) 3 (t³ − t⁴)
      q) 2      r) 2 (x² = 0)

M4. Bézout: find all intersection points in P² with multiplicities (the
    total must be mn).
    a) y = 0 and y = x²                b) x² + y² = 1 and x² + y² = 4
    c) x² + y² = 1 and x² + y² = 2x    d) y² = x³ and y = 0
    e) y² = x³ and x = 0               f) xy = 1 and y = x
    g) xy = 1 and x = 0                h) y² = x³ − x and y = 0
    i) y² = x³ − x and x = 2           j) y² = x³ − x and y = x
    k) y² = x³ + x² and y = x          l) y² = x³ + x² and y = 2x
    m) y = x² and x = y²               n) y = x² and y = x² + 1
    o) x² − y² = 1 and xy = 0          p) y² = x³ and y² = x³ + 1
    q) x² + y² = 1 and y = 1           r) total for general curves of
                                          degrees 4 and 5
    Answers:
      a) [0 : 0 : 1] (2)
      b) [1 : i : 0] (2), [1 : −i : 0] (2)
      c) (1/2, ±√3/2) (1 each), [1 : ±i : 0] (1 each)
      d) [0 : 0 : 1] (3)                e) [0 : 0 : 1] (2), [0 : 1 : 0] (1)
      f) (1, 1), (−1, −1) (1 each)      g) [0 : 1 : 0] (2)
      h) (0, 0), (1, 0), (−1, 0) (1 each)
      i) (2, ±√6) (1 each), [0 : 1 : 0] (1)
      j) (0, 0), ((1 ± √5)/2, (1 ± √5)/2) (1 each)
      k) (0, 0) (3)                     l) (0, 0) (2), (3, 6) (1)
      m) (0, 0), (1, 1), (ω, ω²), (ω², ω) (1 each)
      n) [0 : 1 : 0] (4)                o) (±1, 0), (0, ±i) (1 each)
      p) [0 : 1 : 0] (9)                q) (0, 1) (2)
      r) 20

M5. Genus from the degree formula.
    a) a line                          b) a smooth conic

M6. Genus of the hyperelliptic curve y² = f(x) (smooth compact model).
    a) y² = x³ − x                     b) y² = x⁴ − 1
    c) y² = x⁵ − x                     d) y² = x⁶ − 1
    e) y² = x⁷ + 1                     f) y² = x⁸ − 1
    g) y² = x(x − 1)(x − 2)(x − 3)(x − 4)
    h) y² = x⁹ − 1                     i) y² = x¹⁰ − 1
    j) y² = x³ (not squarefree)        k) y² = x²(x − 1)(x − 2)(x − 3)
    l) y² = (x² − 1)²(x − 2)           m) y² = x²(x² − 1)

    o) number of branch points of x on y² = x⁶ − 1; is ∞ one?

      l) 0 (w² = x − 2)                 m) 0 (w² = x² − 1, a conic)

      p) 15 or 16

M7. Rational maps P¹ → P¹: give the degree n, the ramification points with
    their indices e, and check ∑(e − 1) = 2n − 2.
    a) z²                              b) z³
    c) zⁿ                              d) z + 1/z
    e) z³ − 3z                         f) z³ − z²
    g) z⁴ − 4z                         h) 1/(z² − 1)
    i) (z − 1)/(z + 1)                 j) z² + 1/z
    k) 4z³ − 3z                        l) z⁵
    m) (z² + 1)/(z² − 1)               n) z²(z − 3)
    Answers:

      c) n; 0, ∞ (e = n): 2n − 2      d) 2; ±1 (e = 2): 2
      e) 3; ±1 (e = 2), ∞ (e = 3): 4
      f) 3; 0, 2/3 (e = 2), ∞ (e = 3): 4
      g) 4; 1, ω, ω² (e = 2), ∞ (e = 4): 6
      h) 2; 0, ∞ (e = 2): 2           i) 1; none: 0
      j) 3; the 3 roots of z³ = 1/2 (e = 2), ∞ (e = 2): 4
      k) 3; ±1/2 (e = 2, values ∓1), ∞ (e = 3): 4
      l) 5; 0, ∞ (e = 5): 8           m) 2; 0, ∞ (e = 2): 2
      n) 3; 0, 2 (e = 2), ∞ (e = 3): 4

M8. Riemann-Hurwitz: find the genus (or the requested quantity).
    a) y² = f(x), deg f = 5, squarefree
    b) y² = f(x), deg f = 6, squarefree
    c) y³ = x⁴ − 1                     d) y³ = x³ − 1
    e) yⁿ = xⁿ − 1                     f) y⁵ = x(x − 1)
    g) y⁴ = x(x − 1)(x − 2)            h) y⁴ = x²(x − 1)
    i) an unramified double cover of a genus-2 curve
    j) an unramified 4-fold cover of a genus-3 curve
    k) a double cover of an elliptic curve branched at 2 points
    l) total ramification of a degree-3 map from a genus-2 curve to P¹
    m) can a genus-1 curve cover a genus-2 curve?
    n) a Galois cover of P¹ with group of order 168 branched over 3 points
       with indices 2, 3, 7
    o) x : E → P¹ on y² = x³ − x: degree and number of branch points
    p) y : E → P¹ on y² = x³ − x: degree and ∑(e − 1)
    Answers:
      a) 2      b) 2      c) 3 (4 roots and ∞, each e = 3)
      d) 1 (∞ unbranched: 3 | 3)       e) (n − 1)(n − 2)/2
      f) 2 (0, 1, ∞ with e = 5)
      g) 3 (0, 1, 2, ∞ with e = 4)
      h) 1 (over 0: two points with e = 2; 1 and ∞: e = 4)
      i) 3      j) 9 (g − 1 = 4·2)       k) 2
      l) 8 (2·2 − 2 = 3(−2) + R)
      m) no (0 ≥ n·2 is impossible)
      n) 3 (2g − 2 = 168(−2 + 1/2 + 2/3 + 6/7) = 4: the Klein quartic)
      o) 2; 4 (0, ±1, ∞)
      p) 3; 6 (4 double points where 27y⁴ = 4, and e = 3 at O)

M9. Divisors of functions and differentials.
    On P¹:
    a) div z                           b) div(z² − 1)
    c) div((z − 1)²/z³)                d) div(1/(z² + 1))
    e) div(z³ − z²)                    f) div dz
    g) div(dz/z)                       h) div(dz/(z² − 1))
    i) the residues of dz/(z² − 1) and their sum
    On E : y² = x³ − x, P₀ = (0, 0), P₁ = (1, 0), P₋₁ = (−1, 0), O = ∞:
    j) div x                           k) div y
    l) div(x − 1)                      m) div(x² − 1)
    n) div dx                          o) div(dx/y)
    p) div(x/y)                        q) div(x − 2)
    r) is P − Q principal on E for P ≠ Q?
    s) deg of div f for any meromorphic f on a compact Riemann surface
    Answers:
      a) [0] − [∞]                     b) [1] + [−1] − 2[∞]
      c) 2[1] + [∞] − 3[0]             d) 2[∞] − [i] − [−i]
      e) 2[0] + [1] − 3[∞]             f) −2[∞]
      g) −[0] − [∞]                    h) −[1] − [−1]
      i) 1/2 at 1, −1/2 at −1; sum 0 (∞ is not a pole)
      j) 2P₀ − 2O                      k) P₀ + P₁ + P₋₁ − 3O
      l) 2P₁ − 2O                      m) 2P₁ + 2P₋₁ − 4O
      n) P₀ + P₁ + P₋₁ − 3O            o) 0
      p) P₀ − P₁ − P₋₁ + O             q) (2, √6) + (2, −√6) − 2O
      r) no (it would give a degree-1 map E → P¹)
      s) 0

M10. Riemann-Roch: compute ℓ(D).
     Genus 0 (P¹):
     a) ℓ(3∞)                          b) ℓ(2[0] + [1])
     c) ℓ(−[0])                        d) ℓ(K)
     e) ℓ(2[0] − [1])                  f) a basis of L(2∞)
     Genus 1 (O a point):
     g) ℓ(O)                           h) ℓ(2O)
     i) ℓ(3O)                          j) ℓ(6O)
     k) ℓ(P − Q), P ≠ Q                l) ℓ(2P₁ − 2O) on y² = x³ − x
     Genus 2:
     m) ℓ(K), deg K                    n) ℓ(P)
     o) ℓ(D), deg D = 3                p) ℓ(D), deg D = 5
     q) ℓ(D), deg D = 2, D ≁ K         r) ℓ(2P), P a Weierstrass point
     Genus 3:
     s) ℓ(D), deg D = 5                t) ℓ(D), deg D = 10
     u) ℓ(K), ℓ(2K)                    v) ℓ(3K) for genus g ≥ 2
     Answers:
       a) 4       b) 4       c) 0       d) 0       e) 2
       f) 1, z, z² (in the coordinate z with pole at ∞)
       g) 1       h) 2 (1, x)           i) 3 (1, x, y)       j) 6
       k) 0       l) 1 (x − 1 has this divisor)
       m) 2, 2    n) 1       o) 2       p) 4       q) 1
       r) 2 (e.g. 1 and 1/(x − a) on y² = f(x) with f(a) = 0)
       s) 3       t) 8       u) 3, 6    v) 5(g − 1)

M11. Euler characteristic, canonical degree, holomorphic differentials.
     a) χ(sphere)                      b) χ(torus)
     c) χ for genus 2                  d) χ for genus 5
     e) deg K and ℓ(K) for genus 4     f) genus of a surface with χ = −4
     g) V − E + F for an icosahedron   h) V − E + F for the 7-vertex torus
                                          (V = 7, E = 21, F = 14)
     i) χ of a double cover of the sphere branched at 6 points
     j) a basis of holomorphic differentials on y² = x⁵ − x
     k) the same on y² = x⁷ − 1        l) the same on x⁴ + y⁴ = 1
     m) the same on y² = x³ − x        n) the same on C/Λ
     o) order of dx/y at ∞ on y² = x⁵ − x
     p) div(x dx/y) on y² = x⁵ − x     q) holomorphic differentials on P¹
     r) deg K on a smooth plane curve of degree d
     Answers:
       a) 2       b) 0       c) −2      d) −8
       e) 6, 4    f) 3       g) 12 − 30 + 20 = 2      h) 0
       i) 2·2 − 6 = −2 (genus 2)        j) dx/y, x dx/y
       k) dx/y, x dx/y, x² dx/y
       l) dx/y³, x dx/y³, y dx/y³ (P dx/F_y with deg P ≤ 1)
       m) dx/y    n) dz      o) 2       p) 2P₀
       q) only 0 (g = 0)                r) d(d − 3)

M12. Elliptic curves and compact Riemann surfaces: compute, or say true
     (T) or false (F).
     a) Every compact Riemann surface of genus 0 is isomorphic to P¹.
     b) A non-constant holomorphic map of compact Riemann surfaces is
        surjective.
     c) A compact Riemann surface has non-constant holomorphic functions.
     d) An elliptic function can have a single simple pole.
     e) ℘ is even with a double pole at each lattice point.
     f) ℘′ vanishes exactly at ω₁/2, ω₂/2, (ω₁ + ω₂)/2 mod Λ.
     g) A holomorphic map of degree 1 between compact Riemann surfaces is
        an isomorphism.
     h) j(y² = x³ − x)                 i) j(y² = x³ + 1)
     j) j(y² = x³ − 3x + 3)            k) is y² = x³ − 3x + 2 smooth?
     l) the number of points of order dividing 2 (resp. n) on C/Λ
     m) the degree of multiplication by 2 on C/Λ
     n) Every smooth plane quartic is hyperelliptic.
     o) Every curve of genus 2 is hyperelliptic.
     p) There is a smooth plane curve of genus 2.
     q) Every smooth cubic is isomorphic to some C/Λ.
     r) An unramified cover of P¹ of degree 2 exists.
     Answers:
       a) T       b) T       c) F       d) F (∑ Res = 0)
       e) T       f) T       g) T       h) 1728
       i) 0       j) −6912/5 (= 1728·(−108)/135)
       k) no (4a³ + 27b² = −108 + 108 = 0: node at (1, 0))
       l) 4; n²   m) 4       n) F (the canonical map embeds it)
       o) T       p) F (genera of smooth plane curves are 0, 1, 3, 6, ...)
       q) T       r) F (2g − 2 = 2(−2) = −4 gives g = −1)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always go through the same three steps — homogenise and find
  the points at infinity, find all singular points (affine AND at
  infinity) with their tangent cones, and only then compute the genus.
  For ramification, draw the fibres over a few values and count
  preimages; for divisors, write the local coordinate at each special
  point (y at a branch point, t with x ~ t⁻² at ∞) before reading off
  orders. Keep the three model curves on one card: P¹ (g = 0, K = −2∞),
  y² = x³ − x (g = 1, K = 0), y² = x⁵ − x (g = 2, K = 2∞). Harder: prove
  the Riemann-Roch theorem via Serre duality or via the Weierstrass gap
  theorem; show that the Weierstrass points of y² = f(x) are exactly the
  2g + 2 branch points; prove Abel's theorem (a degree-0 divisor is
  principal iff its Abel-Jacobi image vanishes) for the torus; find all
  automorphisms of the Klein quartic x³y + y³z + z³x = 0 and check the
  Hurwitz bound 84(g − 1); and compute the genus of the modular curves
  X₀(p) for small p by Riemann-Hurwitz.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.12 — STOCHASTIC PROCESSES: MARKOV CHAINS, POISSON PROCESSES, RANDOM
WALKS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - MARKOV CHAIN on a countable S: P(Xₙ₊₁ = j | Xₙ = i, past) = pᵢⱼ; P is
    STOCHASTIC (rows sum to 1); distributions are ROW vectors, μₙ = μ₀Pⁿ;
    pᵢⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾ = (Pⁿ)ᵢⱼ; CHAPMAN-KOLMOGOROV pᵢⱼ⁽ᵐ⁺ⁿ⁾ = ∑ₖpᵢₖ⁽ᵐ⁾pₖⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾. Two states:
    Pⁿ = Π + (1 − a − b)ⁿ(I − Π) for P = [[1 − a, a], [b, 1 − b]].
  - CLASSIFICATION: i → j (accessible), i ↔ j (communicate), COMMUNICATING
    CLASSES, CLOSED classes, IRREDUCIBLE chains. PERIOD d(i) = gcd{n ≥ 1 :
    pᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ > 0}, a class property; a self-loop makes a class aperiodic.
  - RECURRENCE: fᵢ = P(return to i); RECURRENT (fᵢ = 1) ⇔ ∑ₙpᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ = ∞;
    TRANSIENT: visits are geometric with mean 1/(1 − fᵢ). Class property;
    non-closed classes are transient, finite closed classes recurrent.
    POSITIVE (mean return time mᵢ < ∞) versus NULL recurrence (walk on Z).
  - STATIONARY DISTRIBUTION π = πP, ∑πᵢ = 1: an irreducible chain has one
    iff it is positive recurrent; then it is unique and πᵢ = 1/mᵢ. Several
    closed classes ⇒ many π; transient states have πᵢ = 0; doubly
    stochastic ⇒ uniform. CONVERGENCE (irreducible, aperiodic, positive
    recurrent): pᵢⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾ → πⱼ (coupling), error O(|λ₂|ⁿ) for finite chains.
    ERGODIC THEOREM: (1/n)∑f(Xₖ) → ∑πᵢf(i) a.s., periodic chains included.
  - REVERSIBILITY: DETAILED BALANCE πᵢpᵢⱼ = πⱼpⱼᵢ ⇒ stationary; birth-death
    chains are reversible; RANDOM WALK ON A GRAPH: πᵥ = deg(v)/(2|E|);
    KOLMOGOROV's cycle criterion.
  - FIRST-STEP ANALYSIS: hitting probabilities hᵢ = ∑ⱼpᵢⱼhⱼ (hᵢ = 1 on A;
    the MINIMAL non-negative solution), expected times kᵢ = 1 + ∑ⱼpᵢⱼkⱼ
    (kᵢ = 0 on A). ABSORBING chains: N = (I − Q)⁻¹, B = NR, t = N𝟏. Pattern
    waiting times (HH 6, HT 4, HHH 14).
  - GAMBLER'S RUIN: P(reach N from i) = (1 − (q/p)ⁱ)/(1 − (q/p)ᴺ) (p ≠ q),
    i/N (p = q); duration i(N − i) (fair), i/(q − p) − (N/(q − p))hᵢ
    (unfair); against an infinitely rich opponent P(ruin) = min(1, (q/p)ⁱ).
  - BRANCHING (GALTON-WATSON): offspring pgf G, mean m, variance σ²; Zₙ has
    pgf G∘...∘G, E Zₙ = mⁿ, Var Zₙ = σ²mⁿ⁻¹(mⁿ − 1)/(m − 1); EXTINCTION
    probability q = smallest root of G(s) = s in [0, 1]; q = 1 ⇔ m ≤ 1
    (p₁ < 1); with Z₀ = k it is qᵏ; P(Zₙ = 0) = Gₙ(0) ↑ q.
  - POISSON PROCESS PP(λ), equivalent definitions: independent increments
    with N(t + s) − N(s) ~ Po(λt); i.i.d. Exp(λ) gaps; P(1 arrival in
    (t, t + h]) = λh + o(h), P(≥ 2) = o(h). Tₙ ~ Gamma(n, λ); MEMORYLESS;
    given N(t) = n the arrival times are n sorted U(0, t) points.
  - THINNING (keep w.p. p): independent PP(λp), PP(λ(1 − p)); SUPERPOSITION
    PP(λ) + PP(μ) = PP(λ + μ), a point is of type 1 w.p. λ/(λ + μ);
    NON-HOMOGENEOUS rate λ(t): N(t) ~ Po(∫₀ᵗλ); COMPOUND Poisson: E S =
    λt E Y, Var S = λt E Y².
  - CONTINUOUS-TIME CHAINS: generator Q (off-diagonal ≥ 0, rows sum 0),
    holding times Exp(qᵢ), jump chain qᵢⱼ/qᵢ, P(t) = e^(tQ) (BACKWARD
    P′ = QP, FORWARD P′ = PQ), stationary πQ = 0. Birth-death: πₙ =
    π₀λ₀...λₙ₋₁/(μ₁...μₙ); M/M/1: πₙ = (1 − ρ)ρⁿ, L = ρ/(1 − ρ); LITTLE
    L = λW; M/M/∞: Po(λ/μ).
  - RANDOM WALKS: u₂ₙ = P(S₂ₙ = 0) = C(2n, n)/4ⁿ ~ 1/√(πn). PÓLYA: symmetric
    walk on Zᵈ recurrent (null) for d ≤ 2, transient for d ≥ 3 (return
    probability ≈ 0.3405 in Z³). Asymmetric: return probability
    1 − |p − q|, P(reach +1) = min(1, p/q), mean time 1/(p − q) if p > q.
  - PATH COUNTING: REFLECTION PRINCIPLE; BALLOT theorem (a − b)/(a + b);
    P(max_{k≤n}Sₖ ≥ r) = P(Sₙ ≥ r) + P(Sₙ ≥ r + 1); first passage P(Tᵣ = n) =
    (r/n)P(Sₙ = r); first return f₂ₙ = u₂ₙ/(2n − 1); P(no zero up to 2n) =
    u₂ₙ; Catalan numbers; the ARCSINE law.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. The weather is a Markov chain on {1 = dry, 2 = wet} with
    P = [[0.7, 0.3], [0.4, 0.6]]. (a) State the Markov property and prove
    the Chapman-Kolmogorov equations. (b) Compute P², p₁₁⁽³⁾, and P(X₂ = 2)
    when X₀ is dry or wet with probability 1/2 each. (c) Diagonalise P, find
    Pⁿ and lim Pⁿ.
    Answer: P² = [[0.61, 0.39], [0.52, 0.48]], p₁₁⁽³⁾ = 0.583,
    P(X₂ = 2) = 0.435; Pⁿ = (1/7)[[4 + 3(0.3)ⁿ, 3 − 3(0.3)ⁿ],
    [4 − 4(0.3)ⁿ, 3 + 4(0.3)ⁿ]] → [[4/7, 3/7], [4/7, 3/7]].
    Teacher explains: MARKOV PROPERTY: given the present state, the future
    does not depend on the past. Chapman-Kolmogorov: split on the state
    at time m and use the law of total probability,
       P(Xₘ₊ₙ = j | X₀ = i) = ∑ₖ P(Xₘ = k | X₀ = i)·P(Xₘ₊ₙ = j | Xₘ = k, X₀ = i)
                           = ∑ₖ pᵢₖ⁽ᵐ⁾ pₖⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾    (Markov property + homogeneity)
    i.e. P⁽ᵐ⁺ⁿ⁾ = P⁽ᵐ⁾P⁽ⁿ⁾, and by induction P⁽ⁿ⁾ = Pⁿ. Numbers:
       row 1 of P²: (0.7·0.7 + 0.3·0.4, 0.7·0.3 + 0.3·0.6) = (0.61, 0.39)
       row 2 of P²: (0.4·0.7 + 0.6·0.4, 0.4·0.3 + 0.6·0.6) = (0.52, 0.48)
       p₁₁⁽³⁾ = 0.61·0.7 + 0.39·0.4 = 0.427 + 0.156 = 0.583
       μ₂ = (1/2, 1/2)P² = (0.565, 0.435), so P(X₂ = 2) = 0.435.
    (c) trace 1.3, det 0.42 − 0.12 = 0.3, so the eigenvalues are 1 and 0.3
    (always 1 and 1 − a − b for two states, here a = 0.3, b = 0.4). Left
    eigenvector for 1: π = (b, a)/(a + b) = (4/7, 3/7). Writing
    Pⁿ = Π + 0.3ⁿ(I − Π) with Π = [[4/7, 3/7], [4/7, 3/7]] gives the formula
    (check n = 1: (4 + 0.9)/7 = 0.7 ✓). As 0.3ⁿ → 0 every row tends to π:
    the chain forgets where it started. Common mistakes: multiplying a
    COLUMN vector by P (distributions are rows); taking (pᵢⱼ)ⁿ instead of
    (Pⁿ)ᵢⱼ. Now you try: P = [[0.5, 0.5], [0.2, 0.8]]: find p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾.
    (2/7 + (5/7)(0.3)ⁿ)

D2. A chain on {1, ..., 6} has p₁₁ = p₁₂ = 1/2, p₂₁ = p₂₃ = 1/2, p₃₄ = p₄₅ =
    p₅₃ = 1 and p₆₁ = p₆₅ = p₆₆ = 1/3. Find the communicating classes, say
    which are closed, recurrent or transient, find their periods, and
    compute the return probabilities f₁ and f₆. Prove that recurrence and
    period are class properties.
    Answer: {1, 2} transient, aperiodic; {3, 4, 5} closed, recurrent,
    period 3; {6} transient, aperiodic. f₁ = 3/4, f₆ = 1/3.
    Teacher explains: Draw the arrow diagram first:
       6 → 1, 5, 6     1 ⇄ 2     2 → 3 → 4 → 5 → 3
    1 and 2 communicate; 3, 4, 5 communicate; 6 is reachable from nothing
    else. {3, 4, 5} is closed (no arrow leaves) and finite, so recurrent;
    every return to 3 takes 3, 6, 9, ... steps: period 3. {1, 2} is not
    closed (2 → 3 and there is no way back) so it is transient; the loop
    at 1 gives period 1. {6} has a loop: aperiodic, transient. Return
    probabilities by first step: from 1, return at once w.p. 1/2, or go to 2
    (1/2) and then back to 1 (1/2): f₁ = 1/2 + 1/4 = 3/4 < 1, so the number
    of visits to 1 is geometric with mean 1/(1 − 3/4) = 4. f₆ = p₆₆ = 1/3.
    Class properties: if i ↔ j choose r, s with pⱼᵢ⁽ʳ⁾ > 0, pᵢⱼ⁽ˢ⁾ > 0. Then
       pⱼⱼ⁽ʳ⁺ⁿ⁺ˢ⁾ ≥ pⱼᵢ⁽ʳ⁾ pᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ pᵢⱼ⁽ˢ⁾,
    so ∑ₙpᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ = ∞ ⇒ ∑ₙpⱼⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾ = ∞ (recurrence passes from i to j). With n = 0,
    d(j) divides r + s; for any n with pᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ > 0, d(j) divides r + n + s,
    hence divides n; so d(j) | d(i), and by symmetry d(i) = d(j). Common
    mistakes: calling {1, 2} recurrent because 1 ⇄ 2 (it leaks to 3); giving
    a period to a state that is never revisited. Now you try: p₁₂ = 1,
    p₂₁ = p₂₃ = 1/2, p₃₃ = 1. ({1, 2} transient, period 2; {3} absorbing)

D3. State the existence-uniqueness theorem for stationary distributions and
    the convergence theorem. For P = [[0, 1/2, 1/2], [1/3, 1/3, 1/3],
    [1/2, 0, 1/2]] find π, the mean return times and lim p₂₁⁽ⁿ⁾. Explain why
    πᵢ = 1/mᵢ and why aperiodicity matters.
    Answer: π = (4/13, 3/13, 6/13); m₁ = 13/4, m₂ = 13/3, m₃ = 13/6;
    p₂₁⁽ⁿ⁾ → 4/13.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: an irreducible chain has a stationary
    distribution iff it is positive recurrent; it is then unique and
    πᵢ = 1/mᵢ. If moreover it is aperiodic, pᵢⱼ⁽ⁿ⁾ → πⱼ for every i. Solve
    π = πP column by column, then normalise:
       π₂ = π₁/2 + π₂/3          ⇒ π₂ = (3/4)π₁
       π₃ = π₁/2 + π₂/3 + π₃/2   ⇒ π₃/2 = π₁/2 + π₁/4 ⇒ π₃ = (3/2)π₁
       π₁(1 + 3/4 + 3/2) = 1     ⇒ π₁ = 4/13, π₂ = 3/13, π₃ = 6/13.
    (Check the first column: π₂/3 + π₃/2 = 1/13 + 3/13 = 4/13 ✓.) The chain
    is irreducible and finite, and aperiodic (p₂₂ > 0), so p₂₁⁽ⁿ⁾ → 4/13.
    Why πᵢ = 1/mᵢ: the time is cut into excursions from i, of mean length
    mᵢ; by the law of large numbers the fraction of time at i is 1/mᵢ, and
    by the ergodic theorem it is also πᵢ. Why aperiodicity: for
    P = [[0, 1], [1, 0]], π = (1/2, 1/2) but p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾ = 0, 1, 0, 1, ... has no
    limit; only the Cesàro averages converge. The convergence proof runs
    two copies, one started from π, until they meet (COUPLING);
    aperiodicity makes the product chain irreducible, so they do meet.
    Common mistakes: solving πP = π without the equation ∑πᵢ = 1 (the
    system is singular); using P columns as if they summed to 1. Now you
    try: P = [[1/3, 1/3, 1/3], [1/2, 1/2, 0], [1, 0, 0]]. (π = (1/2, 1/3, 1/6))

D4. (a) Show that detailed balance implies stationarity. (b) For random
    walk on a connected graph show πᵥ = deg(v)/(2|E|). (c) A knight moves at
    random on an 8×8 board (each legal move equally likely). Starting in a
    corner, what is the expected number of moves until it returns? (d) The
    Ehrenfest urn: N balls in two urns, at each step a uniformly chosen ball
    changes urn; Xₙ = number in urn 1. Find π and the mean return time to 0.
    Answer: (c) 168 (the knight graph has 168 edges, the corner degree 2).
    (d) πᵢ = C(N, i)/2ᴺ; mean return time to 0 is 2ᴺ.
    Teacher explains: (a) ∑ᵢπᵢpᵢⱼ = ∑ᵢπⱼpⱼᵢ = πⱼ∑ᵢpⱼᵢ = πⱼ. Detailed balance
    is much easier to solve: one equation per EDGE, not per state. (b) pᵤᵥ =
    1/deg(u) for each neighbour v. Try πᵤ = c·deg(u): πᵤpᵤᵥ = c = πᵥpᵥᵤ ✓, and
    ∑deg(u) = 2|E| gives c = 1/(2|E|). (c) Degrees on the board: 2 in the
    corners, 3, 4, 6 and 8 elsewhere; the sum is 336 = 2·168. So
    π_corner = 2/336 and m_corner = 336/2 = 168 (the chain is irreducible
    since the knight graph is connected; its period 2 does not affect the
    mean return time). From a central square, 336/8 = 42. (d) pᵢ,ᵢ₊₁ =
    (N − i)/N, pᵢ,ᵢ₋₁ = i/N. Try πᵢ = C(N, i)/2ᴺ:
       C(N, i)(N − i)/N = C(N, i + 1)(i + 1)/N = N!/(i!(N − i − 1)!N)  ✓
    so π is binomial and m₀ = 1/π₀ = 2ᴺ. For N = 100 molecules that is
    2¹⁰⁰ ≈ 10³⁰ steps: this resolved the 'recurrence paradox' of
    thermodynamics. Common mistakes: using the number of VERTICES instead of
    2|E|; saying a periodic chain has no stationary distribution (it has
    one; Pⁿ just does not converge). Now you try: a king from a corner.
    (degree sum 420, corner degree 3: 140)

D5. GAMBLER'S RUIN. A gambler with £i bets £1 at a time, winning w.p. p and
    losing w.p. q = 1 − p, until she has £0 or £N. Derive the probability
    hᵢ of reaching N. Evaluate for p = 0.4, N = 10, i = 5 and for p = 1/2.
    What happens as N → ∞?
    Answer: hᵢ = (1 − (q/p)ⁱ)/(1 − (q/p)ᴺ) for p ≠ q, hᵢ = i/N for p = 1/2;
    h₅ = 32/275 ≈ 0.1164 (fair game: 0.5); as N → ∞ ruin is certain if
    p ≤ 1/2 and has probability (q/p)ⁱ if p > 1/2.
    Teacher explains: FIRST-STEP ANALYSIS: condition on the first bet.
       hᵢ = p hᵢ₊₁ + q hᵢ₋₁  (0 < i < N),   h₀ = 0,  h_N = 1.
    This is a linear recurrence with characteristic equation pλ² − λ + q = 0,
    roots λ = 1 and λ = q/p. If p ≠ q, hᵢ = A + B(q/p)ⁱ; h₀ = 0 gives B = −A
    and h_N = 1 gives A = 1/(1 − (q/p)ᴺ). If p = q the root is double and
    hᵢ = A + Bi = i/N. Numbers: q/p = 1.5,
       h₅ = (1 − 1.5⁵)/(1 − 1.5¹⁰) = (1 − 7.59375)/(1 − 57.665) = 32/275
    ≈ 0.1164. A slightly unfavourable game (40% per bet) turns an even
    chance into about 12%. As N → ∞: if p < q, (q/p)ᴺ → ∞ and hᵢ → 0, so
    ruin is certain; if p = q, i/N → 0 too; if p > q, hᵢ → 1 − (q/p)ⁱ, so
    P(ruin) = (q/p)ⁱ. Common mistakes: writing the recurrence as hᵢ =
    p hᵢ₋₁ + q hᵢ₊₁ (a win moves UP); forgetting the boundary conditions.
    Now you try: p = 0.6, N = 10, i = 5. (243/275 ≈ 0.8836)

D6. Expected hitting times. (a) Show that the expected duration of the
    fair gambler's ruin game is kᵢ = i(N − i). (b) For p = 0.4, N = 10, i = 5
    find the expected duration. (c) A fair coin is tossed until HH appears;
    find the expected number of tosses; do the same for HT.
    Answer: (a) i(N − i). (b) 211/11 ≈ 19.18. (c) HH: 6, HT: 4.
    Teacher explains: kᵢ counts steps, so every step adds 1:
       kᵢ = 1 + p kᵢ₊₁ + q kᵢ₋₁,   k₀ = k_N = 0.
    (a) p = q = 1/2: kᵢ₊₁ − 2kᵢ + kᵢ₋₁ = −2, so kᵢ is quadratic with second
    difference −2: kᵢ = −i² + Ai + B; k₀ = 0, k_N = 0 give kᵢ = i(N − i).
    With N = 10, i = 3: 21 bets. (b) p ≠ q: a particular solution is
    i/(q − p) (check: 1 + p(i + 1)/(q − p) + q(i − 1)/(q − p) = 1 + (i +
    p − q)/(q − p) = i/(q − p) ✓); add A + B(q/p)ⁱ for the boundary values:
       kᵢ = i/(q − p) − (N/(q − p))·hᵢ,   hᵢ = P(reach N) from D5.
    k₅ = 5/0.2 − (10/0.2)(32/275) = 25 − 64/11 = 211/11 ≈ 19.18. (c) States:
    '' (no progress), H. For HH: k₀ = 1 + (1/2)k_H + (1/2)k₀ and
    k_H = 1 + (1/2)·0 + (1/2)k₀. Then k₀ = 2 + k_H and k_H = 1 + k₀/2, so
    k₀/2 = 3, k₀ = 6. For HT: after an H we wait only for a T (an extra H
    keeps us at H): k_H = 2, k₀ = 2 + k_H = 4. HH takes longer because a
    failure after H throws us back to the start. Common mistakes: forgetting
    the '1 +' for the step just taken; thinking all patterns of length 2 are
    equally quick. Now you try: HHH. (14)

D7. A Galton-Watson process has offspring distribution p₀ = 1/4, p₁ = 1/4,
    p₂ = 1/2 and Z₀ = 1. Find m, σ², E Z₁₀, P(Z₂ = 0), P(Z₃ = 0) and the
    extinction probability q. Prove that q is the smallest root of G(s) = s
    in [0, 1] and that q = 1 iff m ≤ 1 (p₁ < 1).
    Answer: m = 5/4, σ² = 11/16, E Z₁₀ = (5/4)¹⁰ ≈ 9.31; P(Z₂ = 0) = 11/32,
    P(Z₃ = 0) = 809/2048 ≈ 0.395; q = 1/2.
    Teacher explains: G(s) = 1/4 + s/4 + s²/2, m = G′(1) = 1/4 + 1 = 5/4,
    and σ² = G″(1) + m − m² = 1 + 5/4 − 25/16 = 11/16. Generation n
    has pgf Gₙ = G∘Gₙ₋₁ (each of the Z₁ children starts an independent copy),
    so qₙ = P(Zₙ = 0) = Gₙ(0) = G(qₙ₋₁):
       q₁ = G(0) = 1/4
       q₂ = G(1/4) = 1/4 + 1/16 + 1/32 = 11/32
       q₃ = G(11/32) = 1/4 + 11/128 + 121/2048 = 809/2048 ≈ 0.395.
    {Zₙ = 0} increases to {extinction}, so qₙ ↑ q and, G being continuous,
    q = G(q). MINIMAL: if s* ∈ [0, 1] with G(s*) = s*, then q₀ = 0 ≤ s* and
    qₙ = G(qₙ₋₁) ≤ G(s*) = s* (G increasing), so q ≤ s*. Here G(s) = s ⇔
    2s² − 3s + 1 = 0 ⇔ s = 1/2 or 1, so q = 1/2 (0.25, 0.344, 0.395, ...
    creep up to 0.5). G is convex with G(1) = 1; if m = G′(1) ≤ 1 the graph
    lies above the diagonal on [0, 1) (strictly convex unless p₀ + p₁ = 1),
    so 1 is the only root; if m > 1 the graph crosses the diagonal below 1.
    Common mistakes: answering q = 1 because '1 is a root' (it always is);
    thinking m = 1 gives survival (critical processes die out). Now you try:
    p₀ = 1/3, p₂ = 2/3. (m = 4/3, q = 1/2)

D8. Show that definition (iii) of a Poisson process (rate λ, P(one arrival
    in (t, t + h]) = λh + o(h), P(≥ 2) = o(h), independent increments) gives
    N(t) ~ Po(λt) and Exp(λ) inter-arrival times. Buses arrive as a Poisson
    process with λ = 3 per hour: find P(N(1) = 2), P(N(1) ≥ 2), P(no bus in
    the first 30 minutes), E and Var of the time of the 4th bus, and
    P(N(1) = 2, N(3) = 5).
    Answer: 4.5e⁻³ ≈ 0.2240; 1 − 4e⁻³ ≈ 0.8009; e⁻¹·⁵ ≈ 0.2231; 4/3 h and
    4/9 h²; 162e⁻⁹ ≈ 0.0200.
    Teacher explains: Let pₙ(t) = P(N(t) = n). Condition on (t, t + h]:
       pₙ(t + h) = pₙ(t)(1 − λh) + pₙ₋₁(t)λh + o(h),
    so pₙ′ = −λpₙ + λpₙ₋₁ and p₀′ = −λp₀, p₀(0) = 1. Then p₀ = e^(−λt), and
    by induction pₙ(t) = e^(−λt)(λt)ⁿ/n! (check: d/dt of this equals
    −λpₙ + λpₙ₋₁ ✓). P(T₁ > t) = p₀(t) = e^(−λt), so T₁ ~ Exp(λ); by
    independent increments the process restarts after each arrival, so all
    gaps are i.i.d. Exp(λ). Numbers (λ = 3):
       P(N(1) = 2) = e⁻³·9/2 ≈ 0.2240
       P(N(1) ≥ 2) = 1 − e⁻³(1 + 3) ≈ 0.8009
       P(T₁ > 1/2) = e⁻¹·⁵ ≈ 0.2231
       T₄ = sum of 4 Exp(3): E = 4/3 h = 80 min, Var = 4/9 h²
       P(N(1) = 2, N(3) − N(1) = 3) = (4.5e⁻³)(6³e⁻⁶/3!) = 4.5·36e⁻⁹ = 162e⁻⁹.
    Note P(T₂ ≤ 1) = P(N(1) ≥ 2): 'the 2nd bus has come by time 1' is the
    same event as 'at least 2 buses by time 1'. Common mistakes: using
    P(N(3) = 5) instead of splitting into DISJOINT intervals; treating
    N(1) and N(3) as independent. Now you try: P(the 2nd bus arrives before
    time 1). (0.8009)

D9. Customers enter a shop as a PP(10) per hour and each buys something
    with probability 0.3, independently. (a) Show that buyers and
    non-buyers form independent Poisson processes of rates 3 and 7.
    (b) Find P(no buyer in an hour) and, given that 5 customers came in the
    hour, P(exactly 2 bought). (c) Independent PP(2) (red) and PP(3) (blue)
    are merged: find P(the first point is red) and P(3 red points come
    before 2 blue ones). (d) Given N(1) = 4, find P(all four arrivals are in
    the first half hour).
    Answer: (b) e⁻³ ≈ 0.0498 and C(5,2)0.3²0.7³ = 0.3087. (c) 2/5; 0.1792.
    (d) 1/16.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let A = buyers, B = the rest in (0, t]. Given
    N(t) = n, A ~ Bin(n, p), so
       P(A = a, B = b) = e^(−λt)(λt)ᵃ⁺ᵇ/(a + b)! · C(a + b, a)pᵃ(1 − p)ᵇ
                       = [e^(−λpt)(λpt)ᵃ/a!]·[e^(−λ(1−p)t)(λ(1 − p)t)ᵇ/b!]:
    the joint law FACTORISES into Po(3t) × Po(7t), which is independence
    (increments are independent because those of N are). (b) P(A(1) = 0) =
    e⁻³. Given N(1) = 5 the number of buyers is Bin(5, 0.3): 10·0.09·0.343 =
    0.3087. (c) The merged process is PP(5), and each point is red w.p. 2/5
    independently (competing exponentials: P(Exp(2) < Exp(3)) = 2/5). So
    the colours form coin tosses with P(red) = 0.4; 3 reds before 2 blues ⇔
    at least 3 reds among the first 4 points:
       C(4,3)(0.4)³(0.6) + (0.4)⁴ = 0.1536 + 0.0256 = 0.1792.
    (d) Given N(1) = 4 the arrival times are 4 independent U(0, 1) points
    (sorted), so the answer is (1/2)⁴ = 1/16, whatever λ is. Common
    mistakes: thinking thinned processes are dependent 'because they come
    from the same customers'; conditioning on N(1) = 4 and still using λ.
    Now you try: given N(2) = 3, the expected time of the first arrival.
    (the minimum of 3 U(0, 2): 2/4 = 1/2)

D10. Continuous-time chains. (a) For the generator Q = [[−α, α], [β, −β]]
     solve the forward equation and find P(t). Evaluate for α = 2, β = 3:
     P₀₀(0.2) and π. (b) For the M/M/1 queue with arrival rate λ = 2 and
     service rate μ = 3 per hour find π, the mean number in the system L,
     the mean time in the system W, and Lq, Wq.
     Answer: (a) P₀₀(t) = β/(α + β) + (α/(α + β))e^(−(α+β)t); P₀₀(0.2) =
     3/5 + (2/5)e⁻¹ ≈ 0.7472; π = (3/5, 2/5). (b) πₙ = (1/3)(2/3)ⁿ; L = 2,
     W = 1 h, Lq = 4/3, Wq = 2/3 h.
     Teacher explains: (a) Forward equation P′ = PQ, first entry:
        P₀₀′ = −αP₀₀ + βP₀₁ = −αP₀₀ + β(1 − P₀₀) = β − (α + β)P₀₀,
     with P₀₀(0) = 1. This linear ODE gives P₀₀(t) = β/(α + β) +
     (α/(α + β))e^(−(α+β)t) (equivalently P(t) = e^(tQ), eigenvalues 0 and
     −(α + β)). With α = 2, β = 3: P₀₀(t) = 3/5 + (2/5)e^(−5t), and at t = 0.2
     this is 0.6 + 0.4e⁻¹ ≈ 0.7472. πQ = 0: −2π₀ + 3π₁ = 0, so π = (3/5, 2/5).
     (b) M/M/1 is a birth-death chain with λₙ = λ, μₙ = μ. Balance the flow
     across the cut between n and n + 1: λπₙ = μπₙ₊₁, so πₙ = π₀ρⁿ with
     ρ = 2/3, and π₀ = 1 − ρ = 1/3. Then
        L = ∑nπₙ = ρ/(1 − ρ) = 2,     W = L/λ = 1 h     (LITTLE: L = λW)
        Lq = L − ρ = 4/3,              Wq = Lq/λ = 2/3 h
     (ρ = P(server busy) is the mean number in service). Common mistakes:
     writing the stationary equation as Qπ = 0 (π is a row: πQ = 0);
     forgetting that the queue is stable only when λ < μ. Now you try:
     λ = 4, μ = 5. (L = 4, W = 1 h, P(N ≥ 3) = ρ³ = 0.512)

D11. Random walk recurrence. (a) For the simple symmetric walk on Z show
     u₂ₙ = P(S₂ₙ = 0) = C(2n, n)/4ⁿ, compute u₂, u₄, u₆ and prove the walk is
     recurrent. (b) Explain Pólya's theorem in Z² and Z³. (c) For p ≠ 1/2
     show the return probability is 1 − |p − q| and evaluate for p = 0.6.
     Answer: u₂ = 1/2, u₄ = 3/8, u₆ = 5/16; ∑u₂ₙ = ∞ in Z and Z², < ∞ in Z³;
     return probability 0.8 for p = 0.6 (expected number of visits to 0,
     counting time 0, is 5).
     Teacher explains: (a) S₂ₙ = 0 ⇔ exactly n of the 2n steps go up:
     C(2n, n) of the 2²ⁿ equally likely paths. u₂ = 2/4, u₄ = 6/16 = 3/8,
     u₆ = 20/64 = 5/16. Stirling, n! ~ √(2πn)(n/e)ⁿ, gives
        C(2n, n)/4ⁿ ~ √(4πn)(2n/e)²ⁿ/(2πn (n/e)²ⁿ 4ⁿ) = 1/√(πn),
     and ∑1/√n = ∞, so 0 is recurrent (D2 criterion: ∑pᵢᵢ⁽ⁿ⁾ = ∞).
     (b) In Z², rotate by 45°: the two coordinates of the rotated walk are
     INDEPENDENT one-dimensional walks, so P(S₂ₙ = 0) = u₂ₙ² ~ 1/(πn), whose
     sum still diverges: recurrent. In Z³ the return probability at time 2n
     is of order n^(−3/2), which is summable: transient; the walk returns
     with probability ≈ 0.3405 only ('a drunk man finds his way home, a
     drunk bird may not'). (c) ∑ₙ C(2n, n)xⁿ = 1/√(1 − 4x), so with x = pq,
        ∑ₙ≥₀ P(S₂ₙ = 0) = 1/√(1 − 4pq) = 1/|p − q|   (as 1 − 4pq = (p − q)²).
     Expected visits = 1/(1 − F), so F = 1 − |p − q|; p = 0.6: F = 0.8 and
     the expected number of visits is 1/0.2 = 5. Common mistakes: 'recurrent
     means the walk comes back in bounded time' (in Z the mean return time
     is infinite: NULL recurrence); forgetting that S₂ₙ₊₁ ≠ 0 always. Now
     you try: p = 0.7. (return probability 0.6)

D12. REFLECTION PRINCIPLE. (a) Prove: for a, b > 0 the number of walk paths
     from (0, a) to (n, b) that touch 0 equals the number of all paths from
     (0, −a) to (n, b). (b) Deduce the ballot theorem: if A gets a votes and
     B gets b < a votes, counted in random order, P(A is strictly ahead
     throughout) = (a − b)/(a + b); evaluate for a = 6, b = 4. (c) Show
     P(max_{k≤n} Sₖ ≥ r) = P(Sₙ ≥ r) + P(Sₙ ≥ r + 1) and evaluate for n = 4,
     r = 2.
     Answer: (b) 1/5. (c) 5/16 + 1/16 = 3/8.
     Teacher explains: (a) Given a path from a to b that touches 0, let τ be
     its FIRST visit to 0 and reflect the part before τ in the time axis.
     This gives a path from −a to b; conversely every path from −a to b
     must cross 0 (the end-points have opposite signs), and reflecting its
     part before the first zero recovers a touching path: a bijection.
     (b) Record the count as a walk (+1 for A, −1 for B) from 0 to a − b in
     n = a + b steps. 'A always ahead' forces the first step up and then no
     visit to 0 from (1, 1) to (n, a − b):
        #good = N(1 → a − b) − N(−1 → a − b) = C(n − 1, a − 1) − C(n − 1, a)
              = C(n, a)(a − b)/n.
     Dividing by C(n, a): (a − b)/(a + b). For 6 : 4, C(9,5) − C(9,6) = 126 −
     84 = 42 and C(10,6) = 210: 42/210 = 1/5. (c) Split {Mₙ ≥ r} by where Sₙ
     ends. Paths with Sₙ ≥ r count directly; a path with Sₙ = s < r that has
     reached r is reflected after its first visit to r, ending at 2r − s > r.
     This is a bijection onto {Sₙ > r}, so P(Mₙ ≥ r) = P(Sₙ ≥ r) + P(Sₙ > r).
     n = 4, r = 2: P(S₄ ≥ 2) = (4 + 1)/16 and P(S₄ > 2) = P(S₄ = 4) = 1/16;
     total 6/16 = 3/8 (a list of the 16 paths confirms it). Common
     mistakes: reflecting the WHOLE path instead of the part up to the first
     hit; forgetting that 'strictly ahead' forces the first vote. Now you
     try: ballot with a = 5, b = 3. (1/4)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. n-step probabilities. A = [[0.7, 0.3], [0.4, 0.6]], B = [[0, 1], [1, 0]],
    C = [[1/2, 1/2, 0], [1/4, 1/2, 1/4], [0, 1/2, 1/2]],
    D = [[1/2, 1/2], [1/5, 4/5]], E = [[0, 1/2, 1/2], [1/2, 0, 1/2],
    [1/2, 1/2, 0]], F = [[0, 1/2, 1/2], [1/3, 1/3, 1/3], [1/2, 0, 1/2]].
    a) A: p₁₁⁽²⁾                        b) A: p₁₂⁽²⁾
    c) A: p₁₁⁽³⁾                        d) A: P(X₂ = 2), X₀ uniform
    e) A: P(X₁ = 1, X₂ = 2, X₃ = 2 | X₀ = 1)
    f) A: P(X₃ = X₀), X₀ uniform        g) B: p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾
    h) C: p₁₃⁽²⁾                        i) C: p₂₂⁽ⁿ⁾, n ≥ 1
    j) C: p₁₁⁽³⁾                        k) C: p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾, n ≥ 1
    l) D: the matrix D²                 m) D: p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾
    n) E: p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾                        o) E: p₁₂⁽⁴⁾
    p) F: p₁₃⁽²⁾                        q) F: p₂₁⁽²⁾
    r) A: lim p₂₁⁽ⁿ⁾                    s) F: lim p₃₃⁽ⁿ⁾
    t) A: p₁₁⁽ⁿ⁾
    Answers:
      a) 0.61        b) 0.39        c) 0.583       d) 0.435
      e) 0.126       f) 1027/2000 = 0.5135          g) 1 (n even), 0 (n odd)
      h) 1/8         i) 1/2         j) 5/16        k) 1/4 + (1/2)ⁿ⁺¹
      l) [[0.35, 0.65], [0.26, 0.74]]               m) 2/7 + (5/7)(0.3)ⁿ
      n) 1/3 + (2/3)(−1/2)ⁿ          o) 5/16        p) 5/12
      q) 5/18        r) 4/7         s) 6/13        t) 4/7 + (3/7)(0.3)ⁿ

M2. Classify: communicating classes, closed or not, recurrent (PR =
    positive, NR = null) or transient (T), and the period. Arrows show the
    positive transition probabilities.
    a) 1 → 2, 2 → 1                    b) 1 → 1, 1 → 2, 2 → 2
    c) 1 → 2, 2 → 3, 3 → 1             d) 1 → 2, 2 → 3, 3 → 1, 3 → 2
    e) 1 → 2, 2 → 1, 2 → 3, 3 → 4, 4 → 3
    f) 1 → 2, 2 → 3, 3 → 4, 4 → 1, 4 → 3
    g) 1 → 2, 1 → 3, 2 → 4, 3 → 4, 4 → 1
    h) 1 → 2, 2 → 3, 3 → 1, 3 → 4, 4 → 5, 5 → 4
    i) the chain of D2                 j) symmetric simple walk on Z
    k) simple walk on Z, p = 2/3
    l) walk on {0, 1, 2, ...}: up 1/3, down 2/3, p₀₀ = 2/3
    m) the same with up 2/3, down 1/3, p₀₀ = 1/3
    n) the same with up = down = 1/2, p₀₀ = 1/2
    o) symmetric walk on Z²; on Z³     p) Ehrenfest urn, N balls
    q) random walk on C₆; on C₅
    r) success-run chain: i → i + 1 w.p. p, i → 0 w.p. 1 − p (0 < p < 1)
    Answers:
      a) {1, 2} closed, PR, period 2
      b) {1} T, period 1; {2} closed (absorbing), PR, period 1
      c) {1, 2, 3} closed, PR, period 3
      d) {1, 2, 3} closed, PR, aperiodic (cycles of length 3 and 2)
      e) {1, 2} T, period 2; {3, 4} closed, PR, period 2
      f) {1, 2, 3, 4} closed, PR, period 2 (cycles of length 4 and 2)
      g) {1, 2, 3, 4} closed, PR, period 3 (all cycles have length 3)
      h) {1, 2, 3} T, period 3; {4, 5} closed, PR, period 2
      i) {1, 2} T, aperiodic; {3, 4, 5} closed, PR, period 3; {6} T
      j) NR, period 2      k) T, period 2       l) PR, aperiodic
      m) T, aperiodic      n) NR, aperiodic     o) NR, period 2; T, period 2
      p) PR, period 2      q) PR, period 2; PR, aperiodic
      r) PR, aperiodic (πᵢ = (1 − p)pⁱ)

M3. Find all stationary distributions.

       [0, 1/2, 1/2, 0]]

    o) Ehrenfest urn with N = 4

    q) chain l) of M2
    r) [[1, 0, 0], [1/2, 0, 1/2], [0, 0, 1]]

      g) (4/13, 3/13, 6/13)              h) (1/2, 1/3, 1/6)
      i) (15/59, 24/59, 20/59)           j) (1/3, 1/3, 1/3)
      k) (1/6, 1/3, 1/3, 1/6)            l) π_c = 1/2, each leaf 1/6
      m) (1/5, 3/10, 3/10, 1/5) (degrees 2, 3, 3, 2 over 10)
      n) (8/15, 4/15, 2/15, 1/15)        o) (1, 4, 6, 4, 1)/16
      p) πᵢ = (1/2)ⁱ⁺¹                   q) πᵢ = (1/2)ⁱ⁺¹
      r) (a, 0, 1 − a), any 0 ≤ a ≤ 1 (two closed classes: not unique)
      s) (0, 1/2, 1/2) (state 1 is transient)
      t) none (null recurrent; the invariant measure 1 is not summable)

M4. Hitting probabilities and expected hitting times.
    a) symmetric walk on {0, ..., 4}, absorbing ends, start 1: P(hit 4)
       and E(time to absorption)
    b) states 1-4, 1 and 4 absorbing; p₂₁ = 0.3, p₂₂ = 0.2, p₂₃ = 0.5,
       p₃₂ = 0.4, p₃₃ = 0.2, p₃₄ = 0.4: P(absorbed at 4) from 2 and from 3
    c) in b): the fundamental matrix N and E(time) from 2 and from 3
    d)-i) fair-coin tosses until: HH; HT; HHH; HTH; HHHH; HTHT
    j) die rolls until two sixes in a row
    k) P(HH before TH)   l) P(HHT before HTH)   m) P(THH before HHH)
    Random walk on a graph, expected hitting time:
    n) K₅, any two vertices            o) C₆, opposite vertices
    p) cube Q₃, opposite corners       q) path 0 − 1 − ... − 5, from 0 to 5
    r) star K₁,₃, leaf to leaf         s) Petersen: adjacent; non-adjacent
    t) coupon collector, 4 equally likely coupons
    Answers:
      a) 1/4, 3                          b) 5/11, 8/11
      c) N = [[20/11, 25/22], [10/11, 20/11]]; 65/22 ≈ 2.955, 30/11 ≈ 2.727
