TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: always draw the arrow diagram before classifying states,
  and write distributions as ROW vectors. For every hitting question
  write 'condition on the first step' and one equation per state, with
  the boundary values first. Check each stationary distribution by
  multiplying back. For Poisson questions split time into DISJOINT
  intervals before multiplying probabilities, and simulate (a few lines
  of Python) to see the gaps are exponential and the conditioned points
  uniform. Draw random-walk paths on squared paper to make the
  reflection principle visible. Harder: prove the convergence theorem by
  coupling; show that the extinction probability of a branching process
  is the minimal root and find the distribution of the total progeny for
  Po(1) offspring (Borel distribution); derive Pólya's theorem in Z³ via
  ∑ₙ(C(2n, n)/4ⁿ)·(sums of multinomials) or the Fourier integral
  ∫(1 − φ(θ))⁻¹dθ; prove that a stationary birth-death chain is
  reversible and that M/M/1 departures form a Poisson process (Burke's
  theorem); and use optional stopping for the martingales Sₙ and
  Sₙ² − n to re-derive the gambler's ruin formulas.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.13 — DYNAMICAL SYSTEMS AND CHAOS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - DISCRETE DYNAMICS: orbit x, f(x), f²(x), ...; FIXED point f(p) = p;
    PERIODIC point fⁿ(p) = p with PRIME period n; EVENTUALLY periodic
    points. COBWEB diagrams. A fixed point is ATTRACTING if |f′(p)| < 1,
    REPELLING if |f′(p)| > 1 (proof by the mean value theorem), and
    NON-HYPERBOLIC if |f′(p)| = 1 (then decide by higher terms: x − x³
    attracts, x + x³ repels, x + x² is semi-stable). SUPERATTRACTING if
    f′(p) = 0. An n-cycle {p₁, ..., pₙ} has MULTIPLIER (fⁿ)′(p₁) =
    f′(p₁)f′(p₂)...f′(pₙ) (chain rule), the same at every point of the cycle.
  - BIFURCATIONS of one-parameter families: SADDLE-NODE (fold; ẋ = r + x²,
    x ↦ x² + c at c = 1/4), TRANSCRITICAL (ẋ = rx − x²), PITCHFORK (ẋ = rx −
    x³, super/subcritical), PERIOD-DOUBLING (flip; multiplier passes −1,
    x ↦ x² + c at c = −3/4) and HOPF for flows (a complex pair of
    eigenvalues crosses the imaginary axis; a limit cycle of radius ~ √μ
    is born).
  - LOGISTIC MAP fᵣ(x) = rx(1 − x): fixed points 0 and 1 − 1/r (stable for
    1 < r < 3); 2-cycle roots of r²x² − r(r + 1)x + (r + 1) = 0, multiplier
    −r² + 2r + 4, stable for 3 < r < 1 + √6 ≈ 3.4495; cascade r₃ ≈ 3.5441,
    r₄ ≈ 3.5644, accumulating at r∞ ≈ 3.5699; FEIGENBAUM constants
    δ ≈ 4.6692 (universal ratio of parameter gaps), α ≈ 2.5029; period-3
    window opens at r = 1 + √8 ≈ 3.8284; r = 4 is fully chaotic. The
    logistic and quadratic families are conjugate (c = r/2 − r²/4).
  - SHARKOVSKII'S THEOREM (continuous maps of an interval): order
    3 ▷ 5 ▷ 7 ▷ ... ▷ 2·3 ▷ 2·5 ▷ ... ▷ 4·3 ▷ 4·5 ▷ ... ▷ 2³ ▷ 2² ▷ 2 ▷ 1;
    period m and m ▷ k ⇒ period k. LI-YORKE: period 3 implies all periods
    (and chaos). False on the circle and in R² (rotations).
  - MODEL CHAOTIC MAPS: DOUBLING map D(x) = 2x mod 1 = shift of binary
    digits (2ⁿ − 1 points of period dividing n; rationals are eventually
    periodic); TENT map T (2ⁿ such points; preserves Lebesgue measure);
    L₄ = 4x(1 − x), CONJUGATE to T by h(x) = sin²(πx/2), invariant density
    1/(π√(x(1 − x))). Counting PRIME periods by Möbius inversion.
  - SYMBOLIC DYNAMICS: Σ₂ = sequences of 0, 1 with d(s, t) = ∑ᵢ≥₀|sᵢ − tᵢ|/2ⁱ
    (agree in the first n + 1 places ⇔ close within 2⁻ⁿ, roughly); SHIFT
    σ; ITINERARIES code orbits; SUBSHIFTS OF FINITE TYPE Σ_A with #period-n
    points = tr(Aⁿ) and TOPOLOGICAL ENTROPY log(spectral radius of A)
    (golden-mean shift: ln φ).
  - CHAOS (DEVANEY): f is transitive (a dense orbit), periodic points are
    dense, and SENSITIVE DEPENDENCE on initial conditions (the first two
    imply the third on infinite metric spaces). Conjugacies preserve all
    three.
  - LYAPUNOV EXPONENT λ(x) = lim (1/n)∑ₖ₌₀ⁿ⁻¹ ln|f′(fᵏ(x))|: ln 2 for T, D and
    L₄; ln|f′(p)| < 0 at an attracting fixed point; λ > 0 signals chaos;
    errors grow like e^(λn) (the PREDICTABILITY horizon ≈ ln(tol/ε₀)/λ). For
    d-dimensional systems a spectrum λ₁ ≥ ... ≥ λ_d with ∑λᵢ = mean of
    ln|det J| (maps) or of div F (flows).
  - FRACTAL DIMENSION: BOX dimension lim log N(ε)/log(1/ε); SIMILARITY
    dimension d with ∑rᵢᵈ = 1 (open set condition): Cantor ln 2/ln 3 ≈
    0.6309, Koch ln 4/ln 3 ≈ 1.2619, Sierpinski ln 3/ln 2 ≈ 1.5850;
    KAPLAN-YORKE dimension j + (λ₁ + ... + λⱼ)/|λⱼ₊₁|.
  - FLOWS: equilibria and LINEARISATION (Hartman-Grobman for hyperbolic
    points); trace-determinant classification in the plane (node, saddle,
    spiral, centre); limit cycles, POINCARÉ MAPS and Floquet multipliers;
    POINCARÉ-BENDIXSON: bounded planar orbits tend to equilibria or
    cycles, so CHAOS NEEDS 3 dimensions for flows (2 for invertible maps,
    1 for non-invertible maps). Bendixson/Dulac criteria rule out cycles.
  - STRANGE ATTRACTORS: LORENZ ẋ = σ(y − x), ẏ = x(ρ − z) − y, ż = xy − βz
    (σ = 10, β = 8/3, ρ = 28): equilibria 0 and C± = (±√(β(ρ − 1)),
    ±√(β(ρ − 1)), ρ − 1), pitchfork at ρ = 1, subcritical Hopf at ρ_H =
    σ(σ + β + 3)/(σ − β − 1) ≈ 24.74, volumes shrink like e^(−(σ+1+β)t).
    HÉNON (x, y) ↦ (1 − ax² + y, bx) (a = 1.4, b = 0.3): det J = −b, area
    contracts by 0.3 per step, λ₁ ≈ 0.42, dimension ≈ 1.26.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Prove: if f is C¹, f(p) = p and |f′(p)| < 1 then p attracts all nearby
    orbits; if |f′(p)| > 1 nearby orbits move away. Find and classify the
    fixed points of f(x) = 2.5x(1 − x) and iterate from x₀ = 0.1. Then
    discuss the non-hyperbolic fixed point 0 of x − x³, x + x³ and x + x².
    Answer: 0 repelling (f′ = 2.5), 0.6 attracting (f′ = −0.5); x₁, ..., x₅ =
    0.225, 0.4359, 0.6147, 0.5921, 0.6038 → 0.6; x − x³: 0 attracts;
    x + x³: 0 repels; x + x²: semi-stable.
    Teacher explains: Choose K with |f′(p)| < K < 1. By continuity of f′
    there is δ > 0 with |f′(x)| ≤ K on I = (p − δ, p + δ). For x ∈ I the mean
    value theorem gives c between x and p with
       |f(x) − p| = |f(x) − f(p)| = |f′(c)||x − p| ≤ K|x − p|,
    so f(x) ∈ I again and by induction |fⁿ(x) − p| ≤ Kⁿ|x − p| → 0
    (geometric convergence). If |f′(p)| > K > 1 the same argument gives
    |f(x) − p| ≥ K|x − p| near p: orbits are pushed out. Example:
    rx(1 − x) = x ⇔ x = 0 or x = 1 − 1/r = 0.6; f′(x) = 2.5 − 5x, so f′(0) =
    2.5 (repelling) and f′(0.6) = −0.5 (attracting). Iterates:
       0.1 → 0.225 → 0.4359 → 0.6147 → 0.5921 → 0.6038 → ...
    The approach ALTERNATES about 0.6 because f′(p) < 0 (the cobweb spirals
    in); the error roughly halves each step. Non-hyperbolic cases
    (f′(0) = 1): x − x³ has |f(x)| < |x| for small x ≠ 0, so 0 attracts, but
    only like 1/√(2n); x + x³ has |f(x)| > |x|, so 0 repels; x + x² − x = x² >
    0, so orbits increase: those starting just left of 0 creep up to it,
    those just right of it leave (semi-stable). Common mistakes: using
    |f′(p)| < 1 as an 'iff' (f′ = ±1 decides nothing); forgetting that the
    derivative is evaluated AT THE FIXED POINT. Now you try: f(x) = cos x.
    (p ≈ 0.7391, f′(p) = −sin p ≈ −0.6736: attracting)

D2. For the logistic map fᵣ(x) = rx(1 − x) find the period-2 orbit, prove
    that its multiplier is −r² + 2r + 4, and deduce that it exists for r > 3
    and is attracting exactly for 3 < r < 1 + √6. Evaluate at r = 3.2 and
    r = 3.5.
    Answer: p, q = [(r + 1) ± √((r + 1)(r − 3))]/(2r). r = 3.2: {0.5130,
    0.7995}, multiplier 0.16 (attracting). r = 3.5: {3/7, 6/7}, multiplier
    −1.25 (repelling; a 4-cycle attracts instead).
    Teacher explains: The 2-periodic points solve f(f(x)) = x, a quartic.
    It must contain the fixed points, so factor them out:
       f(f(x)) − x = −x(rx − r + 1)(r²x² − r(r + 1)x + (r + 1)).
    The quadratic has discriminant r²(r + 1)² − 4r²(r + 1) = r²(r + 1)(r − 3),
    real and distinct iff r > 3: the 2-cycle is BORN at r = 3, exactly
    where f′(1 − 1/r) = 2 − r passes −1 (period doubling). By the chain rule
    (f²)′(p) = f′(f(p))f′(p) = f′(q)f′(p), the same at p and q. With
    p + q = (r + 1)/r and pq = (r + 1)/r² (Vieta),
       f′(p)f′(q) = r²(1 − 2p)(1 − 2q) = r²[1 − 2(p + q) + 4pq]
                  = r² − 2r(r + 1) + 4(r + 1) = −r² + 2r + 4.
    Stability: −1 < −r² + 2r + 4 < 1 ⇔ r² − 2r − 3 > 0 and r² − 2r − 5 < 0 ⇔
    3 < r < 1 + √6 ≈ 3.4495. At r = 3.2: discriminant term √(4.2·0.2) =
    √0.84 ≈ 0.9165, p, q = (4.2 ∓ 0.9165)/6.4 = 0.5130, 0.7995, multiplier
    −10.24 + 6.4 + 4 = 0.16. At r = 3.5: √(4.5·0.5) = 1.5, so p, q =
    3/7, 6/7 exactly, multiplier −1.25: unstable. Common mistakes: solving
    f(f(x)) = x and reporting the fixed points as a 2-cycle; computing the
    multiplier as f′(p)² instead of f′(p)f′(q). Now you try: r = 3.4.
    ({0.4520, 0.8422}, multiplier −0.76: attracting)

D3. For the quadratic family Q_c(x) = x² + c find all fixed points and
    2-cycles, their multipliers, and the parameter values of the
    saddle-node and period-doubling bifurcations. Sketch the bifurcation
    diagram for −5/4 ≤ c ≤ 1/4. Compare with the normal forms of flows.
    Answer: p± = (1 ± √(1 − 4c))/2, born in a saddle-node at c = 1/4; p₋ is
    attracting for −3/4 < c < 1/4; period doubling at c = −3/4; the
    2-cycle roots of x² + x + c + 1 = 0 have multiplier 4(c + 1) and attract
    for −5/4 < c < −3/4.
    Teacher explains: x² + c = x ⇔ x² − x + c = 0: real roots iff c ≤ 1/4.
    For c > 1/4 all orbits escape to ∞; at c = 1/4 a single fixed point
    x = 1/2 with Q′ = 1 appears, then splits into two: a SADDLE-NODE (fold).
    Q′(p±) = 2p± = 1 ± √(1 − 4c): p₊ always repels; Q′(p₋) = 1 − √(1 − 4c)
    lies in (−1, 1) iff 0 < √(1 − 4c) < 2 ⇔ −3/4 < c < 1/4. At c = −3/4,
    Q′(p₋) = −1: PERIOD DOUBLING. Dividing Q(Q(x)) − x by x² − x + c leaves
    x² + x + c + 1, real roots iff c < −3/4. Its roots q₁, q₂ have q₁q₂ =
    c + 1, so the multiplier is Q′(q₁)Q′(q₂) = 4q₁q₂ = 4(c + 1), in (−1, 1)
    iff −5/4 < c < −3/4. At c = −1 the cycle is {0, −1}, SUPERATTRACTING
    (it contains the critical point 0). Diagram: one stable branch from
    c = 1/4 down to c = −3/4, then two branches (the 2-cycle) down to
    −5/4, where they double again. Flows: ẋ = r + x² (saddle-node at r = 0),
    ẋ = rx − x² (TRANSCRITICAL: two branches exchange stability), ẋ = rx −
    x³ (supercritical PITCHFORK, symmetric), ẋ = rx + x³ (subcritical).
    Period doubling has no one-dimensional flow analogue (flows on a line
    are monotone). Common mistakes: forgetting that c = −3/4 creates a
    2-cycle but does not destroy the fixed point (it only makes it
    repelling). Now you try: c = −1.1. (2-cycle ≈ {0.0916, −1.0916},
    multiplier −0.4: attracting)

D4. Describe the period-doubling route to chaos of the logistic map with
    the numbers r₁, r₂, r₃, r₄, r∞ and Feigenbaum's δ. State Sharkovskii's
    theorem, and prove the key step of 'period 3 implies all periods':
    if f has a 3-cycle a < b < c with f(a) = b, f(b) = c, f(c) = a then f
    has a fixed point and points of every period n.
    Answer: r₁ = 3, r₂ = 1 + √6 ≈ 3.4495, r₃ ≈ 3.5441, r₄ ≈ 3.5644,
    r∞ ≈ 3.5699; (r₂ − r₁)/(r₃ − r₂) ≈ 4.751, (r₃ − r₂)/(r₄ − r₃) ≈ 4.656 →
    δ ≈ 4.6692. Proof below.
    Teacher explains: At each rₖ the attracting 2ᵏ⁻¹-cycle has multiplier
    −1 and hands over to a 2ᵏ-cycle. The gaps shrink geometrically:
       r₂ − r₁ = 0.4495,  r₃ − r₂ = 0.0946,  r₄ − r₃ = 0.0203,
    ratios 4.75, 4.66, ... → δ = 4.6692..., the same for EVERY smooth
    unimodal family (UNIVERSALITY, confirmed in fluid experiments). So
    r∞ ≈ r₄ + (r₄ − r₃)/(δ − 1) ≈ 3.5699. Beyond r∞ chaos is interleaved with
    windows; the period-3 window opens at r = 1 + √8 ≈ 3.8284 by a
    saddle-node of f³. SHARKOVSKII: in the order 3 ▷ 5 ▷ 7 ▷ ... ▷ 2·3 ▷
    2·5 ▷ ... ▷ 2²·3 ▷ ... ▷ 2³ ▷ 2² ▷ 2 ▷ 1, a continuous interval map with
    a point of period m has points of every period k with m ▷ k. Two tools
    from the intermediate value theorem: (i) if f(I) ⊇ I then f has a fixed
    point in I; (ii) if f(I) ⊇ J there is a closed K ⊆ I with f(K) = J. Let
    I₀ = [a, b], I₁ = [b, c]. Then f(I₀) ⊇ [b, c] = I₁ and f(I₁) ⊇ [a, c] =
    I₀ ∪ I₁. Fixed point: f(I₁) ⊇ I₁ by (i). Period n ≥ 2: follow the loop I₀ →
    I₁ → I₁ → ... → I₁ → I₀ (n − 1 steps in I₁, then back to I₀): using (ii)
    repeatedly we shrink to K ⊆ I₀ with fⁿ(K) = I₀, so by (i) some x ∈ K has
    fⁿ(x) = x. Its orbit visits I₀ first and then I₁ n − 1 times; a smaller
    period would put x in I₀ ∩ I₁ = {b}, but the orbit b, c, a, ... leaves I₁ at
    the second step, so the prime period is n. Common mistakes: thinking
    Sharkovskii works in R² or on the circle (rotation by 120° has only period
    3); reading ▷ as 'less than'. Now you try: must a map with a point of period
    12 have one of period 20? of period 6? (yes, 4·3 ▷ 4·5; no)

D5. The doubling map D(x) = 2x mod 1 on [0, 1). (a) Show that D acts as the
    shift on binary expansions. (b) Find all points of period dividing n
    and count the points of prime period 3. (c) Show that rationals are
    eventually periodic and exhibit a dense orbit. (d) Two initial values
    differ by 10⁻¹²: after how many steps can they differ by 0.1?
    (e) Explain why a computer iteration of D in double precision always
    reaches 0.
    Answer: (b) x = k/(2ⁿ − 1), k = 0, ..., 2ⁿ − 2; prime period 3: 6 points
    (two orbits {1/7, 2/7, 4/7}, {3/7, 6/7, 5/7}). (d) 37 steps.
    (e) every double is a dyadic rational m/2ᵏ.
    Teacher explains: (a) If x = 0.b₁b₂b₃...(binary) then 2x = b₁.b₂b₃...,
    and 'mod 1' drops b₁: D(0.b₁b₂...) = 0.b₂b₃.... (b) Dⁿ(x) = 2ⁿx mod 1 =
    x ⇔ (2ⁿ − 1)x ∈ Z ⇔ x = k/(2ⁿ − 1): 2ⁿ − 1 points. For n = 3 these are
    k/7; removing the fixed point 0 leaves 6 points of prime period 3:
    1/7 = 0.(001) → 2/7 → 4/7 → 1/7, and 3/7 = 0.(011) → 6/7 → 5/7 → 3/7.
    (c) A rational has an eventually repeating binary expansion, so its
    orbit is eventually periodic (e.g. 3/8 → 3/4 → 1/2 → 0, 1/6 → 1/3 →
    2/3 → 1/3). Periodic points k/(2ⁿ − 1) are dense. A dense orbit: the
    point whose binary digits list every finite word, 0.0 1 00 01 10 11
    000 001 ...; its orbit comes within 2⁻ⁿ of every point. (d) While the
    two points stay on the same side, their distance doubles: 2ⁿ·10⁻¹² ≥ 0.1 ⇔
    2ⁿ ≥ 10¹¹ ⇔ n ≥ 36.54, so n = 37. This is SENSITIVE DEPENDENCE, with
    Lyapunov exponent ln 2. (e) A double in [0, 1) is m/2ᵏ with k ≤ 1074 (k ≈ 53
    for a typical number, the mantissa length); each doubling mod 1 shifts out
    one bit, so after k steps the computed orbit is exactly 0, while the true
    orbit of almost every point is dense. Common mistakes: counting 2ⁿ periodic
    points (1 is excluded, 0 = 1 in the circle picture); trusting long
    floating-point orbits of chaotic maps. Now you try: the orbit of 1/5. (1/5 →
    2/5 → 4/5 → 3/5 → 1/5: period 4)

D6. Let T(x) = 1 − |1 − 2x| be the tent map and L(x) = 4x(1 − x). (a) Prove
    L ∘ h = h ∘ T for h(x) = sin²(πx/2). (b) Deduce the fixed points,
    2-cycle and a 3-cycle of L from those of T. (c) Show T preserves
    length and deduce the invariant density of L. (d) What fraction of
    time does a typical L-orbit spend in [0, 1/4]?
    Answer: (b) fixed 0, 3/4; 2-cycle (5 ∓ √5)/8 ≈ 0.3455, 0.9045; 3-cycle
    sin²(π/7) ≈ 0.1883, sin²(2π/7) ≈ 0.6113, sin²(3π/7) ≈ 0.9505.
    (c) ρ(y) = 1/(π√(y(1 − y))). (d) 1/3.
    Teacher explains: (a) L(h(x)) = 4 sin²(πx/2) cos²(πx/2) = sin²(πx). For
    x ≤ 1/2, h(T(x)) = h(2x) = sin²(πx); for x ≥ 1/2, h(2 − 2x) = sin²(π − πx) =
    sin²(πx). ✓ h is a homeomorphism of [0, 1] (increasing), so T and L are
    TOPOLOGICALLY CONJUGATE: every orbit property transfers. (b) T fixes 0
    and 2/3 (2 − 2x = x); h(2/3) = sin²(π/3) = 3/4. T has the 2-cycle
    {2/5, 4/5} (T(2/5) = 4/5, T(4/5) = 2 − 8/5 = 2/5); h gives sin²(π/5) =
    (5 − √5)/8 and sin²(2π/5) = (5 + √5)/8 (the formula of D2 with r = 4
    agrees). 3-cycle of T: 2/7 → 4/7 → 6/7 → 2/7, so sin²(π/7) → sin²(2π/7)
    → sin²(3π/7). Both maps have 2ⁿ points of period dividing n. (c) T⁻¹ of
    an interval J is two intervals of half the length, so the total length
    is preserved: Lebesgue measure is invariant. Push it forward by h:
    y = sin²(πx/2), dy = (π/2)sin(πx) dx = π√(y(1 − y)) dx, so x uniform ⇒ y
    has density 1/(π√(y(1 − y))) (the arcsine law). (d) By the ergodic
    theorem the time fraction equals the measure: y ≤ 1/4 ⇔ x ≤ 1/3
    (sin²(π/6) = 1/4), probability 1/3. Common mistakes: expecting the
    orbit to be uniform on [0, 1] (it spends most time near 0 and 1);
    checking the conjugacy only for x ≤ 1/2. Now you try: the time
    fraction in [0, 1/2]. (1/2, by symmetry)

D7. Define the Lyapunov exponent of an orbit of a 1D map. Compute it for
    (a) the tent map, (b) L = 4x(1 − x) (typical orbit), (c) an orbit
    attracted to the fixed point of 2.5x(1 − x), (d) the attracting 2-cycle
    at r = 3.2, (e) the superstable 2-cycle at r = 1 + √5. (f) With λ =
    ln 2, how many steps until an initial error 10⁻¹⁰ grows to 10⁻²?
    Answer: (a) ln 2 ≈ 0.6931; (b) ln 2; (c) ln 0.5 ≈ −0.6931; (d) ½ ln 0.16 =
    ln 0.4 ≈ −0.9163; (e) −∞; (f) about 27 steps.
    Teacher explains: For nearby x, y: |fⁿ(x) − fⁿ(y)| ≈ |(fⁿ)′(x)||x − y| and
    (fⁿ)′(x) = ∏ f′(xₖ), so the average growth rate per step is
       λ(x) = lim (1/n) ∑ₖ₌₀ⁿ⁻¹ ln|f′(xₖ)|.
    (a) |T′| = 2 everywhere (except at 1/2): λ = ln 2. (b) By the ergodic
    theorem λ = ∫₀¹ ln|4 − 8y| ρ(y) dy with ρ from D6; substituting y =
    sin²(πx/2) this becomes ln 2 (the conjugacy h is smooth inside (0, 1),
    and a bounded distortion does not change the exponential rate).
    (c) The terms ln|f′(xₖ)| → ln|f′(0.6)| = ln 0.5, so their average does
    too: λ = −ln 2 < 0 (nearby orbits converge). (d) The average over the
    cycle: (1/2)(ln|f′(p)| + ln|f′(q)|) = (1/2)ln 0.16 = ln 0.4. (e) The cycle
    passes through x = 1/2 where f′ = 0: λ = −∞ (superstable). (f) Error
    ≈ ε₀e^(λn): 10⁻¹⁰·2ⁿ = 10⁻² ⇔ n = 8 ln 10/ln 2 ≈ 26.6, so 27 steps. Each
    extra digit of precision buys only ln 10/λ ≈ 3.3 steps: the
    PREDICTABILITY HORIZON grows logarithmically with precision. Common
    mistakes: averaging f′ instead of ln|f′|; computing λ from a transient
    part of the orbit. Now you try: the map x ↦ 3x mod 1. (ln 3 ≈ 1.0986)

D8. Let Σ₂ be the space of 0-1 sequences s = (s₀s₁s₂...) with d(s, t) =
    ∑ᵢ≥₀ |sᵢ − tᵢ|/2ⁱ and σ the shift. (a) Prove: if sᵢ = tᵢ for i ≤ n then
    d(s, t) ≤ 2⁻ⁿ, and if d(s, t) < 2⁻ⁿ then sᵢ = tᵢ for i ≤ n. (b) Prove σ is
    chaotic in Devaney's sense. (c) How many points of period n does σ
    have? Compute d(0^∞, 1^∞) and d(0^∞, 0001 0^∞).
    Answer: (c) 2ⁿ; d = 2 and 1/8.
    Teacher explains: (a) If the first n + 1 symbols agree, d(s, t) ≤
    ∑ᵢ>ₙ 1/2ⁱ = 2⁻ⁿ. Conversely if sⱼ ≠ tⱼ for some j ≤ n then d ≥ 2⁻ʲ ≥ 2⁻ⁿ.
    So 'close' means 'agree on a long initial block'. σ is continuous
    (agreement on n + 2 places gives agreement of shifts on n + 1).
    (b) DENSE PERIODIC POINTS: given s and n, the periodic point
    (s₀...sₙ)^∞ agrees with s on n + 1 places, so is within 2⁻ⁿ.
    TRANSITIVITY: s* = 0 1 00 01 10 11 000 001 ... (all words listed) has
    a dense orbit: some σᵏ(s*) begins with any given block s₀...sₙ.
    SENSITIVITY with constant 1: given s and n, let t agree with s on
    places 0, ..., n and differ at place n + 1; then d(s, t) ≤ 2⁻ⁿ but
    σⁿ⁺¹(s) and σⁿ⁺¹(t) differ in their first symbol, so their distance is
    ≥ 1. (c) σⁿ(s) = s ⇔ s is the repetition of its first n symbols: 2ⁿ
    choices. d(0^∞, 1^∞) = ∑ 2⁻ⁱ = 2; the other pair differs only at i = 3:
    1/8. Through itineraries (L for x < 1/2, R for x > 1/2) the tent map
    and L₄ are coded by σ, which is why they are chaotic too. Common
    mistakes: indexing from 1 and getting the wrong constants; thinking
    sensitivity means ALL nearby points separate (one is enough). Now you
    try: d((01)^∞, (10)^∞). (2: they differ everywhere)

D9. Define box-counting and similarity dimension. Compute the dimension
    of (a) the middle-thirds Cantor set (check with N(3⁻ⁿ)), (b) the Koch
    curve, (c) the Sierpinski triangle, (d) the self-similar set with two
    pieces scaled by 1/2 and 1/4. (e) Show that the box dimension of
    {1/n : n ∈ N} ∪ {0} is 1/2 although the set is countable.
    Answer: (a) ln 2/ln 3 ≈ 0.6309; (b) ln 4/ln 3 ≈ 1.2619; (c) ln 3/ln 2 ≈
    1.5850; (d) ln φ/ln 2 ≈ 0.6942, φ = (1 + √5)/2; (e) 1/2.
    Teacher explains: BOX dimension: dim_B F = lim_{ε→0} log N(ε)/log(1/ε),
    N(ε) = least number of ε-intervals (boxes) covering F. For a set made
    of m copies of itself scaled by r (no big overlaps), N(rε) = mN(ε), so
    N(ε) ~ ε^(−d) with m rᵈ = 1: d = log m/log(1/r). In general d solves the
    MORAN equation ∑rᵢᵈ = 1. (a) Cantor: 2 copies, r = 1/3; indeed the n-th
    stage consists of 2ⁿ intervals of length 3⁻ⁿ, so N(3⁻ⁿ) = 2ⁿ and
    log 2ⁿ/log 3ⁿ = ln 2/ln 3. (b) Koch: 4 copies of 1/3: ln 4/ln 3; infinite
    length (4/3)ⁿ → ∞, zero area. (c) Sierpinski: 3 copies of 1/2: ln 3/ln 2.
    (d) (1/2)ᵈ + (1/4)ᵈ = 1; put u = (1/2)ᵈ: u + u² = 1, u = (√5 − 1)/2 = 1/φ,
    d = ln φ/ln 2 ≈ 0.6942. (e) With boxes of size ε, the points 1/n with
    1/n − 1/(n + 1) ≈ 1/n² > ε, i.e. n < ε^(−1/2), each need their own box,
    and the rest lie in [0, ε^(1/2)], needing ε^(−1/2) boxes: N(ε) ≍ ε^(−1/2),
    so dim_B = 1/2. Hausdorff dimension (countable ⇒ 0) is better behaved;
    box dimension is what computers estimate. Common mistakes: using the
    NUMBER of pieces removed instead of kept; dividing by log r instead of
    log(1/r) (negative dimensions!). Now you try: the Sierpinski carpet.
    (8 copies of 1/3: ln 8/ln 3 ≈ 1.8928)

D10. For the Lorenz system ẋ = σ(y − x), ẏ = x(ρ − z) − y, ż = xy − βz with
     σ = 10, β = 8/3: (a) find the equilibria and the pitchfork value of ρ;
     (b) find the eigenvalues at the origin for ρ = 28; (c) compute the
     divergence and explain why the attractor has zero volume; (d) the
     Hopf value ρ_H and the eigenvalues at C± for ρ = 28; (e) what is
     special about 24.06 < ρ < 24.74?
     Answer: (a) 0 always; C± = (±√(β(ρ − 1)), ±√(β(ρ − 1)), ρ − 1) for ρ > 1
     (pitchfork at ρ = 1); at ρ = 28: (±6√2, ±6√2, 27) ≈ (±8.485, ±8.485, 27).
     (b) −8/3 and (−11 ± √1201)/2 ≈ 11.83, −22.83: a saddle. (c) −41/3.
     (d) ρ_H = 470/19 ≈ 24.74; eigenvalues ≈ −13.85, 0.094 ± 10.19i.
     (e) stable equilibria coexist with the chaotic attractor.
     Teacher explains: (a) ẋ = 0 ⇒ y = x; ż = 0 ⇒ z = x²/β; ẏ = 0 ⇒ x(ρ − 1 −
     x²/β) = 0. So x = 0 or x² = β(ρ − 1) (needs ρ > 1): a symmetric pair
     born at ρ = 1 (the symmetry (x, y, z) ↦ (−x, −y, z) forces a pitchfork).
     (b) At 0 the Jacobian is [[−σ, σ, 0], [ρ, −1, 0], [0, 0, −β]]: z
     decouples (−β), and the xy-block has λ² + (σ + 1)λ + σ(1 − ρ) = 0, i.e.
     λ² + 11λ − 270 = 0, λ = (−11 ± √1201)/2. One positive eigenvalue: the
     origin is unstable for ρ > 1 (for ρ < 1 it is globally attracting).
     (c) div F = −σ − 1 − β = −41/3 ≈ −13.67 < 0 everywhere, so any volume
     shrinks like e^(−41t/3): the attractor has volume 0 (its dimension is
     ≈ 2.06). (d) At C± the characteristic polynomial is λ³ + (σ + β + 1)λ²
     + β(σ + ρ)λ + 2σβ(ρ − 1). A pair ±iω is a root when (σ + β + 1)β(σ + ρ)
     = 2σβ(ρ − 1), giving ρ_H = σ(σ + β + 3)/(σ − β − 1) = 10(47/3)/(19/3) =
     470/19. Above it (ρ = 28) the complex pair has real part +0.094: C±
     are unstable saddle-foci, so orbits wander forever between the two
     'wings' (the Hopf bifurcation is SUBcritical: no stable cycle takes
     over). (e) For 24.06 < ρ < 24.74 C± are still stable (e.g. ρ = 24:
     real part −0.0225) while the strange attractor already exists:
     bistability and hysteresis. Common mistakes: forgetting the z-equation
     in the Jacobian; claiming chaos because 'there are no stable
     equilibria' (chaos needs more; a limit cycle is also possible, as
     for large ρ). Now you try: ρ = 10: nature of C±. (≈ 4.899 in x and y,
     z = 9; eigenvalues −12.48, −0.60 ± 6.17i: stable spirals)

D11. The Hénon map H(x, y) = (1 − ax² + y, bx) with a = 1.4, b = 0.3.
     (a) Find the fixed points and their eigenvalues. (b) Show H contracts
     areas and is invertible, and find H⁻¹. (c) Find the 2-cycle.
     (d) Given λ₁ ≈ 0.42, find λ₂ and the Kaplan-Yorke dimension. (e) For
     which a does the fixed point with x > 0 first lose stability?
     Answer: (a) (0.6314, 0.1894): eigenvalues −1.924, 0.156; (−1.1314,
     −0.3394): 3.260, −0.092 (both saddles). (b) det J = −b = −0.3;
     H⁻¹(u, v) = (v/b, u − 1 + a v²/b²). (c) (−0.4758, 0.2927) ↔ (0.9758,
     −0.1427). (d) λ₂ = ln 0.3 − λ₁ ≈ −1.62; D ≈ 1.26. (e) a = 3(1 − b)²/4 =
     0.3675 (period doubling).
     Teacher explains: (a) y = bx and x = 1 − ax² + bx: ax² + (1 − b)x − 1 = 0,
        x = (−0.7 ± √(0.49 + 5.6))/2.8 = (−0.7 ± 2.4678)/2.8
     = 0.6314 or −1.1314. J = [[−2ax, 1], [b, 0]], characteristic equation
     λ² + 2axλ − b = 0: at x = 0.6314, λ² + 1.7678λ − 0.3 = 0, λ ≈ −1.924 and
     0.156; at x = −1.1314, λ ≈ 3.260 and −0.092. (b) det J = −b for every
     point: each step multiplies areas by 0.3 and reverses orientation;
     after 10 steps a unit area is 0.3¹⁰ ≈ 5.9·10⁻⁶. To invert, solve
     u = 1 − ax² + y, v = bx: x = v/b, y = u − 1 + a(v/b)². (c) H² = id
     off the fixed points gives (by elimination) the pair above; check
     H(−0.4758, 0.2927) = (1 − 1.4·0.2264 + 0.2927, −0.1427) = (0.9758,
     −0.1427). (d) λ₁ + λ₂ = ln|det J| = ln 0.3 ≈ −1.204, so λ₂ ≈ −1.62.
     Kaplan-Yorke: D = 1 + λ₁/|λ₂| ≈ 1 + 0.42/1.62 ≈ 1.26: a fractal 'curve
     times Cantor set'. (e) A real eigenvalue −1 needs 1 − 2ax − b = 0; with
     the fixed-point equation this gives a = 3(1 − b)²/4 = 0.3675; for
     −(1 − b)²/4 < a < 0.3675 that fixed point attracts. Common mistakes:
     taking det J = b (the sign shows orientation reversal); forgetting
     that a 2D saddle can sit INSIDE a strange attractor (its unstable
     manifold traces the attractor). Now you try: the Jacobian determinant
     for b = −1. (1: area-preserving, orientation-preserving)

D12. Consider ṙ = r(μ − r²), θ̇ = 1 in polar coordinates. (a) Describe the
     Hopf bifurcation at μ = 0. (b) Compute the Poincaré (first return) map
     on the ray θ = 0 and the Floquet multiplier of the limit cycle.
     Evaluate for μ = 1/4. (c) State the Poincaré-Bendixson theorem and
     explain why chaos is impossible for planar flows.
     Answer: (a) for μ > 0 a stable cycle r = √μ (supercritical Hopf).
     (b) P(r) = [1/μ + (1/r² − 1/μ)e^(−4πμ)]^(−1/2), P′(√μ) = e^(−4πμ); for
     μ = 1/4: e^(−π) ≈ 0.0432. (c) see below.
     Teacher explains: (a) In Cartesian form the linearisation at 0 has
     eigenvalues μ ± i: they cross the imaginary axis at μ = 0 with speed
     1. For μ < 0 the origin is a stable spiral; for μ > 0 it is an unstable
     spiral, and ṙ = 0 at r = √μ, with ṙ > 0 inside and ṙ < 0 outside: a
     stable limit cycle of radius √μ is born (SUPERcritical Hopf; the
     amplitude grows like √μ). (b) The return time is 2π since θ̇ = 1.
     Put u = 1/r²: u̇ = −2ṙ/r³ = −2μu + 2, so
        u(t) = 1/μ + (u(0) − 1/μ)e^(−2μt),  P(r) = u(2π)^(−1/2).
     Fixed point r = √μ (u = 1/μ); since u(2π) − 1/μ = e^(−4πμ)(u(0) − 1/μ)
     and r ↦ u is smooth with the same factor at the fixed point, P′(√μ) =
     e^(−4πμ) < 1: the cycle attracts, and the Floquet exponent is −2μ.
     For μ = 1/4: e^(−π) ≈ 0.0432, so perturbations shrink by 96% per lap.
     (c) POINCARÉ-BENDIXSON: if a forward orbit of a C¹ planar flow stays in
     a compact region containing finitely many equilibria, its ω-limit set
     is an equilibrium, a periodic orbit, or equilibria joined by
     connecting orbits. Hence there are no strange attractors in the
     plane: chaos needs a 3D flow (Lorenz, Rössler), a 2D invertible map
     (Hénon) or a 1D non-invertible map (logistic). A trapping annulus
     with no equilibria must contain a periodic orbit (here any annulus
     a < r < b with a < √μ < b). Common mistakes: forgetting to check that
     the annulus contains no equilibria; confusing the Floquet MULTIPLIER
     e^(−4πμ) with the exponent −2μ. Now you try: ṙ = r(1 − r)(2 − r), θ̇ = 1:
     the cycles and their stability. (r = 1 stable, r = 2 unstable)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the fixed points and classify them: A (attracting), R
    (repelling), N (neutral; say how it behaves), S (superattracting).
    a) x/2 + 1                          b) 3x − 2
    c) x²                               d) x² − 2
    e) x³                               f) cos x
    g) eˣ − 1                           h) 2.5x(1 − x)
    i) 3.2x(1 − x)                      j) x − x³
    k) x + x³                           l) arctan x
    m) 1/x on (0, ∞)                    n) x² + 1/4
    o) 4x(1 − x)                        p) x − (x² − 2)/(2x) (Newton for √2)
    q) ax(1 − x), general a > 0         r) x² − x
    Answers:
      a) 2, A (f′ = 1/2)                 b) 1, R (3)
      c) 0 S; 1 R (2)                    d) 2 R (4); −1 R (−2)
      e) 0 S; ±1 R (3)                   f) ≈ 0.7391, A (−0.6736)
      g) 0, N: attracts from the left, repels to the right
      h) 0 R (2.5); 0.6 A (−0.5)          i) 0 R; 0.6875 R (−1.2)
      j) 0, N but attracting (slowly)    k) 0, N, repelling
      l) 0, N but attracting             m) 1, N; every other point has
                                            period 2 (neither A nor R)
      n) 1/2, N: semi-stable (attracts from the left)
      o) 0 R (4); 3/4 R (−2)              p) ±√2, S (Newton: f′ = 0)
      q) 0 (A for a < 1); 1 − 1/a (A for 1 < a < 3, R for a > 3)
      r) 0, N (f′ = −1) but attracting (f²(x) = x − 2x³ + x⁴); 2 R (3)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw cobweb diagrams by hand for every map before
  computing, and iterate on a spreadsheet or in Python to SEE attraction,
  period doubling and chaos; plot the bifurcation diagram of the
  logistic map and mark r = 3, 3.449, 3.544, 3.57 and 3.83 on it. Always
  factor out the fixed points before solving for 2-cycles, and compute a
  multiplier as the product of f′ around the cycle. For dimensions, draw
  the first two stages and count pieces and ratios. Harder: prove
  Sharkovskii's theorem in full; show that the tent map and the shift
  are semi-conjugate and use it to prove Devaney chaos for T and L₄;
  compute Lyapunov exponents numerically (QR method) for Hénon and Lorenz
  and compare with the Kaplan-Yorke formula; prove the Lorenz trapping
  region V = x² + y² + (z − ρ − σ)² ≤ C; derive the renormalisation
  picture behind Feigenbaum's δ; and study the Smale horseshoe as the
  mechanism of chaos near a transverse homoclinic point.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.14 — GRAPH THEORY (ADVANCED) AND ALGORITHMS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - BASICS: G = (V, E), n = |V|, e = |E|; HANDSHAKE ∑deg v = 2e; degree
    sequences and the HAVEL-HAKIMI test for graphicality; BIPARTITE ⇔ no
    odd cycle; walks counted by powers of the ADJACENCY matrix A.
  - TREES: connected + acyclic ⇔ connected with n − 1 edges ⇔ acyclic with
    n − 1 edges ⇔ a unique path between any two vertices. CAYLEY: n^(n−2)
    labelled trees on n vertices, via the PRÜFER code (vertex v appears
    deg v − 1 times). MATRIX-TREE THEOREM: τ(G) = any cofactor of the
    LAPLACIAN L = D − A = (1/n)·(product of the non-zero eigenvalues of L);
    τ(Kₙ) = n^(n−2), τ(K_{m,n}) = m^(n−1)n^(m−1), τ(Cₙ) = n.
  - CONNECTIVITY: κ(G) (vertex) ≤ λ(G) (edge) ≤ δ(G) (WHITNEY), with strict
    inequalities possible. MENGER: the least number of vertices (edges)
    separating u, v equals the most internally vertex-disjoint (edge-
    disjoint) u-v paths; G is k-connected ⇔ any two vertices are joined by
    k internally disjoint paths. Cut-vertices, bridges, blocks.
  - EULER AND HAMILTON: a connected (multi)graph has an EULER circuit ⇔
    every degree is even, an Euler trail ⇔ 0 or 2 odd vertices
    (HIERHOLZER's algorithm, O(e)); CHINESE POSTMAN: add a minimum pairing
    of odd vertices. HAMILTON cycles: NP-complete in general; DIRAC (δ ≥
    n/2) and ORE (deg u + deg v ≥ n for non-adjacent u, v) are sufficient;
    removing any k vertices leaves ≤ k components is necessary. The
    Petersen graph is not Hamiltonian.
  - MATCHINGS: BERGE: a matching is maximum ⇔ there is no AUGMENTING path.
    HALL: a bipartite G with sides X, Y has a matching saturating X ⇔
    |N(S)| ≥ |S| for all S ⊆ X. KÖNIG: in bipartite graphs ν = τ (maximum
    matching = minimum vertex cover). TUTTE: G has a perfect matching ⇔
    odd(G − S) ≤ |S| for all S; PETERSEN: cubic bridgeless graphs have
    one. GALE-SHAPLEY: proposals give a STABLE matching, optimal for the
    proposers. #perfect matchings: K₂ₙ: (2n − 1)!!, K_{n,n}: n!.
  - NETWORK FLOWS: capacities c, flow conservation, value |f|; MAX-FLOW
    MIN-CUT: max |f| = min capacity of an s-t cut; FORD-FULKERSON
    (augment along paths in the RESIDUAL graph), EDMONDS-KARP (shortest
    augmenting paths, O(ve²)); INTEGRALITY: integer capacities ⇒ an integer
    maximum flow. Flows prove Menger, Hall and König.
  - PLANARITY: EULER'S FORMULA v − e + f = 2 (connected plane graph); e ≤
    3v − 6 (v ≥ 3), e ≤ 2v − 4 if triangle-free, e ≤ g(v − 2)/(g − 2) for
    girth g; so K₅, K₃,₃ and Petersen are non-planar; every planar graph
    has a vertex of degree ≤ 5. KURATOWSKI/WAGNER: planar ⇔ no subdivision
    (minor) of K₅ or K₃,₃. Crossing numbers (cr K₅ = 1, cr K₆ = 3); genus
    (γ(Kₙ) = ⌈(n − 3)(n − 4)/12⌉).
  - COLOURING: ω ≤ χ ≤ Δ + 1 (GREEDY); BROOKS: χ ≤ Δ unless G is complete
    or an odd cycle; χ ≥ n/α; MYCIELSKI: triangle-free graphs with any χ
    (Grötzsch graph: χ = 4); FIVE-COLOUR theorem (Kempe chains) and the
    FOUR-COLOUR theorem for planar graphs. EDGE colouring: VIZING Δ ≤ χ′ ≤
    Δ + 1; KÖNIG: χ′ = Δ for bipartite; χ′(Kₙ) = n − 1 (n even), n (n odd);
    χ′(Petersen) = 4.
  - CHROMATIC POLYNOMIAL P(G, t) = number of proper t-colourings;
    DELETION-CONTRACTION P(G) = P(G − e) − P(G/e); P(Kₙ) = t(t − 1)...(t −
    n + 1), trees t(t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹, cycles (t − 1)ⁿ + (−1)ⁿ(t − 1); monic of degree
    n, second coefficient −e, alternating signs, χ = least t with P > 0.
  - ALGORITHMS: BFS (unweighted distances, bipartiteness, O(v + e)); DFS
    (components, cut-vertices, topological sort of a DAG); DIJKSTRA (non-
    negative weights, O(e log v) with a heap); BELLMAN-FORD (negative
    weights, detects negative cycles, O(ve)); FLOYD-WARSHALL (all pairs,
    O(v³)); MINIMUM SPANNING TREES by KRUSKAL (sort edges, union-find) and
    PRIM (grow a tree), justified by the CUT property; P versus NP.
  - SPECTRAL GRAPH THEORY: eigenvalues of A: Kₙ: n − 1, (−1)ⁿ⁻¹; Cₙ:
    2cos(2πk/n); K_{m,n}: ±√(mn), 0; Petersen: 3, 1⁵, (−2)⁴; Qₙ: n − 2k with
    multiplicity C(n, k). tr Aᵏ = number of closed walks of length k
    (triangles = tr A³/6, edges = tr A²/2); bipartite ⇔ spectrum symmetric;
    λ₁ ≥ average degree; WILF χ ≤ 1 + λ₁; HOFFMAN α ≤ n(−λ_min)/(d − λ_min)
    for d-regular graphs.
  - EXTREMAL AND RAMSEY THEORY: MANTEL ex(n, K₃) = ⌊n²/4⌋; TURÁN: the
    largest Kᵣ₊₁-free graph is the balanced r-partite T(n, r), with about
    (1 − 1/r)n²/2 edges. RAMSEY: R(3, 3) = 6, R(3, 4) = 9, R(3, 5) = 14,
    R(4, 4) = 18; R(s, t) ≤ R(s − 1, t) + R(s, t − 1), R(k, k) ≤ C(2k − 2,
    k − 1); ERDŐS (probabilistic method): R(k, k) > 2^(k/2).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove that a tree with n vertices has n − 1 edges and that any two
    of 'connected', 'acyclic', 'n − 1 edges' imply the third. (b) Find the
    Prüfer code of the tree on {1, ..., 7} with edges 12, 13, 34, 35, 56,
    57. (c) Decode (4, 4, 1, 3). (d) Explain why the Prüfer map proves
    Cayley's formula, and count the trees on {1, ..., 6} in which vertex 1
    has degree 3.
    Answer: (b) (1, 3, 3, 5, 5). (c) edges 24, 54, 41, 13, 36. (d) 6⁴ = 1296
    trees in all; C(4, 2)·5² = 150 with deg 1 = 3.
    Teacher explains: (a) A finite tree with n ≥ 2 vertices has a LEAF (the
    end of a longest path has no other neighbour, or there would be a
    longer path or a cycle). Remove it: a tree on n − 1 vertices, so by
    induction e = n − 1. Conversely: a connected graph has a spanning tree
    (delete edges on cycles), with n − 1 edges; so connected + n − 1 edges
    means nothing was deleted: acyclic. An acyclic graph with c components
    has n − c edges; n − 1 edges forces c = 1. (b) Repeatedly delete the
    SMALLEST leaf and write down its neighbour:
       leaves 2, 4, 6, 7: delete 2, write 1
       leaves 1, 4, 6, 7: delete 1, write 3
       delete 4, write 3;  now 3 is a leaf: delete 3, write 5
       delete 6, write 5;  stop at 2 vertices (5, 7):  code (1, 3, 3, 5, 5).
    (c) n = 6. Degrees = 1 + occurrences: d(1) = 2, d(2) = 1, d(3) = 2,
    d(4) = 3, d(5) = 1, d(6) = 1. For each code entry join it to the smallest
    current leaf, then lower both degrees:
       4: leaf 2 → edge 24;  4: leaf 5 → edge 54;  1: leaf 4 → edge 41;
       3: leaf 1 → edge 13;  the last two leaves 3, 6 → edge 36.
    (d) Encoding and decoding are inverse maps between labelled trees on
    n vertices and sequences in {1, ..., n}ⁿ⁻², so there are nⁿ⁻² trees:
    6⁴ = 1296. Vertex 1 has degree 3 ⇔ it appears exactly twice in the code:
    choose the 2 places (C(4, 2) = 6) and fill the other two with any of
    the 5 other labels: 6·25 = 150. Common mistakes: deleting the leaf's
    label instead of writing its NEIGHBOUR; stopping with 1 vertex left
    (stop at 2). Now you try: decode (3, 3, 3, 3). (the star with centre 3)

D2. State the matrix-tree theorem. (a) Use it to count the spanning trees
    of K₄ − e. (b) Check with the Laplacian eigenvalues. (c) Derive
    τ(Kₙ) = nⁿ⁻² and τ(K_{m,n}) = mⁿ⁻¹nᵐ⁻¹ from the spectra. (d) How do the
    adjacency eigenvalues count closed walks and triangles?
    Answer: (a) 8. (b) L-spectrum 0, 2, 4, 4: (2·4·4)/4 = 8. (c) Kₙ: 0, nⁿ⁻¹;
    K_{m,n}: 0, mⁿ⁻¹, nᵐ⁻¹, m + n. (d) tr Aᵏ = ∑λᵢᵏ; triangles = tr A³/6.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM (Kirchhoff): if L = D − A is the Laplacian,
    every cofactor of L equals τ(G), and τ(G) = (1/n)μ₂μ₃...μₙ where 0 = μ₁ ≤
    μ₂ ≤ ... are the eigenvalues of L. (a) Label the vertices so that 1, 2
    are the non-adjacent pair: degrees 2, 2, 3, 3 and
       L = [[2, 0, −1, −1], [0, 2, −1, −1], [−1, −1, 3, −1], [−1, −1, −1, 3]].
    Delete row 1 and column 1:
       det [[2, −1, −1], [−1, 3, −1], [−1, −1, 3]] = 2·8 − 1·4 − 1·4 = 8.
    Direct check: K₄ has 16 spanning trees and by symmetry each of the 6
    edges lies in 16·3/6 = 8 of them, so 16 − 8 = 8 avoid the deleted edge
    ✓. (b) L(K₄ − e) has eigenvalues 0, 2, 4, 4 (vector (1, −1, 0, 0) for 2),
    and (2·4·4)/4 = 8 ✓. (c) L(Kₙ) = nI − J has eigenvalues 0 and n (n − 1
    times): τ = nⁿ⁻¹/n = nⁿ⁻². For K_{m,n}: 0, m (n − 1 times), n (m − 1
    times), m + n, so τ = mⁿ⁻¹nᵐ⁻¹(m + n)/(m + n) = mⁿ⁻¹nᵐ⁻¹; e.g. K₃,₃: 81.
    (d) (Aᵏ)ᵤᵥ counts u-v walks of length k, so tr Aᵏ = ∑λᵢᵏ counts closed
    walks; each triangle gives 6 closed walks of length 3 (3 starts × 2
    directions). K₅: tr A³ = 4³ + 4(−1)³ = 60, so 10 triangles ✓. Common
    mistakes: taking the determinant of L itself (always 0); forgetting
    the factor 1/n in the eigenvalue formula. Now you try: τ(K₂,₃).
    (2²·3¹ = 12)

D3. (a) Prove that a connected multigraph has an Euler circuit iff every
    vertex has even degree. (b) Why is there no walk crossing each bridge
    of Königsberg once? (c) Solve the Chinese postman problem for K₄ with
    unit edge lengths. (d) Prove DIRAC's theorem: if n ≥ 3 and δ ≥ n/2 then
    G is Hamiltonian. (e) Show K₂,₃ is not Hamiltonian.
    Answer: (b) all four land masses have odd degree (5, 3, 3, 3). (c) 8.
    (e) removing the 2 vertices of one side leaves 3 components.
    Teacher explains: (a) ⇒: each passage through a vertex uses two edges.
    ⇐ (HIERHOLZER): starting anywhere, walk along unused edges; with all
    degrees even we can only get stuck at the start, giving a closed trail
    C. If C misses edges, some vertex of C (connectedness) has unused edges;
    all degrees in G − E(C) are still even, so a closed trail starts there;
    splice it into C and repeat. (b) An Euler trail needs 0 or 2 odd
    vertices; Königsberg has 4. (c) K₄ has 4 vertices of degree 3; the
    postman must repeat edges so as to make all degrees even. The cheapest
    is to pair the odd vertices by 2 disjoint edges (length 1 each): total
    6 + 2 = 8. (d) Suppose not, and add edges while keeping G non-
    Hamiltonian (δ ≥ n/2 is preserved) until adding any edge uv creates a
    Hamilton cycle. G ≠ Kₙ, so pick non-adjacent u, v; then G has a Hamilton
    PATH u = x₁, x₂, ..., xₙ = v. Let S = {i : u ~ xᵢ₊₁} and T = {i : v ~ xᵢ},
    subsets of {1, ..., n − 1} with |S| + |T| ≥ n. So some i lies in both,
    and
       x₁ → xᵢ₊₁ → xᵢ₊₂ → ... → xₙ → xᵢ → xᵢ₋₁ → ... → x₁
    is a Hamilton cycle: contradiction. (e) If a Hamilton cycle C exists,
    deleting k vertices from C leaves at most k paths, so G − S has at most
    |S| components. In K₂,₃, deleting the 2-side leaves 3 isolated
    vertices. Similarly the Petersen graph is not Hamiltonian: a Hamilton
    10-cycle plus the remaining perfect matching would give a 3-edge-
    colouring, but χ′(Petersen) = 4. Common mistakes: using Dirac's
    condition as necessary (C₅ has δ = 2 < 5/2 and is Hamiltonian);
    confusing Euler (edges) with Hamilton (vertices). Now you try: the
    Chinese postman length of the cube Q₃. (12 + 4 = 16)

D4. Four workers and four jobs: W1 can do J1, J2; W2: J1; W3: J2, J3, J4;
    W4: J1, J2. (a) Start from the matching {W1J1, W3J2} and find an
    augmenting path. (b) Show that no perfect matching exists using Hall's
    condition. (c) Find a maximum matching and a minimum vertex cover and
    check König's theorem. (d) Prove Hall's theorem.
    Answer: (a) W2 − J1 − W1 − J2 − W3 − J3. (b) S = {W1, W2, W4} has N(S) =
    {J1, J2}. (c) ν = 3 (e.g. W2J1, W1J2, W3J3); cover {J1, J2, W3}.
    Teacher explains: An AUGMENTING path joins two unmatched vertices,
    alternating non-matching / matching edges. (a) W2 is unmatched and J3
    is unmatched:
       W2 −(new)− J1 −(old)− W1 −(new)− J2 −(old)− W3 −(new)− J3.
    Swapping old and new edges gives {W2J1, W1J2, W3J3}, one edge larger
    (BERGE: a matching is maximum iff no augmenting path exists). (b) W1,
    W2, W4 can only do J1 and J2: three workers, two jobs: |N(S)| < |S|. So
    ν ≤ 3, and (a) achieves 3. (c) The cover {J1, J2, W3} meets every edge
    (all edges go to J1 or J2 except those at W3), size 3 = ν ✓. In any
    graph ν ≤ τ (each matching edge needs its own cover vertex); KÖNIG says
    equality holds for bipartite graphs (it fails for K₃: ν = 1, τ = 2).
    (d) Hall ⇒: obvious. ⇐ by induction on |X|. Case 1: |N(S)| ≥ |S| + 1 for
    every non-empty proper S ⊂ X: match any x to a neighbour y, delete both;
    Hall's condition still holds, so induct. Case 2: some proper S has
    |N(S)| = |S|: match S into N(S) by induction; in the graph on X ∖ S and
    Y ∖ N(S), for T ⊆ X ∖ S,
    |N(T) ∖ N(S)| = |N(T ∪ S)| − |N(S)| ≥ |T ∪ S| − |S| = |T|, so Hall holds
    there too; combine the two matchings. Common mistakes: checking Hall only
    for single vertices and pairs; thinking a maximal (cannot be extended)
    matching is maximum. Now you try: can the 3×3 grid (9 vertices) have a
    perfect matching? (no: n is odd; ν = 4)

D5. A network has capacities s→a 10, s→b 8, a→b 4, a→c 6, b→d 9, d→c 3,
    c→t 9, d→t 5. (a) Run Ford-Fulkerson with the augmenting paths s-a-c-t,
    s-b-d-t, s-b-d-c-t. (b) Find the minimum cut from the final residual
    graph. (c) Prove weak duality |f| ≤ c(S, T) and the max-flow min-cut
    theorem. (d) Why may Ford-Fulkerson be slow, and how does Edmonds-Karp
    fix it?
    Answer: (a) 6 + 5 + 3 = 14. (b) S = {s, a, b, d}: cut a→c, d→c, d→t of
    capacity 6 + 3 + 5 = 14 (the cut c→t, d→t is also minimum).
    Teacher explains: (a) Keep a table of residual capacities.
       path s-a-c-t:     bottleneck min(10, 6, 9) = 6
       path s-b-d-t:     bottleneck min(8, 9, 5) = 5
       path s-b-d-c-t:   residuals s→b 3, b→d 4, d→c 3, c→t 3: bottleneck 3
    Flow value 14. (b) Residual graph now: s→a 4, a→b 4, b→d 1 are still
    usable, but a→c, d→c, d→t are saturated. Vertices reachable from s:
    S = {s, a, b, d}; t is not reachable, so no augmenting path exists.
    The edges from S to T = {c, t} are a→c (6), d→c (3), d→t (5): capacity
    14 = |f|. (c) For any cut (S, T) with s ∈ S, t ∈ T, summing conservation
    over S gives |f| = f(S → T) − f(T → S) ≤ c(S, T). If there is no augmenting
    path, let S = vertices reachable in the residual graph: every edge
    S → T is saturated and every edge T → S carries 0, so |f| = c(S, T): the
    flow is maximum and the cut minimum. Conversely a maximum flow has no
    augmenting path. So max flow = min cut. With integer capacities each
    augmentation adds an integer: INTEGRALITY. (d) With capacities like
    10⁶ and a middle edge of capacity 1, bad path choices augment by 1 each
    time (2·10⁶ steps; with irrational capacities it may not even
    terminate). EDMONDS-KARP always augments along a SHORTEST path (BFS):
    at most ve/2 augmentations, O(ve²) time. Common mistakes: forgetting
    backward residual edges; reporting a cut whose capacity counts edges
    from T to S. Now you try: s→a 3, s→b 2, a→b 1, a→t 2, b→t 3. (5)

D6. (a) Prove Whitney's inequalities κ(G) ≤ λ(G) ≤ δ(G). (b) Find κ, λ, δ for
    two K₄'s sharing a vertex, two K₄'s joined by one edge, and two K₅'s
    sharing two vertices. (c) State Menger's theorem and derive the edge
    version from max-flow min-cut. (d) Why does the Petersen graph have
    3 disjoint paths between any two vertices?
    Answer: (b) (1, 3, 3), (1, 1, 3), (2, 4, 4). (d) κ(Petersen) = 3.
    Teacher explains: (a) λ ≤ δ: the edges at a vertex of minimum degree
    form a disconnecting set. κ ≤ λ: let F be a minimum edge cut with sides
    S, T. If all S-T pairs are adjacent, λ = |S||T| ≥ n − 1 ≥ κ. Otherwise
    take non-adjacent x ∈ S, y ∈ T; for each edge of F delete its end in S,
    or its end in T if the S-end is x. At most λ vertices go, neither x nor
    y, and every x-y path is cut: κ ≤ λ. (b) Two K₄'s sharing a vertex:
    removing the shared vertex disconnects (κ = 1), but an edge cut must
    separate one K₄ minus the cut vertex: 3 edges (λ = 3 = δ). Two K₄'s
    joined by an edge: that edge is a bridge (λ = 1) and its end is a
    cut-vertex (κ = 1); δ = 3. Two K₅'s sharing 2 vertices: removing the
    2 shared vertices disconnects (κ = 2); every vertex has degree ≥ 4 and
    separating the non-shared part of one K₅ costs ≥ 4 edges (λ = δ = 4).
    (c) MENGER (edge form): the maximum number of edge-disjoint u-v paths
    equals the minimum number of edges whose removal separates u from v.
    Replace each edge by two opposite arcs of capacity 1; an integral
    maximum flow decomposes into that many edge-disjoint paths, and a
    minimum cut is a minimum separating set. The vertex form follows by
    splitting each vertex into an in-copy and an out-copy joined by an arc
    of capacity 1. (d) The Petersen graph is 3-regular with κ = 3 (no two
    vertices disconnect it), so by Menger any two vertices are joined by
    3 internally disjoint paths. Common mistakes: assuming κ = λ = δ always;
    in Menger forgetting 'internally' (paths may share their ends). Now
    you try: κ, λ, δ of the bowtie (two triangles sharing a vertex).
    ((1, 2, 2))

D7. (a) Prove Euler's formula v − e + f = 2 for connected plane graphs.
    (b) Deduce e ≤ 3v − 6, and e ≤ 2v − 4 if there are no triangles.
    (c) Show K₅, K₃,₃ and the Petersen graph are non-planar. (d) Show every
    planar graph has a vertex of degree at most 5. (e) State Kuratowski's
    and Wagner's theorems.
    Answer: (c) K₅: 10 > 9; K₃,₃: 9 > 8; Petersen (girth 5): 15 > 40/3.
    Teacher explains: (a) Induction on e. A tree has e = v − 1 and f = 1:
    v − (v − 1) + 1 = 2. If G has a cycle, delete an edge on it: the two faces
    on its sides merge, so e and f both drop by 1 and v − e + f is unchanged.
    (b) Each face is bounded by ≥ 3 edges (v ≥ 3) and each edge borders ≤ 2
    faces: 3f ≤ 2e. Then 2 = v − e + f ≤ v − e + 2e/3, so e ≤ 3v − 6. Without
    triangles every face has ≥ 4 edges: 4f ≤ 2e gives e ≤ 2v − 4. For girth g:
    e ≤ g(v − 2)/(g − 2). (c) K₅: e = 10 > 3·5 − 6 = 9. K₃,₃ is bipartite (no
    triangles): 9 > 2·6 − 4 = 8. Petersen has girth 5:
    e ≤ 5(10 − 2)/3 = 13.3 < 15. (d) If every degree were ≥ 6 then 2e ≥ 6v,
    e ≥ 3v > 3v − 6. (This is the start of every colouring proof.) (e)
    KURATOWSKI: G is planar iff it contains no subdivision of K₅ or K₃,₃.
    WAGNER: iff it has no K₅ or K₃,₃ MINOR. The Petersen graph contracts to K₅
    (contract the 5 spokes) and contains a subdivision of K₃,₃. Planarity can be
    TESTED in linear time (Hopcroft-Tarjan). Common mistakes: using e ≤ 3v − 6
    to prove planarity (it is only necessary: K₃,₃ satisfies 9 ≤ 12); applying
    Euler's formula to a disconnected graph (then v − e + f = 1 + c). Now you
    try: can a planar graph with 12 vertices have 31 edges? (no: at most 30)

D8. (a) Prove χ(G) ≤ Δ + 1 by the greedy algorithm and state Brooks's
    theorem. (b) Prove the five-colour theorem. (c) Find χ and χ′ for the
    wheels W₅, W₆ (5 or 6 rim vertices plus a hub) and the Petersen graph.
    (d) What do Vizing's and König's edge-colouring theorems say?
    Answer: (c) W₅: χ = 4, χ′ = 5; W₆: χ = 3, χ′ = 6; Petersen: χ = 3, χ′ = 4.
    Teacher explains: (a) Colour the vertices in any order, each with the
    least colour not used by its already-coloured neighbours: at most Δ are
    blocked, so Δ + 1 colours suffice. BROOKS: a connected G that is neither
    complete nor an odd cycle has χ ≤ Δ. (b) Induction on n. By D7(d) there
    is v with deg v ≤ 5. Colour G − v with 5 colours. If v's neighbours use
    ≤ 4 colours, v gets the fifth. Otherwise the neighbours v₁, ..., v₅ (in
    clockwise order) have colours 1, ..., 5. Consider the KEMPE CHAIN: the
    component H₁₃ of the subgraph coloured 1 or 3 containing v₁. If v₃ ∉
    H₁₃, swap 1 ↔ 3 in H₁₃: v₁ becomes 3 and colour 1 is free for v. If
    v₃ ∈ H₁₃, there is a 1-3 path from v₁ to v₃ which, with v, forms a
    closed curve separating v₂ from v₄; so the 2-4 chain from v₂ cannot
    reach v₄, and swapping 2 ↔ 4 there frees colour 2. (c) W₅: the rim C₅
    needs 3 colours and the hub, adjacent to all, a 4th; the hub has
    degree 5 so χ′ ≥ 5, and 5 suffices. W₆: rim C₆ uses 2 colours, hub 3;
    χ′ = Δ = 6. Petersen: it has 5-cycles (χ ≥ 3) and Brooks gives χ ≤ 3;
    χ′ = 4 (it is cubic but has no 3-edge-colouring: 'snark'). (d) VIZING:
    Δ ≤ χ′ ≤ Δ + 1 for simple graphs ('class 1' or 'class 2'). KÖNIG:
    bipartite graphs have χ′ = Δ (so Latin squares exist). Common mistakes:
    thinking the four-colour theorem bounds all graphs (K₅ needs 5);
    confusing χ (vertices) with χ′ (edges). Now you try: χ′(K₅) and χ′(K₆).
    (5; 5)

D9. (a) Prove the deletion-contraction formula P(G) = P(G − e) − P(G/e).
    (b) Find P(C₄) and prove P(Cₙ) = (t − 1)ⁿ + (−1)ⁿ(t − 1). (c) Find
    P(K₄ − e). (d) Prove that P(G, t) is a monic polynomial of degree n whose
    tⁿ⁻¹ coefficient is −e. (e) How many proper 3-colourings do C₅ and the
    Petersen graph have?
    Answer: (b) t⁴ − 4t³ + 6t² − 3t. (c) t(t − 1)(t − 2)². (e) 30; 120.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let e = uv. The t-colourings of G − e split into
    those with c(u) ≠ c(v) (exactly the colourings of G) and those with
    c(u) = c(v) (exactly the colourings of G/e, where u, v are merged). So
    P(G − e) = P(G) + P(G/e). (b) Delete an edge of Cₙ: the path Pₙ, with
    P = t(t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹ (colour one end, then each next vertex avoids 1 colour);
    contract it: Cₙ₋₁. Hence P(Cₙ) = t(t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹ − P(Cₙ₋₁). Induction from
    P(C₃) = t(t − 1)(t − 2) = (t − 1)³ − (t − 1):
       t(t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹ − (t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹ − (−1)ⁿ⁻¹(t − 1) = (t − 1)ⁿ + (−1)ⁿ(t − 1).
    For n = 4: (t − 1)⁴ + (t − 1) = t⁴ − 4t³ + 6t² − 3t. (c) Colour the two
    adjacent degree-3 vertices (t(t − 1) ways); each of the other two
    vertices sees exactly those two colours: (t − 2) choices each. So
    t(t − 1)(t − 2)². (d) Induction on e: for e = 0, P = tⁿ. G − e has n
    vertices and e − 1 edges: tⁿ − (e − 1)tⁿ⁻¹ + ...; G/e has n − 1 vertices:
    tⁿ⁻¹ − .... Subtracting gives tⁿ − etⁿ⁻¹ + ..., and the signs keep
    alternating. (e) C₅: 2⁵ − 2 = 30. Petersen: P(3) = 120 (by computer, or
    by fixing the outer 5-cycle's colouring). Common mistakes: adding
    instead of subtracting in deletion-contraction; forgetting that
    contraction may create multiple edges (they can be merged). Now you
    try: P of the path P₄ and of the star K₁,₃. (both t(t − 1)³: all trees on
    n vertices share t(t − 1)ⁿ⁻¹)

D10. Undirected weighted graph: s-a 4, s-b 2, b-a 1, a-c 5, b-c 8, b-d 10,
     c-d 2, c-t 6, d-t 2. (a) Run Dijkstra from s, showing the order in
     which vertices are finalised. (b) Prove Dijkstra's algorithm is
     correct for non-negative weights. (c) In the directed graph s→a 4,
     s→b 5, b→a −3, a→c 2, c→t 1, b→t 6, show that Dijkstra fails and run
     Bellman-Ford. (d) State the complexities.
     Answer: (a) order s 0, b 2, a 3, c 8, d 10, t 12; shortest s-t path
     s-b-a-c-d-t. (c) Dijkstra gives d(t) = 7; the truth is 5 (s-b-a-c-t).
     Teacher explains: (a) Keep tentative distances; repeatedly finalise the
     unfinished vertex with the smallest one and relax its edges.
        finalise s (0):  a 4, b 2
        finalise b (2):  a min(4, 3) = 3, c 10, d 12
        finalise a (3):  c min(10, 8) = 8
        finalise c (8):  d min(12, 10) = 10, t 14
        finalise d (10): t min(14, 12) = 12
        finalise t (12)
     Following predecessors back: t ← d ← c ← a ← b ← s. (b) Invariant: when
     u is finalised, d(u) is the true distance. Suppose not, and let P be a
     shortest s-u path; let y be the first vertex of P not yet finalised,
     x its predecessor (finalised, so correct and relaxed). Then d(y) ≤
     dist(y) ≤ dist(u) < d(u) (non-negative weights on the rest of P), so y
     would have been chosen before u: contradiction. (c) Dijkstra
     finalises a with 4 (before b = 5), so the cheaper route s-b-a (2) comes
     too late: c = 6, t = min(7, 11) = 7. BELLMAN-FORD relaxes EVERY edge
     v − 1 = 4 times; after round 1 (in the order listed): a 4, b 5, then
     b→a gives a 2, a→c gives c 4, c→t gives t 5; later rounds change
     nothing. A further change in round v would reveal a NEGATIVE CYCLE.
     (d) Dijkstra O(e log v) (binary heap), Bellman-Ford O(ve), BFS O(v + e),
     Floyd-Warshall O(v³). Common mistakes: finalising a vertex when it is
     first reached rather than when it is the minimum; 'fixing' negative
     weights by adding a constant to every edge (that favours paths with
     fewer edges). Now you try: Dijkstra from 1 in the graph 1-2 7, 1-3 9,
     1-6 14, 2-3 10, 2-4 15, 3-4 11, 3-6 2, 4-5 6, 5-6 9: d(5)? (20, via
     1-3-6-5)

D11. Edges: AB 4, AC 3, BC 2, BD 5, CD 7, CE 8, DE 6, DF 9, EF 1. (a) Prove
     the cut property: for any cut, a lightest crossing edge lies in some
     minimum spanning tree (in every one if weights are distinct).
     (b) Run Kruskal and Prim (from A). (c) Explain union-find and the
     running times. (d) Is the MST unique?
     Answer: (b) MST {EF, BC, AC, BD, DE}, weight 17, for both. (d) yes
     (distinct weights).
     Teacher explains: (a) Let T be an MST and e = uv a lightest edge across
     the cut (S, V ∖ S). If e ∉ T, the path in T from u to v crosses the cut
     at some edge f; T − f + e is a spanning tree of weight ≤ w(T) since
     w(e) ≤ w(f). So some MST contains e; with distinct weights w(e) < w(f)
     would contradict minimality, so every MST contains e. (b) KRUSKAL:
     sort and add unless a cycle forms:
        EF 1 ✓, BC 2 ✓, AC 3 ✓, AB 4 ✗ (A-C-B), BD 5 ✓, DE 6 ✓ — 5 edges, stop.
     Total 1 + 2 + 3 + 5 + 6 = 17. PRIM from A: always add the lightest edge
     leaving the current tree:
        AC 3, CB 2, BD 5, DE 6, EF 1           total 17.
     Each step of each algorithm adds a lightest edge across some cut, so
     (a) proves both correct. (c) Kruskal needs to know whether u and v are
     already connected: UNION-FIND with union by rank and path compression
     makes this almost O(1), so the sort dominates: O(e log e). Prim with a
     binary heap: O(e log v). (d) With distinct weights the MST is unique
     (from (a)). Common mistakes: in Prim choosing the lightest edge at the
     LAST vertex added instead of the whole tree; confusing an MST with a
     shortest-path tree (from A the path to F in the MST has length 3 + 2
     + 5 + 6 + 1 = 17, but A-C-E-F has length 12). Now you try: the MST
     weight of K₄ on p, q, r, s with pq 1, pr 3, ps 4, qr 2, qs 5, rs 6.
     (1 + 2 + 4 = 7)

D12. (a) Prove Mantel's theorem: a triangle-free graph on n vertices has at
     most ⌊n²/4⌋ edges. (b) State Turán's theorem and find the maximum
     number of edges of a K₄-free graph on 10 vertices. (c) Prove
     R(3, 3) = 6. (d) Prove Erdős's bound R(k, k) > 2^(k/2) (k ≥ 3).
     Answer: (b) T(10, 3) with parts 4, 3, 3: 33 edges. (c) 2-colour K₆ ⇒ a
     monochromatic triangle; C₅ and its complement show R > 5. (d) the
     probabilistic method.
     Teacher explains: (a) For an edge uv, triangle-freeness means N(u)
     and N(v) are disjoint: d(u) + d(v) ≤ n. Summing over edges,
     ∑ᵤᵥ(d(u) + d(v)) = ∑ᵥ d(v)² ≤ ne. By Cauchy-Schwarz ∑d(v)² ≥ (2e)²/n,
     so 4e²/n ≤ ne, e ≤ n²/4. Equality: K_{n/2,n/2}. (b) TURÁN: the Kᵣ₊₁-free
     graphs with most edges are the complete r-partite graphs with parts
     as equal as possible, T(n, r). For n = 10, r = 3: parts 4, 3, 3 and
     e = 4·3 + 4·3 + 3·3 = 33. (c) In a red/blue K₆ a vertex v has 5 edges,
     so ≥ 3 of one colour, say red to x, y, z. If any of xy, yz, zx is red we
     have a red triangle with v; otherwise xyz is blue. So R(3, 3) ≤ 6.
     Colour K₅ with the pentagon red and the pentagram blue: no
     monochromatic triangle, so R(3, 3) > 5. (d) Colour K_n randomly, each
     edge red or blue with probability 1/2. A fixed k-set is monochromatic
     with probability 2·2^(−C(k,2)). If C(n, k)·2^(1−C(k,2)) < 1 then some
     colouring has NO monochromatic Kₖ, so R(k, k) > n. For n = ⌊2^(k/2)⌋,
     C(n, k) < nᵏ/k! ≤ 2^(k²/2)/k!, and the expected number is < 2^(1+k/2)/k!
     < 1 for k ≥ 3. E.g. R(10, 10) > 32 (the truth is between 798 and
     23556). We know R(4, 4) = 18 but not R(5, 5) (43 to 46). Common
     mistakes: 'a triangle-free graph is bipartite' (C₅); believing the
     probabilistic proof gives an explicit colouring (it does not). Now
     you try: the maximum number of edges in a triangle-free graph on 7
     vertices. (⌊49/4⌋ = 12, K₃,₄)
