       q) C₆, P₃, P₄, K₂,₃, K₁,₄, Q₃, K₃,₃ (symmetric spectra)

M12. Extremal graph theory and Ramsey numbers.
     a) ex(n, K₃) for n = 5, 6, 7, 10
     b) edges of T(7, 3), T(10, 3), T(8, 4), T(9, 3), T(12, 4)
     c) the Turán estimate (1 − 1/r)n²/2 for n = 10, r = 3
     d) R(3, 3), R(3, 4), R(3, 5), R(4, 4), R(3, 6)
     e) the multicolour number R(3, 3, 3)
     f) the bound C(2k − 2, k − 1) for k = 3, 4, 5
     g) the recursive bound for R(3, 4) from R(2, 4) and R(3, 3)
     h) Erdős: the lower bound for R(10, 10) from 2^(k/2)
     i) least number of monochromatic triangles in a 2-coloured K₆
     j) Erdős-Szekeres: the length of sequence of distinct reals that
        forces an increasing subsequence of length 4 or a decreasing one of
        length 5
     k) Schur number S(2) (largest n such that {1, ..., n} can be
        2-coloured with no monochromatic x + y = z); S(3)
     l) van der Waerden W(2, 3)
     m) ex(4, C₄), ex(5, C₄)
     n) max edges with maximum degree ≤ 3 on 7 vertices
     o) Caro-Wei lower bound ∑1/(d(v) + 1) for α(C₅), α(Petersen)
     Answers:
       a) 6, 9, 12, 25                    b) 16, 33, 24, 27, 54
       c) ≈ 33.3 (exact 33)               d) 6, 9, 14, 18, 18
       e) 17                              f) 6, 20, 70
       g) ≤ 4 + 6 = 10 (in fact 9)        h) R(10, 10) > 32
       i) 2 (Goodman)                     j) 4·3 + 1 = 13
       k) 4; 13                           l) 9
       m) 4, 6                            n) 10 (⌊3·7/2⌋)
       o) 5/3, so α ≥ 2; 5/2, so α ≥ 3

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw every graph and label every vertex before computing;
  for algorithms keep a TABLE with one row per step (tentative distances,
  residual capacities, the set of tree edges) and check the final answer
  by a second method (Kruskal against Prim, max flow against a cut, the
  matrix-tree theorem against a direct count for small graphs). For
  planarity and colouring proofs, first check the degree/edge counts;
  most non-planarity proofs are one line of Euler's formula. Use
  networkx or SageMath to verify spectra, chromatic polynomials and
  spanning-tree counts. Harder: prove Tutte's 1-factor theorem and
  deduce Petersen's theorem; prove Kuratowski's theorem; show that
  Edmonds-Karp makes O(ve) augmentations; prove Brooks's theorem; prove
  Turán's theorem by Zykov symmetrisation; show R(4, 4) ≥ 18 with the
  Paley graph on 17 vertices; implement Edmonds's blossom algorithm for
  matchings in general graphs; and explore expanders through the
  spectral gap (Cheeger's inequality).


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.15 — INFORMATION THEORY: ENTROPY, CODING THEOREMS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - ENTROPY of a discrete random variable X with pmf p: H(X) = −∑ p(x) log₂ p(x)
    bits (0 log 0 = 0; with ln the unit is the nat, 1 nat = log₂ e bits).
    0 ≤ H(X) ≤ log₂|𝒳|, with H = 0 iff X is constant and H = log₂|𝒳| iff X
    is uniform. H depends only on the probabilities, not on the values.
    BINARY ENTROPY h(p) = −p log₂ p − (1 − p) log₂(1 − p): symmetric about
    1/2, concave, h(1/2) = 1, h′(p) = log₂((1 − p)/p).
  - JOINT and CONDITIONAL entropy: H(X, Y) = −∑ p(x, y) log p(x, y),
    H(Y|X) = ∑ p(x) H(Y|X = x). CHAIN RULE H(X, Y) = H(X) + H(Y|X), and
    H(X₁, ..., Xₙ) = ∑ H(Xᵢ|X₁, ..., Xᵢ₋₁). CONDITIONING REDUCES ENTROPY:
    H(X|Y) ≤ H(X) (on average; H(X|Y = y) may exceed H(X)).
    H(f(X)) ≤ H(X).
  - MUTUAL INFORMATION I(X; Y) = H(X) − H(X|Y) = H(X) + H(Y) − H(X, Y)
    = D(p(x, y) ‖ p(x)p(y)) ≥ 0, with equality iff X, Y are independent;
    I(X; X) = H(X). Conditional versions and the chain rule for I.
    The Venn-diagram picture of H(X), H(Y), H(X|Y), H(Y|X), I(X; Y).
  - RELATIVE ENTROPY (Kullback-Leibler divergence) D(p‖q) = ∑ p log(p/q):
    GIBBS' INEQUALITY D(p‖q) ≥ 0 with equality iff p = q (proof by Jensen
    or ln x ≤ x − 1); NOT symmetric and no triangle inequality, so not a
    metric; D = ∞ if q(x) = 0 < p(x). Cross-entropy H(p, q) = H(p) +
    D(p‖q). D(p‖uniform) = log n − H(p).
  - INEQUALITIES: Jensen, log-sum inequality, concavity of H in p, DATA
    PROCESSING (X → Y → Z Markov ⇒ I(X; Z) ≤ I(X; Y)), FANO:
    H(X|Y) ≤ h(Pₑ) + Pₑ log₂(|𝒳| − 1), so Pₑ ≥ (H(X|Y) − 1)/log₂|𝒳|.
  - ASYMPTOTIC EQUIPARTITION PROPERTY (AEP): for i.i.d. Xᵢ,
    −(1/n) log₂ p(X₁, ..., Xₙ) → H in probability (weak law of large
    numbers). TYPICAL SET A_ε⁽ⁿ⁾: P(A) > 1 − ε for large n, |A| ≤ 2^(n(H+ε)),
    |A| ≥ (1 − ε)2^(n(H−ε)); each typical sequence has probability
    ≈ 2^(−nH). The most likely sequence is usually NOT typical.
  - SOURCE CODES: nonsingular ⊃ uniquely decodable (UD) ⊃ PREFIX
    (instantaneous). KRAFT INEQUALITY: a D-ary prefix code with lengths
    lᵢ exists iff ∑ D^(−lᵢ) ≤ 1; McMILLAN: every UD code satisfies it too.
    Canonical construction of a prefix code from admissible lengths.
  - SOURCE CODING THEOREM: every UD code has expected length L ≥ H_D(X);
    SHANNON lengths lᵢ = ⌈log_D(1/pᵢ)⌉ give H ≤ L < H + 1; coding blocks of
    n symbols gives H ≤ Lₙ/n < H + 1/n → H. Equality L = H iff all pᵢ are
    powers of D (dyadic). Cost of a wrong model q: L ≈ H(p) + D(p‖q).
  - HUFFMAN CODING: repeatedly merge the D least likely symbols (add dummy
    symbols of probability 0 so that (n − 1) is divisible by D − 1); it is
    OPTIMAL among all UD codes; lengths need not be unique but L is.
    SHANNON-FANO (split into halves of nearly equal probability),
    SHANNON-FANO-ELIAS (lᵢ = ⌈log 1/pᵢ⌉ + 1, midpoints of the cdf),
    ARITHMETIC coding and Lempel-Ziv (universal) as practical schemes.
  - CHANNELS: a discrete memoryless channel (DMC) is a transition matrix
    p(y|x); CAPACITY C = max over p(x) of I(X; Y). BSC(p): C = 1 − h(p);
    BEC(ε): C = 1 − ε; SYMMETRIC channels: C = log₂|𝒴| − H(row), achieved by
    the uniform input; Z-CHANNEL: C = log₂(1 + (1 − p)p^(p/(1−p))); noisy
    typewriter; BLAHUT-ARIMOTO algorithm in general.
  - CHANNEL CODING THEOREM (Shannon 1948): every rate R < C is achievable
    (codes of length n with 2^(nR) codewords and error probability → 0,
    proved by RANDOM CODING and jointly typical decoding); CONVERSE: rates
    R > C are not (Fano). SOURCE-CHANNEL SEPARATION: send a source over a
    channel iff H < C.
  - CONTINUOUS CASE: differential entropy h(X) = −∫ f log f (can be
    negative; h(aX) = h(X) + log|a|); Gaussian N(μ, σ²): h = ½ log₂(2πeσ²),
    the MAXIMUM for given variance. GAUSSIAN CHANNEL Y = X + Z, power P,
    noise N: C = ½ log₂(1 + P/N) bits per use; band-limited C =
    W log₂(1 + P/(N₀W)) bits/s; WATER-FILLING for parallel channels.
  - RATE-DISTORTION: R(D) = min I(X; X̂) over E d(X, X̂) ≤ D. Bernoulli(p)
    with Hamming distortion: R(D) = h(p) − h(D) for 0 ≤ D ≤ min(p, 1 − p),
    else 0. Gaussian N(0, σ²) with squared error: R(D) = ½ log₂(σ²/D) for
    D ≤ σ², i.e. D(R) = σ²2^(−2R): each bit gains 6.02 dB.
  - ERROR-CORRECTING CODES: Hamming distance, minimum distance d detects
    d − 1 and corrects ⌊(d − 1)/2⌋ errors; HAMMING (sphere-packing) BOUND
    M·∑_{i≤t} C(n, i) ≤ 2ⁿ (PERFECT codes: Hamming (2ʳ − 1, 2ʳ − 1 − r, 3),
    Golay (23, 12, 7)); SINGLETON k ≤ n − d + 1; GILBERT-VARSHAMOV
    existence M ≥ 2ⁿ/∑_{i<d} C(n, i); syndrome decoding of Hamming (7, 4).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Define the entropy H(X) and compute it (in bits) for X taking four
    values with probabilities 1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/8. Interpret the answer as
    an average number of yes/no questions. Prove that 0 ≤ H(X) ≤ log₂ n
    for X with n values, with equality on the right iff X is uniform.
    Answer: H = 7/4 = 1.75 bits; bounds proved by Jensen's inequality.
    Teacher explains: H(X) = ∑ p(x) log₂(1/p(x)), the average SURPRISE.
       H = ½·1 + ¼·2 + ⅛·3 + ⅛·3 = 0.5 + 0.5 + 0.375 + 0.375 = 1.75 bits.
    Questions: 'Is it a?' (settled for half the time after 1 question),
    'Is it b?' (2 questions), 'Is it c?' (3 questions for c and d). The
    average number of questions is exactly 1.75 = H — the best possible,
    as the source coding theorem will show. Lower bound: each term
    −p log p ≥ 0 because 0 < p ≤ 1; the sum is 0 iff every term is 0, i.e.
    every p is 0 or 1: X is constant. Upper bound: log is concave, so by
    Jensen's inequality
       H(X) = E[log₂(1/p(X))] ≤ log₂ E[1/p(X)] = log₂ ∑_{p(x)>0} 1 ≤ log₂ n,
    with equality iff 1/p(X) is constant, i.e. X is uniform on n values.
    Common mistakes: using ln and calling the answer bits (1.75 bits =
    1.213 nats); thinking entropy depends on the VALUES of X (relabelling
    changes nothing); forgetting the convention 0 log 0 = 0. Now you try:
    H of (1/2, 1/4, 1/4). (1.5 bits)

D2. The pair (X, Y) takes values (0, 0), (0, 1), (1, 1) each with
    probability 1/3. Compute H(X), H(Y), H(X, Y), H(X|Y), H(Y|X) and
    I(X; Y). Prove the chain rule H(X, Y) = H(X) + H(Y|X) and check it.
    Answer: H(X) = H(Y) = h(1/3) ≈ 0.91830, H(X, Y) = log₂ 3 ≈ 1.58496,
    H(X|Y) = H(Y|X) = 2/3, I(X; Y) ≈ 0.25163 bits.
    Teacher explains: Table (rows X, columns Y):
                Y = 0    Y = 1
       X = 0     1/3      1/3      p(X = 0) = 2/3
       X = 1      0       1/3      p(X = 1) = 1/3
    So X ~ (2/3, 1/3) and similarly Y ~ (1/3, 2/3):
       h(1/3) = (1/3) log₂ 3 + (2/3) log₂(3/2) = log₂ 3 − 2/3 ≈ 0.91830.
    (X, Y) is uniform on 3 points: H(X, Y) = log₂ 3. Conditional: given
    X = 0, Y is uniform on {0, 1} (1 bit); given X = 1, Y = 1 (0 bits), so
    H(Y|X) = (2/3)·1 + (1/3)·0 = 2/3; by symmetry H(X|Y) = 2/3. Chain rule
    proof: p(x, y) = p(x)p(y|x), so
       −log p(x, y) = −log p(x) − log p(y|x);
    take expectations: H(X, Y) = H(X) + H(Y|X). Check: 0.91830 + 0.66667 =
    1.58496 ✓. Finally I = H(X) − H(X|Y) = 0.91830 − 0.66667 = 0.25163.
    Common mistakes: computing H(Y|X) as H(Y|X = x) for a single x;
    writing H(X|Y) = H(X, Y)/H(Y) (it is a DIFFERENCE, not a ratio). Now you
    try: p(0, 0) = p(0, 1) = 1/4, p(1, 1) = 1/2. (H(X) = 1, H(Y) = 0.81128,
    H(X, Y) = 1.5, I = 0.31128)

D3. Define the relative entropy D(p‖q). Prove Gibbs' inequality D(p‖q) ≥ 0
    with equality iff p = q, and deduce I(X; Y) ≥ 0 and H(X|Y) ≤ H(X).
    Compute D(p‖q) and D(q‖p) for p = (1/2, 1/2), q = (1/4, 3/4).
    Answer: D(p‖q) = 1 − ½ log₂ 3 ≈ 0.20752, D(q‖p) = ¾ log₂ 3 − 1 ≈ 0.18872:
    not symmetric.
    Teacher explains: D(p‖q) = ∑ p(x) log₂(p(x)/q(x)). Use ln y ≤ y − 1
    (equality iff y = 1) with y = q/p, summing over x with p(x) > 0:
       −D(p‖q)·ln 2 = ∑ p ln(q/p) ≤ ∑ p (q/p − 1) = ∑_{p>0} q − 1 ≤ 0.
    Equality needs q/p = 1 wherever p > 0 and no q-mass elsewhere: p = q.
    Now I(X; Y) = ∑ p(x, y) log(p(x, y)/(p(x)p(y))) = D(p_{XY} ‖ p_X p_Y) ≥ 0,
    with equality iff p(x, y) = p(x)p(y) (independence). Since I =
    H(X) − H(X|Y) ≥ 0, conditioning cannot increase entropy on average.
    Numbers:
       D(p‖q) = ½ log₂(½/¼) + ½ log₂(½/¾) = ½ + ½(1 − log₂ 3) = 0.20752
       D(q‖p) = ¼ log₂(¼/½) + ¾ log₂(¾/½) = −¼ + ¾(log₂ 3 − 1) = 0.18872
    Meaning: D(p‖q) is the extra number of bits per symbol paid when data
    from p is coded with a code designed for q. Common mistakes: expecting
    D(p‖q) = D(q‖p); forgetting D = ∞ when q(x) = 0 < p(x); applying Jensen
    to log with the wrong inequality direction. Now you try:
    D((1/2, 1/4, 1/4) ‖ uniform on 3). (log₂ 3 − 1.5 ≈ 0.08496)

D4. State and prove the data processing inequality. Illustrate it with two
    binary symmetric channels BSC(0.1) in series, input uniform. Then
    state Fano's inequality and use it: X is uniform on 4 values and
    H(X|Y) = 1.5 bits; bound the error probability of any guess X̂ = g(Y).
    Answer: I(X; Y) = 0.531 > I(X; Z) = 1 − h(0.18) ≈ 0.31992. Fano gives
    Pₑ ≥ 0.25 (weak form) and Pₑ ≥ 0.3545 (exact form).
    Teacher explains: If X → Y → Z is a Markov chain (Z depends on X only
    through Y), expand I(X; Y, Z) by the chain rule in two ways:
       I(X; Y, Z) = I(X; Z) + I(X; Y|Z) = I(X; Y) + I(X; Z|Y).
    Markov ⇒ I(X; Z|Y) = 0, and I(X; Y|Z) ≥ 0, so I(X; Z) ≤ I(X; Y):
    processing cannot create information. Two BSC(0.1) in series flip a
    bit iff exactly one of them flips: probability 2·0.1·0.9 = 0.18. With
    uniform input, I(X; Y) = 1 − h(0.1) = 1 − 0.469 = 0.531 and
    I(X; Z) = 1 − h(0.18) = 1 − 0.68008 = 0.31992 ≤ 0.531 ✓. FANO: with
    Pₑ = P(X̂ ≠ X), H(X|Y) ≤ h(Pₑ) + Pₑ log₂(|𝒳| − 1). Since h ≤ 1 and
    log₂(|𝒳| − 1) < log₂|𝒳|, Pₑ ≥ (H(X|Y) − 1)/log₂|𝒳| = (1.5 − 1)/2 = 0.25.
    Solving h(Pₑ) + Pₑ log₂ 3 = 1.5 numerically (bisection) gives the
    sharper Pₑ ≥ 0.3545. Common mistakes: applying the inequality to a chain
    that is not Markov; thinking Fano gives an upper bound on the error
    (it is a LOWER bound: no decoder can beat it). Now you try: three
    BSC(0.1) in series. (crossover 0.244, capacity 1 − h(0.244) ≈ 0.19837)

D5. State the AEP and define the typical set. For i.i.d. Bernoulli(0.1)
    bits with n = 100, find H, the approximate size and probability of the
    typical set, the probability of each typical sequence, and compare
    with the all-zeros sequence. What does this say about compression?
    Answer: H = h(0.1) ≈ 0.469; |A| ≈ 2^46.9 ≈ 1.3 × 10¹⁴ out of 2^100
    ≈ 1.27 × 10³⁰ sequences; each typical sequence has probability
    ≈ 2^(−46.9) ≈ 7.6 × 10⁻¹⁵; the all-zeros sequence (probability 0.9^100
    ≈ 2.66 × 10⁻⁵) is the most likely but NOT typical; about 47 bits
    suffice to describe 100 source bits.
    Teacher explains: p(x₁, ..., xₙ) = ∏ p(xᵢ), so −(1/n) log₂ p(Xⁿ) =
    (1/n) ∑ −log₂ p(Xᵢ), an average of i.i.d. variables with mean H. By
    the weak law of large numbers it tends to H in probability: that is
    the AEP. A_ε⁽ⁿ⁾ = {xⁿ : |−(1/n) log₂ p(xⁿ) − H| ≤ ε}. Every typical
    sequence has 2^(−n(H+ε)) ≤ p ≤ 2^(−n(H−ε)); summing, 1 ≥ P(A) ≥
    |A|·2^(−n(H+ε)) gives |A| ≤ 2^(n(H+ε)), and P(A) > 1 − ε gives
    |A| ≥ (1 − ε)2^(n(H−ε)). Here nH = 100·0.46900 = 46.9. A sequence with
    exactly 10 ones has probability 0.9⁹⁰·0.1¹⁰ = 2^(−46.9) exactly, and
    there are C(100, 10) ≈ 1.73 × 10¹³ = 2^43.98 of them (sequences whose
    number of ones is close to 10 make up the rest of the typical set).
    The all-zeros string has −(1/n) log₂ p = −log₂ 0.9 = 0.152, far from
    0.469: it is individually likely but it is only ONE sequence. The
    typical set is a fraction 2^(−53.1) ≈ 10⁻¹⁶ of all sequences yet
    carries almost all the probability: index it with ≈ n(H + ε) bits
    and use a flag bit plus n raw bits for the rest; the average length
    is ≈ nH. Common mistakes: 'typical = most probable'; confusing the
    NUMBER of typical sequences (2^(nH)) with their total PROBABILITY
    (≈ 1). Now you try: Bernoulli(1/2), n = 100: what is the typical set?
    (all 2^100 sequences; no compression)

D6. Prove the Kraft inequality for binary prefix codes and its converse,
    and state McMillan's theorem. Deduce the source coding theorem
    H(X) ≤ L < H(X) + 1 for an optimal code. Apply the Shannon lengths
    ⌈log₂ 1/p⌉ to p = (0.4, 0.3, 0.2, 0.1) and write down a prefix code.
    Answer: lengths 2, 2, 3, 4; Kraft sum 11/16 ≤ 1; code 00, 01, 100,
    1010; L = 2.4, while H ≈ 1.84644.
    Teacher explains: KRAFT. Put the codewords on the infinite binary tree.
    Let l_max be the longest length. A codeword of length l has 2^(l_max − l)
    descendants at depth l_max; for a PREFIX code these descendant sets
    are disjoint, so ∑ 2^(l_max − lᵢ) ≤ 2^(l_max), i.e. ∑ 2^(−lᵢ) ≤ 1.
    CONVERSE: sort l₁ ≤ l₂ ≤ ...; take as the i-th codeword the first lᵢ
    binary digits of the number ∑_{j<i} 2^(−lⱼ) < 1; no codeword is a prefix
    of a later one (the intervals [∑_{j<i}, ∑_{j≤i}) are disjoint).
    McMILLAN: every uniquely decodable code satisfies Kraft too (expand
    (∑ 2^(−lᵢ))ᵏ: it is ≤ k·l_max for all k, impossible if the sum is > 1).
    LOWER BOUND: with r = 2^(−lᵢ)/c, c = ∑ 2^(−lⱼ) ≤ 1,
       L − H = ∑ pᵢ lᵢ + ∑ pᵢ log pᵢ = D(p‖r) − log₂ c ≥ 0.
    UPPER BOUND: lᵢ = ⌈log₂ 1/pᵢ⌉ satisfies ∑ 2^(−lᵢ) ≤ ∑ pᵢ = 1, and
    lᵢ < log₂ 1/pᵢ + 1, so L < H + 1. Numbers: log₂(1/0.4) = 1.32 → 2;
    log₂(1/0.3) = 1.74 → 2; log₂ 5 = 2.32 → 3; log₂ 10 = 3.32 → 4.
       Kraft: 1/4 + 1/4 + 1/8 + 1/16 = 11/16
       Codewords from 0, 0.25 = 0.01₂, 0.5 = 0.100₂, 0.625 = 0.1010₂:
          00, 01, 100, 1010
       L = 0.8 + 0.6 + 0.6 + 0.4 = 2.4;  H = 1.84644;  2.4 < H + 1 ✓
    The Shannon code is not optimal (Huffman gives 1.9, see D7). Common
    mistakes: using ⌊ ⌋ instead of ⌈ ⌉ (Kraft may then fail); believing
    Kraft ≤ 1 makes a GIVEN code prefix (it only says one EXISTS with those
    lengths). Now you try: do binary lengths 1, 2, 2, 3 admit a prefix
    code? (no: 1/2 + 1/4 + 1/4 + 1/8 = 9/8 > 1)

D7. Describe Huffman's algorithm and prove that it is optimal. Construct
    a Huffman code for p = (0.4, 0.2, 0.2, 0.1, 0.1); compute L, H and the
    efficiency. Show that two different sets of lengths are both optimal.
    Answer: L = 2.2 bits; H ≈ 2.12193; efficiency ≈ 96.45%; lengths
    (2, 2, 2, 3, 3) and (1, 2, 3, 4, 4) both give 2.2.
    Teacher explains: ALGORITHM: list the probabilities; merge the two
    smallest into a node; repeat until one node remains; label the two
    branches of each merge 0 and 1. OPTIMALITY (induction on the number of
    symbols): some optimal code has (i) longer words for less likely
    symbols (else swap and L decreases), (ii) the two longest words of equal
    length (else trim the longer), (iii) the two least likely symbols as
    SIBLINGS at the deepest level (swap). Merging those two siblings into
    one symbol of probability p₁ + p₂ gives a code for the reduced source
    with L′ = L − (p₁ + p₂); the reduction is exactly Huffman's first step,
    so optimal for the reduced source ⇔ optimal for the original. Example:
       0.4  0.2  0.2  0.1  0.1   merge 0.1 + 0.1 = 0.2
       0.4  0.2  0.2  0.2        merge two 0.2's = 0.4
       0.4  0.4  0.2             merge 0.4 + 0.2 = 0.6
       0.6  0.4                  merge = 1
    Depending on which 0.2 and which 0.4 are merged (ties), one gets
    lengths (2, 2, 2, 3, 3): L = 0.8 + 0.4 + 0.4 + 0.3 + 0.3 = 2.2, or
    (1, 2, 3, 4, 4): L = 0.4 + 0.4 + 0.6 + 0.4 + 0.4 = 2.2. Both are optimal;
    the first has the smaller VARIANCE of length (good for buffers).
    H = 0.4 log₂ 2.5 + 0.4 log₂ 5 + 0.2 log₂ 10 = 2.12193, efficiency
    H/L = 0.9645. Common mistakes: merging the LARGEST probabilities;
    re-sorting incorrectly after a merge; thinking Huffman lengths are
    ⌈log 1/p⌉ (they are not in general). Now you try: Huffman code for
    (1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/8). (lengths 1, 2, 3, 3; L = H = 1.75)

D8. A source emits i.i.d. bits with P(1) = 0.1. Explain why a symbol-by-
    symbol code needs 1 bit per symbol although H = 0.469. Huffman-code
    blocks of 2 and of 3 symbols and compute the rate per source symbol.
    State the block coding theorem, and describe arithmetic coding as the
    limit of this idea.
    Answer: pairs: L₂ = 1.29, rate 0.645; triples: L₃ = 1.598, rate
    ≈ 0.53267 bits per symbol; H ≤ Lₙ/n < H + 1/n.
    Teacher explains: With 2 symbols every codeword has at least 1 bit,
    so L ≥ 1 for a symbol code: efficiency 0.469. Pairs: probabilities
    00: 0.81, 01: 0.09, 10: 0.09, 11: 0.01. Huffman: merge 0.01 + 0.09 = 0.10;
    merge 0.09 + 0.10 = 0.19; merge 0.19 + 0.81. Lengths 1, 2, 3, 3:
       L₂ = 0.81 + 2·0.09 + 3·0.09 + 3·0.01 = 1.29, i.e. 0.645 per symbol.
    Triples: 000 has 0.729; three strings with one 1 have 0.081; three with
    two 1s have 0.009; 111 has 0.001. Huffman gives lengths 1 (0.729),
    3 (each 0.081) and 5 (the other four):
       L₃ = 0.729 + 3·3·0.081 + 5·(3·0.009 + 0.001) = 0.729 + 0.729 + 0.14
          = 1.598, i.e. 0.53267 per symbol.
    BLOCK THEOREM: the block (X₁, ..., Xₙ) has entropy nH, so an optimal
    code has nH ≤ Lₙ < nH + 1; dividing by n, Lₙ/n → H. The overhead of at
    most 1 bit is shared by n symbols. ARITHMETIC CODING does this without
    building a huge tree: the message is mapped to the subinterval of [0, 1)
    of width p(x₁...xₙ) obtained by repeatedly splitting in proportion to
    the probabilities, and ⌈log₂ 1/p(xⁿ)⌉ + 1 bits identify a point inside
    it; total length < nH + 2. Common mistakes: forgetting to divide the
    block length by n; thinking a better code can go below H (impossible by
    the source coding theorem). Now you try: pairs from P(1) = 1/4. (lengths
    1, 2, 3, 3 on 9/16, 3/16, 3/16, 1/16: L₂ = 27/16, 0.84375 per symbol;
    H = 0.81128)

D9. Define the capacity of a discrete memoryless channel. Derive the
    capacity of the binary symmetric channel BSC(p) and of the binary
    erasure channel BEC(ε), and evaluate for p = 0.1 and ε = 0.2.
    Answer: C_BSC = 1 − h(p) = 0.531; C_BEC = 1 − ε = 0.8 bits per use; both
    achieved by the uniform input.
    Teacher explains: C = max_{p(x)} I(X; Y). BSC: Y = X ⊕ Z with Z ~
    Bernoulli(p) independent of X. Given X = x, Y is Bernoulli with
    entropy h(p), so H(Y|X) = h(p) whatever the input, and
       I(X; Y) = H(Y) − H(Y|X) = H(Y) − h(p) ≤ 1 − h(p),
    with equality when Y is uniform, which happens for uniform X. So
    C = 1 − h(0.1) = 1 − 0.46900 = 0.53100. BEC: Y = X with probability
    1 − ε, Y = e (erased) with probability ε. Let P(X = 1) = π. Given Y = e
    we know nothing (H(X|Y = e) = H(X)); given Y ∈ {0, 1} we know X. So
       H(X|Y) = ε H(X),  I(X; Y) = H(X) − H(X|Y) = (1 − ε) H(X) ≤ 1 − ε,
    maximised by π = 1/2: C = 0.8. Intuition: if the receiver were told
    which symbols are erased, a fraction 1 − ε gets through; Shannon says
    this is achievable WITHOUT feedback. Common mistakes: C = 1 − p for the
    BSC (that is the erasure answer); thinking BSC(0.9) is useless (flip
    all outputs: C = 0.531 again; BSC(1/2) is the useless one). Now you
    try: C of BSC(0.25). (1 − h(1/4) = 2 − ¾ log₂ 3 ≈ 0.18872)

D10. Compute the capacity of the Z-channel in which 0 is always received
     correctly and 1 is received as 0 with probability 1/2. Find the
     optimal input distribution. Then prove that for a SYMMETRIC channel
     (rows permutations of each other, columns permutations of each other)
     C = log₂|𝒴| − H(row), and apply it to the ternary channel whose rows
     are the cyclic shifts of (0.5, 0.3, 0.2).
     Answer: C = log₂(5/4) ≈ 0.32193 with P(X = 1) = 2/5; the ternary
     channel has C = log₂ 3 − H(0.5, 0.3, 0.2) ≈ 0.09949.
     Teacher explains: Z-channel, P(X = 1) = π: Y = 1 iff X = 1 and no
     error, so P(Y = 1) = π/2. H(Y|X) = (1 − π)·0 + π·h(1/2) = π. Hence
        I(π) = h(π/2) − π.
     Differentiate: I′(π) = ½ log₂((1 − π/2)/(π/2)) − 1 = 0 gives
     (1 − π/2)/(π/2) = 4, π/2 = 1/5, π = 2/5. Then
        C = h(1/5) − 2/5 = 0.72193 − 0.4 = 0.32193 = log₂(5/4).
     Not uniform: the noisy input is used LESS. SYMMETRIC channel: H(Y|X =
     x) = H(row) for every x, so I = H(Y) − H(row) ≤ log₂|𝒴| − H(row);
     with uniform input, P(y) = (1/|𝒳|)·(column sum), and all column sums
     are equal, so Y is uniform and the bound is attained. Ternary example:
     H(0.5, 0.3, 0.2) = 1.48548, log₂ 3 = 1.58496, C = 0.09949. Common
     mistakes: assuming the uniform input is always optimal (it is for
     symmetric channels, not for the Z-channel); forgetting to check the
     column condition (weakly symmetric suffices: rows permutations and
     equal column sums). Now you try: the ternary symmetric channel with
     rows (0.8, 0.1, 0.1) and its cyclic shifts. (log₂ 3 − H(0.8, 0.1, 0.1)
     ≈ 0.66303)

D11. State Shannon's channel coding theorem and prove the converse from
     Fano's inequality. On a BSC(0.1), compare the repetition code of
     length 3 and the Hamming (7, 4) code with the capacity. Decode the
     received word 1011011 with the Hamming (7, 4) code whose parity-check
     matrix has as j-th column the binary expansion of j.
     Answer: every R < C = 0.531 is achievable, no R > C is. Repetition:
     R = 1/3, bit error 0.028; Hamming (7, 4): R = 4/7 ≈ 0.571 > C, block
     error ≈ 0.150, and no family of codes of rate 4/7 can have error → 0
     on this channel. The syndrome of 1011011 is 7: flip bit 7 to get
     1011010.
     Teacher explains: THEOREM: for every R < C and δ > 0 there are codes
     with 2^(nR) codewords of length n whose maximal error probability is
     < δ for large n; for R > C the error probability is bounded away from
     0. ACHIEVABILITY: pick 2^(nR) codewords at random i.i.d. from the
     optimal p(x); decode to the unique codeword jointly typical with the
     output; a wrong codeword is jointly typical with probability ≈
     2^(−nI), and there are 2^(nR) of them, so the average error → 0 when
     R < I; some code does at least as well as the average. CONVERSE: a
     uniform message W with nR bits, W → Xⁿ → Yⁿ → Ŵ. By Fano and data
     processing,
        nR = H(W) = H(W|Yⁿ) + I(W; Yⁿ) ≤ 1 + Pₑ nR + I(Xⁿ; Yⁿ) ≤ 1 + Pₑ nR + nC,
     so Pₑ ≥ 1 − C/R − 1/(nR), bounded away from 0 if R > C. Repetition
     code: majority vote fails iff ≥ 2 of 3 bits flip:
        3(0.1)²(0.9) + (0.1)³ = 0.027 + 0.001 = 0.028.
     Hamming decoding: the syndrome is the XOR of the column labels at the
     positions of the 1s. 1011011 has 1s at positions 1, 3, 4, 6, 7:
        1 ⊕ 3 = 2,  2 ⊕ 4 = 6,  6 ⊕ 6 = 0,  0 ⊕ 7 = 7,
     so a single error at position 7 is assumed: codeword 1011010 (its
     syndrome 1 ⊕ 3 ⊕ 4 ⊕ 6 = 0 ✓). Common mistakes: thinking the theorem
     gives zero error at finite n; believing capacity is about one channel
     use rather than long blocks. Now you try: decode 1100000. (syndrome
     1 ⊕ 2 = 3: codeword 1110000)

D12. Define differential entropy and show that among densities of
     variance σ² the Gaussian maximises it, with h = ½ log₂(2πeσ²). State
     the capacity of the Gaussian channel and evaluate for P/N = 15. State
     the rate-distortion functions for a Gaussian source (squared error)
     and a Bernoulli(1/2) source (Hamming distortion) and evaluate at
     D = σ²/4 and D = 0.1.
     Answer: N(0, 1): h ≈ 2.04710 bits; C = ½ log₂ 16 = 2 bits per use;
     R(σ²/4) = 1 bit; R(0.1) = 1 − h(0.1) = 0.531 bits per symbol.
     Teacher explains: h(X) = −∫ f log₂ f. For φ the N(0, σ²) density,
     −log₂ φ(x) = ½ log₂(2πσ²) + (x²/(2σ²)) log₂ e, so for ANY f with
     variance σ² (mean 0), −∫ f log₂ φ = ½ log₂(2πσ²) + ½ log₂ e = h(φ).
     Therefore
        0 ≤ D(f‖φ) = −h(f) − ∫ f log₂ φ = h(φ) − h(f).
     For σ = 1: ½ log₂(2πe) = ½ log₂ 17.0795 = 2.04710. GAUSSIAN CHANNEL
     Y = X + Z, Z ~ N(0, N), E X² ≤ P: I = h(Y) − h(Z) ≤ ½ log₂(2πe(P + N))
     − ½ log₂(2πeN) = ½ log₂(1 + P/N), attained by X ~ N(0, P). With
     P/N = 15, C = ½ log₂ 16 = 2. RATE-DISTORTION: to describe X ~ N(0, σ²)
     with E(X − X̂)² ≤ D one needs R(D) = ½ log₂(σ²/D) bits (D ≤ σ²):
     D = σ²/4 gives 1 bit; equivalently D(R) = σ²2^(−2R), and each extra
     bit divides the error by 4 (6.02 dB). Bernoulli(p), Hamming distortion:
     R(D) = h(p) − h(D) for D ≤ min(p, 1 − p); p = 1/2, D = 0.1 gives
     1 − 0.469 = 0.531. The converse uses I(X; X̂) ≥ H(X) − H(X ⊕ X̂) ≥
     h(p) − h(D). Common mistakes: expecting h(X) ≥ 0 (U[0, 1/2] has
     h = −1); using SNR in dB inside the formula (30 dB means P/N = 1000).
     Now you try: Gaussian channel with P/N = 63; and R(D) for N(0, 9),
     D = 1. (3 bits; log₂ 3 ≈ 1.58496 bits)

M7. Shannon, Shannon-Fano, Shannon-Fano-Elias and arithmetic codes.

      i) 2, 2, 2, 3, 3; 2.3
      j) 2, 2, 2, 3, 3; 2.31 against Huffman's 2.30 (1, 3, 3, 3, 3)
      k) 0.125, 0.5, 0.8125, 0.9375    l) 3, 2, 4, 4
      m) 001, 10, 1101, 1111           n) 2.75
      o) [0.512, 0.64)                 p) [0.8, 0.96)
      q) 4 (width 0.128, ⌈2.966⌉ + 1)
      r) 1.75, i.e. D(p‖q) = 0.25 bits more than H(p) = 1.5

M8. Typical sets and the AEP (i.i.d. sources; ≈ is enough).
    a) nH for Bernoulli(0.1), n = 100
    b) approximate size of the typical set in a)
    c) fraction of all 2^100 sequences that are typical in a)
    d) probability of one sequence with exactly 10 ones in a)
    e) probability of the all-zeros sequence in a); is it ε-typical for
       ε = 0.1?
    f) number of sequences with exactly 10 ones, as a power of 2
    g) Bernoulli(1/2), n = 100: size of the typical set
    h) Bernoulli(0.2), n = 50: nH
    i) Bernoulli(0.25), n = 1000: bits needed to store the output
    j) Bernoulli(0.1), n = 1000: bits needed
    k) i.i.d. uniform on 4 letters, n = 10: size of the typical set
    l) (1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/8) source, n = 100: size of the typical set and
       probability of each typical sequence
    m) p = (1/2, 1/4, 1/4) on {a, b, c}: −(1/n) log₂ p(x) for x = aabc
    n) the same for x = cccc; its distance from H
    o) number of 20-bit strings with four 1s, against 2^(20h(0.2))
    p) rate to which Bernoulli(0.11) can be compressed
    Answers:
      a) 46.900       b) 2^46.9 ≈ 1.3 × 10¹⁴
      c) 2^(−53.1) ≈ 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁶         d) 2^(−46.9) ≈ 7.6 × 10⁻¹⁵
      e) 0.9^100 ≈ 2.66 × 10⁻⁵; no (0.152 is 0.317 away from 0.469)
      f) C(100, 10) ≈ 1.73 × 10¹³ ≈ 2^43.98
      g) 2^100 (every sequence)          h) 36.096
      i) ≈ 812 bits (811.28)             j) ≈ 469 bits
      k) 4¹⁰ = 2²⁰                        l) 2^175; 2^(−175)
      m) 1.5 = H (typical for every ε)   n) 2; 0.5
      o) 4845 ≈ 2^12.24 against 2^14.44 (polynomial factor)
      p) ≈ 1/2 bit per symbol (h(0.11) ≈ 0.49992)

M9. Channel capacities (bits per channel use).
    a) BSC(0)                          b) BSC(0.01)
    c) BSC(0.05)                       d) BSC(0.1)
    e) BSC(0.11)                       f) BSC(0.25)
    g) BSC(1/3)                        h) BSC(1/2)
    i) BSC(0.9)                        j) BEC(0.2)
    k) BEC(0.75)                       l) Z-channel, 1 → 0 with prob. 1/2:
                                          C and the optimal P(X = 1)
    m) Z-channel with 1 → 0 with probability 0.1
    n) Z-channel with 1 → 0 with probability 0.25
    o) ternary symmetric, rows cyclic shifts of (0.8, 0.1, 0.1)
    p) ternary symmetric, rows (0.5, 0.25, 0.25) and shifts
    q) rows cyclic shifts of (0.5, 0.3, 0.2)
    r) weakly symmetric [[1/3, 1/6, 1/2], [1/3, 1/2, 1/6]]
    s) noisy typewriter on 26 letters (each letter → itself or the next,
       probability 1/2 each)
    t) two BSC(0.1) in cascade         u) binary erasure-and-error channel:
                                          erase 0.1, flip 0.1

      a) 1            b) 0.91921      c) 0.71360      d) 0.53100
      e) 0.50008      f) 0.18872      g) 0.08170      h) 0
      i) 0.53100      j) 0.8          k) 0.25
      l) log₂(5/4) ≈ 0.32193 with P(X = 1) = 2/5
      m) 0.76285      n) 0.55824      o) 0.66303      p) 0.08496
      q) 0.09949      r) log₂ 3 − H(1/2, 1/3, 1/6) ≈ 0.12581
      s) log₂ 13 ≈ 3.70044            t) 1 − h(0.18) ≈ 0.31992
      u) 0.9(1 − h(1/9)) ≈ 0.44707    v) 1/2
      w) ≈ 531        x) log₂ 3 ≈ 1.58496

M10. Differential entropy (bits) and the Gaussian channel.
     a) U[0, 1]                        b) U[0, 8]
     c) U[0, 1/2]                      d) N(0, 1)
     e) N(0, 4)                        f) N(5, 16)
     g) Exponential(1)                 h) Exponential(2) (rate 2)
     i) Laplace with scale 1           j) h(2X) − h(X)
     k) bivariate normal with Σ = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]
     l) the variance σ² for which N(0, σ²) has h = 0
     Capacity ½ log₂(1 + P/N):
     m) P/N = 1                        n) P/N = 3
     o) P/N = 15                       p) P/N = 63
     q) 30 dB                          r) −10 dB (P/N = 0.1)
     s) telephone line W = 3000 Hz, 30 dB: C in bits/s
     t) W = 1 MHz, P/(N₀W) = 15: C in bits/s
     u) P/N needed for 3 bits per use
     v) water-filling: noises N₁ = 1, N₂ = 3, total power 6: powers and C
     w) the same with total power 1

       e) ≈ 3.04710   f) ≈ 4.04710    g) log₂ e ≈ 1.44270

       j) 1           k) ½ log₂((2πe)²·3) ≈ 4.88667
       l) 1/(2πe) ≈ 0.05855            m) 0.5          n) 1
       o) 2           p) 3            q) ≈ 4.98361    r) ≈ 0.06875
       s) ≈ 29 900 bits/s (3000 log₂ 1001)             t) 4 × 10⁶ bits/s
       u) 63
       v) water level 5: P₁ = 4, P₂ = 2; C = ½ log₂(25/3) ≈ 1.52945
       w) only channel 1 is used: C = ½ log₂ 2 = 0.5

M11. Rate-distortion (bits per source symbol).
     Bernoulli(p) with Hamming distortion, R(D) = h(p) − h(D):
     a) p = 1/2, D = 0                 b) p = 1/2, D = 0.1
     c) p = 1/2, D = 0.11              d) p = 1/2, D = 0.25
     e) p = 1/2, D = 0.5               f) p = 0.2, D = 0.1
     g) p = 0.1, D = 0.05              h) p = 0.3, D = 0.3
     i) p = 1/2: the distortion reachable at R = 1/2
     Gaussian N(0, σ²), squared error, R(D) = ½ log₂(σ²/D):
     j) σ² = 1, D = 1/4                k) σ² = 1, D = 1/16
     l) σ² = 4, D = 1                  m) σ² = 1, D = 0.01
     n) σ² = 9, D = 1                  o) σ² = 1, D = 2
     p) D(R) for σ² = 1, R = 1         q) D(R) for σ² = 4, R = 3
     r) D(R) for σ² = 1, R = 1/2       s) gain in dB per extra bit
     t) a Bernoulli(1/2) source sent over a BSC(0.1), one use per symbol:
        the least possible bit error rate
     Answers:
       a) 1           b) 0.53100      c) 0.50008      d) 0.18872
       e) 0           f) 0.25293      g) 0.18260      h) 0 (D ≥ p: send
          nothing, guess 0)           i) D ≈ 0.110
       j) 1           k) 2            l) 1            m) log₂ 10 ≈ 3.32193
       n) log₂ 3 ≈ 1.58496            o) 0            p) 1/4
       q) 1/16        r) 1/2          s) 10 log₁₀ 4 ≈ 6.02 dB
       t) 0.1 (from 1 − h(D) ≤ 1 − h(0.1)): uncoded transmission is optimal

M12. Error-correcting codes (binary unless stated).
     a) Hamming bound on M for n = 7, d = 3; is (7, 4) perfect?
     b) the same for n = 15, d = 3
     c) check that the Golay code (23, 12, 7) is perfect
     d) Hamming bound on M for n = 10, d = 3
     e) Hamming bound on M for n = 6, d = 3
     f) does a binary linear (11, 6, 5) code exist?
     g) Singleton bound on k for n = 7, d = 3
     h) Gilbert-Varshamov: a code with n = 10, d = 3 and at least how many
        codewords exists?
     i) parameters (n, k) and rates of the Hamming codes with r = 3, 4, 5, 6
     Syndrome decoding with the Hamming (7, 4) code (columns 1, ..., 7):
     j) 1011011                        k) 1100000
     l) 0000111                        m) 1111111
     n) bit error of the repetition code of length 3 over BSC(0.1)
     o) the same for length 5
     p) block error of Hamming (7, 4) over BSC(0.01)
     q) minimum distance of {00000, 01101, 10110, 11011}
     r) errors detected and corrected by a code with d = 7
     s) check that the ternary Golay code (11, 6, 5) is perfect
     Answers:
       a) 16; yes (16·8 = 128 = 2⁷)   b) 2048; yes, (15, 11)
       c) 1 + 23 + 253 + 1771 = 2048 = 2¹¹, and 2¹²·2¹¹ = 2²³ ✓
       d) 93 (1024/11)                e) 9 (64/7)
       f) no: 2⁶·(1 + 11 + 55) = 4288 > 2¹¹ = 2048
       g) k ≤ 5       h) 19 (M ≥ 1024/56 ≈ 18.3)
       i) (7, 4) 4/7; (15, 11) 11/15; (31, 26) 26/31; (63, 57) 57/63
       j) syndrome 7: 1011010         k) syndrome 3: 1110000
       l) syndrome 4: 0001111         m) syndrome 0: a codeword
       n) 0.028       o) 0.00856      p) ≈ 0.00203
       q) 3           r) detects 6, corrects 3
       s) 1 + 11·2 + 55·4 = 243 = 3⁵ and 3⁶·3⁵ = 3¹¹ ✓

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: start every entropy calculation with a table of p and
  log₂(1/p) and multiply column by column; draw the Venn diagram of H(X),
  H(Y), H(X|Y), H(Y|X) and I(X; Y) for every joint table and check that the
  pieces add up. Build every Huffman code on paper as a tree, re-sorting
  after each merge, and always compare L with H and H + 1. For capacity,
  always write I = H(Y) − H(Y|X) and ask which input makes H(Y) largest.
  Use a few lines of Python to check logarithms. Harder: prove McMillan's
  theorem in full; implement Huffman, arithmetic coding and the
  Blahut-Arimoto algorithm and test them on the channels of M9; prove the
  achievability half of the channel coding theorem with jointly typical
  decoding; derive the Z-channel formula for a general crossover; prove
  that the Gaussian maximises differential entropy under a covariance
  constraint in several dimensions; derive the Bernoulli rate-distortion
  function with the test channel; and read Shannon's 1948 paper 'A
  Mathematical Theory of Communication', checking every example in it.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.16 — MULTIVARIATE STATISTICS AND STATISTICAL LEARNING
(INTRODUCTION)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - RANDOM VECTORS: mean μ = E X, covariance Σ = E(X − μ)(X − μ)ᵀ
    (symmetric, positive SEMI-definite); E(AX + b) = Aμ + b,
    Cov(AX + b) = AΣAᵀ, Var(aᵀX) = aᵀΣa, Cov(aᵀX, bᵀX) = aᵀΣb. Correlation
    matrix R = D^(−1/2) Σ D^(−1/2) with D = diag Σ. Total variance tr Σ,
    generalised variance det Σ.
  - DATA: n × p matrix with rows xᵢᵀ; sample mean x̄, sample covariance
    S = (1/(n − 1)) ∑ (xᵢ − x̄)(xᵢ − x̄)ᵀ (unbiased), rank S ≤ n − 1 (singular
    when n ≤ p or the data lie on a line). MAHALANOBIS distance
    D²(x) = (x − μ)ᵀΣ⁻¹(x − μ): accounts for scale and correlation;
    contours are ellipsoids.
  - MULTIVARIATE NORMAL Nₚ(μ, Σ): density (2π)^(−p/2) (det Σ)^(−1/2)
    exp(−D²/2); X is MVN iff every aᵀX is normal. Linear maps, MARGINALS
    and CONDITIONALS are normal: X₁|X₂ = x₂ ~ N(μ₁ + Σ₁₂Σ₂₂⁻¹(x₂ − μ₂),
    Σ₁₁ − Σ₁₂Σ₂₂⁻¹Σ₂₁) (Schur complement). For MVN, uncorrelated ⇔
    independent (false without joint normality). D²(X) ~ χ²ₚ. Zeros of Σ⁻¹
    mean CONDITIONAL independence (partial correlation).
  - INFERENCE: Wishart distribution of (n − 1)S; HOTELLING's
    T² = n(x̄ − μ₀)ᵀS⁻¹(x̄ − μ₀) with (n − p)T²/(p(n − 1)) ~ F_{p, n−p};
    two-sample T² with pooled S; simultaneous confidence regions; why one
    T² test beats p separate t-tests.
  - PRINCIPAL COMPONENT ANALYSIS: directions of maximal variance =
    eigenvectors of S (or of R when units differ); eigenvalues λ₁ ≥ ... ≥
    λₚ are the component variances; proportion of variance λₖ/∑λⱼ; scree
    plot; scores; reconstruction error = sum of discarded eigenvalues; link
    with the SVD of the centred data matrix.
  - CLASSIFICATION BY DISCRIMINANT ANALYSIS: Bayes rule (maximise πₖfₖ(x)).
    LDA (equal Σ): linear boundary wᵀx = c with w = Σ⁻¹(μ₂ − μ₁), shifted by
    ln(π₁/π₂); error Φ(−Δ/2), Δ the Mahalanobis distance between the
    means; FISHER's discriminant maximises between/within variance. QDA
    (unequal Σ): quadratic boundaries.
  - LINEAR REGRESSION: y = Xβ + ε; least squares β̂ = (XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀy from the
    NORMAL EQUATIONS; ŷ = Hy with the HAT matrix H = X(XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀ (orthogonal
    projection, tr H = p, leverages hᵢᵢ); R², s² = RSS/(n − p);
    Var β̂ = σ²(XᵀX)⁻¹; GAUSS-MARKOV: OLS is BLUE.
  - REGULARISATION: RIDGE β̂ = (XᵀX + λI)⁻¹Xᵀy (shrinks, always invertible,
    Gaussian prior); LASSO (ℓ¹ penalty, soft-thresholding, sparse
    solutions, Laplace prior); tuning λ by CROSS-VALIDATION (K-fold,
    leave-one-out; for OLS the LOOCV residual is eᵢ/(1 − hᵢᵢ)).
  - BIAS-VARIANCE: E(y − f̂(x))² = σ² + bias² + variance; flexible models
    have low bias and high variance (overfitting), rigid ones the
    opposite; training error always falls with flexibility, test error is
    U-shaped.
  - LOGISTIC REGRESSION: log(p/(1 − p)) = βᵀx; e^(βⱼ) is an odds ratio;
    log-likelihood ∑ yᵢ log pᵢ + (1 − yᵢ) log(1 − pᵢ) is concave, gradient
    Xᵀ(y − p), Newton/IRLS; no MLE under perfect separation; log-loss.
  - NONPARAMETRIC AND UNSUPERVISED METHODS: k-NEAREST NEIGHBOURS (small k =
    low bias/high variance); DECISION TREES (splits chosen by Gini
    1 − ∑pₖ² or entropy −∑pₖ log₂ pₖ; information gain; pruning); k-MEANS
    (Lloyd's algorithm: assign to nearest centre, recompute means; the
    objective never increases; converges to a LOCAL minimum).
  - EVALUATION: confusion matrix, accuracy, precision (PPV), recall
    (sensitivity), specificity, F₁, ROC curve and AUC; base-rate effects;
    train/validation/test split.
  - SUPPORT VECTOR MACHINES AND THEORY: maximal-margin hyperplane (minimise
    ½‖w‖² subject to yᵢ(wᵀxᵢ + b) ≥ 1, margin 2/‖w‖), support vectors,
    soft margin with hinge loss, kernels (polynomial, Gaussian). VC
    DIMENSION: largest set shattered (half-planes in R²: 3, hyperplanes
    in Rᵈ: d + 1, intervals: 2); Sauer's lemma; generalisation bounds of
    the form error ≤ training error + O(√(d log n / n)).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. For the data (2, 5), (4, 7), (6, 6), (8, 10) compute the sample mean
    vector, the sample covariance matrix S, the correlation r and det S.
    Prove that every sample covariance matrix is positive semi-definite,
    and explain when it is singular.
    Answer: x̄ = (5, 7); S = [[20/3, 14/3], [14/3, 14/3]]; r = 14/√280
    ≈ 0.83666; det S = 28/3.
    Teacher explains: Centre the data: subtract x̄ = ((2+4+6+8)/4,
    (5+7+6+10)/4) = (5, 7):
       deviations (−3, −2), (−1, 0), (1, −1), (3, 3)
       ∑ d₁² = 9 + 1 + 1 + 9 = 20,  ∑ d₁d₂ = 6 + 0 − 1 + 9 = 14,
       ∑ d₂² = 4 + 0 + 1 + 9 = 14.
    Divide by n − 1 = 3: S = [[20/3, 14/3], [14/3, 14/3]]. Correlation
    r = 14/√(20·14) = 14/√280 ≈ 0.83666 (the 1/3's cancel). det S =
    (280 − 196)/9 = 28/3. PSD: for any a,
       aᵀSa = (1/(n − 1)) ∑ (aᵀ(xᵢ − x̄))² ≥ 0,
    the sample variance of the projections aᵀxᵢ. It is 0 iff all points
    lie on the hyperplane aᵀx = aᵀx̄, so S is singular exactly when the
    data lie in a proper affine subspace — always when n ≤ p, since the
    n centred vectors sum to 0 and span at most n − 1 dimensions.
    Common mistakes: dividing by n and calling the result unbiased;
    computing ∑xᵢxᵢᵀ without centring. Now you try: S for (1, 2), (3, 4),
    (5, 6). ([[4, 4], [4, 4]], singular: the points are collinear)

D2. Let X = (X₁, X₂) ~ N₂(μ, Σ) with μ = (1, 2), Σ = [[4, 2], [2, 3]]. Prove
    the conditional distribution formula for the MVN and find the law of
    X₁ given X₂ = 5, and of X₁ + X₂. Show that uncorrelated jointly normal
    variables are independent, and that joint normality is needed.
    Answer: X₁|X₂ = 5 ~ N(3, 8/3); X₁ + X₂ ~ N(3, 11).
    Teacher explains: Let B = Σ₁₂Σ₂₂⁻¹ and W = X₁ − BX₂. Then (W, X₂) is a
    linear image of X, hence jointly normal, and
       Cov(W, X₂) = Σ₁₂ − BΣ₂₂ = 0,
    so W is independent of X₂ (for MVN the density factorises when the
    off-diagonal block is 0). Hence given X₂ = x₂, X₁ = W + Bx₂ with
       mean μ₁ + B(x₂ − μ₂),  covariance Var W = Σ₁₁ − Σ₁₂Σ₂₂⁻¹Σ₂₁.
    Numbers: B = 2/3, mean 1 + (2/3)(5 − 2) = 3, variance 4 − 4/3 = 8/3.
    Knowing X₂ reduces the variance of X₁ from 4 to 8/3, whatever the
    value x₂. For the sum a = (1, 1): aᵀμ = 3, aᵀΣa = 4 + 3 + 2·2 = 11.
    Independence: if Σ is block-diagonal the exponent D² splits into a sum
    and the density into a product. Counterexample without joint
    normality: X ~ N(0, 1), S = ±1 a fair sign independent of X, Y = SX.
    Then Y ~ N(0, 1), Cov(X, Y) = E(S)E(X²) = 0, but |Y| = |X|: dependent
    (and X + Y has an atom at 0, so (X, Y) is not MVN). Common mistakes:
    letting the conditional VARIANCE depend on x₂; claiming 'normal
    marginals ⇒ jointly normal'. Now you try: X₂ given X₁ = 3. (N(3, 2))

D3. Define the Mahalanobis distance. With μ = (1, 2), Σ = [[4, 2], [2, 3]],
    compare the points P = (3, 3) and Q = (1, 4) by Euclidean and by
    Mahalanobis distance. Show that D²(X) ~ χ²₂ for X ~ N₂(μ, Σ) and find
    the probability that X lies inside the ellipse through P, and the 95%
    ellipse.
    Answer: Euclidean √5 ≈ 2.236 for P, 2 for Q; Mahalanobis D² = 1 for P,
    2 for Q. P(D² ≤ 1) = 1 − e^(−1/2) ≈ 0.39347; the 95% ellipse is
    D² ≤ 5.991.
    Teacher explains: Σ⁻¹ = (1/8)[[3, −2], [−2, 4]] (det Σ = 8).
       P − μ = (2, 1):  D² = (1/8)(3·4 − 2·2·2·1 + 4·1) = (12 − 8 + 4)/8 = 1
       Q − μ = (0, 2):  D² = (1/8)(4·4) = 2
    Q is nearer in the Euclidean sense but FARTHER in the Mahalanobis
    sense: P lies along the direction of positive correlation, which the
    distribution expects; Q moves X₂ alone by more than one of its
    standard deviations (√3) while X₁ stays put. Distribution: write
    Σ = LLᵀ and Z = L⁻¹(X − μ) ~ N(0, I); then D² = ZᵀZ = Z₁² + Z₂² ~ χ²₂,
    whose cdf is 1 − e^(−c/2). So P(D² ≤ 1) = 1 − e^(−0.5) = 0.39347, and
    1 − e^(−c/2) = 0.95 gives c = 2 ln 20 = 5.991. Points with D² > 5.991
    are flagged as possible outliers. Common mistakes: using Σ instead of
    Σ⁻¹; standardising each coordinate separately (ignores correlation).
    Now you try: D² of (−1, 0). (3/2)

D4. Derive principal components: show that the unit vector a maximising
    the variance aᵀSa is the top eigenvector of S, and that the k-th
    component is the k-th eigenvector. Carry out PCA for
    S = [[5, 2], [2, 2]]: eigenvalues, loadings, proportion of variance,
    and the first score of the centred observation (1, 1).
    Answer: λ₁ = 6 with a₁ = (2, 1)/√5, λ₂ = 1 with a₂ = (−1, 2)/√5; PC1
    explains 6/7 ≈ 85.7%; score 3/√5 ≈ 1.34164.
    Teacher explains: Maximise aᵀSa subject to aᵀa = 1. Lagrangian
    aᵀSa − λ(aᵀa − 1); gradient 2Sa − 2λa = 0, so Sa = λa: a must be an
    eigenvector, and then aᵀSa = λ: take the LARGEST eigenvalue. The second
    component maximises the same variance subject also to a ⊥ a₁; since S
    maps a₁^⊥ into itself (symmetry), the answer is the second eigenvector,
    and so on (spectral theorem). Numbers: characteristic polynomial
       λ² − 7λ + 6 = 0,  λ = 6, 1.
    (S − 6I)a = 0: −a₁ + 2a₂ = 0, a = (2, 1)/√5. The second is orthogonal:
    (−1, 2)/√5. Total variance tr S = 7; PC1 explains 6/7 = 0.857. Score of
    (1, 1): a₁ᵀx = (2 + 1)/√5 = 1.34164. Keeping only PC1 loses on average
    λ₂ = 1 unit of squared error per observation. Via the SVD X_c = UDVᵀ of
    the centred data matrix, the columns of V are the loadings and
    λⱼ = dⱼ²/(n − 1). Common mistakes: forgetting to CENTRE the data;
    doing PCA on raw variables with different units (use R instead of S);
    reporting unnormalised eigenvectors. Now you try: S = [[9, 4], [4, 3]].
    (λ = 11, 1; a₁ = (2, 1)/√5; 11/12 ≈ 91.7%)

D5. A sample of n = 10 bivariate observations has x̄ = (3, 4) and
    S = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]. Test H₀ : μ = (2, 3) at the 5% level with
    Hotelling's T². Explain the F-transformation and why a single T² test
    is preferable to two separate t-tests. Repeat with n = 25.
    Answer: T² = 20/3 ≈ 6.667, F = 80/27 ≈ 2.963 < F_{2,8}(0.05) ≈ 4.459:
    do not reject. With n = 25: T² = 50/3, F ≈ 7.986 > F_{2,23}(0.05)
    ≈ 3.422: reject.
    Teacher explains: T² = n(x̄ − μ₀)ᵀS⁻¹(x̄ − μ₀) is the squared Mahalanobis
    distance of x̄ from μ₀ measured in units of its own estimated
    covariance S/n. Here d = (1, 1), S⁻¹ = (1/3)[[2, −1], [−1, 2]],
       dᵀS⁻¹d = (1/3)(2 − 1 − 1 + 2) = 2/3,  T² = 10·(2/3) = 20/3.
    Under H₀ (normal data), (n − p)T²/(p(n − 1)) ~ F_{p, n−p}:
       F = (8/18)·(20/3) = 80/27 ≈ 2.963.
    For 2 numerator degrees of freedom P(F > f) = (1 + 2f/ν)^(−ν/2), so the
    critical value solves (1 + f/4)^(−4) = 0.05: f = 4·(20^(1/4) − 1) =
    4.459. Not significant. With n = 25: T² = 50/3, F = (23/48)(50/3) =
    7.986, critical 3.422: significant. Why T²: two t-tests at 5% each
    have overall error up to ≈ 10%, ignore the correlation, and can miss a
    shift that is small in each coordinate but large in the direction of
    low joint variance; T² equals the MAXIMUM over a of the squared t
    statistic of aᵀx (union-intersection), with exact overall level 5%.
    Common mistakes: using χ² critical values with small n; forgetting the
    factor n. Now you try: T² with n = 10, x̄ − μ₀ = (1, −1), same S. (T²
    = 10·(1/3)(2 + 1 + 1 + 2) = 20: the shift is against the correlation)

D6. Derive the least-squares estimator β̂ = (XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀy and show that
    ŷ = Hy is an orthogonal projection. Fit y = β₀ + β₁x to the data
    x = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, y = 2, 4, 5, 4, 5; compute R², s², the standard error
    and t-statistic of β̂₁, and the leverages. State the Gauss-Markov
    theorem.
    Answer: ŷ = 2.2 + 0.6x; R² = 0.6; s² = 0.8; SE(β̂₁) ≈ 0.28284,
    t ≈ 2.1213; leverages 0.6, 0.3, 0.2, 0.3, 0.6.
    Teacher explains: Minimise ‖y − Xβ‖². The gradient −2Xᵀ(y − Xβ) = 0
    gives the NORMAL EQUATIONS XᵀXβ = Xᵀy; if X has full column rank,
    β̂ = (XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀy. Geometrically the residual e = y − Xβ̂ is orthogonal to
    the column space: ŷ = Hy with H = X(XᵀX)⁻¹Xᵀ, symmetric and H² = H.
    Numbers: x̄ = 3, ȳ = 4, Sxx = 10, Sxy = (−2)(−2) + (−1)(0) + 0 + 1·0 +
    2·1 = 6, Syy = 4 + 0 + 1 + 0 + 1 = 6. β̂₁ = 6/10 = 0.6, β̂₀ = 4 − 1.8
    = 2.2. RSS = Syy − β̂₁Sxy = 6 − 3.6 = 2.4, R² = 1 − 2.4/6 = 0.6,
    s² = 2.4/(5 − 2) = 0.8, SE(β̂₁) = √(s²/Sxx) = √0.08 = 0.28284, t = 0.6/
    0.28284 = 2.1213 (3 d.f.: not significant at 5%, critical 3.182).
    Leverages hᵢᵢ = 1/n + (xᵢ − x̄)²/Sxx = 0.2 + (xᵢ − 3)²/10; they sum to
    tr H = 2. GAUSS-MARKOV: if E ε = 0 and Cov ε = σ²I, then for every
    linear unbiased estimator β̃ = Cy, Var(cᵀβ̃) ≥ Var(cᵀβ̂): OLS is the
    Best Linear Unbiased Estimator. Common mistakes: inverting XᵀX when
    columns are collinear; reading R² as 'the probability the model is
    right'. Now you try: x = 0, 1, 2, 3, y = 2, 2, 4, 4. (ŷ = 1.8 + 0.8x,
    R² = 0.8)

D7. Derive the ridge estimator and explain how it shrinks. With
    XᵀX = [[2, 1], [1, 2]] and Xᵀy = (3, 3) compute OLS and ridge for
    λ = 1, 2. For an orthonormal design (XᵀX = I) with OLS estimate
    b = (3, −0.5, 1.2), give the ridge (λ = 1) and lasso (λ = 1) estimates,
    where ridge minimises ‖y − Xβ‖² + λ‖β‖² and lasso ½‖y − Xβ‖² + λ‖β‖₁.
    Answer: OLS (1, 1); ridge (3/4, 3/4) and (3/5, 3/5). Orthonormal:
    ridge b/2 = (1.5, −0.25, 0.6); lasso (2, 0, 0.2).
    Teacher explains: Gradient of ‖y − Xβ‖² + λ‖β‖²: −2Xᵀ(y − Xβ) + 2λβ = 0,
    so (XᵀX + λI)β = Xᵀy and β̂_λ = (XᵀX + λI)⁻¹Xᵀy. XᵀX + λI is positive
    definite for λ > 0, so ridge works even when p > n or with
    collinearity. With XᵀX = VD²Vᵀ, in the eigen-direction vⱼ the OLS
    coefficient is multiplied by dⱼ²/(dⱼ² + λ): weak directions shrink
    most. Numbers: XᵀX + λI = [[2 + λ, 1], [1, 2 + λ]] applied to (c, c)
    gives (3 + λ)c = 3, so c = 3/(3 + λ): λ = 0: 1; λ = 1: 3/4; λ = 2: 3/5.
    Orthonormal case: ridge is b/(1 + λ) = b/2. Lasso separates into
    ½(βⱼ − bⱼ)² + λ|βⱼ| per coordinate, minimised by SOFT-THRESHOLDING
    βⱼ = sign(bⱼ)(|bⱼ| − λ)₊: 3 → 2, −0.5 → 0, 1.2 → 0.2. Lasso sets small
    coefficients exactly to 0 (variable selection) because the ℓ¹ ball has
    corners; ridge never does. Bayesian view: ridge is the posterior mode
    under a Gaussian prior on β, lasso under a Laplace prior. Common
    mistakes: penalising the intercept; forgetting to standardise the
    predictors before penalising. Now you try: ridge with λ = 4 in the
    first example. ((3/7, 3/7))

D8. Prove the bias-variance decomposition of the expected squared error of
    an estimator. For X₁, ..., X₁₀ i.i.d. with mean μ = 1 and σ² = 4,
    compare the estimators cX̄ for c = 1, 1/2 and the best c. Explain the
    decomposition for k-nearest neighbours, and how K-fold and leave-one-
    out cross-validation estimate test error; compute the LOOCV error of
    the regression in D6.
    Answer: MSE(c) = (c − 1)² + 0.4c²: 0.4 for c = 1, 0.35 for c = 1/2,
    minimum 2/7 ≈ 0.2857 at c = 5/7. LOOCV MSE ≈ 1.45638 (training MSE
    0.48).
    Teacher explains: For an estimator θ̂ of θ, add and subtract E θ̂:
       E(θ̂ − θ)² = E(θ̂ − Eθ̂)² + (Eθ̂ − θ)² + 2(Eθ̂ − θ)E(θ̂ − Eθ̂)
                 = Var θ̂ + bias²,
    since the cross term has E(θ̂ − Eθ̂) = 0. For a prediction at x with
    y = f(x) + ε, add the irreducible noise: E(y − f̂)² = σ² + bias² + var.
    For cX̄: bias (c − 1)μ, variance c²σ²/n = 0.4c², so MSE(c) =
    (c − 1)² + 0.4c². Derivative 2(c − 1) + 0.8c = 0 gives c = 1/1.4 = 5/7
    and MSE = (2/7)² + 0.4(25/49) = 4/49 + 10/49 = 2/7: a BIASED estimator
    beats the unbiased one. k-NN: f̂(x₀) averages k noisy responses:
    variance σ²/k, bias grows with k (the neighbours are farther away);
    small k overfits. CROSS-VALIDATION: split the data into K folds, fit
    on K − 1, test on the held-out fold, average; LOOCV is K = n. For OLS
    no refitting is needed: the LOOCV residual is eᵢ/(1 − hᵢᵢ). In D6,
    e = (−0.8, 0.6, 1.0, −0.6, −0.2), h = (0.6, 0.3, 0.2, 0.3, 0.6):
       e/(1 − h) = (−2, 0.857, 1.25, −0.857, −0.5),
       LOOCV = (4 + 0.7347 + 1.5625 + 0.7347 + 0.25)/5 = 1.45638,
    three times the training MSE 2.4/5 = 0.48: training error is
    optimistic. Common mistakes: choosing the model with the smallest
    TRAINING error; tuning λ on the test set. Now you try: MSE of cX̄ with
    μ = 2 at c = 5/7. ((2/7)²·4 + 10/49 = 26/49 ≈ 0.531 > 0.4: the best c
    depends on the unknown μ)

D9. Derive the Bayes classifier for two Gaussian classes with a common
    covariance (LDA). With μ₁ = (0, 0), μ₂ = (2, 2), Σ = [[1, 0.5],
    [0.5, 1]] and equal priors, find the boundary, classify (1.5, 0.2) and
    (1, 1.5), compute the Bayes error, and find the boundary when π₁ = 0.8.
    Answer: boundary x₁ + x₂ = 2; (1.5, 0.2) → class 1, (1, 1.5) → class 2;
    error Φ(−Δ/2) ≈ 0.12411 with Δ² = 16/3; with π₁ = 0.8 the boundary is
    x₁ + x₂ = 2 + (3/4) ln 4 ≈ 3.03972.
    Teacher explains: Choose class 2 iff π₂f₂(x) > π₁f₁(x). Taking logs, the
    quadratic terms xᵀΣ⁻¹x cancel (same Σ):
       wᵀx > wᵀ(μ₁ + μ₂)/2 + ln(π₁/π₂),   w = Σ⁻¹(μ₂ − μ₁).
    Σ⁻¹ = (4/3)[[1, −0.5], [−0.5, 1]], w = (4/3)(2 − 1, −1 + 2) =
    (4/3, 4/3). Midpoint (1, 1): threshold (4/3)·2 = 8/3. So class 2 iff
    x₁ + x₂ > 2. (1.5, 0.2): 1.7 < 2, class 1; (1, 1.5): 2.5 > 2, class 2.
    Error: in each class wᵀX is normal with variance wᵀΣw = Δ², where
    Δ² = (μ₂ − μ₁)ᵀΣ⁻¹(μ₂ − μ₁) = (4/3)(2·1 + 2·1) = 16/3; standardised,
    the two class means are Δ apart and the threshold lies halfway, so
    each error is Φ(−Δ/2) = Φ(−1.1547) = 0.12411. Unequal priors: the
    threshold becomes 8/3 + ln 4, i.e. x₁ + x₂ = 2 + (3/4) ln 4 = 3.0397:
    the boundary moves toward the LESS likely class. FISHER'S view: w
    maximises (wᵀ(μ₂ − μ₁))²/(wᵀΣw), the separation of projected means
    relative to the spread, with no normality assumption. QDA (different
    Σₖ) keeps the quadratic terms. Common mistakes: using Euclidean
    distance to the means (wrong when Σ ≠ σ²I); moving the boundary the
    wrong way for unequal priors. Now you try: 1-D, N(0, 4) against
    N(4, 4), π₁ = 0.9. (class 2 iff x > 2 + ln 9 ≈ 4.19722)

D10. Describe logistic regression, write its log-likelihood and show it is
     concave with gradient Xᵀ(y − p). For the fitted model logit p =
     −3 + 0.5x compute p at x = 0, 4, 6, 10, the odds ratio per unit and
     the x with p = 0.9. A classifier with threshold 0.5 gives TP = 40,
     FP = 10, FN = 20, TN = 130: compute accuracy, precision, recall,
     specificity and F₁. Explain the base-rate effect for a test with
     sensitivity 0.99, specificity 0.95 and prevalence 1%.
     Answer: p = 0.04743, 0.26894, 0.5, 0.88080; OR = e^0.5 ≈ 1.64872;
     x ≈ 10.394. Accuracy 0.85, precision 0.8, recall 2/3, specificity
     13/14 ≈ 0.92857, F₁ = 8/11 ≈ 0.72727. PPV = 1/6 ≈ 0.16667.
     Teacher explains: Model P(y = 1|x) = σ(βᵀx) with σ(z) = 1/(1 + e^(−z)),
     equivalently log-odds linear in x. Log-likelihood
        ℓ(β) = ∑ [yᵢ βᵀxᵢ − log(1 + e^(βᵀxᵢ))].
     Since σ′ = σ(1 − σ), ∇ℓ = ∑ (yᵢ − pᵢ)xᵢ = Xᵀ(y − p) and the Hessian is
     −XᵀWX with W = diag(pᵢ(1 − pᵢ)), negative semi-definite: ℓ is concave,
     so Newton's method (IRLS) finds the global maximum when it exists. If
     a hyperplane separates the classes perfectly, ℓ increases forever
     along its normal: no MLE (coefficients → ∞). Numbers: z = −3, −1, 0, 2
     give σ = 0.04743, 0.26894, 0.5, 0.88080. One more unit of x
     multiplies the odds by e^0.5 = 1.64872. p = 0.9 ⇔ z = ln 9 = 2.19722
     ⇔ x = (3 + 2.19722)/0.5 = 10.394. Metrics: accuracy (40 + 130)/200 =
     0.85; precision 40/50 = 0.8; recall 40/60 = 2/3; specificity 130/140;
     F₁ = 2·0.8·(2/3)/(0.8 + 2/3) = 8/11. Base rate: in 10000 people, 100
     sick of whom 99 test positive; 9900 healthy of whom 495 test
     positive; PPV = 99/594 = 1/6. Common mistakes: reading β₁ as a change
     in PROBABILITY (it is a change in log-odds); judging a rare-event
     classifier by accuracy alone. Now you try: p at x = 8 and the
     log-loss of predicting 0.8 for a case with y = 0. (0.73106; −ln 0.2
     ≈ 1.60944)

D11. (a) A node has 10 positive and 10 negative cases. Compare the splits
     A: (8+, 2−) | (2+, 8−) and B: (10+, 6−) | (0+, 4−) by Gini gain and
     information gain. (b) Run k-means with k = 2 on 1, 2, 4, 7, 9, 10 from
     initial centres 1 and 2, giving the objective after each step, and
     prove that Lloyd's algorithm never increases the objective. Show by an
     example that it can stop at a non-optimal clustering.
     Answer: (a) Gini gains 0.18 (A) and 0.125 (B); information gains
     0.27807 (A) and 0.23645 (B): both prefer A. (b) centres (1, 6.4) →
     (1.5, 7.5) → (7/3, 26/3); final clusters {1, 2, 4}, {7, 9, 10},
     objective 28/3 ≈ 9.333.
     Teacher explains: (a) Parent: Gini 1 − ½² − ½² = 0.5, entropy 1.
     Split A: each child (0.8, 0.2) has Gini 1 − 0.64 − 0.04 = 0.32 and
     entropy h(0.2) = 0.72193, weights ½ each: gains 0.5 − 0.32 = 0.18 and
     1 − 0.72193 = 0.27807. Split B: left (10/16, 6/16) has Gini 1 −
     (100 + 36)/256 = 0.46875, entropy h(0.375) = 0.95443; right is pure.
     Weighted: 0.8·0.46875 = 0.375 and 0.8·0.95443 = 0.76355; gains 0.125
     and 0.23645. A creates two fairly pure children and wins. (b) Step 1:
     assign to the nearer of 1 and 2: {1}, {2, 4, 7, 9, 10}; objective
     J = 0 + (0 + 4 + 25 + 49 + 64) = 142; new centres 1 and 6.4. Step 2:
     {1, 2}, {4, 7, 9, 10}: J = 26.84; centres 1.5, 7.5. Step 3:
     {1, 2, 4}, {7, 9, 10}: J = 15.5; centres 7/3, 26/3, and J = 28/3;
     no point changes cluster: stop. PROOF of monotonicity: J(C, μ) =
     ∑ ‖xᵢ − μ_{c(i)}‖². The assignment step minimises J over C for fixed μ;
     the update step minimises J over μ for fixed C (the mean minimises the
     sum of squared distances). Neither step increases J, and there are
     finitely many partitions, so the algorithm stops. LOCAL minimum:
     the corners (0, 0), (0, 2), (4, 0), (4, 2) with centres started at
     (2, 0), (2, 2) split top/bottom with J = 16, a fixed point, while the
     left/right split has J = 4. Use several random starts. Common
     mistakes: weighting child impurities equally instead of by size;
     forgetting to recompute the centres. Now you try: Gini of a node with
     classes (4, 4, 2). (0.64)

D12. For the points (2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 2) labelled +1 and (0, 0), (1, 0),
     (0, 1) labelled −1, find the maximal-margin separating line, the
     margin and the support vectors. Explain the soft margin, hinge loss
     and the kernel trick. Define the VC dimension and show that
     half-planes in R² have VC dimension 3. Evaluate Sauer's bound for
     d = 3 and n = 10.
     Answer: w = (2/3, 2/3), b = −5/3 (the line x₁ + x₂ = 2.5); margin
     3/√2 ≈ 2.12132; support vectors (2, 2), (1, 0), (0, 1). VC dimension 3;
     at most 176 labellings of 10 points.
     Teacher explains: Hard margin: minimise ½‖w‖² subject to
     yᵢ(wᵀxᵢ + b) ≥ 1; the distance between wᵀx + b = ±1 is 2/‖w‖. By the
     symmetry of the data in the line x₁ = x₂, take w = (a, a). The
     closest points are (2, 2) (sum 4) on the + side and (1, 0), (0, 1)
     (sum 1) on the − side; making them active: 4a + b = 1, a + b = −1, so
     a = 2/3, b = −5/3. All other points satisfy the constraints strictly
     ((2, 3): 10/3 − 5/3 = 5/3 ≥ 1). Margin 2/‖w‖ = 2/(2√2/3) = 3/√2 —
     the distance between the lines x₁ + x₂ = 1 and x₁ + x₂ = 4. In the
     dual, w = ∑ αᵢyᵢxᵢ with αᵢ > 0 only for support vectors. SOFT margin:
     minimise ½‖w‖² + C ∑ max(0, 1 − yᵢf(xᵢ)), the HINGE loss, for data
     that are not separable. KERNELS: the dual uses only inner products,
     so replace xᵀz by k(x, z) (e.g. (1 + xᵀz)², exp(−‖x − z‖²/2)) to get
     nonlinear boundaries. VC DIMENSION: the largest n such that some n
     points can be labelled in all 2ⁿ ways by the class. Three points not
     on a line: each of the 8 labellings is separated by a line (cut off
     one vertex, or none). Four points: if one lies inside the triangle
     of the others, label it differently from them; otherwise they form a
     convex quadrilateral and the diagonal labelling (XOR) is not
     separable. So VC = 3 (in Rᵈ, d + 1). SAUER: a class of VC dimension d
     realises at most ∑_{i≤d} C(n, i) labellings: 1 + 10 + 45 + 120 = 176,
     far below 2¹⁰ = 1024; this polynomial growth drives the bound
     test error ≤ training error + O(√(d log n / n)). Common mistakes:
     margin 1/‖w‖ versus 2/‖w‖ (state which); thinking VC dimension is
     the number of parameters (sin(ωx) thresholds have 1 parameter and
     infinite VC dimension). Now you try: + at 3, 4 and − at 0, 1 on the
     line. (w = 1, b = −2, margin 2, support vectors 1 and 3)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Sample mean x̄, sample covariance S (divisor n − 1) and correlation r.
    a) (1, 1), (2, 3), (3, 2)          b) (0, 0), (2, 0), (0, 2), (2, 2)
    c) (1, 2), (3, 4), (5, 6)          d) (1, 5), (2, 3), (3, 1)
    e) (2, 1), (4, 3), (3, 5), (7, 3)  f) (4, 1), (2, 3), (6, 2), (8, 6)
    g) (0, 1), (1, 0), (2, 3), (5, 4)  h) (1, 2), (2, 4), (3, 6), (4, 8)
    i) (2, 5), (4, 7), (6, 6), (8, 10)
    j) (1, 0, 1), (2, 1, 0), (3, 2, 2): x̄ and S
    k) which of a)–j) have singular S, and why?
    Answers:
      a) (2, 2); [[1, 1/2], [1/2, 1]]; r = 1/2
      b) (1, 1); [[4/3, 0], [0, 4/3]]; r = 0
      c) (3, 4); [[4, 4], [4, 4]]; r = 1
      d) (2, 3); [[1, −2], [−2, 4]]; r = −1
      e) (4, 3); [[14/3, 2/3], [2/3, 8/3]]; r = √7/14 ≈ 0.18898
      f) (5, 3); [[20/3, 10/3], [10/3, 14/3]]; r = √70/14 ≈ 0.59761
      g) (2, 2); [[14/3, 10/3], [10/3, 10/3]]; r = √35/7 ≈ 0.84515
      h) (5/2, 5); [[5/3, 10/3], [10/3, 20/3]]; r = 1
      i) (5, 7); [[20/3, 14/3], [14/3, 14/3]]; r ≈ 0.83666
      j) (2, 1, 1); [[1, 1, 1/2], [1, 1, 1/2], [1/2, 1/2, 1]]
      k) c), d), h) (collinear points) and j) (n = 3 = p, and the first two
         coordinates differ by a constant)

M2. Covariance algebra. Σ = [[4, 2], [2, 9]] unless stated.
    a) the correlation ρ₁₂             b) Var(X₁ + X₂)
    c) Var(X₁ − X₂)                    d) Var(2X₁ − 3X₂)
    e) Cov(X₁ + X₂, X₁ − X₂)           f) Cov(AX) for A = [[1, 1], [1, −1]]
    g) det Σ (generalised variance)    h) tr Σ (total variance)
    i) correlation of [[1, −0.5], [−0.5, 4]]
    j) correlation matrix of [[9, 3, 0], [3, 4, −2], [0, −2, 16]]
    k) Var(X₁ + X₂ + X₃) for j)
    l) is [[1, 2], [2, 1]] a covariance matrix?
    m) is [[2, 1, 1], [1, 2, 1], [1, 1, 2]]? (eigenvalues)
    n) is [[1, 0.9, 0], [0.9, 1, 0.9], [0, 0.9, 1]]? (eigenvalues)
    o) the least ρ for which the 3 × 3 equicorrelation matrix is valid
    p) is the equicorrelation matrix with ρ = −0.6 valid? (eigenvalues)
    Answers:
      a) 1/3          b) 17           c) 9            d) 73
      e) −5           f) [[17, −5], [−5, 9]]          g) 32
      h) 13           i) −1/4
      j) [[1, 1/2, 0], [1/2, 1, −1/4], [0, −1/4, 1]]  k) 31
      l) no (eigenvalues 3, −1)       m) yes (4, 1, 1)
      n) no (1 − 0.9√2 ≈ −0.27279, 1, 1 + 0.9√2 ≈ 2.27279)
      o) −1/2 (eigenvalues 1 + 2ρ, 1 − ρ, 1 − ρ)
      p) no (−0.2, 1.6, 1.6)

M3. Mahalanobis distance D² from μ = (1, 2) with Σ = [[4, 2], [2, 3]]
    (Σ⁻¹ = (1/8)[[3, −2], [−2, 4]]), and related questions.
    a) (1, 2)                          b) (3, 3)
    c) (3, 2)                          d) (1, 4)
    e) (−1, 0)                         f) (0, 0)
    g) (1, 0)                          h) (−1, 1)
    i) (5, 4)                          j) (3, 6)
    k) Σ = diag(4, 9), μ = 0, point (2, 3)
    l) Σ = [[2, 1, 0], [1, 2, 0], [0, 0, 1]], μ = 0, point (1, 1, 1)
    m) P(D² ≤ 1), P(D² ≤ 2) for a bivariate normal
    n) c with P(D² ≤ c) = 0.9 and 0.95 (p = 2)
    o) which of b)–j) lie outside the 95% ellipse?
    Answers:
      a) 0            b) 1            c) 3/2          d) 2
      e) 3/2          f) 11/8         g) 2            h) 1
      i) 4            j) 11/2         k) 2            l) 5/3
      m) 1 − e^(−1/2) ≈ 0.39347; 1 − e^(−1) ≈ 0.63212
      n) 2 ln 10 ≈ 4.60517; 2 ln 20 ≈ 5.99146
      o) none (the largest is 5.5 < 5.991)

M4. Multivariate normal marginals and conditionals. X ~ N₂((1, 2),
    [[4, 2], [2, 3]]) in a)–h); Y ~ N₃(0, [[2, 1, 0], [1, 2, 1], [0, 1, 2]])
    in i)–o).
    a) law of X₁                       b) ρ(X₁, X₂)
    c) X₁ | X₂ = 5                     d) X₁ | X₂ = 2
    e) X₂ | X₁ = 3                     f) X₁ + X₂
    g) X₁ − X₂                         h) c with X₂ − cX₁ independent of X₁
    i) are Y₁ and Y₃ independent?      j) Y₁ | Y₂ = 1
    k) Y₁ | (Y₂, Y₃) = (1, 1)          l) Cov of (Y₁, Y₃) given Y₂
    m) partial correlation of Y₁, Y₃ given Y₂
    n) Σ⁻¹ for Y                       o) law of Y₁ + Y₂ + Y₃
    p) P(X₁ + X₂ > 3)
    Answers:
