TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work with 2 × 2 covariance matrices until every step is
  automatic (inverse, eigenvalues, conditional variance), and always draw
  the ellipse, the principal axes and the data. Compute every regression,
  PCA and k-means example first by hand and then check with numpy or R;
  plot training and test error against flexibility to see the
  bias-variance trade-off. Use the table 'model — assumptions — what is
  estimated — how it is tuned' for LDA, logistic regression, k-NN, trees
  and SVMs. Harder: derive the MLEs x̄ and ((n − 1)/n)S for the MVN;
  prove the Gauss-Markov theorem; show that ridge equals OLS on augmented
  data; derive the SVM dual and the KKT conditions; prove that half-spaces
  in Rᵈ have VC dimension d + 1 (Radon's theorem); prove the
  Eckart-Young theorem behind PCA; and implement cross-validated lasso on
  a real data set, comparing it with best-subset selection.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.17 — DIFFERENTIAL TOPOLOGY: TRANSVERSALITY, DEGREE THEORY, SARD'S
THEOREM
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CRITICAL POINTS AND VALUES: for smooth F : M^m → N^n, p is CRITICAL if
    dF_p is not onto (for m < n every point is critical); y is a CRITICAL
    VALUE if F⁻¹(y) contains a critical point, otherwise a REGULAR VALUE
    (points not in the image are regular values). MEASURE ZERO in a
    manifold is defined in charts (C¹ maps send null sets to null sets).
  - SARD'S THEOREM: the critical values of a smooth (C^k, k ≥ max(1,
    m − n + 1)) map have measure zero; so regular values are DENSE (Brown).
    Special case m < n: F(M) has measure zero (no smooth space-filling
    curves; Peano curves are only continuous). Whitney's C¹ function on R²
    that is not constant on a connected arc of critical points shows the
    smoothness hypothesis matters.
  - CONSEQUENCES OF SARD: (easy) WHITNEY EMBEDDING into R^(2m+1) and
    immersion into R^(2m); NO RETRACTION of the ball Dⁿ onto its boundary;
    BROUWER'S FIXED POINT THEOREM (every continuous f : Dⁿ → Dⁿ has a fixed
    point).
  - TRANSVERSALITY: F ⋔ Z (Z ⊆ N a submanifold) if Im dF_p + T_{F(p)}Z =
    T_{F(p)}N at every p ∈ F⁻¹(Z); then F⁻¹(Z) is a submanifold of M with
    codim F⁻¹(Z) = codim Z. Two submanifolds X, Z of N are transverse if
    T_pX + T_pZ = T_pN at every common point; then dim(X ∩ Z) = dim X +
    dim Z − dim N. Disjoint sets are transverse; if dim X + dim Z < dim N,
    transverse means disjoint. Regular value = transverse to a point.
  - TRANSVERSALITY THEOREM (genericity): if F : M × S → N is ⋔ Z, then
    f_s = F(·, s) is ⋔ Z for almost every s (Sard applied to F⁻¹(Z) → S).
    Transversality is STABLE (survives small perturbations) and GENERIC
    (any map can be perturbed to be transverse): two curves in R³
    generically miss each other; a curve and a surface meet in points.
  - MOD 2 DEGREE: for M compact, N connected, dim M = dim N, deg₂ F = #F⁻¹(y)
    mod 2 for any regular value y; it does not depend on y and is a
    HOMOTOPY invariant (preimages of a homotopy form a compact 1-manifold,
    whose boundary points pair up). deg₂ ≠ 0 ⇒ F is onto.
  - ORIENTED DEGREE (M, N oriented): deg F = ∑_{p ∈ F⁻¹(y)} sign det dF_p.
    Homotopy invariant; deg(G ∘ F) = deg G · deg F; not onto ⇒ deg 0; if F
    extends over a compact W with ∂W = M then deg F = 0. deg F · ∫_N ω =
    ∫_M F*ω. Standard values: z ↦ zⁿ on S¹: n; z ↦ z̄: −1; zᵐz̄ⁿ: m − n;
    reflection of Sⁿ: −1; ANTIPODAL map of Sⁿ: (−1)ⁿ⁺¹; x ↦ Ax/|Ax|:
    sign det A; a polynomial of degree n on the Riemann sphere: n.
  - APPLICATIONS OF DEGREE: HAIRY BALL THEOREM (Sⁿ has a nowhere-zero
    vector field ⇔ n is odd); FUNDAMENTAL THEOREM OF ALGEBRA; WINDING
    NUMBERS and the ARGUMENT PRINCIPLE (the winding number of p(γ) about 0
    counts roots inside γ; Rouché's theorem).
  - INTERSECTION NUMBERS: for f : X → N ⋔ Z with dim X + dim Z = dim N,
    I(f, Z) = ∑ ±1 (orientation of dfT_pX ⊕ T_yZ against T_yN); homotopy
    invariant; I₂ is the mod 2 count. On the torus, curves of classes
    (p, q), (r, s) have I = ps − qr. Self-intersection of the diagonal (or
    the zero section of TM) is χ(M). LEFSCHETZ number L(f) = I(Δ, graph f)
    = ∑_k (−1)ᵏ tr(f_* on Hₖ); L(f) ≠ 0 ⇒ f has a fixed point; for a linear
    torus map, L = det(I − A).
  - INDEX OF A VECTOR FIELD at an isolated zero p: the degree of v/|v| on a
    small sphere around p. Source, sink, centre: +1; saddle: −1; linear
    v = Ax (det A ≠ 0): sign det A; z^m: m; z̄^m: −m; gradient at a
    non-degenerate critical point of Morse index λ: (−1)^λ.
  - POINCARÉ-HOPF: on a compact manifold, ∑ indices = χ(M) for every field
    with isolated zeros: χ(S²) = 2, χ(T²) = 0, χ(Σ_g) = 2 − 2g, χ(RP²) = 1,
    χ(S^(2k)) = 2, χ(S^(2k+1)) = 0.
  - MORSE FUNCTIONS: all critical points non-degenerate (Hessian
    invertible); MORSE LEMMA: near p, f = f(p) − x₁² − ... − x_λ² + ... + xₘ²
    (λ = INDEX); Morse functions are generic (e.g. almost every height
    function); χ(M) = ∑ (−1)^λ c_λ and the MORSE INEQUALITIES c_λ ≥ b_λ; a
    Morse function on Σ_g has at least 2g + 2 critical points.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Let F : R² → R², F(x, y) = (x, y³ + xy) (the Whitney cusp). Find the
    critical points and the critical values, check that the critical
    values have measure zero, and count the preimages of (−3, 0), (3, 0) and
    (−3, 2). State Sard's theorem.
    Answer: critical set x = −3y² (a parabola); critical values (−3y², −2y³),
    a cusp curve 4a³ + 27b² = 0, of measure zero; 3, 1 and 2 preimages.
    Teacher explains: dF = [[1, 0], [y, 3y² + x]], det dF = 3y² + x. So
    the critical points are the parabola x = −3y², and their images are
       F(−3y², y) = (−3y², y³ − 3y³) = (−3y², −2y³),
    a curve with a cusp at the origin. With (a, b) = (−3y², −2y³):
    4a³ + 27b² = −108y⁶ + 108y⁶ = 0. A smooth curve in R² has measure zero
    (cover it by small squares), as Sard predicts. Preimages of (a, b):
    x = a and y solves the cubic y³ + ay − b = 0, which has 3 real roots
    when 4a³ + 27b² < 0 (inside the cusp) and 1 when > 0 (outside).
       (−3, 0): y³ − 3y = 0, y = 0, ±√3: 3 preimages (all regular);
       (3, 0): y³ + 3y = 0, only y = 0: 1 preimage;
       (−3, 2): y³ − 3y − 2 = (y + 1)²(y − 2): y = −1 (double) and y = 2:
       2 preimages, and (−3, −1) is critical — (−3, 2) is a critical value.
    The number of preimages is constant on each component of the set of
    regular values and jumps by 2 across the fold curve. SARD: if F : M → N
    is smooth, the set of critical values has measure zero in N. Note the
    set of critical POINTS can be large (a constant map has every point
    critical), but its IMAGE is small. Common mistakes: confusing critical
    points with critical values; thinking a regular value must lie in the
    image (a value with empty preimage is regular). Now you try: critical
    values of f(x, y) = x⁴ − 2x² + y². (critical points (0, 0), (±1, 0);
    critical values 0 and −1)

D2. (a) Prove that if F : M^m → N^n is smooth and m < n, then F(M) has
    measure zero. (b) Prove Sard's theorem for f : R → R. (c) Deduce that
    there is no smooth surjection R → R², though continuous ones (Peano
    curves) exist, and that the regular values of any smooth F are dense.
    Answer: (a) extend F to an n-dimensional map whose every point is
    critical; (b) near critical points |f′| < ε, so the image of the
    critical set in [a, b] has measure ≤ ε(b − a).
    Teacher explains: FACT: a C¹ map G : U ⊆ Rⁿ → Rⁿ sends measure-zero sets
    to measure-zero sets (locally G is Lipschitz, so it multiplies the
    volumes of small cubes by at most a constant). This is also why
    "measure zero in a manifold" is well defined. (a) Work in charts:
    F : U ⊆ R^m → Rⁿ. Define G : U × R^(n−m) → Rⁿ, G(x, t) = F(x). Then
    F(U) = G(U × {0}), and U × {0} has measure zero in Rⁿ, so its image
    under the C¹ map G has measure zero. Countably many charts cover M
    (second countability!), and a countable union of null sets is null.
    (b) Let C = {x : f′(x) = 0} and fix [a, b], ε > 0. f′ is uniformly
    continuous on [a, b]; choose δ with |f′(x) − f′(y)| < ε when |x − y| < δ.
    Cut [a, b] into intervals Iₖ of length < δ. If Iₖ meets C, then |f′| < ε
    on Iₖ, so by the mean value theorem f(Iₖ) is an interval of length
    ≤ ε|Iₖ|. Hence f(C ∩ [a, b]) is covered by intervals of total length
    ≤ ε(b − a); as ε is arbitrary, it has measure zero, and R is a
    countable union of [−k, k]. (c) By (a) (m = 1 < 2 = n) the image of a
    smooth curve is null, so not all of R²; Peano curves are continuous but
    nowhere differentiable. Density: the complement of a null set contains
    points in every open set (open sets have positive measure). Common
    mistakes: thinking "each critical value is one point, so there are few
    of them" (there can be uncountably many critical points); forgetting
    second countability when passing from charts to M. Now you try: why is
    the graph of a smooth f : R → R null in R²? (it is the image of
    t ↦ (t, f(t)), with 1 < 2)

D3. Prove Brouwer's fixed point theorem for smooth maps f : Dⁿ → Dⁿ from
    the no-retraction theorem, and prove the no-retraction theorem using
    Sard. Find the fixed point of f(x, y) = (y/2 + 0.2, x/2) on D².
    Answer: a fixed-point-free f gives a retraction r : Dⁿ → Sⁿ⁻¹; a
    retraction has a regular value whose preimage is a compact 1-manifold
    with only one boundary point — impossible; fixed point (4/15, 2/15).
    Teacher explains: NO RETRACTION: suppose r : Dⁿ → Sⁿ⁻¹ is smooth with
    r(x) = x on Sⁿ⁻¹. By Sard choose a regular value y ∈ Sⁿ⁻¹ of r (also
    regular for r|S, which is the identity). Then r⁻¹(y) is a compact
    1-manifold with boundary r⁻¹(y) ∩ Sⁿ⁻¹ = {y}. But a compact 1-manifold is
    a finite union of circles and arcs, and has an EVEN number of boundary
    points: contradiction. BROUWER: if f(x) ≠ x for all x, let r(x) be the
    point where the ray from f(x) through x leaves the ball:
       r(x) = x + t(x)(x − f(x)),  t(x) ≥ 0 solving |r(x)| = 1,
    a quadratic in t whose non-negative root depends smoothly on x (the
    discriminant is positive since f(x) ≠ x). r is smooth, r(x) = x for
    |x| = 1, so r is a retraction: impossible. (Continuous f are handled by
    smooth approximation.) Example: |f(x, y)| ≤ |(x, y)|/2 + 0.2 ≤ 0.7, so f
    maps D² into itself. Fixed point: x = y/2 + 0.2, y = x/2 ⇒ x = x/4 + 0.2,
    x = 4/15, y = 2/15. (f is a contraction, so here the fixed point is also
    unique; Brouwer gives existence only.) Common mistakes: drawing the ray
    from x through f(x) (it must start at f(x) and pass through x, so that
    points of the boundary are fixed); forgetting that Brouwer needs a
    CLOSED ball (x ↦ x/2 on the half-open interval (0, 1] has no fixed
    point). Now you try: does the rotation of D² by 90° have a
    fixed point? (yes, the centre; a rotation of the annulus has none)

D4. Define transversality of two submanifolds. For c ∈ R decide whether the
    x-axis Z ⊆ R² is transverse to the parabola P_c = {y = x² − c}, and give
    Z ∩ P_c. Then decide whether the unit sphere S² ⊆ R³ is transverse to
    the planes z = 1/2 and z = 1, and to the cylinder x² + y² = 1/4, and
    find the dimensions of the intersections.
    Answer: transverse iff c ≠ 0 (c > 0: two points; c < 0: empty); S² ⋔
    {z = 1/2} (a circle, dim 1), not ⋔ {z = 1} (tangent at the pole); S² ⋔
    the cylinder (two circles).
    Teacher explains: X ⋔ Z in N means T_pX + T_pZ = T_pN at EVERY p ∈ X ∩ Z
    (vacuous if X ∩ Z = ∅). Then X ∩ Z is a submanifold of dimension
    dim X + dim Z − dim N. Parabola: X ∩ Z = {x² = c}. c > 0: points (±√c, 0),
    where P_c has tangent (1, ±2√c); together with (1, 0) (tangent of Z)
    it spans R² iff 2√c ≠ 0 ✓: transverse, 2 points (dim 1 + 1 − 2 = 0).
    c = 0: only (0, 0), where both tangents are (1, 0): the sum is a line,
    NOT transverse (tangency). c < 0: empty, transverse. Sphere and planes:
    at p ∈ S² ∩ {z = a}, T_pS² = p^⊥ and T(plane) = span{e₁, e₂}; the sum is
    R³ unless p^⊥ = span{e₁, e₂}, i.e. p = (0, 0, ±1). For a = 1/2 the
    intersection is the circle x² + y² = 3/4 avoiding the poles: transverse,
    dimension 2 + 2 − 3 = 1. For a = 1 it is the north pole: tangent, not
    transverse. Cylinder: normals are (x, y, z) (sphere) and (x, y, 0)
    (cylinder); the tangent planes coincide iff the normals are parallel,
    i.e. z = 0 and x² + y² = 1 — not on the cylinder of radius 1/2. So
    transverse, and S² ∩ cylinder = {x² + y² = 1/4, z = ±√3/2}: two circles.
    With the cylinder x² + y² = 1 the intersection is the equator, where
    the surfaces are tangent: not transverse. KEY FEATURES: transversality
    is STABLE (c = 0.01 still gives 2 points) and GENERIC (almost every c
    gives a transverse picture). Common mistakes: "transverse = intersects"
    (disjoint sets are transverse); "tangent at one point is fine" (it must
    hold at every intersection point). Now you try: are the x-axis and the
    y-axis transverse in R²? in R³? (yes; no — the tangent spaces span only
    a plane, so in R³ they must be disjoint to be transverse)

D5. State the transversality theorem. Apply it to (a) fₛ(x) = x³ − 3x − s,
    transverse to {0} ⊆ R; (b) the family gₐ(t) = (t, t³ − at) and the
    x-axis; (c) two curves in R³, one translated by s ∈ R³. Which parameter
    values are bad, and why do they form a null set?
    Answer: (a) bad s = ±2; (b) bad a = 0; (c) bad s form the set
    {p − q : p ∈ C₁, q ∈ C₂}, the image of a 2-dimensional map into R³,
    which is null; for good s the curves are disjoint.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: let F : M × S → N be smooth and transverse
    to Z ⊆ N. Then for almost every s ∈ S the map fₛ = F(·, s) is transverse
    to Z. Proof idea: W = F⁻¹(Z) is a submanifold of M × S; a short linear
    algebra check shows fₛ ⋔ Z ⇔ s is a regular value of the projection
    π : W → S, and Sard says almost every s is regular. (a) F(x, s) =
    x³ − 3x − s is a submersion (∂F/∂s = −1), hence ⋔ {0}. fₛ ⋔ {0} ⇔ s is
    a regular value of x³ − 3x, whose critical values are f(±1) = ∓2. So
    s ∉ {−2, 2}, a null set. For s = 2: x³ − 3x − 2 = (x + 1)²(x − 2), a
    double root (tangency). (b) gₐ⁻¹(x-axis) = {t : t³ = at}: t = 0, ±√a. At
    t = 0 the curve has slope −a; at t = ±√a, slope 3a − a = 2a. Transverse
    ⇔ no slope is 0 at a crossing ⇔ a ≠ 0. For a > 0: 3 crossings; a < 0:
    1; a = 0: the inflection y = t³ is tangent to the axis. (c) With
    curves C₁, C₂ ⊆ R³ and F(p, s) = p + s on C₁ × R³ (a submersion),
    C₁ + s meets C₂ ⇔ s ∈ C₂ − C₁ = image of (p, q) ↦ q − p on C₁ × C₂, a
    smooth map from a 2-manifold to R³: null by Sard (m < n). So for almost
    every s the curves are disjoint, which is what transversality means
    when 1 + 1 < 3. Common mistakes: applying the theorem to fₛ directly
    instead of to the family F; forgetting that the bad set is null, not
    necessarily finite. Now you try: for which s is the circle x² + y² = s
    transverse to the line y = 1? (every s > 0 except s = 1)

D6. Define the mod 2 degree of F : M → N (M compact, N connected, equal
    dimensions). Explain why it is independent of the regular value and of
    the homotopy class. Compute deg₂ of z ↦ z², z ↦ z³ on S¹ and of the
    map e^(iθ) ↦ e^(i(θ + 3 sin θ)) using the value −1. Deduce that a map of
    odd mod 2 degree is onto.
    Answer: deg₂ = #F⁻¹(y) mod 2: 0, 1, 1 (three preimages of −1).
    Teacher explains: For a regular value y, F⁻¹(y) is a compact
    0-manifold: finitely many points. Two facts. (i) HOMOTOPY: if
    H : M × [0, 1] → N is a homotopy and y is regular for H, H₀, H₁, then
    H⁻¹(y) is a compact 1-manifold (circles and arcs) whose boundary is
    H₀⁻¹(y) ∪ H₁⁻¹(y); arcs have two ends, so #H₀⁻¹(y) ≡ #H₁⁻¹(y) mod 2.
    (ii) VALUE: for y, y′ regular choose a diffeomorphism h of N isotopic
    to the identity with h(y) = y′ (N connected); then h ∘ F ≃ F and
    (h ∘ F)⁻¹(y′) = F⁻¹(y). Examples: z ↦ z²: the value 1 has preimages ±1,
    2 ≡ 0. z ↦ z³: three cube roots, 3 ≡ 1. For g(θ) = θ + 3 sin θ we need
    g(θ) ≡ π (mod 2π) on [0, 2π): g increases on [0, 1.911], decreases on
    [1.911, 4.373] and increases on [4.373, 2π] (g′ = 1 + 3cos θ), with
    g(1.911) ≈ 4.739 and g(4.373) ≈ 1.544. So g = π exactly three times:
       θ ≈ 0.8627,   θ = π,   θ ≈ 5.4205,
    and g′ ≠ 0 there, so −1 is a regular value and deg₂ = 3 mod 2 = 1. If
    deg₂ F = 1 and some y were not in the image, y would be a regular value
    with 0 preimages, giving deg₂ = 0: so F is ONTO. Similarly a map with
    deg₂ = 1 is not homotopic to a constant. Common mistakes: counting
    preimages of a critical value; forgetting that the degree needs M
    compact (x ↦ x on R → R: every value has 1 preimage, but a homotopy can
    push everything to infinity). Now you try: deg₂ of the inclusion of the
    equator S¹ → S² — why is degree theory not applicable? (the dimensions
    differ: 1 ≠ 2)

D7. Define the oriented degree. Show that z ↦ zⁿ on S¹ has degree n and
    z ↦ z̄ has degree −1. For g(e^(iθ)) = e^(i(θ + 3 sin θ)) compute the
    degree at the value −1 with signs, and check it at the value 1. What is
    the degree of zᵐz̄ⁿ?
    Answer: deg = ∑ sign det dF_p; for g: +1 − 1 + 1 = 1 at −1, and +1 at 1
    (one preimage, θ = 0); deg(zᵐz̄ⁿ) = m − n.
    Teacher explains: For oriented M, N of the same dimension and a regular
    value y,
       deg F = ∑_{p ∈ F⁻¹(y)} sign det dF_p
    (+1 if dF_p preserves orientation, −1 if it reverses it). The same
    cobordism argument as in D6, now with oriented arcs (the two ends of an
    arc carry opposite signs), shows it is independent of y and of the
    homotopy class. On S¹ with angle θ, a map θ ↦ g(θ) has sign det dF =
    sign g′(θ). zⁿ: g(θ) = nθ, the value 1 has n preimages 2πk/n, each with
    g′ = n > 0: degree n. z̄: g(θ) = −θ, one preimage with g′ = −1: degree −1.
    For g(θ) = θ + 3 sin θ, g′(θ) = 1 + 3cos θ:
       θ ≈ 0.8627: g′ ≈ 2.951 > 0 → +1
       θ = π:      g′ = −2 < 0     → −1
       θ ≈ 5.4205: g′ ≈ 2.951 > 0 → +1
    total +1. At the value 1 (g ≡ 0) the only preimage is θ = 0 with g′ = 4:
    total +1 again ✓ (the count 3 versus 1 changes, the signed count does
    not). Indeed g is homotopic to the identity through θ + 3t sin θ.
    zᵐz̄ⁿ = z^(m−n) on |z| = 1: degree m − n. In general, for S¹ the degree is
    the WINDING NUMBER (1/2π)∮ dg = (g(2π) − g(0))/2π. Integral formula:
    deg F · ∫_N ω = ∫_M F*ω for any top form ω. Common mistakes: counting
    preimages without signs; forgetting that z̄ reverses orientation. Now
    you try: degree of the composition of z ↦ z³ and z ↦ z̄². (−6)

D8. Show that the reflection ρ(x) = (−x₁, x₂, ..., xₙ₊₁) of Sⁿ has degree −1,
    the antipodal map a(x) = −x has degree (−1)ⁿ⁺¹, and deduce the hairy
    ball theorem: Sⁿ has a nowhere-vanishing continuous tangent vector field
    iff n is odd. Give such a field on S³.
    Answer: a = product of n + 1 reflections; a nowhere-zero field would give
    a homotopy from id to a, forcing 1 = (−1)ⁿ⁺¹; on S³: v(x) = (−x₂, x₁,
    −x₄, x₃).
    Teacher explains: REFLECTION: the value e₂ has the single preimage e₂,
    and dρ at e₂ is the reflection of T_{e₂}Sⁿ = e₂^⊥ in the x₁-direction,
    which reverses orientation: deg ρ = −1. More generally x ↦ Ax/|Ax| for
    A ∈ GL(n + 1, R) has degree sign det A (deform A through GL to I or to
    a reflection). ANTIPODAL: a = ρ₁ ∘ ρ₂ ∘ ... ∘ ρₙ₊₁ (reflections in all
    coordinates), so deg a = (−1)ⁿ⁺¹: +1 on S¹ (a rotation by π), −1 on S²,
    +1 on S³. HAIRY BALL: suppose v is a tangent field with v(x) ≠ 0 for all
    x; normalise u = v/|v| (so u(x) ⊥ x, |u| = 1). Then
       H(x, t) = cos(πt) x + sin(πt) u(x)
    stays on Sⁿ (|H|² = cos² + sin² since x ⊥ u(x)) and joins H₀ = id to
    H₁ = a. Homotopic maps have the same degree: 1 = (−1)ⁿ⁺¹, so n is odd.
    Conversely, on S^(2k−1) ⊆ R^(2k) the field v(x) = (−x₂, x₁, −x₄, x₃, ...)
    has v(x)·x = 0 and |v(x)| = |x| = 1. For S³: v(x) = (−x₂, x₁, −x₄, x₃)
    (it is x ↦ ix in quaternion or complex coordinates). So you cannot comb
    a hairy sphere S², and at any moment there is a point on Earth with no
    horizontal wind. Common mistakes: forgetting to check that the homotopy
    stays on the sphere; claiming the antipodal map always has degree −1.
    Now you try: the degree of x ↦ Ax/|Ax| on S² for A = diag(2, −1, 3).
    (sign det A = −1)

D9. Prove the fundamental theorem of algebra by degree theory. Then use
    winding numbers (the argument principle) and Rouché's theorem to count
    the roots of p(z) = z⁴ + 6z + 3 in |z| < 1, in |z| < 2 and in the
    annulus 1 < |z| < 2.
    Answer: a root-free p would make the degree-n map z ↦ p(z)/|p(z)| on a
    large circle null-homotopic; 1, 4, 3 roots.
    Teacher explains: Let p(z) = zⁿ + aₙ₋₁zⁿ⁻¹ + ... + a₀, n ≥ 1. For
    |z| = R large, pₜ(z) = zⁿ + t(aₙ₋₁zⁿ⁻¹ + ... + a₀), t ∈ [0, 1], never
    vanishes (|zⁿ| = Rⁿ exceeds the rest), so on |z| = R the map
    z ↦ p(z)/|p(z)| is homotopic to z ↦ zⁿ/|z|ⁿ, of degree n. If p had no
    root, then z ↦ p(rz)/|p(rz)| (0 ≤ r ≤ R) would be a homotopy from
    degree n (r = R) to a constant (r = 0, degree 0): contradiction.
    ARGUMENT PRINCIPLE: for a circle γ avoiding the roots, the winding
    number of p(γ) about 0 = the number of roots inside γ (with
    multiplicity). ROUCHÉ: if |g| < |f| on γ then f + g and f have the same
    number of roots inside (the homotopy f + tg never vanishes on γ).
    Apply: on |z| = 1, |6z| = 6 > |z⁴ + 3| ≤ 4, so p has as many roots as 6z
    in |z| < 1: ONE. On |z| = 2, |z⁴| = 16 > |6z + 3| ≤ 15, so as many as
    z⁴: FOUR. Hence 4 − 1 = 3 roots in 1 < |z| < 2 (none on |z| = 1 or
    |z| = 2 by the strict inequalities). Numerically the roots are
    ≈ −0.511, −1.604 and 1.058 ± 1.593i (moduli ≈ 0.511, 1.604, 1.912 ✓).
    Common mistakes: using Rouché with ≤ instead of <; forgetting that the
    winding number counts multiplicities. Now you try: roots of z⁵ + 10z + 1
    in |z| < 1 and in |z| < 2. (1 and 5)

D10. Define the oriented intersection number I(X, Z) of compact oriented
     submanifolds of complementary dimension. On the torus T² = R²/Z² let
     a, b be the closed curves given by straight lines in the directions
     (1, 2) and (3, 1). Compute I(a, b) and the number of intersection
     points. Explain why I(X, X) for the diagonal of M × M, or for the zero
     section of TM, equals χ(M).
     Answer: I(a, b) = det [[1, 3], [2, 1]] = −5; 5 points, all with the same
     sign; I(Δ, Δ) = χ(M) by Poincaré-Hopf.
     Teacher explains: If X ⋔ Z in N with dim X + dim Z = dim N, each
     intersection point p gets the sign +1 if (oriented basis of T_pX,
     oriented basis of T_pZ) is an oriented basis of T_pN, else −1;
     I(X, Z) = ∑ signs. It is a homotopy invariant (deform one of them until
     transverse, then use the cobordism argument). Torus: a line of
     direction (p, q) closes up after one period and meets a line of
     direction (r, s) at points whose number is |ps − qr| (the area of the
     parallelogram they span, in units of the torus area). At each point
     the tangent vectors are (1, 2) and (3, 1), with
        det [[1, 3], [2, 1]] = 1 − 6 = −5 < 0,
     so every crossing has sign −1 and I(a, b) = −5, with exactly 5 points.
     Order matters: I(b, a) = +5 (for curves in a surface, I(b, a) =
     −I(a, b)). No homotopy can reduce the number of crossings below 5,
     since I is invariant. The mod 2 intersection number is 1, so a and b
     can never be pulled apart. Self-intersection: push the zero section Z
     of TM off itself using a vector field v (the graph of v); the new copy
     meets Z exactly at the zeros of v, with sign = index. So I(Z, Z) =
     ∑ ind = χ(M) by Poincaré-Hopf (for S², 2). The diagonal Δ ⊆ M × M has
     normal bundle ≅ TM, giving I(Δ, Δ) = χ(M); more generally I(Δ, graph f)
     = L(f), the LEFSCHETZ number. Common mistakes: forgetting orientation
     signs (then the count is not invariant); computing ps + qr. Now you
     try: I((2, 1), (1, 1)) and the number of points. (2·1 − 1·1 = 1; one
     point)

D11. Define the index of an isolated zero of a vector field in R² and
     compute it for v = (x, y), (x, −y), (−y, x), (x² − y², 2xy) and
     (x² − y², −2xy). State the Poincaré-Hopf theorem and use it to find
     χ(S²), χ(T²) and χ(Σ_g), and to show that a field on S² with exactly
     three zeros cannot have all of them non-degenerate.
     Answer: indices 1, −1, 1, 2, −2; χ(S²) = 2, χ(T²) = 0, χ(Σ_g) = 2 − 2g;
     three indices ±1 have odd sum ≠ 2.
     Teacher explains: ind_p v = the degree of x ↦ v(x)/|v(x)| on a small
     circle around p (the number of turns v makes as we go once round p).
     Source (x, y): v/|v| = x/|x| on the circle, degree 1. Saddle (x, −y):
     it is z ↦ z̄, degree −1. Centre (−y, x) = iz: a rotation of the source,
     degree 1. (x² − y², 2xy) = z²: going once round, v turns twice: 2.
     (x² − y², −2xy) = z̄²: −2 (the gradient of the monkey saddle
     x³ − 3xy², up to the factor 3). For a non-degenerate zero the index is
     sign det dv_p. POINCARÉ-HOPF: if M is compact and v has isolated zeros,
     ∑ ind_p v = χ(M). Uses: on S² the "north-south" field (the gradient of
     the height z) has a source and a sink: χ(S²) = 1 + 1 = 2. On T² the
     field ∂θ₁ has no zeros: χ(T²) = 0. For Σ_g standing upright, the
     gradient of the height has one maximum, one minimum and 2g saddles:
     χ = 1 − 2g + 1 = 2 − 2g. (Consistent with V − E + F: cube 8 − 12 + 6 = 2.)
     Three non-degenerate zeros have indices ±1, whose sum is odd, never 2:
     impossible on S². But ONE zero of index 2 is possible (the "dipole"
     field, pushing z² to the sphere). Common mistakes: confusing the index
     with the multiplicity; thinking every zero has index ±1. Now you try:
     the index of v = z³z̄ (as a field on R²) at 0, and of (x², y). (2; 0 —
     v/|v| misses the left half of the circle)

D12. Show that f(θ, φ) = cos θ + cos φ is a Morse function on the torus T²:
     find its critical points, Hessians and Morse indices, and verify
     χ(T²) = ∑(−1)^λ c_λ. State the Morse lemma and the Morse inequalities,
     and deduce the least number of critical points of a Morse function on
     Σ_g.
     Answer: 4 critical points: (0, 0) max (λ = 2), (π, π) min (λ = 0),
     (0, π) and (π, 0) saddles (λ = 1); 1 − 2 + 1 = 0; at least 2g + 2.
     Teacher explains: ∇f = (−sin θ, −sin φ) = 0 ⇔ θ, φ ∈ {0, π}: four
     points. Hessian = diag(−cos θ, −cos φ):
        (0, 0): diag(−1, −1), λ = 2, f = 2 (maximum)
        (π, π): diag(1, 1),   λ = 0, f = −2 (minimum)
        (0, π): diag(−1, 1),  λ = 1, f = 0 (saddle)
        (π, 0): diag(1, −1),  λ = 1, f = 0 (saddle)
     All Hessians are invertible: f is MORSE. The gradient has index
     (−1)^λ at each critical point (sign det Hess), so by Poincaré-Hopf
        χ(T²) = c₀ − c₁ + c₂ = 1 − 2 + 1 = 0 ✓.
     MORSE LEMMA: near a non-degenerate critical point p there are
     coordinates with f = f(p) − x₁² − ... − x_λ² + x_(λ+1)² + ... + xₘ². So
     the topology of {f ≤ c} changes only at critical values, by attaching
     a λ-cell. MORSE INEQUALITIES: c_λ ≥ b_λ (Betti numbers), and
     ∑(−1)^λ c_λ = χ(M). For Σ_g, b₀ = b₂ = 1, b₁ = 2g, so at least
     1 + 2g + 1 = 2g + 2 critical points, attained by the upright height
     function. GENERICITY: for almost every a ∈ R³ the function x ↦ a·x on
     a surface in R³ is Morse (Sard applied to the Gauss map), which is why
     Morse functions exist on every manifold. Common mistakes: counting
     (0, π) and (π, 0) as extrema; forgetting that a degenerate critical
     point (x³ at 0) spoils the count. Now you try: the critical points and
     indices of f(x, y) = x⁴ − 2x² + y² on R². ((0, 0), λ = 1; (±1, 0), λ = 0)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the critical points and the critical values.
    a) f(x) = x³ − 3x                  b) f(x) = x⁴ − 2x²
    c) f(x) = sin x                    d) f(x) = x³
    e) f(x) = eˣ                       f) f(x, y) = x² + y²
    g) f(x, y) = x² − y²               h) f(x, y) = x³ − 3x + y²
    i) f(x, y) = x³ − 3xy²             j) f(x, y) = x²y²
    k) f(x, y) = sin x + sin y         l) f(x, y, z) = x² + y² − z² + 2z
    m) F(x, y) = (x, y³)               n) F(x, y) = (x², y)
    o) F(t) = (t, t²), R → R²          p) the height z on S²
    q) cos θ + cos φ on T²             r) f(x, y) = (x² − 1)² + y²
    s) F(z) = z³, C → C                t) F(z) = z³ − 3z, C → C
    Answers:
      a) ±1; values −2 (at 1) and 2 (at −1)
      b) 0, ±1; values 0 and −1
      c) π/2 + kπ; values ±1
      d) 0; value 0 (not an extremum)
      e) none; no critical values
      f) (0, 0); 0                    g) (0, 0); 0
      h) (±1, 0); −2 and 2            i) (0, 0) (degenerate); 0
      j) both axes (uncountably many points); the single value 0
      k) (π/2 + kπ, π/2 + lπ); values −2, 0, 2
      l) (0, 0, 1); 1                 m) the line y = 0; the line v = 0
      n) the line x = 0; the line u = 0
      o) every point (m < n); the whole image, the parabola y = x²
      p) the poles; ±1                q) (0, 0), (0, π), (π, 0), (π, π);
                                         2, 0, −2
      r) (0, 0), (±1, 0); 1 and 0     s) 0; 0
      t) ±1; −2 (at 1) and 2 (at −1)

M2. Measure zero? Answer Y (measure zero) or N; for i)–o) answer the
    question.
    a) Q ⊆ R                           b) the Cantor set ⊆ R
    c) a line in R²                    d) S¹ ⊆ R²
    e) the image of a smooth curve R → R²
    f) the image of a Peano curve [0, 1] → [0, 1]²
    g) the critical values of a smooth f : R³ → R
    h) the critical points of a constant map R² → R
    i) the graph of a smooth f : R² → R, in R³
    j) the image of a smooth F : R² → R³
    k) the set of singular matrices in M(n, R) ≅ R^(n²)
    l) the set of non-diagonalisable matrices in M(2, C)
    m) is the set of regular values of a smooth map always dense?
    n) is it always open?
    o) is the set of regular values of a smooth F : M → N with M compact
       open?
    p) for which k does Sard hold for C^k maps R^m → Rⁿ?
    Answers:
      a) Y      b) Y      c) Y      d) Y      e) Y (Sard, 1 < 2)
      f) N (it is the whole square)            g) Y (Sard)
      h) N (all of R²)                         i) Y
      j) Y      k) Y (the hypersurface det = 0)
      l) Y (the discriminant (a − d)² + 4bc = 0 is a proper algebraic set)
      m) yes (Brown)      n) no in general
      o) yes (the critical set is compact, so its image is closed)
      p) k ≥ max(1, m − n + 1) (Whitney's C¹ counterexample on R²)

M3. Are the submanifolds transverse (T) or not (N)? If T, give the
    dimension of the intersection (∅ if empty).
    a) x-axis and y = x² + 1 in R²      b) x-axis and y = x²
    c) x-axis and y = x² − 1            d) x-axis and y = x³
    e) x-axis and y = x³ − x            f) S¹ and the line x = 1
    g) S¹ and the line x = 1/2
    h) the circles x² + y² = 1 and (x − 2)² + y² = 1
    i) the circles x² + y² = 1 and (x − 1)² + y² = 1
    j) the xy-plane and the z-axis in R³
    k) the xy-plane and the x-axis in R³
    l) the xy-plane and the xz-plane in R³
    m) the x-axis and the y-axis in R³
    n) the lines {(t, 0, 0)} and {(0, t, 1)} in R³
    o) S² and the plane z = 1; S² and z = 1/2
    p) S² and the cylinder x² + y² = 1
    q) S² and the cylinder x² + y² = 1/4
    r) the paraboloid z = x² + y² and the plane z = 0
    s) the sphere of radius √2 and the plane x + y + z = 0
    t) span{e₁, e₂} and span{e₃, e₄} in R⁴; span{e₁, e₂} and span{e₂, e₃}
    Answers:
      a) T, ∅        b) N (tangent at 0)                c) T, 0 (2 points)
      d) N (tangent at 0)             e) T, 0 (3 points)
      f) N (tangent at (1, 0))        g) T, 0 (2 points)
      h) N (tangent at (1, 0))        i) T, 0 (2 points)
      j) T, 0 (a point)               k) N (the sum is only a plane)
      l) T, 1 (the x-axis)            m) N (1 + 1 < 3 and they meet)
      n) T, ∅ (disjoint)              o) N (tangent at the pole); T, 1
      p) N (tangent along the equator)
      q) T, 1 (two circles at z = ±√3/2)
      r) N (tangent at the origin)    s) T, 1 (a great circle)
      t) T, 0 (the origin); N (the sum is 3-dimensional)

M4. Transversality of maps and dimension counts.
    a) generic dimension of the intersection of two surfaces in R³
    b) of a curve and a surface in R³  c) of two curves in R³
    d) of submanifolds of dimensions k and l in Rⁿ
    e) of two 3-dimensional submanifolds of R⁵
    f) f(t) = (t, t²): is f ⋔ the x-axis? ⋔ the line y = 1?
    g) when is F : R² → R transverse to {c}?
    h) f(x) = x² : R → R: ⋔ {0}? ⋔ {1}?
    i) fₛ(x) = x² − s: for which s is fₛ ⋔ {0}?
    j) fₐ(x) = x³ − ax: for which a is fₐ ⋔ {0}?
    k) f(x) = x³ + ax + b: the (a, b) for which f is not ⋔ {0}
    l) the inclusion S¹ → R² and the line y = c: for which c transverse?
    m) γ(t) = (cos t, sin 2t) and the x-axis: transverse? how many
       parameter values t ∈ [0, 2π) map into the axis?
    n) dim F⁻¹(Z) for F : M⁵ → N⁴ transverse to Z² ⊆ N
    o) when is graph(f) ⋔ Δ in M × M at a fixed point p?
    p) f(x) = x² on R: fixed points, and is graph(f) ⋔ Δ? And for
       g(x) = x² + 1/4?
    Answers:
      a) 1           b) 0 (points)  c) empty (−1)   d) k + l − n
      e) 1           f) no (tangent at 0); yes (t = ±1)
      g) c is a regular value of F
      h) no; yes     i) s ≠ 0        j) a ≠ 0
      k) 4a³ + 27b² = 0 (a repeated root)
      l) |c| ≠ 1     m) yes; 4 (t = 0, π/2, π, 3π/2; slope 2cos 2t = ±2)
      n) 3           o) 1 is not an eigenvalue of df_p
      p) 0 and 1, f′ = 0, 2 ≠ 1: yes; g has the single fixed point 1/2
         with g′(1/2) = 1: not transverse

M5. Degrees of maps S¹ → S¹ (z = e^(iθ)).

M6. Degrees of maps Sⁿ → Sⁿ and of the Riemann sphere.

M7. Winding numbers and roots (argument principle, Rouché).
    a) roots of z² − 3z + 2 in |z| < 3/2

M8. Intersection numbers.
    a) the torus curves (1, 0) and (0, 1): I   b) (1, 2) and (3, 1)

M9. Index at the isolated zero 0 of the vector field v.
    a) (x, y)                          b) (−x, −y)

M10. Euler characteristics and Poincaré-Hopf.
     a) χ(S²)        b) χ(T²)        c) χ(Σ₃)        d) χ(RP²)
     e) χ(Klein bottle)              f) χ(S³)        g) χ(S⁴)
     h) χ(S² × S²)   i) χ(CP²)       j) χ(T² × S²)
     k) a field on S² whose zeros are only sources and sinks: how many?
     l) can a field on S² have exactly three non-degenerate zeros?
     m) a field on Σ₂ whose zeros are all saddles: how many?
     n) a Morse function on Σ_g with one max and one min: how many saddles?
     o) does S⁵ carry a nowhere-zero vector field? S⁶? T³? RP³?
     p) the index of the only zero of a field on S² with one zero
     q) the sum of the indices of ∇(height) on an upright torus
     r) V − E + F for the surface of a cube, a tetrahedron, an octahedron
     s) χ of a closed surface triangulated with V = 7, E = 21, F = 14
     Answers:
       a) 2      b) 0      c) −4     d) 1      e) 0      f) 0
       g) 2      h) 4      i) 3      j) 0
       k) exactly 2 in total (e.g. one source and one sink)
       l) no (three indices ±1 have an odd sum ≠ 2)
       m) 2      n) 2g
       o) yes; no; yes; yes (odd-dimensional closed manifolds have χ = 0)
       p) 2      q) 0 (+1 − 1 − 1 + 1)
       r) 2, 2, 2                     s) 0 (the 7-vertex torus)

M11. Morse functions: critical points, Morse indices λ (D = degenerate).
     a) x² + y²                        b) x² − y²
     c) −x² − y²                       d) x³ − 3x + y²
     e) x³ − 3xy²                      f) x⁴ + y²
     g) x⁴ − 2x² + y²                  h) cos θ + cos φ on T²
     i) the height on S²               j) xy
     k) x² + y² − z²; xy + z²          l) the Hessian [[2, 3], [3, 2]]
     m) x² + 4xy + y²                  n) x² + xy + y²
     o) the Hessian diag(1, −2, −3)
     p) the least number of critical points of a Morse function on S²,
        T², Σ_g, RP²
     q) a Morse function on S² with 3 minima: the least number of saddles
     r) c₀ − c₁ + c₂ for a Morse function on Σ₂
     Answers:
       a) (0, 0), 0        b) (0, 0), 1        c) (0, 0), 2
       d) (1, 0): 0; (−1, 0): 1              e) (0, 0), D
       f) (0, 0), D        g) (0, 0): 1; (±1, 0): 0
       h) (0, 0): 2; (0, π), (π, 0): 1; (π, π): 0
       i) south pole 0, north pole 2         j) (0, 0), 1
       k) 1; 1 (eigenvalues ±1, 2)           l) 1 (eigenvalues 5, −1)
       m) 1 (eigenvalues 6, −2)              n) 0 (eigenvalues 3, 1)
       o) 2                  p) 2, 4, 2g + 2, 3 (mod 2 Betti numbers)
       q) 2 (c₁ = c₀ + c₂ − 2 ≥ 3 + 1 − 2)   r) −2

M12. Lefschetz numbers and fixed points (T² = R²/Z², a matrix A ∈ M(2, Z)
     acting linearly).
     a) L(f) for f : S² → S² of degree d; for the antipodal map
     b) L(f) for f : Sⁿ → Sⁿ of degree d
     c) A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]]: L and the number of fixed points
     d) A = 2I                         e) A = 3I
     f) A = [[0, −1], [1, 0]]: L and the fixed points
     g) the identity of T²             h) the identity of M
     i) A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
     j) any f : D² → D²                k) any f : RP² → RP²
     l) must a map S² → S² of degree 5 have a fixed point? of degree −1?
     m) z ↦ z^d on S¹: L and the number of fixed points (d ≠ 1)
     n) z ↦ z̄ on S¹                    o) the antipodal map of S³
     p) a rotation of S² by θ ≠ 0: L, the fixed points and their indices
     Answers:
       a) 1 + d; 0                     b) 1 + (−1)ⁿd
       c) det(I − A) = −1; 1 fixed point, (0, 0), of index −1
       d) 1; 1 (the origin)            e) 4; 4 (x, y ∈ {0, 1/2})
       f) 2; (0, 0) and (1/2, 1/2)     g) 0
       h) χ(M)                         i) 0 (fixed circles, not points)
       j) 1: a fixed point (Brouwer)   k) 1: always a fixed point
       l) yes (L = 6); not necessarily (L = 0: the antipodal map)
       m) 1 − d; |d − 1| (the (d − 1)-th roots of unity)
       n) 2; ±1                        o) 0 (no fixed points)
       p) 2; the two poles, each of index +1

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw everything in the plane first: critical values of
  functions R → R and R² → R, curves crossing or touching the x-axis, and
  arrows of vector fields on a small circle (count how often the arrow
  turns). For degrees on S¹, always plot the lifted angle g(θ) and count
  the crossings of a horizontal line with the sign of the slope. Keep a
  table of standard degrees and indices (zⁿ, z̄ⁿ, reflections, antipodal
  maps, sources, saddles). Harder: prove Sard's theorem in general (the
  Milnor proof by induction on dimension); prove the easy Whitney
  embedding theorem into R^(2m+1); prove the Borsuk-Ulam theorem (a map
  S² → R² identifies some pair of antipodal points) using mod 2 degree;
  prove the Hopf theorem that maps Sⁿ → Sⁿ are classified up to homotopy
  by degree; compute the linking number of the Hopf link by the Gauss
  integral; and prove Poincaré-Hopf from the self-intersection of the
  diagonal.


================================================================================
TOPIC 11.18 — UNDERGRADUATE RESEARCH PROJECT / THESIS; READING CLASSIC PAPERS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - THE PROJECT: choosing a question that is small, well defined and new
    to the student (not necessarily to the world); agreeing it with a
    supervisor; a written plan with milestones (reading, first results,
    draft, final version, talk); a weekly log of what was tried,
    including failures; regular meetings with a written agenda.
  - FINDING LITERATURE: MathSciNet and zbMATH (reviews, citation
    tracking forwards and backwards), arXiv (preprints; the math.XX
    categories), the Mathematics Subject Classification (MSC2020: e.g.
    11 number theory, 05 combinatorics, 60 probability, 62 statistics,
    94 information theory, 03 logic, 68 computer science, 01 history),
    OEIS for integer sequences, survey articles, textbooks, and the
    original papers.
  - READING A PAPER: three passes — (1) title, abstract, introduction,
    statements of the main theorems, conclusion; (2) the definitions,
    examples and structure of the proofs; (3) every line, reconstructing
    each proof with the book closed, checking small cases, filling gaps,
    and noting what each hypothesis is used for. Read old papers in a good
    translation with modern notation beside them, and know how rigour and
    notation have changed.
  - CLASSIC PAPERS TO KNOW: Euclid, Elements IX.20 (infinitely many
    primes); Euler 1734/35 (the Basel problem ∑1/n² = π²/6) and 1737 (the
    Euler product); Gauss, Disquisitiones Arithmeticae 1801 (congruences,
    quadratic reciprocity, Gauss sums); Abel 1826 and Cauchy's error on
    series of continuous functions; Riemann 1859 (ζ(s) as a complex
    function); Cantor 1874 and 1891 (uncountability, the diagonal
    argument); Hilbert 1900 (23 problems); Gödel 1931 (incompleteness via
    Gödel numbering); Turing 1936 (computable numbers, the halting
    problem); Shannon 1948 (entropy, coding theorems); Erdős 1947 (the
    probabilistic lower bound for Ramsey numbers).
  - EXPERIMENTAL MATHEMATICS: compute small cases, tabulate, look up the
    sequence, GUESS a formula, test it on further cases, then PROVE it.
    Patterns can fail late (n² + n + 41 at n = 40, circle regions at n = 6,
    Fermat numbers at n = 5, Goldbach's 'other' conjecture at 5777, Euler's
    sum-of-powers conjecture); finite checking is never a proof.
  - HEURISTICS (Pólya): specialise, generalise, look at a special or
    extreme case, work backwards, find an invariant, draw a picture, drop
    a hypothesis and look for a counterexample, strengthen the induction
    hypothesis, find an analogous solved problem.
  - PROOF CHECKING: spot the flawed step in a false proof (division by
    zero, invalid algebra for complex numbers, induction with a broken
    base or step, rearranging divergent series, pointwise versus uniform
    limits, reading a figure); check every quantifier; test the
    statement on examples and on the edge cases of its hypotheses.
  - WRITING: structure of a thesis (abstract, introduction with the main
    results, background, main chapters, examples, conclusion and open
    questions, bibliography); definition–theorem–proof style with
    motivation; LaTeX (amsmath, amsthm environments, \label and \ref,
    BibTeX), correct notation (\sin, \log, \mathbb{R}, \left( \right),
    braces for multi-character exponents); figures produced by code.
  - INTEGRITY: cite every source, including web pages and software;
    quote exactly; never present someone else's proof as your own;
    declare the use of computer algebra and AI tools; keep data and code
    so results can be reproduced.
  - PRESENTING: a 20-minute talk has one main idea, a worked example and
    a sketch (not a full proof); anticipate questions in the oral
    examination (viva): definitions, why each hypothesis is needed, what
    fails without it, what the next question would be.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Reconstruct Euclid's proof (Elements IX.20) that there are
    infinitely many primes. Explain why 'p₁p₂···pₙ + 1 is prime' is a
    misreading, with a counterexample, and give a second proof (Euler's
    or one using n! + 1).
    Answer: every prime factor of N = p₁···pₙ + 1 is a new prime; N itself
    need not be prime: 2·3·5·7·11·13 + 1 = 30031 = 59·509.
    Teacher explains: Euclid's statement: 'prime numbers are more than any
    assigned multitude of primes'. Given any finite list p₁, ..., pₙ of
    primes, put N = p₁···pₙ + 1 > 1. N has a prime factor q (the least
    divisor > 1 of N is prime). If q were some pᵢ, then q divides both N
    and p₁···pₙ, hence their difference 1: impossible. So q is a prime
    NOT in the list; no finite list contains all primes. The proof is
    constructive but does not claim N is prime:
       2 + 1 = 3, 7, 31, 211, 2311 are prime, but
       30031 = 59·509,  510511 = 19·97·277.
    Second proof: for n ≥ 2, any prime factor of n! + 1 exceeds n (every
    k ≤ n divides n!), so there are primes larger than every n. Euler's
    proof (1737): ∏_{p} (1 − 1/p)⁻¹ = ∑ 1/n = ∞, so the product cannot
    be finite; it gives more — ∑ 1/p diverges. Reading lesson: state the
    theorem in modern form, identify the key idea (a number coprime to
    all listed primes), and look for variants. Common mistakes: 'Euclid
    proved it by contradiction' (his proof is direct); claiming the
    Euclid numbers are prime. Now you try: adapt the proof to show there
    are infinitely many primes ≡ 3 (mod 4). (use N = 4p₁···pₙ − 1 with the
    pᵢ ≡ 3 (mod 4); N ≡ 3 (mod 4) has a prime factor ≡ 3 (mod 4), not in
    the list)

D2. Reconstruct Euler's 1734/35 solution of the Basel problem from the
    product sin x/x = ∏(1 − x²/(n²π²)). Which step was not rigorous in
    1735 and how was it justified later? Check the result numerically by
    partial sums, and estimate the tail.
    Answer: ∑ 1/n² = π²/6 ≈ 1.644934; the infinite product needed
    Weierstrass's factorisation theorem (1876). S₁₀ ≈ 1.549768, error
    ≈ 1/10.5 ≈ 0.0952.
    Teacher explains: sin x/x has zeros exactly at x = ±nπ (n ≥ 1) and value
    1 at 0. Euler treats it like a polynomial with these roots:
       sin x/x = (1 − x²/π²)(1 − x²/4π²)(1 − x²/9π²)···
    Compare coefficients of x². From the Taylor series sin x/x =
    1 − x²/6 + x⁴/120 − ..., the coefficient is −1/6. In the product, the
    x² coefficient is −∑ 1/(n²π²). Hence ∑ 1/n² = π²/6. Gap: an entire
    function is NOT determined by its zeros (e^x sin x/x has the same
    zeros); one needs the Weierstrass (or Hadamard) product theorem, or a
    different proof (Fourier series of x², Parseval, or a double integral).
    Euler himself checked his answer numerically to many decimals — a
    model of experimental mathematics. Partial sums: S₁₀ = 1.549768,
    S₁₀₀ = 1.634984, S₁₀₀₀ = 1.643935. The tail ∑_{n>N} 1/n² lies between
    1/(N + 1) and 1/N (compare with ∫ dx/x²) and ≈ 1/(N + ½): for N = 10,
    π²/6 − S₁₀ = 0.095166 ≈ 1/10.5 = 0.095238. Common mistakes: comparing
    x⁴ coefficients without the cross terms; thinking Euler's argument was
    accepted unquestioned (Daniel Bernoulli objected). Now you try:
    compare x⁴ coefficients to find ∑ 1/n⁴. (∑_{m<n} 1/(m²n²π⁴) = 1/120;
    with (∑1/n²)² = ∑1/n⁴ + 2∑_{m<n}1/(m²n²) this gives π⁴/90)

D3. Explain Cantor's diagonal argument (1891) that the set of infinite
    binary sequences is uncountable, apply it to a given list, and deduce
    that R is uncountable and that |A| < |P(A)| for every set A. How does
    it differ from his 1874 proof?
    Answer: the diagonal sequence differs from the n-th listed sequence
    in the n-th place; |P(A)| > |A| by the set {a : a ∉ f(a)}.
    Teacher explains: Suppose s₁, s₂, s₃, ... lists ALL binary sequences.
    Define d by dₙ = 1 − sₙ(n). Then d ≠ sₙ for every n (they differ at
    place n), contradicting completeness of the list. Example:
       s₁ = 0 1 0 1 1 ...
       s₂ = 1 1 0 0 1 ...
       s₃ = 0 0 0 1 0 ...
       s₄ = 1 0 1 1 1 ...
       s₅ = 0 1 1 0 0 ...
    diagonal 0 1 0 1 0, so d begins 1 0 1 0 1. To pass to R, map a
    sequence to ∑ dₙ3⁻ⁿ (base 3 avoids the 0.0111... = 0.1000...
    ambiguity): injective into [0, 1/2]. General theorem: if f : A → P(A),
    let D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)}. If D = f(b), then b ∈ D ⇔ b ∉ f(b) = D:
    contradiction; so no f is onto, while a ↦ {a} is one-to-one: |A| <
    |P(A)|. The 1874 proof used nested intervals: given a sequence of
    reals, build nested closed intervals each avoiding the next term,
    and use completeness — the same idea as in 10.1 D4. The 1891 argument
    is purely combinatorial and applies to ANY set; the same 'diagonal'
    idea reappears in Gödel, Turing and the halting problem. Common
    mistakes: 'adding d to the list fixes it' (the argument applies to
    the new list too); forgetting the two decimal expansions of some
    reals. Now you try: apply the diagonal to 00000..., 11111...,
    01010..., 10101..., 00110.... (diagonal 0, 1, 0, 0, 0; d = 1 0 1 1 1)

D4. Reconstruct Turing's 1936 proof that the halting problem is
    undecidable. Explain how a halting decider would settle Goldbach's
    conjecture, and why this shows the problem cannot be 'just hard'.
    Answer: a decider H yields a program D that halts on its own code iff
    it does not halt: contradiction.
    Teacher explains: Suppose a program H(P, x) always halts and outputs
    'yes' iff program P halts on input x. Build D(P): run H(P, P); if the
    answer is 'yes', loop forever; if 'no', halt. Now run D on its own
    code: D halts on D ⇔ H(D, D) = 'no' ⇔ D does not halt on D.
    Contradiction, so H cannot exist. This is Cantor's diagonal again:
    list programs P₁, P₂, ... and make D differ from Pₙ on input Pₙ. The
    halting set is still SEMI-decidable: run P and say yes if it stops;
    what cannot be done is to say no correctly in general. Goldbach: let
    G search n = 4, 6, 8, ... and halt at the first even n that is NOT a
    sum of two primes. G halts ⇔ Goldbach's conjecture is false. A halting
    decider would answer this, and similarly every Π₁ statement (twin-
    prime-type statements are harder). Turing's paper also defined
    computable numbers and answered Hilbert's Entscheidungsproblem: no
    algorithm decides provability in first-order logic. Common mistakes:
    thinking the proof shows that some SPECIFIC program's halting is
    unknowable (for each fixed program the answer is simply yes or no);
    confusing 'undecidable' with 'unprovable'. Now you try: is it
    decidable whether a program halts within 10⁶ steps? (yes: run it for
    10⁶ steps)

D5. Explain Gödel numbering (1931). With symbol codes 0 ↦ 1, S ↦ 2, = ↦ 3,
    + ↦ 4 and a string s₁s₂...sₖ coded as 2^c₁·3^c₂·5^c₃···, encode
    'S0=S0' and '0+0=0', and decode 9000 and 360. Outline how numbering
    leads to the first incompleteness theorem.
    Answer: 'S0=S0' ↦ 2²·3·5³·7²·11 = 808500; '0+0=0' ↦ 2·3⁴·5·7³·11 =
    3056130; 9000 = 2³·3²·5³ ↦ '=S=' (not a formula); 360 = 2³·3²·5 ↦
    '=S0' (not a formula).
    Teacher explains: Uniqueness of prime factorisation makes the coding
    injective and decodable. 'S0=S0' has codes 2, 1, 3, 2, 1:
       2²·3¹·5³·7²·11¹ = 4·3·125·49·11 = 808500.
    '0+0=0' has codes 1, 4, 1, 3, 1: 2·81·5·343·11 = 3056130. Decoding
    9000 = 2³·3²·5³ gives codes 3, 2, 3: '=S='. Proofs (finite sequences
    of formulas) are coded in the same way, so 'n codes a proof of the
    formula with number m' becomes an arithmetic relation Prf(n, m), which
    Gödel showed is expressible in the theory. The DIAGONAL LEMMA gives a
    sentence G with G ↔ ¬∃n Prf(n, ⌜G⌝) ('I am not provable'). If the
    theory is consistent, G is not provable; if it is ω-consistent
    (Rosser: just consistent), ¬G is not provable either. So any
    consistent, effectively axiomatised theory containing enough
    arithmetic is INCOMPLETE; the second theorem: it cannot prove its own
    consistency. Common mistakes: 'Gödel showed that mathematics is
    inconsistent' or 'some true statements can never be proved in any
    system' (G is provable in a stronger system). Now you try: encode
    '0=0'. (2·3³·5 = 270)

D6. Shannon (1948) characterised entropy by three properties: continuity,
    monotonicity in n for uniform distributions, and the GROUPING rule.
    State the grouping rule, check it for (1/2, 1/3, 1/6), and compute the
    entropy per letter and total information of the strings BANANA and
    MISSISSIPPI (using letter frequencies). What are the limitations of
    this measure?
    Answer: H(1/2, 1/3, 1/6) = H(1/2, 1/2) + ½H(2/3, 1/3) ≈ 1.45915 bits;
    BANANA 1.45915 bits per letter (≈ 8.755 bits in total); MISSISSIPPI
    1.82307 bits per letter (≈ 20.054 bits).
    Teacher explains: GROUPING: a choice may be made in stages; if we first
    decide between the first outcome and 'one of the others', then choose
    among the others, H must be the weighted sum of the stage entropies.
    Check: left side ½ log₂ 2 + (1/3) log₂ 3 + (1/6) log₂ 6 = 0.5 + 0.52832
    + 0.43083 = 1.45915; right side 1 + ½(0.91830) = 1.45915 ✓. Shannon's
    theorem (Appendix 2 of the paper): the only functions with the three
    properties are H = −K ∑ pᵢ log pᵢ. Sketch: A(n) = H(1/n, ..., 1/n)
    satisfies A(mn) = A(m) + A(n) by grouping, and monotonicity forces
    A(n) = K log n; rational p follow by grouping, real p by continuity.
    BANANA: frequencies A 3/6, N 2/6, B 1/6, the same as above: 1.45915
    bits per letter, ×6 = 8.755. MISSISSIPPI: I 4, S 4, P 2, M 1 out of 11:
    1.82307 per letter, ×11 = 20.054. Limitations: letter frequencies
    ignore order (ANANAB gets the same value), so this is only a
    single-letter model; English has ≈ 4.1 bits per letter from letter
    frequencies alone but about 1 bit per letter when long context is
    used (Shannon 1951). Common
    mistakes: confusing log₂ and ln; thinking entropy measures meaning.
    Now you try: entropy per letter of ABRACADABRA. (≈ 2.04037 bits)

D7. Reconstruct Erdős's 1947 proof that R(k, k) > 2^(k/2) for k ≥ 3.
    Compute the largest n for which the exact inequality
    C(n, k)·2^(1 − C(k, 2)) < 1 holds for k = 4, 5, 10, and compare with
    2^(k/2) and with the known R(4, 4) = 18.
    Answer: k = 4: n = 6; k = 5: n = 11; k = 10: n = 100 (against 2⁵ = 32).
    So R(4, 4) > 6, R(5, 5) > 11, R(10, 10) > 100.
    Teacher explains: Colour each edge of Kₙ red or blue independently
    with probability ½. A fixed set of k vertices is monochromatic with
    probability 2·2^(−C(k, 2)). By the union bound,
       P(some k-set is monochromatic) ≤ C(n, k)·2^(1 − C(k, 2)).
    If this is < 1, some colouring has NO monochromatic Kₖ, so R(k, k) > n.
    With n = ⌊2^(k/2)⌋: C(n, k) < nᵏ/k! ≤ 2^(k²/2)/k!, and
       C(n, k)2^(1 − C(k,2)) < 2^(1 + k/2)/k! < 1 for k ≥ 3.
    Exact values: k = 4, C(6, 4)/32 = 15/32 < 1 but C(7, 4)/32 = 35/32:
    n = 6. k = 5: C(11, 5)/512 = 462/512 < 1, C(12, 5) = 792 > 512: n = 11.
    k = 10: C(100, 10)/2⁴⁴ ≈ 0.984 < 1, C(101, 10)/2⁴⁴ ≈ 1.092: n = 100.
    The method proves EXISTENCE without constructing a colouring — no
    explicit construction of comparable quality is known. For k = 4 it is
    weak (R(4, 4) = 18), but asymptotically it gives the exponential lower
    bound √2ᵏ against the upper bound 4ᵏ. Common mistakes: forgetting the
    factor 2 (red OR blue); believing the union bound needs independence
    (it does not). Now you try: expected number of monochromatic triangles
    in a random 2-colouring of K₆. (C(6, 3)/4 = 5)

D8. EXPERIMENTAL MATHEMATICS. Place n points on a circle in general
    position and join all pairs; count the regions for n = 1, ..., 6. Show
    why the guess 2ⁿ⁻¹ fails, find the correct formula and prove it.
    Answer: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 31; regions = C(n, 4) + C(n, 2) + 1 (57 for
    n = 7, 99 for n = 8).
    Teacher explains: Drawing gives 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, then 31, not 32 —
    count carefully with n = 6 in general position (no three chords
    through one point). Finite differences of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 31:
       1, 2, 4, 8, 15 → 1, 2, 4, 7 → 1, 2, 3 → 1, 1
    The fourth differences look constant, which suggests a polynomial of
    degree 4 in n. Proof by counting: each new
    crossing point and each new chord adds one region. Start with 1
    region (the disc). Each chord, drawn one at a time, adds 1 + (number of
    crossings on it). The chords are C(n, 2), the crossings C(n, 4) (every
    4 points give exactly one crossing of the two 'diagonals'). So
       regions = 1 + C(n, 2) + C(n, 4).
    n = 6: 1 + 15 + 15 = 31 ✓; n = 7: 1 + 21 + 35 = 57. (Alternatively use
    Euler's formula V − E + F = 2 for the planar graph.) Lesson: five
    matching values of a sequence prove nothing; a PROOF must explain the
    count. Common mistakes: using a regular hexagon (three diagonals meet
    at the centre: 30 regions); stopping the experiment at n = 5. Now you
    try: predict n = 10. (1 + 45 + 210 = 256 = 2⁸, a coincidence)

D9. TESTING CONJECTURES. (a) Test 'n² + n + 41 is prime for all n ≥ 0'.
    (b) Fermat conjectured that Fₙ = 2^(2ⁿ) + 1 is always prime; Euler (1732)
    found a factor of F₅. Using the fact that every prime factor of F₅
    has the form 64k + 1, show how 641 is found, and verify
    641 | 2³² + 1. (c) What does this teach about conjectures such as
    Euler's sum-of-powers conjecture?
    Answer: (a) prime for n = 0, ..., 39; 40² + 40 + 41 = 41². (b) 641 =
    64·10 + 1 divides F₅ = 4294967297 = 641·6700417. (c) small cases can
    mislead; counterexamples may be large (27⁵ + 84⁵ + 110⁵ + 133⁵ = 144⁵,
    found by computer in 1966).
    Teacher explains: (a) A table n = 0, 1, 2, ... gives 41, 43, 47, 53, 61,
    ..., all prime up to n = 39 (1601). At n = 40: 1600 + 40 + 41 = 1681 =
    41²; indeed n = 40 gives 40·41 + 41 = 41². Seeing WHY it fails (at
    n = 40 and at n = 41 every summand is a multiple of 41) is better than
    finding the failure by brute force. No non-constant polynomial takes only
    prime values. (b) If p | F₅ then 2³² ≡ −1, 2⁶⁴ ≡ 1 (mod p), so the order
    of 2 is 64 and 64 | p − 1. Candidates 64k + 1: 193 (k = 3), 257
    (k = 4), 449 (k = 7), 577 (k = 9), 641 (k = 10) — the primes among them
    are tried in turn. Check 641: 641 = 5·2⁷ + 1 = 5⁴ + 2⁴. Then
    5·2⁷ ≡ −1, so 5⁴·2²⁸ ≡ 1; also 5⁴ ≡ −2⁴; hence −2³² ≡ 1 (mod 641):
    641 | 2³² + 1 ✓. (c) Euler conjectured that at least k k-th powers are
    needed to sum to a k-th power; Lander and Parkin found the fifth-power
    counterexample above by computer search. Common mistakes: 'verified
    for the first 1000 cases, hence true'; forgetting that a single
    counterexample settles a universal statement. Now you try: is 2¹¹ − 1
    prime? (no: 2047 = 23·89, although 11 is prime)

D10. Cauchy's Cours d'analyse (1821) 'proved' that a convergent series of
     continuous functions has a continuous sum. Abel (1826) gave the
     counterexample ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹ sin(nx)/n. Find the sum of Abel's series on
     (−π, π], locate the flaw in Cauchy's argument, and state the
     corrected theorem. Then find the flawed step in the 'proof' that all
     horses have the same colour.
     Answer: the sum is x/2 on (−π, π) and 0 at x = π: discontinuous at
     odd multiples of π. The flaw: the N needed depends on x (pointwise,
     not uniform, convergence). Horses: the step n = 1 → 2 fails (the two
     subsets do not overlap).
     Teacher explains: The Fourier series of x/2 on (−π, π) is
     ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹ sin(nx)/n; at x = π every term is 0. Each term is continuous
     but the sum jumps from π/2 to −π/2 across x = π. Cauchy argued:
     s = s_N + r_N; s_N is continuous and r_N is small for large N, so s is
     continuous. The gap: 'r_N is small' was used simultaneously at x and
     at nearby points x + h with ONE N — true only if sup_x |r_N(x)| → 0,
     i.e. UNIFORM convergence. Near x = π the remainder of Abel's series
     stays of size about π/2 however large N is (Gibbs phenomenon).
     Corrected theorem (Weierstrass, Seidel, Stokes): a uniform limit of
     continuous functions is continuous. HORSES: 'In any set of n + 1
     horses remove one horse, the rest have one colour; remove another,
     the rest have one colour; the two groups overlap, so all have one
     colour.' For n + 1 = 2 the groups {h₁} and {h₂} do not overlap: the
     step P(1) ⇒ P(2) is false, so the induction collapses. Common
     mistakes: looking for the error in the base case (P(1) is true); in
     Cauchy's case 'blaming' the continuity of the terms. Now you try:
     find the flaw in 'a = b ⇒ a² − b² = ab − b² ⇒ (a + b)(a − b) =
     b(a − b) ⇒ a + b = b ⇒ 2 = 1'. (division by a − b = 0)

D11. You are starting a project on 'sums of reciprocals of primes'.
     Describe how to search and classify the literature and how to read a
     paper in three passes. Then write correct LaTeX for: the Euler
     product; the definition of the Möbius function; a theorem
     environment stating Mertens' second theorem; and a reference to it.
     Answer: MSC 11A41 (primes), 11N05 (distribution of primes), 11M06
     (ζ(s)); LaTeX as below.
     Teacher explains: Start from a textbook (Hardy-Wright, Apostol) and a
     survey; search MathSciNet/zbMATH for 'Mertens theorem' and the MSC
     codes; follow citations backwards (what does the paper use?) and
     forwards (who cites it?); check arXiv for recent work; record every
     source in a BibTeX file as you go. Three passes: skim (what is
     claimed?), structure (definitions, lemmas, the proof skeleton),
     details (re-prove with the paper closed; test on numbers:
     ∑_{p≤100} 1/p ≈ 1.8028 against log log 100 + 0.2615 ≈ 1.7887). LaTeX:
        \zeta(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}
                =\prod_{p}\left(1-p^{-s}\right)^{-1}, \qquad \Re s>1
        \mu(n)=\begin{cases} 1 & n=1,\\
                (-1)^{k} & n=p_1\cdots p_k \text{ distinct primes},\\
                0 & p^{2}\mid n \text{ for some prime } p.
               \end{cases}
        \begin{theorem}[Mertens, 1874]\label{thm:mertens}
          $\sum_{p\le x}\frac{1}{p}=\log\log x+M+O\!\left(\frac{1}
          {\log x}\right)$, where $M\approx 0.2615$.
        \end{theorem}
        By Theorem~\ref{thm:mertens}, \dots
     Points: \sum, \prod, \log and \Re are commands (upright), exponents
     with more than one character need braces (p^{-s}), \left( \right)
     scale the brackets, ~ prevents a line break before the reference,
     and \label/\ref keep numbering automatic. Common mistakes: $log x$
     (italic 'log'); x^-s (only the minus is raised); hard-coding
     'Theorem 3'. Now you try: LaTeX for ∫₀^∞ e^(−x²) dx = √π/2.
     (\int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-x^{2}}\,dx=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2})

D12. GENERALISING A RESULT. Starting from Fermat's little theorem
     (a^(p−1) ≡ 1 (mod p) for p ∤ a), (a) generalise to Euler's theorem and
     compute 3²⁰²⁶ mod 100; (b) test the CONVERSE ('if a^(n−1) ≡ 1 (mod n)
     for all a coprime to n, then n is prime') and find the smallest
     counterexample, checking Korselt's criterion; (c) show that the
     Miller-Rabin refinement exposes 561 with base 2. Describe how such an
     investigation becomes a project.
     Answer: (a) a^φ(n) ≡ 1 (mod n) for gcd(a, n) = 1; 3²⁰²⁶ ≡ 29 (mod 100).
     (b) 561 = 3·11·17 (Carmichael). (c) 2³⁵, 2⁷⁰, 2¹⁴⁰, 2²⁸⁰ ≡ 263, 166,
     67, 1 (mod 561): 67 is a square root of 1 other than ±1.
     Teacher explains: (a) Euler: the units mod n form a group of order
     φ(n); by Lagrange the order of a divides φ(n). φ(100) = 40 and
     2026 = 50·40 + 26, so 3²⁰²⁶ ≡ 3²⁶ (mod 100). In fact 3²⁰ ≡ 1 (mod 100)
     (3¹⁰ = 59049 ≡ 49, 49² = 2401 ≡ 1), so 3²⁶ ≡ 3⁶ = 729 ≡ 29. (b) Test
     composites: 341 = 11·31 satisfies 2³⁴⁰ ≡ 1 but 3³⁴⁰ ≡ 56 (mod 341), so
     base 3 exposes it. KORSELT: n is a Carmichael number iff n is
     squarefree and p − 1 | n − 1 for every prime p | n. For 561 = 3·11·17:
     560 is divisible by 2, 10 and 16 ✓. Hence a⁵⁶⁰ ≡ 1 (mod 561) for all a
     coprime to 561; 561 is the least such composite (also 1105 = 5·13·17,
     1729 = 7·13·19). (c) 560 = 2⁴·35. Compute 2³⁵ ≡ 263 and square
     repeatedly: 263² ≡ 166, 166² ≡ 67, 67² ≡ 1 (mod 561). In a field the
     only square roots of 1 are ±1, so 67 proves 561 composite. A project
     would: survey the literature (Pomerance's work, Alford-Granville-
     Pomerance 1994: infinitely many Carmichael numbers), compute the
     Carmichael numbers below 10⁶ with code, formulate and test
     conjectures on their structure (at least three prime factors — prove
     it), and write it up with proofs and tables. Common mistakes:
     applying Euler's theorem when gcd(a, n) > 1; confusing 'probable
     prime' with 'prime'. Now you try: check Korselt for 1729. (1728 is
     divisible by 6, 12 and 18 ✓)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Euclid and the primes.
    Factorise the Euclid numbers p₁···pₙ + 1 (first n primes):
    a) n = 1, 2, 3     b) n = 4          c) n = 5          d) n = 6
    e) n = 7           f) n = 8
    Factorise n! + 1:
    g) n = 3           h) n = 4          i) n = 5          j) n = 6
    k) n = 7
    l) give 5 consecutive composite numbers using n! + k
    m) 4·3 − 1, 4·3·7 − 1, 4·3·7·11 − 1: factorise, and find a prime
       factor ≡ 3 (mod 4) in each
    n) ∏_{p≤7} p/(p − 1) against H₁₀ = ∑_{n≤10} 1/n: which is larger, and
       why?
    o) π(100) and π(1000)
    Answers:
      a) 3, 7, 31 (prime)             b) 211 (prime)
      c) 2311 (prime)                 d) 30031 = 59·509
      e) 510511 = 19·97·277           f) 9699691 = 347·27953
      g) 7            h) 25 = 5²      i) 121 = 11²    j) 721 = 7·103
      k) 5041 = 71²
      l) 6! + 2, ..., 6! + 6 = 722, 723, 724, 725, 726
      m) 11 (prime, 11 ≡ 3); 83 (prime, 83 ≡ 3); 923 = 13·71 (71 ≡ 3)
      n) 2·(3/2)·(5/4)·(7/6) = 35/8 = 4.375 > H₁₀ = 7381/2520 ≈ 2.92897
         (the product is ∑ 1/n over ALL n with prime factors ≤ 7, which
         include 1, ..., 10)
      o) 25; 168

M2. Euler, zeta values and the Basel problem.
    a) ζ(2)                           b) ζ(4)
    c) ζ(6)                           d) ∑ 1/(2k − 1)² (odd squares)
    e) ∑ (−1)ⁿ⁺¹/n²                   f) ∑ 1/n² over n not divisible by 3
    g) ∑ 1/(n(n + 1))                 h) S₁₀ = ∑_{n≤10} 1/n²
    i) S₁₀₀                            j) S₁₀₀₀
    k) the tail estimate 1/(N + ½) for N = 10 and the true tail
    l) Euler product ∏_{p≤5} (1 − p⁻²)⁻¹ and ∏_{p≤7}
    m) probability that two random integers are coprime
    n) ζ(0) and ζ(−1) (analytic continuation)
    o) ζ(−3)                          p) B₂, B₄, B₆ (Bernoulli numbers)
    q) check ζ(4) = −B₄(2π)⁴/(2·4!)
    r) coefficient of x² in sin x/x, and of x⁴
    Answers:
      a) π²/6 ≈ 1.644934              b) π⁴/90 ≈ 1.082323
      c) π⁶/945 ≈ 1.017343            d) π²/8 ≈ 1.233701
      e) π²/12 ≈ 0.822467             f) (8/9)(π²/6) = 4π²/27 ≈ 1.462164
      g) 1            h) ≈ 1.549768   i) ≈ 1.634984   j) ≈ 1.643935
      k) 0.095238; 0.095166
      l) (4/3)(9/8)(25/24) = 25/16 = 1.5625; ×49/48: ≈ 1.595052
      m) 6/π² ≈ 0.607927              n) −1/2; −1/12
      o) 1/120        p) 1/6, −1/30, 1/42
      q) (1/30)·16π⁴/48 = π⁴/90 ✓     r) −1/6; 1/120

M3. Gauss: sums, quadratic residues and Gauss sums.
    a) 1 + 2 + ... + 100              b) 1 + 2 + ... + n
    c) 3 + 7 + 11 + ... + 99          d) 1² + 2² + ... + 10²
    e) 1³ + ... + 10³                 f) 1⁴ + ... + 5⁴ (check the formula
                                         n(n + 1)(2n + 1)(3n² + 3n − 1)/30)
    Legendre symbols:
    g) (2/7)          h) (3/7)          i) (5/11)         j) (7/11)
    k) (2/17)         l) (3/17)         m) (−1/13)        n) (−1/19)
    o) (10/13)        p) (6/23)

    Countable (C) or uncountable (U)?

    g) binary sequences                 h) eventually-zero binary sequences

    o) 11011..., 10001..., 01110..., 00100..., 11111...

    s₁...sₖ ↦ 2^c₁ 3^c₂ 5^c₃ 7^c₄ 11^c₅ ···.

    e) 90             f) 360            g) 9000           h) 1500

    True or false?

    i) A(n) = log₂ n: A(12) − A(3) − A(4)
