M8. Test the conjecture on small cases; find the first counterexample or
    say 'true' (and give the reason).
    a) n² + n + 41 is prime for all n ≥ 0
    b) 2^(2ⁿ) + 1 is prime for all n ≥ 0
    c) 2ᵖ − 1 is prime for every prime p
    d) 31, 331, 3331, ... (k threes then 1) are all prime
    e) every odd n > 1 is a prime or a prime plus twice a square
    f) n points on a circle give 2ⁿ⁻¹ regions
    g) n! + 1 is never a perfect square
    h) p² − 1 is divisible by 24 for every prime p ≥ 5
    i) n⁵ − n is divisible by 30 for every n
    j) 1 + 3 + 5 + ... + (2n − 1) = n²
    k) every even n with 4 ≤ n ≤ 30 is a sum of two primes: give a
       decomposition for 28 and count those of 30
    l) no fifth power is a sum of four fifth powers
    m) n² − 79n + 1601 is prime for all n ≥ 0
    n) the Collatz iteration from every n ≤ 10 reaches 1
    o) every n with φ(n) = n − 1 is prime
    p) if n | 2ⁿ − 2 then n is prime
    Answers:
      a) n = 40: 1681 = 41²
      b) n = 5: 4294967297 = 641·6700417
      c) p = 11: 2047 = 23·89
      d) 333333331 = 17·19607843 (8 threes)
      e) 5777 (then 5993): Goldbach's other conjecture fails
      f) n = 6: 31 regions
      g) false: 4! + 1 = 5², 5! + 1 = 11², 7! + 1 = 71² (Brocard's problem)
      h) true: p² − 1 = (p − 1)(p + 1), divisible by 8 and by 3
      i) true: divisible by 2, 3, 5 by Fermat's little theorem
      j) true (induction or a picture of nested L-shapes)
      k) 28 = 5 + 23 = 11 + 17; 30 = 7 + 23 = 11 + 19 = 13 + 17 (3 ways)
      l) false: 27⁵ + 84⁵ + 110⁵ + 133⁵ = 144⁵
      m) n = 80: 1681 = 41² (it is (n − 40)² + (n − 40) + 41)
      n) true (checked: e.g. 9 takes 19 steps); open in general
      o) true: φ(n) = n − 1 forces every k < n to be coprime to n
      p) n = 341 = 11·31

M9. Experimental mathematics with sequences: give the next term and a
    formula (then prove it).
    a) 1, 4, 9, 16, 25                b) 1, 3, 6, 10, 15
    c) 2, 6, 12, 20, 30               d) 1, 3, 7, 15, 31
    e) 1, 5, 14, 30, 55               f) 1, 9, 36, 100, 225
    g) 2, 5, 10, 17, 26               h) 1, 7, 19, 37, 61
    i) 1, 4, 10, 20, 35               j) 3, 10, 29, 66, 127
    k) 1, 2, 6, 24, 120               l) 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8
    m) 1, 2, 5, 14, 42                n) 1, 2, 5, 15, 52
    o) 0, 1, 2, 9, 44                 p) 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 31
    q) 1/2, 2/3, 3/4 as partial sums of 1/(1·2) + 1/(2·3) + ...
    r) 1, 11, 21, 1211, 111221        s) 0, 1, 1, 2, 4, 7, 13
    t) 1, 3, 11, 43, 171 (aₙ₊₁ = 4aₙ − 1)
    Answers:
      a) 36; n²                       b) 21; n(n + 1)/2
      c) 42; n(n + 1)                 d) 63; 2ⁿ − 1
      e) 91; n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6       f) 441; (n(n + 1)/2)²
      g) 37; n² + 1                   h) 91; 3n(n − 1) + 1 (hexagonal)
      i) 56; C(n + 2, 3)              j) 218; n³ + 2 (third differences 6)
      k) 720; n!                      l) 13; Fibonacci Fₙ₊₁ = Fₙ + Fₙ₋₁
      m) 132; Catalan C(2n, n)/(n + 1) (n = 1, 2, ...)
      n) 203; Bell numbers (set partitions)
      o) 265; derangements Dₙ = (n − 1)(Dₙ₋₁ + Dₙ₋₂)
      p) 57; C(n, 4) + C(n, 2) + 1
      q) 4/5 = ∑_{k≤4} 1/(k(k + 1)); n/(n + 1) (telescoping)
      r) 312211; look-and-say (no closed formula; Conway's constant)
      s) 24; tribonacci aₙ = aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₋₂ + aₙ₋₃
      t) 683; (2·4ⁿ⁻¹ + 1)/3

M10. Find the flawed step in each false 'proof'.
     a) a = b ⇒ a² − b² = ab − b² ⇒ (a + b)(a − b) = b(a − b) ⇒ a + b = b
        ⇒ 2 = 1
     b) 1 = √1 = √((−1)(−1)) = √(−1)·√(−1) = i² = −1
     c) −1 = (−1)^(2/2) = ((−1)²)^(1/2) = 1
     d) ∫ dx/x by parts with u = 1/x, dv = dx gives ∫ dx/x = 1 + ∫ dx/x,
        so 0 = 1
     e) S = 1 − 1 + 1 − ... = (1 − 1) + (1 − 1) + ... = 0 and
        S = 1 − (1 − 1) − ... = 1
     f) all horses have the same colour (induction on n)
     g) x² = x + x + ... + x (x times); differentiate: 2x = 1 + ... + 1 = x
     h) 2 ln(−1) = ln((−1)²) = ln 1 = 0, so ln(−1) = 0
     i) ∑ 1/n converges because 1/n → 0
     j) the staircase curves tend uniformly to the diagonal of the unit
        square and each has length 2, so √2 = 2
     k) Euclid's proof shows that p₁···pₙ + 1 is prime
     l) n² + n + 41 is prime for n = 0, ..., 39, hence for all n
     m) a limit of continuous functions is continuous (Cauchy 1821)
     n) x = 0.999...; 10x = 9.999...; 9x = 9; x = 1: flawed or valid?
     o) every triangle is isosceles (proof from a figure with the angle
        bisector and perpendicular bisector meeting inside the triangle)
     p) P(A|B) = P(B|A), so a test with 99% sensitivity means a positive
        patient is 99% likely to be ill
     Answers:
       a) division by a − b = 0
       b) √(xy) = √x√y fails for negative x, y
       c) (xᵐ)ⁿ = x^(mn) fails for negative bases with fractional exponents
       d) the antiderivatives differ by a constant (C absorbs the 1)
       e) brackets cannot be regrouped in a divergent series
       f) the step 1 → 2: the two subsets do not overlap
       g) the number of terms depends on x (and x need not be an integer)
       h) ln is multivalued on C: ln(z²) ≠ 2 ln z in general (ln(−1) = iπ)
       i) terms → 0 is necessary, not sufficient (harmonic series)
       j) arc length is not continuous under uniform convergence
       k) only its prime FACTORS are new (30031 = 59·509)
       l) finite checking is not proof; n = 40 fails
       m) needs uniform convergence (Abel's counterexample)
       n) valid, once 0.999... is defined as the limit of the partial sums
          (the manipulations are those of convergent series)
       o) the meeting point lies OUTSIDE the triangle; the figure is wrong
       p) confuses sensitivity with PPV; the base rate matters (Bayes)

M11. Writing mathematics: LaTeX and the MSC.
     Write in LaTeX:
     a) ∑_{n=1}^∞ 1/n² = π²/6          b) lim_{n→∞} (1 + 1/n)ⁿ = e
     c) C(n, k) = n!/(k!(n − k)!)       d) ∀ε > 0 ∃δ > 0
     e) {x ∈ R : x² < 2}                f) Z/nZ
     g) ⌊x⌋ and ⌈x⌉                     h) a 2 × 2 matrix with rows (a, b),
                                           (c, d)
     i) e^(iπ) + 1 = 0                  j) ∮_γ f(z) dz = 2πi ∑ Res
     Correct the error:
     k) $sin x + log x$                 l) $x^10$
     m) $e^-x^2$                        n) $(\frac{a}{b})$ with small brackets
     MSC2020 top-level (two-digit) class of:
     o) the Riemann zeta function      p) Ramsey numbers
     q) entropy of a source            r) the halting problem
     s) PCA and Hotelling's T²         t) Euler's life and work
     u) Brownian motion                v) Galois groups
     Answers:
       a) \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}
       b) \lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{n}=e
       c) \binom{n}{k}=\frac{n!}{k!\,(n-k)!}
       d) \forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0
       e) \{x\in\mathbb{R} : x^{2}<2\}
       f) \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
       g) \lfloor x\rfloor, \lceil x\rceil
       h) \begin{pmatrix} a & b\\ c & d \end{pmatrix}
       i) e^{i\pi}+1=0
       j) \oint_{\gamma} f(z)\,dz=2\pi i\sum\operatorname{Res}
       k) $\sin x+\log x$             l) $x^{10}$
       m) $e^{-x^{2}}$                n) $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)$
       o) 11 (11M06)  p) 05 (05D10, 05C55)            q) 94 (94A17)
       r) 03 (03D) or 68              s) 62 (62H)     t) 01 (01A50)
       u) 60 (60J65)  v) 12 (12F10) or 11 (11R32)

M12. Generalising results: state the generalisation and check it.
     a) 1 + 2 + ... + n = n(n + 1)/2 → ∑k²: check n = 10
     b) ∑k³ = (∑k)²: check n = 10
     c) Faulhaber: ∑_{k≤5} k⁴
     d) Fermat → Euler: 3^φ(10) mod 10 and 7^φ(15) mod 15
     e) Wilson: (p − 1)! ≡ −1 (mod p); what is (n − 1)! mod n for composite
        n > 4?
     f) Pythagorean triples (m² − n², 2mn, m² + n²): (m, n) = (2, 1),
        (3, 2), (4, 1), (5, 2)
     g) Heron → Brahmagupta: area of the cyclic quadrilateral with sides
        3, 4, 5, 6
     h) Euler's V − E + F = 2: check cube, octahedron, icosahedron; the
        value for a torus decomposed into 4 × 4 squares
     i) Euclid → primes ≡ 3 (mod 4): factor 4·3·7·11 − 1
     j) Basel → ∑ 1/n² over odd n not divisible by 3
     k) AM-GM from 2 to n numbers: check for 1, 2, 4
     l) Cantor → |P(A)| > |A|: |P(P(∅))| and |P(P(P(∅)))|
     m) the triangle inequality → Minkowski: ‖(1, 2) + (2, −1)‖₃ against
        ‖(1, 2)‖₃ + ‖(2, −1)‖₃
     n) 3 × 3 magic squares → magic constant of an n × n square for
        n = 3, 4, 5
     Answers:
       a) 385 = 10·11·21/6              b) 3025 = 55²
       c) 979           d) 3⁴ = 81 ≡ 1; 7⁸ ≡ 1
       e) 0 (n composite, n > 4); n = 4 gives 2
       f) (3, 4, 5), (5, 12, 13), (15, 8, 17), (21, 20, 29)
       g) √((9−3)(9−4)(9−5)(9−6)) = √360 = 6√10 ≈ 18.974
       h) 8 − 12 + 6, 6 − 12 + 8, 12 − 30 + 20 all = 2; torus 16 − 32 + 16
          = 0 (V − E + F = 2 − 2g)
       i) 923 = 13·71, and 71 ≡ 3 (mod 4)
       j) ζ(2)(1 − 1/4)(1 − 1/9) = π²/9 ≈ 1.096623
       k) (1 + 2 + 4)/3 = 7/3 ≥ ∛8 = 2 ✓
       l) 2; 4
       m) ‖(3, 1)‖₃ = 28^(1/3) ≈ 3.0366 ≤ 2·9^(1/3) ≈ 4.1602 ✓
       n) n(n² + 1)/2: 15, 34, 65

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: read one classic proof a week in a modern edition and
  re-write it in the student's own words and notation, with every
  implicit step made explicit; always compute examples before and after
  reading a proof. Keep a research diary from the first day, and meet
  the supervisor with written questions. Make the student type every
  solution in LaTeX from the start, so that the thesis is not a
  last-minute typing exercise. For experimental work, insist on 'guess,
  test on NEW cases, then prove', and on keeping the code. Harder: read
  Riemann's 1859 paper and check the functional equation numerically;
  read Turing 1936 and program a universal Turing machine; reproduce a
  table from Shannon 1948; find in the OEIS a sequence with a conjectured
  formula and try to prove it; write a 20-page expository essay on one
  classic theorem with its history, modern proofs and open questions, and
  give a 20-minute talk on it to a non-specialist audience.


================================================================================
MIXED REVISION TEST — STAGE 11
================================================================================
Two parts with the same number of questions: Part A is descriptive
(proofs, explanations and worked problems, written with full working),
Part B is mathematical only. Two questions from every topic in each part,
in topic order (11.1, 11.1, 11.2, 11.2, ...). 1 mark each, 72 marks in
all. Use it as two or three timed papers.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE, 36 marks
  1.  State the monotone and dominated convergence theorems. Use the DCT to
      find lim ∫₀^∞ (1 + x/n)⁻ⁿ dx, naming the dominating function.
  2.  Prove that the Cantor set has Lebesgue measure 0 and is uncountable.
  3.  State Hölder's inequality and use it to show L²(0, 1) ⊆ L¹(0, 1) with
      ‖f‖₁ ≤ ‖f‖₂. Give a function in L¹(0, 1) that is not in L²(0, 1).
  4.  Prove that ℓ¹ ⊆ ℓ² with ‖x‖₂ ≤ ‖x‖₁, and give an element of ℓ² that
      is not in ℓ¹.
  5.  Prove that a linear map between normed spaces is continuous iff it is
      bounded. Find the norm of (Tf)(x) = ∫₀ˣ f(t) dt on C[0, 1] with the
      sup norm.
  6.  State the open mapping theorem and deduce: if X is complete under two
      norms with ‖x‖₁ ≤ C‖x‖₂ for all x, then the norms are equivalent.
  7.  Let d ≡ 1 (mod 4) be square-free. Show that the ring of integers of
      Q(√d) is Z[(1 + √d)/2].
  8.  Show that 6 = 2·3 = (1 + √−5)(1 − √−5) are two factorisations into
      irreducibles in Z[√−5], and write (6) as a product of prime ideals.
  9.  Prove the Euler product ζ(s) = ∏ₚ (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ for s > 1 and deduce
      that there are infinitely many primes and that ∑1/p diverges.
 10.  State the prime number theorem in two equivalent forms. Compare
      π(10⁶) = 78498 with 10⁶/ln 10⁶ and with li(10⁶) ≈ 78628.
 11.  Show that S² is a smooth 2-manifold using the two stereographic
      charts, and compute the transition map.
 12.  Compute [X, Y] for X = ∂/∂x, Y = x ∂/∂y on R², and interpret the
      answer by following the flows of X and Y around a small "square".
 13.  Show that SO(3) is a Lie group of dimension 3, that its Lie algebra is
      the skew-symmetric matrices, and that so(3) ≅ (R³, ×).
 14.  Compute exp(tA) for A = [[0, −1], [1, 0]] and identify the
      one-parameter subgroup it gives.
 15.  Prove that a commutative ring is Noetherian iff every ideal is
      finitely generated. State Hilbert's basis theorem.
 16.  Describe Z_(p) (the localisation of Z at the prime p), find all its
      prime ideals and show that it is a local ring.
 17.  Construct the character table of S₃ and decompose the permutation
      representation of S₃ on C³.
 18.  Use ∑(dim)² = |G| and the number of 1-dimensional representations to
      find the dimensions of the irreducible representations of D₄ (order
      8).
 19.  On E : y² = x³ − 2 let P = (3, 5). Compute 2P and explain why P has
      infinite order.
 20.  State the Mordell and Mazur theorems. Use Nagell-Lutz to find the
      torsion subgroup of y² = x³ + 1 over Q.
 21.  Find the genus of a smooth plane quartic, and use Riemann-Hurwitz to
      find the genus of y² = f(x) with f of degree 5 and distinct roots.
 22.  State the Riemann-Roch theorem and find ℓ(D) for a divisor D of
      degree 5 on a curve of genus 2.
 23.  For the Markov chain with P = [[0.7, 0.3], [0.4, 0.6]] find the
      stationary distribution and P(X₂ = 1 | X₀ = 1).
 24.  A symmetric simple random walk starts at 3 and stops at 0 or 10. Find
      the probability that it reaches 10 first and the expected duration.
 25.  For the logistic map x ↦ rx(1 − x) find the fixed points and their
      stability, and explain what happens at r = 3.
 26.  Show that the doubling map x ↦ 2x mod 1 has Lyapunov exponent ln 2
      and exactly 2ⁿ − 1 points of period dividing n.
 27.  Prove Euler's formula v − e + f = 2 for connected plane graphs and
      deduce that K₅ and K₃,₃ are not planar.
 28.  Use the matrix-tree theorem to count the spanning trees of K₄, and
      check your answer with Cayley's formula.
 29.  Find the entropy of the source with probabilities 1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/8
      and construct a Huffman code for it. Compare.
 30.  Find the capacity of the binary symmetric channel with crossover
      probability 0.11, and state Shannon's channel coding theorem.
 31.  Carry out PCA for the covariance matrix [[5, 2], [2, 2]]: principal
      directions and the proportion of variance explained by the first.
 32.  Explain the bias-variance decomposition of prediction error and how
      ridge regression trades one against the other.
 33.  State Sard's theorem and use it to prove that there is no smooth
      retraction of the closed ball Dⁿ onto Sⁿ⁻¹. Deduce Brouwer's theorem.
 34.  Find the degree of z ↦ zⁿ on S¹ and of the antipodal map on Sⁿ, and
      deduce the hairy ball theorem for S².
 35.  Describe how to read a classic paper, and summarise Euclid's proof
      that there are infinitely many primes as it would appear in a thesis
      introduction.
 36.  Test the conjecture "n² + n + 41 is prime for every n ≥ 0" and explain
      the roles of examples and counterexamples in research.

ANSWER KEY
  Part A (accept any clear, correct method and reasoning)
   1. MCT: 0 ≤ fₙ ↑ f ⇒ ∫fₙ → ∫f; DCT: fₙ → f a.e., |fₙ| ≤ g ∈ L¹ ⇒ ∫fₙ →
      ∫f; (1 + x/n)ⁿ increases in n, so for n ≥ 2 the integrand is ≤ g =
      (1 + x/2)⁻², ∫₀^∞ g = 2; the limit is ∫₀^∞ e⁻ˣ dx = 1
   2. Cₙ is 2ⁿ intervals of length 3⁻ⁿ, so m(C) ≤ (2/3)ⁿ → 0; C = numbers
      with ternary digits 0 and 2 only, and halving the digits maps C onto
      [0, 1] (binary expansions), so C is uncountable
   3. ∫|fg| ≤ ‖f‖ₚ‖g‖_q (1/p + 1/q = 1); with g = 1, p = q = 2: ‖f‖₁ ≤
      ‖f‖₂ · 1; f(x) = x^(−1/2)
   4. |xₙ| ≤ ‖x‖₁ for each n, so ∑|xₙ|² ≤ ‖x‖₁∑|xₙ| = ‖x‖₁²; xₙ = 1/n
   5. bounded ⇒ ‖Tx − Ty‖ ≤ ‖T‖‖x − y‖ (Lipschitz); continuous at 0 ⇒
      some δ with ‖x‖ ≤ δ ⇒ ‖Tx‖ ≤ 1, so ‖Tx‖ ≤ ‖x‖/δ; |Tf(x)| ≤ x‖f‖∞ ≤
      ‖f‖∞ with equality for f = 1 at x = 1: ‖T‖ = 1
   6. the identity (X, ‖·‖₂) → (X, ‖·‖₁) is a bounded linear bijection of
      Banach spaces, so by the open mapping theorem its inverse is
      bounded: ‖x‖₂ ≤ C′‖x‖₁
   7. α = a + b√d (a, b ∈ Q) is integral iff its trace 2a and norm a² − db²
      are integers; for d ≡ 1 (mod 4) this allows a, b ∈ ½ + Z together
      (and a, b ∈ Z), which is exactly Z[(1 + √d)/2]
   8. N(a + b√−5) = a² + 5b²: N(2) = 4, N(3) = 9, N(1 ± √−5) = 6; no element
      has norm 2 or 3, so all four are irreducible, and they are not
      associates (units ±1); (2) = P², P = (2, 1 + √−5); (3) = QQ′, Q =
      (3, 1 + √−5), Q′ = (3, 1 − √−5); (6) = P²QQ′
   9. (1 − p⁻ˢ)⁻¹ = ∑ₖ p⁻ᵏˢ; multiplying over p ≤ N gives ∑ n⁻ˢ over n with
      all prime factors ≤ N (unique factorisation), which tends to ζ(s);
      finitely many primes would keep ζ(s) bounded as s → 1⁺, but ζ(s) ≥
      ∑_{n≤M} n⁻ˢ → H_M; ln ζ(s) = ∑p⁻ˢ + O(1) → ∞, so ∑1/p = ∞
  10. π(x) ~ x/ln x (ratio → 1) ⇔ ψ(x) = ∑_{n≤x} Λ(n) ~ x; 10⁶/ln 10⁶ ≈
      72382 (error ≈ 6116), li(10⁶) ≈ 78628 (error ≈ 130): li is far
      better
  11. σ_N(x, y, z) = (x, y)/(1 − z) on S² ∖ {N}, σ_S(x, y, z) = (x, y)/(1 +
      z) on S² ∖ {S}; σ_S ∘ σ_N⁻¹(u) = u/|u|² on R² ∖ {0}, smooth with
      smooth inverse (itself)
  12. [X, Y]f = ∂ₓ(x f_y) − x ∂_y f_x = f_y, so [X, Y] = ∂/∂y; X for time s
      then Y for time t: (x + s, y + t(x + s)); Y then X: (x + s, y + tx);
      the gap st in the y-direction is the bracket
  13. SO(3) = F⁻¹(I) ∩ {det > 0} for F(A) = AᵀA into symmetric matrices
      (dimension 6); I is a regular value, so dim = 9 − 6 = 3; group
      operations are polynomial (smooth); T_I = {X : X + Xᵀ = 0}; the map
      a ↦ [a]× (the matrix of v ↦ a × v) satisfies [[a]×, [b]×] = [a × b]×
  14. A² = −I, so exp(tA) = (cos t)I + (sin t)A = [[cos t, −sin t],
      [sin t, cos t]]: the rotations, SO(2) ≅ S¹
  15. ACC ⇒ f.g.: otherwise choose a₁, a₂, ... with (a₁) ⊊ (a₁, a₂) ⊊ ...;
      f.g. ⇒ ACC: the union of a chain is an ideal, its finitely many
      generators lie in some Iₙ, so the chain stops there; Hilbert: R
      Noetherian ⇒ R[x] Noetherian
  16. Z_(p) = {a/b : p ∤ b}; its ideals are 0 and pᵏZ_(p); primes: 0 and
      pZ_(p); every element outside pZ_(p) is a unit, so pZ_(p) is the
      unique maximal ideal (a local ring, in fact a DVR)
  17. classes e, (12)-type (3 elements), (123)-type (2): trivial (1, 1, 1),
      sign (1, −1, 1), standard (2, 0, −1); permutation character (3, 1, 0)
      = trivial + standard
  18. D₄/[D₄, D₄] ≅ Z₂ × Z₂ gives four 1-dimensional representations; 8 −
      4 = 4 = 2²: dimensions 1, 1, 1, 1, 2
  19. λ = 3x²/(2y) = 27/10, x(2P) = λ² − 6 = 129/100, y(2P) = λ(3 − 129/100)
      − 5 = −383/1000; 2P has non-integral coordinates, so by Nagell-Lutz
      2P, hence P, is not torsion
  20. Mordell: E(Q) ≅ Zʳ ⊕ T, T finite; Mazur: T ≅ Zₙ (1 ≤ n ≤ 10 or 12)
      or Z₂ × Z₂ₙ (1 ≤ n ≤ 4); for y² = x³ + 1, torsion points have y = 0
      or y² | 27: O, (−1, 0), (0, ±1), (2, ±3); (2, 3) has order 6: T ≅ Z₆
  21. g = (d − 1)(d − 2)/2 = 3; x : C → P¹ has degree 2, branched at the 5
      roots and at ∞: 2g − 2 = 2(−2) + 6, g = 2
  22. ℓ(D) − ℓ(K − D) = deg D − g + 1; deg(K − D) = 2 − 5 < 0, so ℓ(K − D) =
      0 and ℓ(D) = 5 − 2 + 1 = 4
  23. πP = π: 0.3π₁ = 0.4π₂, π = (4/7, 3/7); P²₁₁ = 0.7² + 0.3 × 0.4 = 0.61
  24. Xₙ is a martingale: 10p = 3, p = 3/10; E T = k(N − k) = 3 × 7 = 21
  25. fixed points 0 and 1 − 1/r (for r > 1); f′(0) = r, f′(1 − 1/r) = 2 −
      r: 0 stable for r < 1, 1 − 1/r stable for 1 < r < 3; at r = 3 the
      derivative passes −1: a period-doubling bifurcation creates a stable
      2-cycle
  26. |f′| = 2 everywhere, so λ = lim (1/n) ln|(fⁿ)′| = ln 2 > 0 (nearby
      points separate like 2ⁿ); fⁿ(x) = x ⇔ (2ⁿ − 1)x ∈ Z: x = k/(2ⁿ − 1),
      k = 0, ..., 2ⁿ − 2
  27. induction on e: a tree has e = v − 1, f = 1; deleting an edge of a
      cycle lowers e and f by 1; each face has ≥ 3 edges and each edge
      borders ≤ 2 faces: 3f ≤ 2e ⇒ e ≤ 3v − 6, but K₅ has 10 > 9;
      triangle-free: e ≤ 2v − 4, but K₃,₃ has 9 > 8
  28. L = 4I − J; a cofactor is det(4I₃ − J₃) = 4 · 4 · 1 = 16 (eigenvalues
      4, 4, 1); Cayley: 4⁴⁻² = 16
  29. H = 1/2 + 2/4 + 3/8 + 3/8 = 1.75 bits; Huffman lengths 1, 2, 3, 3
      (0, 10, 110, 111), average 1.75 = H (dyadic probabilities)
  30. C = 1 − H₂(0.11) ≈ 1 − 0.49992 ≈ 0.5 bits per use; every rate R < C
      is achievable with error probability → 0, and no rate R > C is
  31. λ² − 7λ + 6 = 0: λ = 6, 1; directions (2, 1)/√5 and (1, −2)/√5;
      6/7 ≈ 85.7%
  32. E(y − f̂(x))² = σ² + bias² + variance; β̂_ridge = (XᵀX + λI)⁻¹Xᵀy
      shrinks the coefficients: larger λ, more bias, less variance; choose
      λ by cross-validation
  33. critical values of a smooth map have measure 0; a retraction r : Dⁿ
      → Sⁿ⁻¹ has a regular value y, and r⁻¹(y) is a compact 1-manifold whose
      boundary is r⁻¹(y) ∩ Sⁿ⁻¹ = {y}, a single point: impossible (compact
      1-manifolds have an even number of boundary points); a map f : Dⁿ →
      Dⁿ with no fixed point gives such a retraction (ray from f(x) through
      x)
  34. deg zⁿ = n; the antipodal map is a product of n + 1 reflections:
      degree (−1)ⁿ⁺¹; a nowhere-zero tangent field v on Sⁿ gives the
      homotopy cos(πt)x + sin(πt)v(x)/|v(x)| from the identity to the
      antipodal map, so 1 = (−1)ⁿ⁺¹ and n is odd; not on S²
  35. three passes: (1) title, abstract, introduction, statements; (2) the
      examples, figures and structure of the argument; (3) every proof,
      redoing each computation; Euclid: for any finite list of primes, N
      = p₁p₂⋯pₙ + 1 leaves remainder 1 on division by each, so a prime
      factor of N is a new prime
  36. primes for n = 0, ..., 39; n = 40 gives 1681 = 41² (and n = 41 gives
      41 × 43); examples suggest and test conjectures, one counterexample
      disproves a universal statement, only a proof establishes it

Scoring guide (out of 72):
  62-72 Ready for Stage 12 (master's / first-year graduate coursework).
  46-61 Nearly there — practise the topics of the missed questions for
        two weeks, then try those questions again.
  0-45  Keep working through Stage 11 (revise Stage 10 if needed); test
        again in a month.
  Compare the two parts: strong in B but weak in A means the student needs
  more practice writing proofs and explanations; strong in A but weak in B
  means more drill practice for speed and accuracy.


================================================================================
END OF STAGE 11 — READINESS CHECKLIST FOR STAGE 12
================================================================================
The student is ready for Stage 12 (master's / first-year graduate
coursework and qualifying exams) when they can usually:
  Analysis
  [ ] Build Lebesgue measure, decide measurability, and justify every
      exchange of limit, sum, derivative and integral with the monotone
      and dominated convergence theorems and Fubini-Tonelli
  [ ] Work in Lp and ℓp: Hölder, Minkowski, completeness, inclusions,
      density and duality
  [ ] Work in Banach and Hilbert spaces: operator norms, projections,
      orthonormal bases, adjoints, and apply Hahn-Banach, the open mapping
      and closed graph theorems and uniform boundedness
  Number theory and arithmetic geometry
  [ ] Compute rings of integers, discriminants, norms and traces, factor
      primes into prime ideals and compute small class numbers
  [ ] Use Dirichlet series, Euler products and arithmetic functions, and
      explain the prime number theorem and its link to ζ(s)
  [ ] Add points on elliptic curves, count points over finite fields and
      find torsion subgroups over Q
  Geometry, algebra and topology
  [ ] Work with charts, smooth maps, tangent spaces, vector fields, flows
      and Lie brackets on manifolds
  [ ] Find Lie algebras of matrix groups, compute exponentials, brackets
      and dimensions of classical groups
  [ ] Use Noetherian rings, localisation, prime spectra and Krull
      dimension
  [ ] Build character tables of small groups and decompose
      representations using orthogonality
  [ ] Compute genera of curves (degree formula, Riemann-Hurwitz) and use
      Riemann-Roch on Riemann surfaces
  [ ] Use regular values, transversality, Sard's theorem and degree
      theory (Brouwer, hairy ball, Poincaré-Hopf)
  Applied and discrete mathematics
  [ ] Classify Markov chain states, find stationary distributions and
      hitting probabilities, and use Poisson processes and random walks
  [ ] Analyse fixed points, periodic orbits, bifurcations, Lyapunov
      exponents and fractal dimensions of dynamical systems
  [ ] Prove structural results about graphs (planarity, colouring,
      matchings, flows) and run and analyse the standard graph algorithms
  [ ] Compute entropy, mutual information and channel capacity, build
      optimal codes and state Shannon's coding theorems
  [ ] Carry out multivariate analysis (multivariate normal, PCA,
      discriminant analysis) and fit and validate basic learning models
  Research
  [ ] Read a classic paper critically, test conjectures by computation,
      find counterexamples, and write a clear, correctly referenced
      project report in LaTeX, then present it in a talk

Graduate real, functional and complex analysis, graduate algebra,
algebraic topology, differential geometry, measure-theoretic probability
and PDE — together with a first specialisation — are the main new work
of Stage 12.
================================================================================
